# The Astrans

# Part I The Spark

## 

## **Chapter 1: The Tokaplex**

| Price of *³He* | $20,000,000/kg |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** | **20 kg/year** |
| **Current Consumption** | **12 kg/year** |
| **Time Since Start** | **0 to 1 day** |

Dr. Elin Sarai braced against the cold wind as she checked the pressure readings on her slate. *Ilha Fusão* sprawled across an artificial island fifty miles off Lisbon. *D* would meet *³He*, releasing clean fusion energy. The tokamak had worked before, but today wasn’t a test. This was a live connection to Portugal’s grid. One hundred megawatts wasn’t enough to change the world. But it would prove fusion could feed a commercial grid.

The wind shifted, blowing her hair across her face. When she turned back to go inside, she saw Gonçalo Silva coming out.

He joined her on the walkway and looked across the island. “*Bom dia, doutora.*” He looked like he had been up all night. “The *³He* tank is installed. We are ready.”

Elin looked out to sea. “That tank nearly bankrupted us. It’s half the world’s production.” She looked back at Gonçalo. “Are we ready?”

“She’s an angry old *senhora* who’s waited a long time to feel important.”

Gonçalo’s remark lingered in her mind. She knew he meant the plant, but his words fit her, too. Today, this plant would show the world what it could do.

She pulled up the system checklist on her slate. Two weeks ago, it had been full of red and yellow flags. Now there was only a single yellow flag: a pressure differential that was outside expectations but within tolerance. She kept her eyes on the slate. A red warning flickered briefly as the deuterium extractor started up, then turned to green when the flow became steady. Another yellow flickered on her slate, then went green again. “*Valha-nos Deus*,” she muttered. Working this long near Lisbon had affected the curses she used.

A soft rumble passed beneath them. Deuterium tanks were hissing. Trace amounts of *³He* were being let out in tiny bursts. Somewhere far below, in the containment torus, the *D-³He* reaction chamber was beginning its warm-up cycle. The corridor curved, descending in a spiral toward the turbine hall. Elin led the way. They passed a crew of techs hunched over an access panel. In front of them, three massive units stood silent, waiting for steam. Elin paused at the observation window. These were conventional technology, superheated steam driving generator rotors, maybe forty percent efficient if they were lucky. Functional, proven, but wasteful.

“This is the bottleneck,” she said. “We’re extracting thermal energy with nineteenth-century technology.”

Gonçalo shrugged. “Efficiency was never the point. It works. And for a hundred megawatts, it’s enough.”

“For now,” Elin replied.

Each level had its own voice, its own smell. Salt and metal. Polymer and heat. And everywhere vibration, small, subsonic, and constant. Like the plant was holding its breath.

By the time they reached the observation ring around the containment torus, Elin was sweating through her collar. Gonçalo keyed the access door.

Inside, the world changed. The torus was not quiet, but it was sacred. A ring of magnet housings loomed around a core of dense shielding. Inside, a reaction chamber poised to hold a plasma at six hundred million degrees. Cameras fed real-time diagnostics to the translucent wall panels.

The field was up. The magnets were humming.

“I never get used to it,” Gonçalo said.

Elin shook her head. “You shouldn’t.”

She took a last look at the checklist. The yellow flag was still there, but the pressure had not moved. She signed off on it. “*Está pronta*.” 

Gonçalo looked down at his slate, now showing her approval. “You know what Raines told me yesterday?”

Elin sighed. “Do I want to know?”

“He said the press has to see the helium loading into the reactor live on camera. He’s planning to let them into the control room.”

She laughed. It was sharp and brief. “I talked to him about that; there is no way the press or anyone else is going into the control room. I mounted a large twin-blade knife switch on the wall so they can all watch him throw it. The engineers will know what to do. I also wired an Edison bulb to the generator, up above the switch. As the turbine spins up, the bulb will glow.”

After the inspection, they found Raines near the conference room, surrounded by investors, dignitaries, and the Minister of Energy from Argentina, who seemed excited and had already begun hinting at the possibility of a second plant, maybe closer to the tropics. Raines was in his element. Laughing, clasping shoulders, turning charm into sales contracts.

He saw Elin and immediately excused himself, cutting the circle short with a gesture and a few well-oiled words. The group didn’t mind. 

“Elin,” he said, voice warm and certain, like he already knew the answer. “She’s ready, isn’t she?”

She didn’t nod. Just looked him in the eye. “She’ll hold.”

He exhaled and grinned, letting the tension slide off him like a coat. “That’s all I need to hear.”

Raines clapped her gently on the shoulder. “I know how much you hate this day. But thank you.”

“Don’t thank me until she makes it through the week.”

“Deal.”

He turned, buoyant again, already slipping back into his public face, back into the performance of selling the dream to the world. 

They came by tiltrotor and by ferries. Premiers and presidents in motorcades threaded the island’s narrow ring road. The King of England, dressed with understated dignity, arrived by helicopter. Billionaires who had staked fortunes were there, and a small press corps trimmed to just twenty, each one combed, curated, and cleared.

They all gathered in the Assembly Hall. Polished steel and glass under a vaulted ceiling. There was seating for two hundred. Everyone had a view of the oversized screen, and beyond it, the observation pane that stared down to the reactor.

Tobias Raines stepped up to the podium, relaxed, with a smile on his lips.

“When I created Helion Dynamics,” he said, stepping out from behind the podium, “fusion was a fantasy told by physicists. I found a few who believed that fantasy. We needed someplace to build, but the fear of unproven nuclear power was strong. Lisbon agreed to let us build off her shores. We built this island with our own hands. Nothing worked. We’ve replaced the tokamak five times. But now we have tamed the reaction. We have made something real. The world is hungry for energy. Today, this marvel will feed the world’s hunger.”

When he finished, the screen retracted into the ceiling, revealing a switch and a bulb. The audience began murmuring and whispering to each other. Raines held up his hand, and the crowd went silent. He grabbed the green glass handle firmly, lifting it. The twin blades of the knife switch were a foot long, heavy, polished copper. Bracing with his legs, he drove it home.

An indicator light came on behind the glass in the control room. The supervisor called out. “Raines is ready.”

A technician set down his *bica* and pulled gently on a lever. Engineers moved like ghosts across the command tier. The reactor hissed. Somewhere deep in her belly, valves opened. *D* met *³He*.

There was no explosion. No flash. Just the warm, unremarkable glow of a single, antique Edison bulb wired to a ceremonial loop.

It came to life slowly, dimly at first, then glowing brilliantly.

The room erupted in applause.

\*\*\*

Hours later, after the reception emptied, Elin and Raines stood in the observation room, watching the Edison bulb glow. The plant hummed steadily beneath them. 

Elin pulled herself onto a chair, resting her arms on the table, bowing her head.

Raines turned, gesturing broadly with his arms. “Argentina’s in,” he said. “They claim fusion is central to their national industrialization plans. They plan to have an artificial island off the coast ready in six months, large enough for a ten-gigawatt tokaplex. How soon can we have it built?”

She slowly turned to him. “That scale would require a ton of *³He* every year. We don’t have that. We’ve talked about this before.”

“Elin, I know it is expensive, but you have no idea how much money they will give, now that it is running. I have already scheduled calls with suppliers. They are charging about twenty thousand dollars per gram.”

“There are no suppliers at the scale you’re talking about. The nuclear industry only produces about twenty kilograms per year. Total. We need tons.”

Her eyes moved back to the numbers on her slate, tracking the plasma and first-wall temperatures.

“That’s fine. That’s what the industry is doing today. But we can breed tritium. It decays to become *³He*.”

“Yes, that’s viable,” Elin answered. “But it’s a few kilograms, and depends on scraping traces from fission systems that were not built for this. And tritium has a half-life of a decade. That trickle is enough to keep this tokaplex running, but not a larger tokaplex.”

“Tritium is only a part of the picture. I know a supplier pulling helium from the Minnesota Topaz site. He can get a thousand tons for the right price.” 

“The Topaz site is very rich in *³He*. But that thousand tons might hold a hundred grams of it,” she said. “The rest is helium ash. That’s not helpful for us. Tobias, *Ilha Fusão* was built to prove fusion can power electric generation. We have proved that. But if we don’t open up more *³He* reserves, this tokaplex is nothing but a curio.”

Tobias Raines said nothing. He watched the Edison bulb powered by their steady reactor, then turned toward the window.

Elin was watching the gibbous Moon rise.

## 

## **Chapter 2: Helium Fever**

| Price of *³He* | $8,000,000/kg |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** | **1 ton/year** |
| **Current Consumption** | **12 kg/year** |
| **Time Since Start** | **1 week to 2 months** |

Li Yun Tan was alone in her apartment, watching the news. Locally, a traffic accident in downtown Phoenix resulted in multiple injuries. The ambulances moved freely to the intersection, lights flashing, traffic moving aside. In Kuala Lumpur, when she was twelve, the streets had been gridlocked for hours.

In financial news, the story opened with canned footage of the Edison bulb in Ilha Fusão. Helion Dynamics was offering a two-year price guarantee of eight million dollars per kilogram for lunar *³He*, and a loan of up to one million dollars for transit.

She looked around her apartment; no one to call, no debt, no lease, just a photo of her and her parents when she was twelve. She opened her accounts and worked through the numbers. Her savings, plus accrued vacation time from Arizona Public Service, were enough to buy the necessary gear. Getting that gear into space was the real cost. Heavy lift from Texas was the cheapest way to get up, at twenty dollars per kilogram. There was a Russian ferry operator that could haul up to twenty-five tons from low Earth orbit to the Moon, but he was charging as much as heavy lift. The transit loan terms were steep; twenty percent interest, but they let her pay it back in *³He*. With a steady hand, she hit the “APPLY” button.

She waited as they processed her application. Every day, more people were headed to the Moon. Big corporations were building advanced equipment and sending well-outfitted crews. Others were cobbling together used equipment and going on their own, all of them driven by the fever for *³He*.

Li didn’t tell anyone at work. Every day when she got home, she checked the claim map, watching as clusters of claims grew. The *³He* distribution was nearly uniform; she knew it didn’t matter. Still, she wondered what they knew that she didn’t. She hoped the good sites wouldn’t all be gone by the time she got the loan. If she got the loan. 

She was in the office at her desk when she got the notification on her slate. Work suddenly meant nothing. She stopped by HR and quit, leaving immediately. On the way home, she began checking Craigslist, under Resumes/Moonwork for an equipment operator. She found Darrin Brooks maintaining offshore turbines in the North Sea. For years, he had logged every inspection and checked each seal twice. That was enough. She offered him a contract, passage included. Darrin looked up at the Moon and said yes within an hour.

Li went through a lawyer to get the claim. Each claim was limited to one square mile per person, which, on average, could yield just over one ton of *³He*. By including Darrin Brooks on her paperwork, she was able to file a claim for two square miles in the southwest corner of Ball Crater. She got the paperwork back three days later. Her name was written as Tan, Li Yun. Fixing it would take a week; new claims were being filed every day. Her name was now Tan. She sold what remained of her assets and met Darrin in Tucson, at the market that had grown beside the Air Force Boneyard.

The vendors at the Lunar Supply Market traded in surplus and salvage, ex-military and scientific equipment, and a few new components from specialty manufacturers. They started at the aisle of helium extractors.

Li found large ovens with ten-ton tumblers and one-minute cycle times. The power rating was hundreds of megawatts. 

Darrin called her across the aisle. “We can’t run those. That’s for industrial operations. RF extractors don’t heat the rock, just the gas.”

Li joined him. “How does that work?”

Darrin was reading a brochure. “It uses something called ‘Larmor resonance.’ The magnets adjust it, and the RF generator excites the *³He* atoms more than it excites the other atoms.”

“So we get pure *³He*?”

“We’ll be lucky to get five percent, but it is still a huge savings in bottling and shipping.”

Darrin was looking for trusted brands. Li kept her eye on the power requirements and the shipped weight. 

They started looking at one extractor because it mounted on stilts to let them dump processed regolith easily, and had a large hopper so collection could be independent of operation. 

The vendor talked them through the operation. “On the moon, regolith isn’t all the same. You’ve got ilmenite and other magnetic minerals mixed unevenly with anorthosite and other non-magnetic minerals. You can see sometimes it’s darker, sometimes lighter. When you run it, you adjust the magnetic field strength to compensate. That’s what the levers are for.”

That made no sense to Li. “How would I know where to set the levers? Test the load before it goes in?”

“If only it were that simple. You can’t see directly what the right field strength is, but you can see the effect it is having. You watch this screen. A mass spec is sampling just a few atoms at a time every few seconds. The screen will show peaks representing how much *³He*, *⁴He*, and hydrogen you are pulling.”

“So I keep the levers where I am getting more *³He* than hydrogen.”

“Yes and no. *³He* is rare. You will always get more hydrogen and *⁴He* than you get *³He*. You maximize the *³He* ratio. Keep it cold, watch the hydrogen and *⁴He*. If you let the field get too weak, hydrogen gets released faster. If it is too strong, more of the energy heats the rock, and your *⁴He* extraction increases. You want to live in the sweet spot between them. Don’t just set the levers. You keep adjusting them the entire time. Generally, your purity will start to fall after you extract about half the *³He*. That’s a good time to stop and process another batch.”

Li pulled out her slate and took a few notes. She and Darrin stepped a few stalls away. “Darrin, I know you like this one, but it weighs twelve hundred pounds more than some others, and it will cramp our budget.”

“It weighs more, mostly because of the regolith feeding equipment. That will make mining much more productive. The other ones that have hoppers and stilts weigh as much.”

“Lunar regolith eats everything regardless of reliability.”

“This one is built for easy repair.”

Li looked at a few more extractors, but Darrin was right. She returned and bought the one he recommended.

Darrin had started looking at living quarters while Li finished the extractor purchase. He found one he liked and called Li over. “Hey, what do you think of this? Dual Bosch air recyclers and a top-quality airlock.”

“Are you kidding? Other huts have much bigger airlocks.”

“I know, but this is from a top manufacturer, durable and solid. And small is better. Every time we cycle the airlock, we lose oxygen. The smaller the volume, the less we lose.”

Li studied the layout. It was a tiny Quonset hut, nine feet square, the size of a four-man tent. The bed was a three-quarter mattress, four feet across. The bathroom was just an alcove, without a door or even a curtain, and the shower had a hose with a low-volume mist emitter. The kitchen cabinet supported a small microwave oven with an electric heating coil on top. 

Li pointed to the eighteen-inch window, which was packed separately. “What’s this for?”

The vendor looked at it, a hint of amusement in his eyes. “People on the Moon like to look at Earth. When you anchor the hut, you can cut a small hole in the roof, and this will fit in and seal up the hole. You align it to frame Earth. Because the Moon is tidally locked, Earth doesn’t move much. Pretty much every hut on the near side has an Earth window.”

She checked the price. It was higher than most huts of comparable size, but the compact dimensions and low mass made launch affordable.

It came with a heavy crate containing the life support core. The core was a column of cylindrically wound polypropylene tubing with embedded LEDs, configured as a closed algal loop. It took in wet and dry waste and stale air, returning oxygen, heat, light, and edible biomass.

“The hand grinder and impeller are more work, but it’s one less motor to maintain,” Li said, checking the heat welds. “This closes the nutrient loop.”

Darrin stared at the emerald-stained plastic, his face paling. “Nutrient loop? We have to eat that?”

Li wrinkled her nose. “Not appealing, but we are a long way from Earth. I’m also buying a seed package so we can grow microgreens and sprouts. We’ll manage.”

They looked at rovers and crawlers next. Li liked the faster, lighter rovers for covering ground quickly. But Darrin shook his head. “We need to haul twenty tons of gear to the site, then move hundreds of tons of regolith once we’re there. The crawlers are slower, but they can handle the load.”

Li bought the crawler, but wasn’t completely satisfied. “We won’t always have a full load.”

They got to the comms section. Li was looking at the radios. “Life up there will be strange, always saying ‘copy’ and ‘over’.”

A vendor stepped up. “People don’t do that much. They use Local Band. Here’s a headset, try it out. Don’t worry about etiquette, just talk naturally.”

Li and Darrin both put one on. Talking felt normal. They could both talk at once, hear other people clearly when close, while voices faded with distance. 

She listened carefully, hearing two other conversations at the same time. “So natural.” 

“It is an AM band. Multiple signals can add together, just like talking here on Earth. The one odd thing is that sometimes you get a heterodyne squeal when two systems get close for the first time. Just a short chirp.”

“So everyone can hear everything we say?”

“Most radio sets have a few encoded FM bands for restricted conversations. Then you have to use terms like ‘copy’.”

They collected all their gear and weighed it. Fifteen tons. Li bought ten tons of solar panels to reach the twenty-five-ton limit. They loaded everything into a semi-trailer and hauled it to Texas.

The launch team packed it alongside cargo from a few other crews. Li was perfectly calm until the elevator ride to the passenger compartment. The platform was higher than she expected. Below her, everything that had been important looked so small. She had been too busy to say goodbye. Again. As they strapped her into her seat, she closed her eyes.

The lift to orbit took twelve minutes at three G. Li’s vision narrowed, and her chest compressed. Every breath was a fight. Darrin’s knuckles were white against the armrest beside her. Then the pressure vanished. Weightlessness. They had made it.

In low Earth orbit, they transferred to a lunar ferry. It was a retrofitted module with Cyrillic lettering and a Russian pilot who did not waste words. The trip to the Moon took three days, cramped and vibrating the whole way. 

A news alert flickered on her slate: three miners dead at Mare Serenitatis. Suit failures. The preliminary report cited “substandard seals and counterfeit pressure bladders.” The company issued a statement: “We are cooperating fully with the investigation.”

Li closed the feed and looked at the suits Darrin had bought. She’d found suits for a third of the price and thought they’d be adequate, but Darrin had insisted on these expensive suits. He bought decommissioned Indian Space Research Organization stock and insisted on inspecting every valve himself. At the time, she called it paranoia. Now she was grateful. She tightened her grip on the gear straps. Soon, they’d see how well every choice held up.

The ferry set them down three miles west of Ball Crater. Just regolith fines and glare, with the crater wall rising a thousand feet to the east. No cloud of dust on arrival. Nothing frightening or welcoming. The horizon was closer than on Earth, but it didn’t feel small; it felt empty.

Darrin and the pilot pulled the loaded crawler out of the cargo bay. Li noticed where the pilot’s gaze fell. It was always on people or equipment, never the Moon. They loaded their gear on the crawler and set off. Li was fine with the slow movement of the crawler. She was fine with absolutely everything.

She followed the lunar claim map on her slate. Darrin sat beside her, watching the terrain roll past. “How far?” he asked.

“Three miles to the rim. Our claim is just inside, on the southwestern floor.” 

They’d spent the entire ferry ride planning where to position the solar panels for optimal sun exposure, how to route the power lines, and which section to start extraction from first. “First thing we do is anchor the hut,” Darrin said. “Get life support stable before we even think about extraction.”

Li looked up at the Earth, judging its phase. “We have about ten days left before lunar night. I’ll set up the panels, then I’ll run a site survey. Map the concentrations before we start sweeping.” 

“You think we can pull an entire bottle before lunar night?” 

“No, but even a half bottle is probably worth shipping back.” 

Darrin laughed. “Then what?” 

“Then we do it again. And again.” She glanced at him. “You ready for this?” 

“I’ve been ready since you called me in the North Sea.” He looked up at the Earth. “Waxing gibbous. The best phase to start something new.” 

Li looked at the claim. She considered where she would post it when they set up camp. The rim of Ball Crater rose ahead of them, a wall of ancient rock and debris about a kilometer high. The slope looked to be maybe twenty degrees at the approach point Li had mapped. “There’s our door,” she said, pointing to a pass where the rim had degraded into a rough ramp of rubble. 

The crawler moved easily even as it began the climb. The treads bit into loose regolith, found purchase on harder substrate beneath. They climbed slowly, steadily, the wall rising around them. They reached the rim twenty minutes later. Before they could see into the crater, Local Band gave a loud heterodyne squeal. Less than a second, and it was gone.

Li looked at Darrin.

Darrin said nothing. The crawler pulled over the lip and onto the relatively flat ring of the crater’s edge.

“Li, something is wrong here. Verify our claim on the map.” Darrin leaned forward. 

Li stopped the crawler. The coordinates matched. She checked twice. This was their claim, filed three months ago, survey boundaries confirmed. But someone was already there. The rig was no temporary setup. Solar mats stretched in neat rows, cables buried under regolith berms. An RF extractor hummed over processed material. Two huts sat leveled on foundation pads, their airlocks sealed, power lines running between them. This crew had been here for weeks. Maybe longer. 

“That’s our claim,” Darrin said quietly.

“I know, Darrin.” 

“So what do we do?” 

She stared at the operation below them. She pulled up the claim on her slate. Morrison & Chen, certified attorneys. Tucson filing. All the seals and signatures. Everything proper. “We show them the paperwork,” she said. 

Darrin looked at her, then back at the established rig below. “You think that’ll work?” 

“It has to.” She started the crawler down the interior slope toward their claim.

When they arrived, Darrin climbed down, holding their paperwork. “This sector of Ball Crater is Li Yun Tan’s claim. You and your team need to pack up and move.” 

The other miners had started gathering. From the side, one used his survey laser, cutting the paper in half while Darrin held it.

The leader looked at Darrin. “No.”

Li recoiled. 

“We can’t just leave,” Darrin said. “We have no other claim. No way to get home.” 

“We’ll get a surveyor in here to settle this,” Li said. 

The leader turned his gaze to Li. “You need to leave now.”

One of the miners leveled a survey laser at Darrin’s chest. Just enough power to see the green dot. Another miner etched a warning line into the dust, a foot from Darrin’s boots.

Li’s throat went dry. The world collapsed into that green, speckled dot. A gentle squeeze and the laser would cut right through his pressure suit.

She understood these men would kill them. These men would keep the equipment she and Darrin had so carefully chosen, they would work the claim, and sleep fine. Darrin and Li would simply stop existing, and no one would ever know or care.

The paperwork meant nothing. The signatures, the seals, and the lawyer’s certification were all worthless. No one was coming. No one would ever come.

She looked at her claim, cut in half, on the crater floor. “Darrin, get back in the crawler.” Her voice sounded distant, like it belonged to someone else.

Darrin climbed back in. Li turned and moved the crawler as fast as it would go. She wished they had a rover. The claim-jumpers kept a green dot on Darrin until they made it over the pass.

Coming out of the pass, they continued straight southwest. Her hands were shaking. She’d believed the paperwork mattered. She’d paid lawyers, filed claims, done everything right. None of it meant anything.

They’d figure out the rest once they had distance. The Moon felt hostile, but there was nowhere to go but the Moon.

A few ridges later, they passed a wrecked crawler. Li slowed. No markings, no beacon. Solar panels shattered. The cabin hatch hung open, dark inside.

“Should we check it?” Darrin asked.

Li stopped, and they got out. “Salvagers already did. Look at the hatch. It was forced open from the outside.”

“What happened to the crew?”

“No obvious damage. Darkside, maybe. Ran too long on batteries, didn’t make it back before their battery died. Two weeks of night out here with no power…” She let the thought hang. “Their suits didn’t last.”

Darrin was quiet. The crawler was intact except where it had been pried open. Darrin found two oxygen bottles in the cargo space on the back. Empty. He tossed them over to their crawler. “Li, we don’t know when we may need another oxygen bottle. These folks don’t need theirs anymore. What do you say?”

“Sure. Let’s see if we can pull their battery pack, too. Our crawler can handle the load.”

Li peered inside the broken crawler, then recoiled. The driver lay in the back, where salvagers had tossed him after stripping his suit. Mummified. Face dark as jerky, eyes deflated. Li backed away.

“Darrin, forget about the battery.”

“I’ve almost got it, Li. Just a few more bolts.”

“Darrin. Let’s just go. Scavengers took their suits.”

They got back in, and Li drove without stopping. Someone else’s mistake. Someone else’s claim now. It wasn’t hostility. The Moon didn’t care.

They kept moving until there were no more tracks. No one else had gone this far. The ground got rough, with frequent small craters. No more claims showed on the map. The crawler moved slowly, its tracks whispering across the dust.

Then Li saw it.

A wall a mile high, maybe more. White in the sunlight, rising from the curve of the Moon like a broken edge of the world. She stopped the crawler.

“What is it?” Darrin asked.

She didn’t answer. She stared at the wall, at the way it climbed into the black sky, massive and silent and utterly indifferent. Navigation showed the crater ahead was Tycho. She’d heard of it. Everyone had. Ancient. Huge. Empty.

She started the crawler again, steering directly toward it.

## 

## **Chapter 3: The Moon is Hard**

| Price of *³He* | $8,000,000/kg |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** | **6 tons/year** |
| **Current Consumption** | **12 kg/year** |
| **Time Since Start** | **2 months to 7 months** |

The wall pulled her. It looked like a fortress. She needed to see it up close. They took turns driving. There were eight days left before lunar night when they finally arrived. The slope towered over them, jagged and geologically young.

Li climbed down from the cab, boots sinking into the fines. She tilted her head back, following the wall up until it met the stars.

“Li, that is too dangerous to climb, and it will take more than a day,” Darrin said.

She looked at him. Then back at the wall.

“We have over a week until sundown. I want to climb this.”

They stopped at the base and ran the first anchors. The slope wasn’t vertical, but it was steep enough that the crawler’s drive alone wouldn’t hold. The fine regolith was more like silted glass than soil.

Li checked the line tension, then engaged the front winches. The cables drew tight, whining as the crawler began to pull itself upward. The treads fought for grip. The whole frame shuddered, metal straining against rock.

“Keep the pace slow,” Darrin said over the intercom. “Too fast and we’ll shear a line.”

Li didn’t answer. The crawler inched forward, steady but tense.

A hundred yards up, the left tread slipped, sending regolith out in an arc, scattering on the slope below. The crawler lurched sideways until the cables caught, jerking them to a stop. Darrin cursed, unstrapped, and climbed out to reset the anchor. He moved carefully, checking every clasp before locking it again.

Back in the cab, Li feathered the winch controls until the cables vibrated like bowstrings. She slowly engaged the treads, and the machine crawled forward another few meters.

They guided the crawler up the wall with winches and determination. The treads slipped and bit again, throwing regolith with each surge. They fell into a rhythm of stopping, driving new pitons into the slope, and setting fresh lines every twenty meters. They spoke little. There was nothing to say except what the equipment already told them.

Halfway up, Darrin’s voice came through again. “We’re off alignment. It’s pulling sideways in the rear, and the track is close to coming off. If we keep this path, we’ll twist the frame.”

Li paused the winches. “Then we reset.”

“That’s another hour.”

“Then it’s an hour.”

They reanchored, realigned, and started again.

The last few meters of the climb were smooth. The treads engaged well, pulling them onto the rim with a final surge of power. They still had six days before lunar night. 

Li sat back, breathing hard. Her heart hammered. Sweat slicked her suit liner. She looked out across the crater.

The floor spread below them, gray, smooth, and silent, fifty miles to the far wall. A central peak rose in the middle distance, stark against the black. Nothing moved. Nothing had moved here in a hundred million years.

She climbed out of the cab slowly, leaving the first footprints on the crater. She walked to the inner edge of the rim where the crater opened at her feet. It was vast, empty, and utterly still.

Something tightened in her chest. Not fear. Not relief. Something harder.

Hers.

“The rest of the Moon can keep its dust,” Li said. “This crater is ours. That wall is our claim, and anyone who wants to contest it will have to climb it first.”

She raised her survey laser and aimed it across the crater. A bright green pulse struck the far wall, and a scatter of regolith tumbled down, leaving a clear scar.

“And then face us.”

She didn’t look at him. She looked at the scar she’d made, at the crater spread below, at the walls rising in every direction.

Darrin said nothing for a long moment. Then, quietly: “What if someone does come? What if they make the climb?”

Her lips smiled, but it didn’t reach her eyes.

“Then those cheechakos will learn here what we learned at Ball Crater.”

Darrin looked at Li and saw the quiet hardness around her mouth. It sent a chill through him.

He changed the subject. “Li, if we are staying here, let’s set up the panels on the rim. Inside, we will get more shadow.”

“Does it really matter?”

“An extra ten hours per cycle, compared to the plain, but an extra twenty hours compared to the crater floor. I think it matters.”

They set them on a prominence and ran electrical cables down into the crater. On the Moon, fuel didn’t work. Nuclear would work, but was impossible to license, so solar was king: cheap, predictable, and reliable, with batteries for lunar night. 

Then they anchored fresh lines and began their descent into the interior. The ratchet and pawl clicked in slow rhythm as they descended. They stopped on each terrace until the brakes were cool enough to proceed. The descent was steeper than the climb had been, and went down much further, but the winches did almost all the work, lowering them from terrace to terrace until they reached the floor.

Li looked across the floor of the crater. “That’s where we’ll live,” she said, pointing to the central prominence. 

Darrin sighed. He would have preferred someplace closer, but the prominence would be fine. They unloaded their gear on the north side of the prominence and set up their hut. It looked so tiny in the crater. Darrin installed the Earth window so they could see Earth when lying down in bed. He set up a tube to protect the Earth view, then buried the hut in several feet of regolith, shielding it from temperature swings, micrometeoroids, and radiation. Inside, earthshine cast a blue glow, a steady reminder of home.

The first survey sweep crushed Li’s optimism. 3.2 parts per billion on the western floor. Four point eight near the central peak. She’d hoped for fifteen, more typical of the lunar surface, but Tycho was young and hadn’t had time to accumulate that much helium.

Darrin saw it in her face. “How bad?”

“One cycle to fill one bottle. Maybe. We have to work hard and smart.” She showed him the numbers. “We skip the first rendezvous, catch the second one. If the yields hold, we can ship a full bottle then. That should be worth enough to start making improvements.”

Darrin filled the chamber, and Li began their first extraction. She monitored the residual gas analyzer, narrowing the resonance window until the *³He* nuclei’s Larmor frequency matched the RF’s output. A microtesla drift, and the energy would bleed into the *⁴He*. She balanced maximum extraction with purity, settling on ten percent *³He*. She triggered the pulse, feeling the hum shift as the RF energy coupled directly into the target atoms, vibrating them loose from the mineral lattice while the surrounding regolith stayed cold and silent.

The extraction process was relentless trial and error. The RF extractor hummed beneath her boots, pulsing energy through regolith. First load: 4.7 milligrams. Not enough. Not nearly enough. She adjusted the magnetic field and extended the timing. Load by load, yields improved. 

“We hit eight\!” Li exclaimed. “That’s the target. If we maintain this level, we can fill a bottle per cycle. We are moving a lot more regolith than we planned, but we can do it.”

Darrin smiled weakly. “OK, Li.” He knew he would have to be as efficient as she was when it was his shift on the RF. The RF extractor defined them. It had to run constantly during the lunar day, or production would lag. Sweeping was much simpler. They could pile up an Earth day’s worth of regolith in six to ten hours. But they took shifts sleeping out of sequence, so someone was always up, ensuring the RF extractor never stopped.

The sun dropped below Tycho’s western rim. There was no dusk; the world became blue with earthshine. The temperature outside plunged to a hundred seventy below zero Celsius within hours. The solar panels up on the rim gathered power for a few more hours.

Inside the buried hut, the heaters for the algae tank kept them warm. In the last twenty minutes of solar power, they had both taken a brief shower before shutting off the water heater. Li was last. There was no door or privacy. The water was all redistilled for reuse. When Li stepped out of her brief shower, she looked at the name tag on her pressure suit. TAN, LI YUN. Because of the mix-up, everyone on the Moon called her Tan. Except Darrin. He got it right. He’d always gotten it right. 

She dried off and put on her inner layer. They wouldn’t wear their pressure suits until they were ready to go out again, after lunar night. She looked over at Darrin, who had done the same. He was inspecting his suit. She followed his lead. 

Darrin caught her looking at her name badge. “Why don’t you get that corrected?”

“Wasn’t important enough to delay our launch then; don’t care what people here call me now.”

In spite of the cold, heat loss through the regolith was slow. Li and Darrin shut down everything but life support. The only light was coming from the algae system. Just air recycling and warmth, and a little heat for cooking.

She sat at the console, legs folded under her, eyes half-closed. Darrin handed her a bowl of algae broth seasoned by a flavor packet with the vague taste of shrimp. She took it without thanks. The packets from Earth made it nutritionally complete and more palatable. It was not so much that it was bad as it was monotonous. 

“I miss the flavors I grew up with in Kuala Lumpur,” Li said.

“Maybe next time we order, we can get some curry fish head flavor packets.”

Li kept an eye on the power meters. She had calculated how much battery capacity they would need and added twenty percent. More batteries would have meant fewer solar panels. 

Day six, Darrin was watching Li check the monitors. “Are we going to live through it?”

“I’ve worked in power since they died. If the city had power, the ambulances could have gotten through in time. We’ll have enough.”

Day twelve of darkness, battery at twenty-six percent. They had let the *CO₂* climb to four thousand PPM, making it harder to concentrate, but reducing the electrical demands on the algae system. By day thirteen, the batteries had dwindled to nineteen percent.

“Twenty-six hours,” Li said, looking at the phase of the Earth.

On day fourteen, the sun returned. It hit the panels on the rim ten hours earlier than it hit the interior of the crater. The batteries began charging. They’d made it through their first lunar night. Li thought back to the wrecked rover. Not everyone did. 

They set the *CO₂* levels back down to eight hundred PPM. Li took one long breath, then got back to work.

In the second cycle, they were faster. They had a better strategy for moving regolith, they knew how to manage the magnetic field on the extractor, and had better routing for the crawler to minimize time between loads.

Darrin learned to anticipate failures before they happened. A slight vibration in the RF extractor housing meant a mounting bolt had worked loose. A half-degree temperature drift meant a seal was starting to degrade.

By the end of the second cycle, they’d pulled almost a hundred grams. They had started on their second bottle. 

The next night, they stopped with four hours of sunlight on the panels left, making sure to fully charge the crawler. They rested as before for ten days, then they loaded the bottle into the crawler along with every blanket they had and made the climb out of Tycho. The climb out was easier because they had left the cables mounted on the rim. The winches pulled them up and lowered them down. They kept it slow and cautious. When they got to the rendezvous site, about fifty miners were there waiting, quiet and watchful. This was their first time around other miners since Ball Crater, and Li was uneasy. They stayed in their crawler, covered in blankets, keeping warm. 

The cargo ship landed twenty hours before dawn. Li read the manifest. It listed the pilot as Andrei Volkov. The name didn’t ring a bell.

When Andrei climbed out, he recognized Darrin and Li immediately. “Li, Darrin, how are you?”

Li recognized the voice. He was the pilot who had ferried them here. She looked at his face. Different in a helmet, but definitely the same man. “Hi, Andrei\!” 

Andrei broke into a wide grin. “Come, see my ship.” He brought them over to his ship, where he had stenciled “LUNAR TUG” on the hull. “I incorporated in Miami. This is a growing business.”

“Smart move. Are you expanding?” she asked.

“As fast as I can hire pilots. Rendezvous are a huge business, even bigger than fares.” 

Most of the miners were here to ship helium to Earth. Andrei weighed each bottle, with no concern for what was in it. His only concern was the cost of shipping. Assay and helium purchase were handled by Helion Dynamics after the bottles got to Earth. Li shipped their first bottle to Helion Dynamics. The QR code for her account was engraved on the bottle. 

After he loaded their bottle, Li and Darrin said their goodbyes.

“Andrei, nice to see you again. We will see you at the next rendezvous.”

“No, don’t go, wait until the business is done. I have something for you.”

Li looked at Darrin. They negotiated with their eyes. “Sure, we can stay for a bit.” 

Andrei continued chatting as he processed everyone’s bottles. “So you two were headed for Ball Crater, right?”

“Yeah, well, change of plans. We ended up in Tycho.”

“Tycho\! That’s too far. Why would you go there?”

“It was available.”

“Da, everything on the Moon is available.”

With all the bottles processed, Andrei started distributing packages that people had bought. Water and air dominated, but replacement parts and other enhancements were also common.

When everything had been delivered and all the other miners had left, Andrei showed them what he had for them. “See these? Extra water, oxygen bottles, even some batteries this time. People paid for them, so I brought them. But the buyers of these packages darksided before I landed. It happens on every rendezvous.”

“You are giving them to us?”

“*Da*, I don’t have any reason to haul them back to LEO. You keep them.”

Li picked up one of the water containers. A family photo and inscription were laminated to the side. She set it down in the crawler, facing inward. They never got to say goodbye, either.

“Thank you, Andrei. This will be very helpful.” 

The sun hit some peaks around them. They were burning daylight. They needed to get back to extracting to fill the next bottle. To repeat the cycle.

Tycho was too far from the rendezvous for most miners. The climb in and out was hard, and the yields were not good. Those challenges gave them security. No one else would want what they had.

It was thirty hours into the lunar day by the time they made it to their hut. They started the RF extractor as soon as they got back. They had left the hopper half full. They both went straight to work, collecting regolith, extracting helium, and bottling it.

It would be a week before their shipment was assayed and sold. A week before, they’d know what it was worth, before they’d have money to improve anything. For now, there was only the work.

When the deposit finally hit, they cleared $730,000. Helion Dynamics took $146,000 to service the loan, leaving her with $584,000. Li stared at the number. “Darrin, it’s shopping time. If we order now, they will deliver it at the next rendezvous. Besides water and oxygen, what do we need?”

“Real food. I hate algae biscuit soup.”

“It’s nutritious, and it recycles our waste,” she chided. “Everyone on the Moon is boiling those biscuits.”

“Doesn’t make it good.” 

“No. But it keeps us alive.” She pulled up the logistics map. “We will need air, water, and food. Every time out of the airlock, we lose a little air and water, even though we pump it down. And our suits are well sealed, but some escapes while we are outside. My second priority is getting the equipment we need to make this all more efficient. First, batteries. They didn’t last as long as I expected last time.”

The rhythm became a relentless, exhausting routine. Mine, extract, bottle, sell. Only one of them would go to the rendezvous, so the other could start the RF extractor as soon as there was enough power. It was a hard, brutish existence. 

Four months in, Li tallied their production. Two hundred eighty-three grams so far. They were making it. They could afford real food now, not just algae. They filled a bottle every cycle and had enough profit for improvements: more batteries, more panels, and a fractional distiller, separating pure *³He* from the *⁴He* and other gases. Purified, they could put ten times as much *³He* in a bottle. Li calculated that shipping costs were over 15% of their income. Distillation cut shipping to less than 2%. She hated making the run every lunar night, anyway, time away, needless risks, power consumption. Li smiled to herself.

Darrin was doing maintenance on the RF. He pulled the housing cover and cursed. Dust again. The regolith was fine as smoke and sharp as broken glass. The jagged microscopic shards had never known the smoothing effect of wind or water. They crept into every gap, abraded all the moving parts, and shorted contacts. Constant maintenance was the only way to keep the equipment running well.

Then she read the new claim filings. “Darrin,” she called.

He came over, reading over her shoulder. “Ocean State filed a claim west of Tycho crater. That’s fine by me. But the plat is rectangular, and it contains ten acres of our crater floor. We have to defend it.”

“They filed from Earth. Haven’t even landed yet.” He zoomed in on the overlap. “Probably don’t know we’re here.”

“We have to defend our crater, but they outnumber us. We can’t win a fight. We need to find another way.”

“It’s their legal claim, Li. Let it go,” Darrin suggested, but knew that suggestion would be rejected as soon as he said it. 

Li looked at the calendar. Twenty-one days until Ocean State arrived. Seven days of light, then two weeks of night. 

“We strip it,” she said. “We pull every gram we can before they arrive. We clean them out, then put the stripped regolith back, making them think it was never touched.”

Darrin stared at her. “Li, that’s…”

“What?” She met his eyes. “Illegal? They filed paperwork from Earth for a crater they’ve never seen. We’re here. We hold this ground. That’s the only law that matters here.”

He remembered Ball Crater. The green laser dot on his chest. He didn’t answer. 

“We’ve got seven days before lunar night,” she continued. “Double shifts. When they land, there won’t be anything left worth taking. This is our crater.”

They were fast and efficient. Sweeping was their priority job. This cycle, they would not hit their extraction goals, but their claim had to be defended. They worked in shifts. Li would sweep for sixteen hours straight. Halfway through, Darrin would wake up and run as much of it through the RF extractor as possible. When she was done, she would head to bed, and he would start sweeping, continuing the long, methodical sweeps. The regolith piled up, coming in faster than they could process it. 

They had the power to keep running the crawler through the lunar night, but not the RF. They knew their margins pretty well. They went more slowly, saving power, but still sweeping. A week into the night, she climbed out to clear a jammed valve. Her glove came back with a shallow cut across the outer layer. She checked it. The suit held pressure. Any rational person would have stopped. Li taped over it and continued. A day before lunar dawn, they’d stripped about eight acres of the overlap. Darrin began quickly covering the rest with processed tailings. A shallow assay would show this area had no helium. At the same time, Li kept extracting from what remained, pulling every gram she could.

Suddenly, Darrin’s voice cracked over comms. “Li… incoming. West ridge.”

Li froze, the sweeper still whirring beside her. 

One last bag. The scoop arm dipped, dragging another scoop into the sack.

She reversed the crawler, slow and deliberate, the bag less than half full.

“How many?”

“Three rovers. Heavy-frame. Ocean State livery.”

A tone hit her headset. “Unidentified vehicle, you are inside a mineral claim held by Ocean State Resources. You are in violation. Acknowledge.”

She keyed the mic. “Tan, Tycho Outpost. Leaving now.”

Static. Then: “Leaving with our regolith.”

Three rovers crested the ridge, moving faster than her crawler could retreat. They stopped thirty meters out. Three figures stepped out of the lead rover, suits marked in Ocean State blue. The other two vehicles waited behind.

She pulled her survey laser from its mount, not pointing it, just holding it, ready.

“Tan,” the lead miner said. “You’re actively collecting inside our claim.”

“Leaving your claim,” she corrected. The crawler kept backing away. Five meters. Ten.

One of them raised his survey laser, aiming toward her cab.

Li’s laser snapped up, centering on the leader’s helmet. Her arm was steady. The crawler continued to reverse, engine humming.

“This is an Ocean State claim,” the leader said. “You can’t take our ore.”

A fourth figure stepped from the rear rover and began taking samples.

“This crater is mine,” she said. “Not some of it. All of it.”

“It’s not yours, Tan. It’s ours. Leave the bag.”

The fourth figure crouched again, dropping fragments into his mass spectrometer.

“This area doesn’t make sense for you anyway,” Li said, still backing away. “Most of your claim is on the far side of the crater. You planning to haul your loads over that wall every shift?”

A bright green dot appeared on the second man’s chest. Perfectly round.

Everyone froze.

Li recognized it. Darrin had climbed the ridge behind the hut. He was dug in under regolith, safe from any return laser shots.

The Ocean State team stopped moving. Li kept backing away, her laser still trained. Forty meters. Forty-five.

“You can work this ridge if you want,” she said evenly. “But every time you leave, I’ll be back. Every time you’re over that wall, I’ll strip it clean. You’ll burn more fuel climbing than you’ll ever pull in helium.”

The fourth figure said something over a private channel. She couldn’t hear it, but she knew what he’d seen. Darrin had covered that section with stripped regolith. It would look worthless.

“This crater is mine,” she said. “I’m here. You’re not. Stay west of the wall. You’ll find more helium there anyway, and you won’t have to climb a mile every shift. Stay out of Tycho.”

Silence. Then the lead miner tilted his helmet, turned, and the three of them climbed back into their rovers. They headed west, over the ridge and out of sight.

Li cut the channel. When she could no longer see them, she jumped out of the rig and secured the bag properly. Her hands shook. Once the seals held, she turned the crawler around. Forward wasn’t much faster than reverse, but she wanted distance.

No one followed.

Back at the hut, the processors hummed, pulling scraps of *³He* from the dust. Darrin stood by the airlock, arms folded.

“You didn’t have to,” she said.

He shrugged. “Didn’t seem like they wanted to talk.”

She looked at him for a long moment, then turned to the gauges. “They’ll be back.”

“Maybe. That ridge slows them down. Easier to work their western sites. And that sample they pulled showed no helium.”

“They’ll remember your name now,” Darrin said quietly.

“They already did.” She checked the pressure readings.

They were preparing for the next cycle when Darrin pulled up a relay feed. “Another darkside incident, Li. Just discovered.”

Li glanced at the slate. Four miners. They’d pushed their extraction run too long, trying to fill one more load after nightfall. Batteries died two days before dawn. Their hut lost heat, then oxygen. By the time the sun came up, it was too late.

“They were gambling on one more load,” Darrin said quietly. “They thought they knew their capacity, but they overplayed it.”

“They lost.” Li stared at the screen. Four names, four families somewhere on Earth who’d get a notification. “That’s what the solar power does. It makes you choose between shutting down for two weeks or gambling with your life.”

She closed the feed.

“We can’t keep shutting down for two weeks every month,” she said. “We can’t continue depending on sunlight.”

She paused, then added, “We need a reactor. Something that runs through nightfall.”

He hesitated. “Nukes are banned, Li.”

She gave him a long look. “So is pulling helium from someone else’s claim.”

“That’s not the same…”

“The law only matters if someone shows up to enforce it. If I can get one, it changes everything. No more blackout periods. No more power rationing. Just work, all cycle long.”

“And if they find out?”

“They will. Eventually. But if we’re smart, we’ll be too useful to stop.” She paused, then added, “Out here, production keeps the peace.”

## **Chapter 4: Cold Storage**

| Price of *³He* | $8,000,000/kg |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** | **18 tons/year** |
| **Current Consumption** | **12 kg/year** |
| **Time Since Start** | **18 months** |

Tobias Raines sat in his office at Helion Dynamics headquarters in Geneva, looking out over Jet d’Eau in Lac Léman. The bells at Saint-Pierre marked the hour. His policy was no meetings before nine, but today he had a visitor from China, where it was already noon. 

Energy Minister Chen Zhengrong walked in with three assistants just as the sixth bell pealed.

“Good morning, Mr. Raines,” Chen said. “Our premier sends his greetings. He wished to attend personally, but was caught up in internal affairs.”

“Good morning, Mr. Chen. What can I do for you?” Raines asked.

“Fusion power is an enticing concept, but our concern is stability. We have been watching the Lisbon performance data.”

“We publish the Ilha Fusão logs specifically to allow verification.”

“Yes, of course, Mr. Raines. We have reviewed your extraordinary logs. We can see routine maintenance ongoing, without a flicker in voltage or power output since operations began eighteen months ago. This stability is what has piqued our interest.”

“Fantastic. You’ve done your homework. Several countries have purchased our ten-gigawatt tokaplex. The first one, in Argentina, will go live soon. Would you like to purchase one of those? We have strict requirements on infrastructure, which we can go over…”

“No, Mr. Raines. We do not wish to purchase a ten-gigawatt tokaplex.” Chen folded his hands. “We have read your documentation on required infrastructure, and for China, we find that to be inadequate. Instead, we would like to purchase a one-terawatt tokaplex.”

“Mr. Chen, our production of the ten-gigawatt tokaplexes is deeply streamlined. All major components are produced on an assembly line in Biloxi. We can get one set up for you quickly.”

“No, Mr. Raines. You do not understand our needs. We wish to centralize production, freeing our country to use electricity in new and better ways. We call it hyperelectrification. With your terawatt tokaplex, we will be able to provide the power needed to enable that step forward.”

“That’s… forward-thinking.”

“It is necessary. Ten-gigawatt facilities are insufficient for our scale. We would begin with one terawatt installation. Operational validation. Then expand. Eventually, we would expect several such plants.”

“Mr. Chen, our terawatt facilities have not proven as popular yet with other nations. I’m not certain the infrastructure specifications have yet been made public.”

“We would appreciate your alacrity in this matter,” Chen said. “China will do what we can to help. Our engineers can draft infrastructure proposals to move the power out across the country. And we can assure you that you will not face any bureaucratic difficulties.”

“Mr. Chen, as I’m sure you’re aware, we have not yet made a final determination on the price of such facilities. I’ll get back to you with that…”

“Yes, of course, Mr. Raines. However, our premier wishes to assure you of our commitment to the future. He has sent a note with me.”

Chen handed Raines a red envelope. Inside was a card with an account number with a Hong Kong bank, a PIN, and a balance.

$500,000,000,000.

He kept his hand steady as he folded it and slipped it into his jacket pocket.

“Please tell the premier that we appreciate his commitment and will put this together now. I’m sure you realize we have a significant backlog…”

“The premier wishes to tell you this is not a down payment, but a gift for your consideration in this matter.”

Chen rose, they shook hands, and his assistants filed out behind him.

Raines stood at the window, looking out over the lake. His slate was already buzzing. Meetings, messages, a dozen fires needing attention.

He called Elin.

Elin’s answer cut right through all the pleasantries. “Raines, why are you calling so late? It’s almost midnight. I am at my wits’ end\! We’re out of molybdenum. Production stops in two days without a resupply.”

“Elin, I am so glad you are still up. I will be arriving in Biloxi tomorrow at noon. I would love to see you while I am there. Molybdenum? Let me check into that.”

He pulled up his calendar. A meeting with India in three hours. Egypt that afternoon. Poland and South Africa tomorrow.

He canceled them all.

A half hour later, his Gulfstream was still climbing when he reached Miguel Flores, a metals broker in Lima.

“Miguel, I need molybdenum.”

“Tobias, it’s so nice to hear from you. Unfortunately, you have caught us at a bad moment. We have eighteen tons here, but it’s already been sold to a buyer in Shanghai.”

Raines said nothing.

“Let me make a call.”

Raines touched down at the Gulfport airport. He waited for the molybdenum to arrive, got it loaded in a semi, and went to find Elin in Back Bay. She was giving a talk to newly hired welders when he arrived.

“Elin, wonderful to see you. I found some moly to tide you over. Eighteen tons,” he said, gesturing to the truck.

Elin got a crew to unload the truck.

“I appreciate this, Tobias. We have five hundred tons coming from South Africa next week, but we have improved our production cadence. We’re building faster than the supply contracts anticipated.”

“That’s the third time this month.”

“I know. Every time we improve the process, every time we get faster, we burn through materials faster. The contracts can’t keep up. Production here has been a tornado.”

“That tornado has been a miracle, Elin. I can’t believe how much you have done. Eighteen months ago, this was bare ground.”

Tobias looked around at her manufacturing facility. It looked nothing like a factory. The grounds stretched for miles in every direction. Six cooling towers stood in various stages of completion. The cranes moved overhead, welders beneath sending up showers of sparks, heat rising through the salt air. In the distance, they could see the metal storage yards, large Quonset huts filled with refractory and base metals.

“Argentina’s core is being loaded onto a barge now,” she said. “Japan’s will leave in a month. We’re moving, Tobias.”

They stepped inside one of the buildings. Raines watched as electricians wired up control panels.

“We can build every major component in about a year. With pipelining, our goal was to complete one per month. I am trying to catch up with orders, but the faster we move, the further we are behind. Each step forward brings its own headaches.” 

Raines responded, “From your side.”

She stopped. “What does that mean?”

“You’re improving the process continually. Each improvement reveals new constraints, new risks, and new obstacles. From my side, it’s a cascade. Every plant we sell triggers demand for ten more. Interest becomes orders before we can prepare. I couldn’t slow this if I tried.”

“Maybe you should try,” she said quietly. “Let’s get some lunch.”

They took a car to the Beau Rivage.

The doorman greeted Elin by name when they arrived. Elin brought Raines to her favorite restaurant. “Coralline’s. They have great seafood and a private dining room.”

Raines sat down. “How do you like staying here?”

“It is close enough to the manufacturing site.”

The waiter left with their orders. Raines waited for the door to close. “I spoke with China yesterday. They want a terawatt tokaplex.”

Elin put her fork down. “Seriously?”

“Minister Chen was serious. They call it hyperelectrification. Centralized power production for the entire country.”

“That’s…” She pulled out her slate, already running calculations. “Tobias, that’s exactly what this technology is meant for. Economies of scale kick in at the terawatt level. The current design is a compromise. I favored simplicity and speed in construction to meet the volume. Moving up, though, I will be able to use direct energy capture. Efficiency improves dramatically, and non-fuel expenses will be similar to the current design. You could power…” She stopped, staring at the numbers on her slate. “Oh.”

“What?”

“Tungsten requirements.” She turned the slate toward him. “Eighty thousand tons per plant. Global annual production is under one hundred thousand.”

Raines nodded slowly. “I see.”

“And that’s just one material. Molybdenum, tantalum, and beryllium all scale the same way. Square-cube problem: a hundred times the power needs a thousand times the materials.”

“The manufacturing facility is designed for ten-gigawatt tokaplexes. We’re good at it. But terawatt? That’s not just bigger. It requires materials that don’t exist.”

“I understand.”

“I want to build it, Tobias. This is what fusion should be. But I can’t.”

“What if you didn’t have to worry about materials?”

She looked at him. “What does that mean?”

“China is working on infrastructure design. When they’re done, I want you to review them. Can you do that much? Not the real plant, but how they plan to distribute electricity.”

“Of course. But it won’t matter if there is no tokaplex to power it all.”

“In the meantime, I would like you to consider what a terawatt structure would look like. Not detailed, but more than an overview.”

“Design for when the materials magically appear?”

“When I figure out how to get them.”

She took a slow drink of carbonated water. “Okay,” she said finally. “I’ll indulge your fantasy. I will check China’s specs, and I will daydream about a terawatt facility. Is that enough?”

“That’s all I am asking.”

“Until then, I have a backlog of twenty tokaplexes. Before we are done with those, I expect you will drop another thirty on us. I have real work to do.”

After lunch, Raines returned to the airport. He had one more stop.

Dutch Harbor was gray and cold when his jet touched down. The working port stretched along the waterfront where fishing boats were unloading king crab, processing plants were running overtime, and warehouses were stacked with pallets of frozen seafood.

His facility looked like all the others. A low warehouse, maybe eight hundred square feet, with weathered siding, no signage. Cryogenic containers came off the boats, got wheeled inside, and others came back out empty and were reloaded on boats. Routine. 

Today, Raines needed solitude. He walked in. The floor was ordinary. A few rows of 150 liter *³He* cryobottles using *⁴He* as a shield to prevent loss. Two workers in coveralls were moving helium tanks.

He went to the back office and closed the door.

This was a cold storage facility for bottles of *³He*. Behind a partition, there was a compressor takeoff feeding an armored pipe. That pipe ran through bedrock into the North Pacific to the edge of the continental shelf. From there, it descended eight thousand meters into the Aleutian Trench, where the pressure reached twelve thousand psi. Three spheres, each the size of a city block, were filling with *³He*, their flexible bladders displacing seawater as they filled.

Current level: 12.3 tons. Capacity: 600 tons. 

Enough buffer capacity to run multiple terawatt plants for a year while lunar production scaled. 

He opened a secure line to his purchasing team in Geneva.

“I need contracts,” he said. “Tungsten, tantalum, beryllium, titanium. Long-term agreements. I’m authorizing up to double the current spot prices. Our manufacturing facility can’t stop.”

“Understood. I’ll have proposals by end of week.”

He ended the call and pulled up Elin’s materials list.

For the ten-gigawatt plants, the contracts would work. Premium prices, aggressive terms, but the metals existed. He could buy them.

For terawatt-scale… he scrolled through the numbers. Tungsten requirements: 80,000 tons per plant. Global annual production: 95,000 tons. China wanted one now, but would probably want three more as soon as they got that one. Then all the major economies. These were no longer replacing old generators or expanding generation. Hyperelectrification would change everything. 

But the math didn’t work.

He could stockpile helium. He could lock in metal contracts for current production. He could keep Elin supplied, keep the ten-gigawatt plants rolling out, keep the orders coming.

But terawatt? He pulled Chen’s *hongbao* from his jacket. 

He had no way to deliver what they wanted.

He sat there for a long time, staring at the numbers.

Then he walked outside. The wind off the Bering Sea was sharp and cold. The dock was empty. Just the ocean, the cold, and the problem he couldn’t fix.

He stood there, hands in his pockets, until the light began to fade. There was no moon tonight.

He walked back to the airport. There was no way on Earth, and the moon didn’t have the refractory metals he needed. That would not stop him.

## **Chapter 5: Argentina**

| Price of *³He* | $8,000,000/kg |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** | **19 tons/year** |
| **Current Consumption** | **11 tons/year** |
| **Time Since Start** | **19 months** |

Elin’s Gulfstream descended into San Fernando International Airport, just north of Buenos Aires. While the aircraft taxied into the hangar, she reviewed the inspection briefs. She stepped out with only an overnight bag and her slate.

The Ministry of Energy representative approached and extended his hand. “Dr. Elin Sarai?” he asked. “Welcome. I’m Álvaro Luis, Director of Strategic Projects. The preparations for this tokaplex have been my responsibility. Would you like to inspect our infrastructure?”

She shook his hand. “No worries. Show me what you want me to see.”

Álvaro smiled and gestured toward the car. “We’ll take the river drive. You’ll see the northern substations on the way.”

They pulled away from the airfield in silence, the black sedan’s windows sealed tight against the outside heat. Cranes loomed behind transit depots, where new transformers were being craned into place. There were Ministry placards posted on utility poles and a banner stretched across an overpass. *“LA NUEVA RED – PARA UNA ARGENTINA ELÉCTRICA.”*

He glanced at her. “You’ll find we’re ready.”

She didn’t reply.

Past the Retiro yards and the old port, they came to a hardened facility surrounded by armored fencing and a ring of security. Álvaro leaned forward. “This is Subestación Belgrano Norte. One of eight new HVDC hubs. It’s part of the megavolt redundancy mesh.”

Elin checked the converters and cabling. She had already seen the testing documentation. She had verified test data. Now she was verifying build quality. 

After passing a few more sites, Elin noticed a generator truck. “Commissioning under load?” she asked.

“Yes. We are connecting a mountain village that was previously off-grid. They are installing a step-down transformer.”

Elin and Álvaro got out and donned hard hats. The familiar scent of ozone and mineral oil told her testing was underway. She inspected the wiring, noting the runs were sharply dressed and tied down. She talked to the foreman. “They never had power?”

“Pero claro. They have a diesel generator, but no road. Mules bring the fuel.” 

Elin kept the helmet with her when they got back in the car. She gazed at the transmission towers crossing the countryside. “You’ve done the hard part. The routing, the redundancy. You built for more than ten gigawatts.”

“We built for forty. Our president is looking to the future,” Álvaro said.

Elin gave a short nod. “Let’s go see the first one.”

The car pulled back into traffic. Elin watched the transmission lines stretch south as they drove to Balneario Monte Hermoso airfield. From there, they boarded a tiltrotor for the fifty-mile flight to the tokaplex.

In the air, she could see three large black lines converging near the sea, connecting to submarine cables. Twenty minutes later, they arrived at the artificial island, Central de Fusión de Monte Hermoso. It was larger but simpler than Ilha Fusão. Álvaro led her off the landing pad to a low-rise brick building with rounded corners and large glass windows on the second floor.

Once inside, Álvaro led her to the N.O.C. door. “Ready to see the beast?”

The room’s walls were lined with screens showing actual and predicted demand, voltages, maintenance schedules, and crew assignments.

“This is the mesh,” Álvaro said, gesturing to the largest screen, which displayed a map of nested loops and arterial corridors. “We’re the first in Latin America to attempt a full megavolt HVDC overlay. Drop any node, and it automatically reroutes without cascade failure.”

Elin studied the model. Interlocking rings in the north, deep ties south to the mining zones, fresh connectors across the pampas where before there had been only thin, century-old transmission lines. Every connection lit in a low amber that pulsed as loads shifted in real time.

“You designed this quickly,” she said, with a matter-of-fact tone.

“We didn’t design it alone,” Álvaro replied, tapping the screen. “ABB handled the HVDC overlay architecture. Our team integrated them with national policy. This isn’t a local tie‑in. It’s a national backbone. If a section goes offline, every node has failover paths without loss of synchronism.”

He tapped a zone northwest of Córdoba.

“Ilha Fusão was smaller,” he said. “A hundred megawatts. Simulations were sufficient to ensure smooth, safe operations. Here, simulations aren’t enough. Every component has been pushed to its limit.”

He pointed to a secondary display. Three heavy black lines radiated from the tokaplex, each connecting to a different node of the mesh. She recognized them from the flight in.

“These are the HVDC links. Fully isolated, with separate substations and protection. If one fails, the others carry the load.”

Elin looked at the links. “Meets the requirements. The real question is what happens when it goes live.” She looked again at the projected grid, breathing, humming, ready to distribute enormous amounts of energy. With Ilha Fusão, she had been there every day for five years before it went live. For Monte Hermoso, all she got was some reports and less than a week for inspections. The stakes were much higher, the risks were much greater, and she had no time to work with the installation.

They didn’t linger at the control center. Álvaro had a schedule, and the schedule moved fast. He brought her to a conference room down the hall. Elin checked her itinerary, and this meeting wasn’t on it. 

Elin had designed the situation room for engineers, with hard lines instead of wireless and integrated displays. Today, it held several ministers, envoys, and one uniformed military observer who said nothing and made no introductions. Elin took her seat near the far end. A plate with chipas and facturas and a glass of sparkling water sat at each place. She broke a chipa open, releasing the steam and the aroma of toasted Reggianito cheese before popping it in her mouth.

The Minister of Energy opened the session briskly. “We’re in final integration for Monte Hermoso,” he said, tapping a projection to his right. “The island is on schedule. The mesh is ready. We would like to discuss future commitments.”

A secondary map appeared with three red outlines across the country, one near Buenos Aires, one near Mar del Plata, and one further south near Comodoro Rivadavia, each marked *SOLICITADO*, all of them with planned tie-ins to the mesh.

“These are the follow-ons,” he said. “This will support development of transportation, industry, and mining operations around the country and balance our mesh.”

Elin didn’t answer at first. Looked around the room. Not a single engineer. She read the names, the grid ties, the siting logic. “You’ve assumed Helion Dynamics will be able to schedule these deployments,” she said finally. “Who confirmed those deployments?”

The Trade Minister answered. “We were your first paying customer, and we have paid in full for Monte Hermoso. We expect priority.”

Elin nodded slowly. “I’ll relay that, but it’s not my contract to make. You were first, but there have been many orders since, all fully paid.”

Another official, this one from the Ministry of Industry, leaned in. “Aluminum and lithium plants have been built already. The demand is high, and they can employ several thousand, but they need power.”

“We know you have Raines’ ear,” said the Energy Minister. “And we know you understand what this kind of energy makes possible. We are not just talking about supply. We are talking about national transformation.” He slid an envelope to Elin.

Elin placed her hand flat on the envelope, stopping it before it reached her. Blushing hotly, she looked at each person in the room.

“I am in manufacturing. I built this place, even this room. Raines decides where they go.”

Elin’s eyes moved from minister to minister. “If you need the power to arrive sooner, build the other islands quickly and connect them to the mesh. You know the requirements. Then talk to Raines.”

After the meeting, Álvaro found Elin. “The tiltrotor is ready, Dr. Sarai. We’ve reserved a room in town and dinner is arranged.”

Elin shook her head. “I’ll stay on the island tonight.”

Álvaro paused. “The apartments are very basic.”

“I know. I designed them.”

He smiled slightly. “The vending machines may not satisfy you. My wife packed a dinner for me in case things went long. It is in the refrigerator. It’s yours if you want it.”

“Thank you, Álvaro. That sounds perfect. I would just like some time alone now.”

“*Buenas noches*, Dr. Sarai. I will see you in the morning.”

She found the milanesa a la napolitana con puré in the refrigerator and warmed it. The solitude was perfect, just her and the quiet machine. After dinner, she walked the corridors alone, listening, connecting. The tokaplex hummed softly on standby: cooling systems, auxiliary pumps, metal contracting in the evening air.

The apartments were simple with just a cot, a sink, and a shared restroom at the end of the hall. Elin had designed them herself, years ago, for overnight crews. She’d never expected to use one. But staying close to the machine was grounding. This is why she was here. She slept well.

The next morning, Álvaro brought her medialunas from home. Elin thanked him as she ate. 

Her first meeting that morning was with the general manager, Gabriel Mansilla. She found him windblown, looking across the floor.

“Elin,” he said, nodding once.

She skipped the pleasantries. “How’s the floor?”

“Cleared, cleaned, and ready,” he said. “We pulled the last crates two nights ago. Gantries are programmed. Power couplings are aligned and tested.”

She glanced down at her notes. “Turbines?”

“Hot,” he confirmed. “Running on simulation heat. We’re venting most of it, but efficiency is higher than spec. The *sCO₂* units stabilize faster than we expected.”

“Any anomalies?”

He hesitated. “Minor fouling on line B. There was some grit in the condensing loop, probably from the cleaning process. Flushed and rebalanced. They are running it again.”

She made a mark on her slate. “And the core?”

“A few hours out. I expect it around noon, plus or minus.”

“Assembly team is ready?”

“Staggered shifts. They’re tuning the robotic frames and walking through dry runs. They’re ready.”

“And you?”

Gabriel gave a tired half-smile. “I’ve got three hours of sleep and a cooling jacket strapped to my lower back. So yes. Ready.”

Her eyes laughed, but her voice stayed steady. “Stay on comms. Notify me when the core clears port.”

Elin headed to the tiltrotor pad. Gonçalo Silva was coming. The tiltrotor dropped onto the eastern pad. Elin was already there when the rotors began to slow. Gonçalo stepped out with his usual pace, unhurried, steady. He wore the same field jacket he had worn in Ilha Fusão, light, practical, unbranded. His bag was slung crosswise, heavy with tools and notebooks. Before he made his way to her, he paused to take in the east gantry, then the core floor scaffolding. She let him walk the first few meters alone.

When they met, they didn’t shake hands. She tilted her head slightly. He smiled.

“Looks smooth,” he said.

“More streamlined than Ilha Fusão,” Elin replied. “We learned from all the mistakes we made there.”

Gonçalo was looking at the skyline. “I can see that. It’s much larger, but it feels less cluttered.”

They started walking toward the operations mezzanine. Between them, the control tower loomed in prefabricated concrete and fiber panels, with broad window slits set under sun-shielding eaves.

They climbed a short flight of metal steps that led up to the observation level. Below them, the control room staff ran diagnostics in near silence, each screen tracing voltages and heat gradients across systems not yet powered. Gabriel stood with one of the interface engineers, looking up at a wall display of turbine feedback loops.

Gonçalo gestured at the far end of the floor. “Gabriel’s getting there. He’s detail-forward, but he’ll learn to step back. You can’t fly a thing this size like you’re tuning it by hand.”

“He respects the systems,” Elin said. “He doesn’t try to outthink them.”

“Is the emergency cooling loop really that simple?” he asked.

Elin brought up a diagram on her slate. “The core is below sea level now. If containment fails or thermal load runs away, gravity pulls seawater straight through. No pumps, no heat exchangers. Just open the valves. Four separate valve sets. The computer controls the automated valve set. If it detects a thermal runaway, it opens the valve.”

“That’s nice if everything works perfectly. What if the computer shuts down or the sensors fail?”

“If the computer fails for any reason, there are two manual valves, accessible from different locations. And even if no one notices, there is one final failsafe, a thermal valve with a fusible plug. If the temperature gets too high, the solder will melt, opening the valve.”

He leaned on the railing. “That’s the difference, isn’t it. Ilha Fusão is safe, but it needs babysitting. This one’s safer because it doesn’t.”

Elin and Gonçalo were eating lunch when the core arrived. Elin stayed in the cafeteria, but Gonçalo got up to watch Mansilla’s crew install it.

The gantry cranes lowered it into position, where a team of a dozen technicians immediately swarmed the core, connecting the feed pipes, monitors, and cooling loops.
