After installation was complete, Elin found Gonçalo. “How did they do?” 

“Where did you get these guys, a Formula One team? Mansilla has trained them very well. Installation went smoothly and much faster than I expected.”

“At Ilha Fusão, we didn’t design for installation. This time around, ease of assembly was a key constraint. It’s supposed to be smooth.”

With the core secured, the site shifted tempo. The Argentine crews moved quickly, hands practiced, expressions focused. The last hookups were made and checked. 

Heavy conduit arms were swung into place and locked down. The s*CO₂* turbine, already hot-tested, was mated to the flow channel. A hiss and pressure check marked the moment when the *CO₂* storage tank came online, dumping its contents into the s*CO₂* turbine.

At the perimeter of the island, a low-power carbon collector clicked back to life, drawing in atmospheric *CO₂* to replenish the storage tank. It would refill the tank in about a month. In theory, they would not need more *CO₂*, but if there were a leak developed, they would need to be fixed and completely recharged.

Power lines were checked, then rechecked. The HVDC terminals blinked status green. Routing software loaded. Backup systems synced. The mesh recognized the plant’s endpoints. For the first time, the island registered as a node in Argentina’s electrical system. 

Assembly was declared complete at 14:42 local time. Just a line item in the log, signed by the work crew chief, seconded by Gabriel.

Gabriel found Elin near the mezzanine, hands in his jacket pockets, boots dusty from the floor. “Dr. Sarai, *ya está listo*.”

Elin notified her contact, a US Navy lieutenant on the amphibious assault ship offshore. It had brought the container down from Dutch Harbor with a destroyer escort around the horn. The US government had asked to secure the delivery due to the extraordinary value. The *³He* in the container was enough to power this plant for a year. It had been scraped from the Moon at enormous cost in labor, equipment, lives, and eight billion dollars. 

The cryotank came in low, under fighter escort. Two F-35s bracketed the CH-53E as it descended on its pad. Propwash kept everyone at a distance.

From the operations platform, Elin watched as the rear ramp unfolded. Marines deployed first, fanning out with unhurried precision. Then the cargo came down, matte silver, heavily insulated, marked with stenciled black codes, and frost blooming around the seals. The cryotank held a year’s supply of *³He*, one metric ton. 

A Marine captain held his salute until Elin acknowledged him. He passed over the digital manifest. Elin inspected it, then signed.

Installation was already underway. The hauler maneuvered the container to the sealed platform near the reactor housing. Hydraulic arms lifted it into position. The cradle clicked into its locks. Engineers verified coolant flow and containment pressure. Readouts held steady.

The Marine escort didn’t linger. Before departing, their commander turned to Gabriel and saluted sharply. Gabriel crisply returned the salute. Then the Marines were back aboard the helicopter and into the air.

She walked with Gabriel to the control floor for the test run. The control room was already full. Gabriel was at the operator’s station, Gonçalo standing behind him, arms crossed, leaning slightly forward. Elin watched from one of the side consoles, monitoring the turbine feedback loops.

On screen, the core came to life in steps. Systems primed. Vacuum held. Field coils charged. Then the plasma ignited briefly, controlled, a soft event visually, just a line of green across the diagnostic pane. Not even enough to raise the turbine feed pressure, but the superconducting ring registered the field. 

Gonçalo leaned in and called for another pulse, this one longer. Seconds, not milliseconds. Heat output rose. The turbine responded, barely. A ripple in the pressure. Cooling held steady. The s*CO₂* cycle hadn’t fully ramped, but it was alive. The storage buffers reported a charge. Grid output was still zero, as expected.

They ran five more bursts. In each, the response was tighter. The plasma shape held. The pressure gradients behaved. Thermal gradients stabilized faster than the simulations had suggested. The reactor wasn’t just stable, it was predictable and controllable.

Gabriel turned and gave Elin a nod. “This thing listens.”

She checked the timestamp. The whole break-in run had lasted under twenty minutes. Long enough to show continuity across fusion, routing, recovery, and control systems, but not enough to prove efficiency. The turbine had barely started to turn. Just a machine confirming it was what they had built.

Gonçalo stepped back from the console. “The next run will be for power, Gabriel.”

The control room was silent in the seconds before ignition. Gabriel keyed the final command. The reactor ramped up in a controlled ascent. Plasma formed, and ignition held. The display confirmed a stable burn in under three seconds. Turbine readouts spiked. Heat flooded the s*CO₂* loop, and pressure hit threshold. Grid output climbed, six, eight, ten gigawatts. Steady. Smooth.

For a moment, it all held.

Then one of the HVDC lines dropped. Just a blink and the connection was lost. Power continued to route smoothly as the mesh’s reroute protocols kicked in. Load spilled to the southern loop and northern loops, then back across the mesh. Power rebalanced.

Gabriel swore under his breath. Gonçalo stared at the numbers, expression flat.

Elin leaned forward and logged the fault. The mesh had responded exactly as designed. But the line had failed under load, and that meant something had slipped.

She didn’t say anything yet.

Instead, they left the control center and followed the long service corridor toward the power conditioning chamber. This was the part no one showed on press tours. The CL filters filled the space like machinery in a cathedral, vast, industrial, sculptural. The capacitors stood fifteen feet tall, six feet across, ribbed in black insulation. Inductors coiled behind caged housings, humming faintly. Each unit shaped and stabilized the rectified current, converting raw internal power into clean HVDC output.

Gonçalo paused in the aisle between them. He thought of Ilha Fusão, where the output had been AC, conditioned by standard hardware. It had been elegant, minimal. Nothing like this. He felt like a mouse in the land of giants.

He turned to Elin. “Isto é outra escala.”

She turned to him. “Another kind of responsibility.”

They continued walking, passing into the thyristor hall, where the problem had occurred. Too much feedback on the western line had caused multiple commutation failures as it tried to ramp up. The valves were blocked to prevent them from melting. Gabriel restarted the process, letting the western line climb slowly. A thousand volts, ten thousand, a hundred thousand. Gabriel touched one more button on his slate, and full power was restored. There was no friction to the power now. It moved outwards with a strange ease.

Back in the control room, the grid displays showed full delivery restored. Argentina was receiving power. Gabriel gave his diagnosis. “It was a harmonic echo. The western line’s fundamental resonance matched our switching frequency. The circuit couldn’t provide sufficient reverse-bias duration to allow the thyristors to shut off between pulses. From now on, we ramp the western line on a staggered curve to stay below that resonant peak.” 

Gonçalo beamed. He didn’t understand DC power nearly as well as Gabriel, but it was clear the plant was in good hands. 

They had 58.3% thermal conversion efficiency. Elin made a note. 

The system had worked, and so had the failure. The recovery system had proven itself at full power. 

The tiltrotor lifted in a smooth vertical climb, skimming above the breakers before banking east. Through the cabin window, Elin watched the artificial island shrink into geometry, cranes stilled, intake arms folded, the blunt white dome of the reactor glinting in sea haze. A line of pylons faded into the mainland.

She turned back to the slate in her lap. Uptime has held steady since the full ignition. Grid telemetry showed stable dispatch at ten-point-four gigawatts, even with the reroute. Cooling margins were conservative. Substation frequency held. Forecasted demand peaks from the southern industrial corridor had already started to adjust upward.

There were six more contracts in early-phase development. Different soils. Different governments. Different tolerances for delay.

She tapped through Gabriel’s final site report. Nothing unexpected. Turbine efficiency was holding.

The plant would run. But more importantly, it could be repeated.

She closed the report and looked back out the window, but the island was already gone. Just sea, now, layered and flat. On the horizon, faint in the morning light, the HVDC towers on the island fed the submarine cables that went to the mainland. She could see where the submarine cables joined the aerial cable towers on land. Argentina was powered. Elin had work to do.

## 

## **Chapter 6: Beyond Helium** 

| Price of *³He* | $8,000,000/kg |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** | **30 tons/month** |
| **Current Consumption** | **30 tons/year** |
| **Time Since Start** | **22 months** |

Li liked the Moon. The silence, the slow drift of shadow, the way boots felt against ground. The Earth was beautiful from the crater.

The Earth was slightly gibbous. She had only a few hours left before the floor turned blue, and another couple of hours before the solar panels on the ridge stopped producing power. She had added to her pack every rendezvous. This lunar night, they would have twenty-five percent more power than they needed.

Every rendezvous, she learned of people who had tried just a little too hard and darksided. She took their orders home. She would pay the next of kin when she could find them.

As they got into bed, Li looked at Darrin. “I’ve been working on a way out of this for almost a year, you know.”

“You will never get enough batteries for us to operate during the lunar night.”

At breakfast the next morning, Darrin prepared the algae biscuit soup, with freeze-dried beef and vegetables. Li took a sip. “Andrei has a friend, Belov. They grew up together. Belov is salvaging old Soviet submarines.”

“And?”

“He was tearing down an Alfa-class submarine.”

Darrin frowned. “You bought a submarine?”

“Just the reactor. An OK-550.”

“You’re kidding.”

Five days in, they were going to bed, watching the Earth through the Earth window. “It’s perfect for us, Darrin. One hundred fifty megawatts thermal. Compact enough to fit in a lander frame if you strip the shielding. Belov will clean it up, replace the fuel rods, and check every weld. When we get it, it will be solid.”

“We barely use one megawatt. What are you planning to do with that much power?”

“There aren’t a lot of tiny reactors lying around. This one meets our needs. We will run it low.”

Li was going over their extraction charts on day ten when Darrin spoke up. “You know nukes are banned here.”

“The law only matters if someone can enforce it. In the meantime, this is power when the sun disappears, and the cold tries to kill us.”

“And if they check?”

“I filed it as a high-output RF extractor. It will come wrapped in tungsten foil and a three-millimeter steel box. Safe enough while it’s cold. And I talked to Andrei Volkov. I gave him an extra ten million to not ask any questions. He can use the cash while he is getting Lunar Tug on its feet.”

Day fourteen, their last night. Earth was almost at its last quarter. “When is this coming?”

“Belov said it would take six months to prepare before he could ship it. He is using Russian heavy lift.” 

“Won’t that cost more than getting it to Texas and using SpaceX?”

“Fewer people will handle it. Fewer questions.”

Darrin set the pit near the north crater wall. The wall would shade the radiators from the sun. He dug ten meters deep, using the rock to shield them and provide distance.

“What if this thing decides to go critical on us?” he asked, leveling the excavator.

Li knelt at the edge, scooping up a handful of regolith and letting it sift through her gloves. “Then we won’t have time to worry about it.”

“That’s comforting.”

“Belov said it’s engineered to fail safely. Control rods drop, reaction stops. It was made for military use, so it’s built to operate under duress.”

Darrin’s helmet turned toward her. “Water?”

“Lead-bismuth coolant, but same principle.” She stood, brushing dust off her knees. “Hot liquid in, hotter liquid out. We tap the heat, run it through generators, ovens, and anything else.”

He went back to digging. “You make it sound like ordinary plumbing.”

“Ordinary, but with molybdenum pipes.”

For the next six months, every time they went to rendezvous, there were extra tungsten plates and lead-bismuth alloy pellets and TZM pipe sections. Li had cut a deal with Lunar Tug to ship them as ballast whenever they had spare capacity. 

Darrin routed an ammonia cooling loop to radiators on the permanently shaded interior face of the crater’s northern wall. Set in reflective troughs, they would radiate heat into space smoothly.

Lunar Tug actually landed inside the crater to deliver the reactor. It was a cube about three meters to a side. It looked like just plain carbon steel plates, scuffed from the journey. Li watched the Lunar Tug crew offload it with the kind of care reserved for live ordnance. No one questioned the claim that it was an oversized RF. Belov had stenciled “RF Extractor” and “*УСТАНОВКА РАДИОЧАСТОТНОЙ ЭКСТРАКЦИИ*” on the steel panels. Li wondered how much the Lunar Tug pilots knew.

“Christ, it’s heavy,” Darrin muttered as they guided it toward the pit.

“Be glad Belov removed the depleted uranium shielding before shipping this. It would have weighed four times as much with that in place. What’s left is just enough for transport.” Li ran her hand along its surface.

They spent the next two days preparing it. First came the tungsten plates Li had been accumulating for real shielding. Each plate was five hundred kilograms. Even on the moon, they weighed too much for Darrin to lift. They bolted them to the reactor’s sides and top, piece by heavy piece, several layers deep.

Only then did they lower it into the pit and connect the molybdenum piping. The hot loop ran out a hundred yards from the reactor, then doubled back. Li had already set the pipe stanchions, giving the pipe about one foot of clearance above the exposed rock. Every four yards, Darrin had installed a tap where equipment could draw heat from the flowing metal. Between the taps, silicon aerogel insulation and protective covers kept the heat contained. When it was completed, they buried it in regolith.

Darrin dropped lead-bismuth pellets in a feeder pipe until it was full. Then he looked to Li, who was seated at the console. “Ready.”

Li turned the handle, lifting the control rods slowly, watching temperatures climb degree by degree. As it warmed, the pellets melted and started flowing deeper. Darrin kept feeding in more. They melted quickly, filling the piping and beginning their circulation as a river of liquid metal.

“Temperature’s climbing,” Darrin called from his console.

Li checked her readouts. “The reactor’s warming smoothly. The metal is at operating temperature. How’s the flow?”

Darrin scanned the regolith with an infrared imager. “Steady. No visible leaks.”

The real test came when they started up the supercritical *CO₂* turbine. Li had connected it to the first tap on the hot loop, where the liquid metal was hottest. The turbine itself was compact, the size of a car engine, but rated for ten megawatts electric. She let the current into the generator, using it as a motor to turn the turbine slowly, making sure that the blisks were not warped by the density difference between the liquid phase and gas phase of the *CO₂* charge. As the liquid entered the heat exchanger on the tap, it heated fast, pushing back against the turbine. She monitored the temperature. It wasn’t moving fast enough. The *CO₂* was heating above its dissociation temperature, chemically attacking the exchanger. She increased the power slightly, and the velocity brought the temperature back down. Pressure and temperature were rising. The *CO₂* passed the critical point. She kept pushing it deeper into the supercritical range, then opened the valve to the chilled ammonia line and stopped driving the turbine. It kept moving. Li and Darrin watched as the output rose, driving it all the way to ten megawatts before throttling it back down to one. 

“We’ve got power,” she announced. “We could run every hut on the Moon with this thing. Let’s set up the ovens.”

Darrin had connected two regolith processors into the second and third taps on the hot loop two days ago. Now, he wired in the electric power to control the rotating drums and the gas extraction. Each oven could hold twenty tons of raw regolith, industrial-scale processors designed to crack rock and extract every trace gas locked inside. Unlike the RF extractor, which gently coaxed helium atoms to vibrate free, these ovens hit the regolith with raw thermal energy from the liquid metal. No finesse. No precision. Just extreme heat.

He loaded a test batch into the first oven, and they watched the internal temperature climb. 700°C is the generally accepted extraction temperature. Darrin let it climb to 1,000°C.

“Jesus,” Li whispered, watching the mass spec data.

The regolith didn’t just release helium. It gave up everything: hydrogen, neon, argon, water vapor, gases that had been locked in the rock for billions of years. Her old distillation equipment, designed for the flow from RF extraction, couldn’t keep up with the torrent of mixed gases pouring out of the load.

“We’re going to need a bigger distillation column,” Darrin said, imitating Brody.

Li laughed. She was already pulling up catalogs on her slate. “Two bigger columns. And a pre-separator to strip out the non-helium gases. We just pulled out 1.8 times more *³He* than our old rig could manage. And that’s not counting all the other gases.”

She showed him the readout. Beyond the helium, they were recovering significant quantities of water vapor and noble gases. On a world where everything had to be shipped from Earth, those byproducts were valuable. 

When lunar night came, for the first time since coming to the Moon, Li ran their hut at full power. Every light blazed. The air recycler hummed at maximum capacity. She cooked dinner without checking the battery levels. They continued sweeping and extracting right through the night under the blue glow of Earth.

The first visitor was a lean miner from just east of Tycho Crater. Li watched him on her external cameras, a single figure in a battered pressure suit, following their tire tracks across the crater floor. She suited up and walked out to meet him.

“Saw your lights,” he said over Local Band. “Not something you see much this time of night.”

“Name’s Sam,” he said. “My claim’s about ten miles east. I’ve heard of you before. Tan, right?”

Li winced at the name but let it pass. “What brings you out?”

He shrugged, the motion faint in the suit. “Mostly curious. I can’t work at night, so I was just looking around.”

“Most people can’t work at night,” Li said.

“Yeah, but you can. I was thinking maybe I haul some of my dirt here, you run it through whatever you’re doing. You take a cut, I can dig more.”

Li glanced at Darrin. They’d talked about this before, but this was the first time someone had walked in with the offer. “What kind of cut?”

“Ten percent. I do the digging, you do the extraction.”

She shook her head. “It’s more than just running it through. We pull almost double the gas, strip the junk, and bottle the *³He* pure in a cryocontainer. Less weight, better price.”

“Cryocontainer? You’ll lose a lot to boil-off. I use K-bottles. No boil-off.”

“I use a pulse tube cooler to boil off sacrificial *⁴He*. Using cryocontainers cuts my freight bill, and the mechanism prevents boil-off. I send back ten times as much *³He* per kilogram of freight. If I process your helium, you won’t see a freight bill. I’ll cover that.”

Sam paused, thinking about the numbers. “Freight’s killing me. Fifteen percent gone before I see a credit. At even half that yield boost, it’s worth it.”

“Run a test batch,” Li said. “If we don’t beat your numbers by fifty percent, it’s free. If you stick with us, I want twenty percent.”

He grinned. “If you can hit those numbers, twenties are cheap. I’ll go get ten tons of my regolith.”

“Bring twenty,” Darrin called out.

When Rook came back, they dropped his bags into the oven. Darrin eased the heat up, slow at first. At 700°C, the helium started to flow, along with everything else. The mass spec matched Sam’s best RF extractor runs. Rook still looked like he was doing the math in his head.

Then Darrin pushed it higher, up to 1,000°C. Pressure climbed. The display ticked past anything Rook had ever seen, one hundred eighty-two percent of his best yield.

He stayed through the rest of the run, watching the *³He* get distilled, weighed, and bottled in the cryocooler. Li sent the credit before the cooler was even sealed.

Sam stared at the number in his account. “I’m used to waiting weeks for a Tug to pick it up, take it to Earth, then wait for Helion Dynamics to pay out.”

“I don’t mind paying fast,” Li said. “It makes accounting easier. So, do we have a deal?”

Sam looked up. “You’ve got yourself a customer.”

A pair of miners Rook knew from east of Tycho asked him why he wasn’t selling helium anymore. 

“You know, since I hooked up with Tan, I don’t need to sell helium. She pays me for it right away, and she ships it.”

“How much does she charge for that?”

“Twenty percent. That’s not much more than I was paying Lunar Tug.”

“Yeah, we are generally between ten and fifteen percent on shipping. But I wouldn’t give up that five percent.”

“That’s what I thought until I tried it. She nearly doubles the yield.”

“Fills the bottle with trash?”

“She does her own fractional distillation. You’ve seen her huge cryocontainers. Ultrapure *³He*.”

“Nice.”

“Better than nice. I shut down my RF extractor. Without that energy hog, I can sweep a lot more. I am pulling four times as much as I used to.”

“Sam, you have to introduce us to Tan.”

Soon Li was processing their regolith, too. She got two more customers at the next rendezvous, then ten more at the rendezvous after that. 

The next rendezvous, Ocean State Resources came knocking. She charged them twenty-five percent, a small surcharge for their rocky start.

Darrin ran the intake. With several clients bringing bags of identical-looking regolith, he needed a way to keep the bags straight. He printed QR codes on the bags, associating each bag with a miner and an account. Every bag got scanned for its QR code, weighed in, logged, and sent to the ovens. The return bags went out with a readout of *³He* recovered, and the money was deposited in their accounts.

The miners called it Tan’s Refinery. They’d butchered her name. She liked it, in a way; it gave her just enough distance to keep people from thinking they really knew her, and the money kept flowing.

They made use of the other gases, too. The water they were recovering was small, but there was never enough water on the Moon. They started selling it at rendezvous. 

Darrin ran a test on extractions. After the first pass through a pile of regolith, he pumped hydrogen back into the oven. When he brought the heat up again, the sweep pushed out another eight percent helium. After that, he made the hydrogen gas sweep part of every extraction.

At rendezvous, Rook got a large shipment of heavy aluminum tubes. Darrin was intrigued and asked about them.

“I hate hauling my dirt over that crater rim. I plan to make a cableway from the base of the wall on my side to the floor on your side. I have a heavy Dyneema cable and a low-power drum to keep it moving.”

“That sounds fascinating. Can I help?”

They spent the entire lunar day getting the pylons anchored and the cable run pulled over. Darrin kept the drive drum on his side, so it was easier to power. 

When it became lunar night, Rook held his breath and clipped a ten-ton bag onto the line. It sagged, but it didn’t touch the ground, and the cable carried it away. Rook started loading more and more bags, and they all just ended up in a pile where Darrin wanted them. Rook came over to see how it was going.

When he arrived, Darrin was looking at the pile of bags. “I love this\! I’m going to build this inside the crater, running in a loop. I won’t need to carry bags on the crawler anymore; they’ll carry themselves. Think you can extend yours? I’d like more people to use it.”

Sam hesitated, one hand on the cable. “Most folks are happy enough not to have to climb the crater rim, but sure. I have time during the lunar night. I can probably extend it another twenty miles.”

Other miners, who hadn’t been using Tan’s Refinery, saw the cableway and came to investigate. Soon, they were using it, too, and Tan had more customers.

A month later, Li saw a new series of towers stretching west. Ocean State Resources had copied Rook’s cableway.

Darrin worked with an engineer from Ocean State Resources, and together they built a transfer station. The station would read a bag’s QR code and automatically route it correctly. After that, Darrin could put empty bags back on the line, and they’d go to the right place. Ocean State Resources also agreed to extend their line another fifty miles west, drawing still more customers for Tan’s Refinery.

Inside the crater, the cableway loop dumped processed regolith tailings against the northeast wall. The pale, inert waste piled up slowly, like industrial snow. Li had a depth probe with her to estimate the volume. She saw another miner in Ocean State gear poking at the same pile. His name tag said Tseng.

“I think I’ve seen you around here before,” she said. “What are you doing?” 

Tseng smiled and pulled a compact mass spec from his belt. He dropped a sample in and vaporized it with his survey laser. “Just seeing what it’s made of,” Tseng said. “I am a geochemist by training. Mining helium is more profitable, but less interesting. I sometimes wonder what metals are in this waste pile.”

“Waste?” she said. “These tailings are my way of scoring how much *³He* we have extracted.” Li made a note on her slate. Tseng loaded another sample.

Darrin still worked his pit when he could, but most of his time went to managing the reactor, keeping its heat curve steady and regulating intake. Regolith arrived constantly, bags flowing in from every miner within reach of the cableway. Li had stopped mining altogether. She was too busy.

New QR codes began appearing on bags from farther out. Some miners built their own cableways, connecting to the network that Rook and Ocean State Resources had started. Darrin built a third one going north towards Ball Crater. Li’s days blurred together under the weight of constant movement.

One evening, she and Darrin were staring out across the crater. “I loved the peace we had out here,” she said softly. “What happened to that?”

“Progress,” he replied.

“Darrin, the regolith in this crater has maybe a fifth of the helium as most places on the Moon. We are making as much from our cut of Rook’s helium as we are from processing our own.”

“I expected that,” Darrin replied. “This is a young crater. It has only been accumulating solar wind for a hundred million years, not the billions of years it has accumulated on most of the Moon.” 

“Well, we’re making more money than ever, but I feel like I’m always running. When I was mining, I had a place I needed to be. It was… relaxing, in its own way. I wish I had somewhere to slow down.”

“You want to buy a larger space? Shipping will be enormous, but you can probably afford it.”

“I’ll think about it. Right now, I need to sleep.”

Li was running intake when a miner came by the refinery the next day, dropping off five bags of regolith. “This is my last load. I’ve been here long enough. Time to get back to Earth and spend some of that helium money.” 

Li loaded the first bag into an oven. “What are you doing with your gear?”

“I just left it there on my claim. I can’t bring it back to Earth.”

“Yeah, that would be crazy. But I’ll give you five grams for your hut. Another five for the rest of your gear, if you put it all on a cableway.”

“That’s ten grams for nothing. I’ll go get it now, while you are still processing my load.” 

The hut arrived sealed and holding pressure. It was followed by his sweeper, batteries, panels, and an RF extractor. Li moved most of the gear to the eastern edge of the crater floor, but kept the hut near the prominence. 

Within a week, a dozen more huts came in, clipped to the cableway. They looked odd among the ten-ton bags. Some were clean. Most were scuffed, dented, and patched. It didn’t matter. Darrin hauled them into place with the crawler, welding modules together and cutting through walls between them. Quonset huts were not the easiest shape to use, but over the next few weeks, the cluster began to take shape as a single structure, thirty by thirty, fifteen feet high.

Li and Darrin set up a large kitchen. A crew pulling out from Shackleton sold her a convection oven and some leftover food, including a dozen \#10 cans of tomato paste. Darrin mounted speakers in the ceiling panels and hooked them into an old media server loaded with rock and Chinese pop. The floor was still bare metal, and the air always carried the scent of lithium grease. But it felt different. It was a place to sit. A place where you could let your mind wander. 

Li watched the cable. A miner was attached. He had come in out of Lexell Crater. His claim lay in shadow now, the sun gone for two weeks, and his crawler was useless without solar power. He clipped his last load to the line, then clipped himself on behind it and followed it in. The QR code on his chest matched the code on his bags, so transfers were automatic.

The ride was silent except for the faint vibration of the cable through his harness. Below him, the Moon stretched away in black and silver, illuminated only by blue earthshine.

When he got to Tycho Crater, he was dazzled by all the lights. This was his first time seeing what was at the end of the cableway. He noticed the large structure they had built. He stood outside for a while, wondering what it was. One section was a mirrored glass window. He looked at himself, the QR code on his chest identifying his claim, dust packed into the seams of his gloves. The lights inside were warm in a way lunar light never was. Li saw him through the window and waved him in.

“There’s stew,” she said when he came in. “Real carrots. Whiskey too, if you’re paying.”

He sat down like he’d forgotten what a chair felt like. The warmth hit first, then the smell of broth and roast beef. He sat, ate everything in the bowl, and drank more than he planned to. He wept as he ate. When he was done eating, he looked out the Earth window, lost in his own thoughts.

The next day, three more miners showed up. They all came from claims where the sun had just dipped below the horizon. Each had dropped off a final bag and decided to follow it down the line, like the miner from Lexell crater. 

She didn’t run it at a loss. Everything cost. A hundred milligrams *³He* for a hamburger, twice that for a beer. A shot of whiskey could be as much as a gram. The prices were high, but *³He* mining paid well. Once the sun set and the work stopped, the food drew them in.

She stayed open through the lunar night. The air recycler couldn’t quite erase the smell of lithium grease. Miners came in, lingered longer than they meant to, and left full. 

Li liked serving the guests, but every hour in the cantina was an hour she wasn’t mining. That was the real problem. She put up a sign, “Cook wanted,” with no expectation anyone would answer. Mining paid too well. 

One night, a man asked about it. He was making decent money on his claim, he said, but the solitude was grinding him down. Li offered five grams a day. He took it immediately. 

With a full-time cook, the quality of the food improved, and Li got even busier serving. Another miner came in asking if she was still looking for a cook. His sweeper had broken. Regolith seeped into everything, and he was done with the constant maintenance. Li hired him as a bartender.

Li still filled in on occasion, but she had time to herself again. People loved the simple meals, warm drinks, and old music. Nobody rushed you out. That was enough. Miners called it Tan’s. Li liked that. She hung a sign outside the airlock. “Tan’s Cantina”.

While they were getting ready for bed, Li talked about the hires. “The cantina gives me a place to think. That’s great. But what really made it work was hiring some staff. Miners get better food and better service. I think we can hire more people to improve operations.”

“We can post some job openings in the cantina. See if anyone is interested,” Darrin said.

The first new hire was Pavel Kerensky. He was a naval reactor tech before he came to the Moon. He’d spent five years in nuclear subs and knew the Soviet reactor’s internals better than anyone else on the Moon. 

He didn’t talk much, just circled the reactor pit with a dosimeter and a notebook, checking welds, testing the coolant flow, running diagnostics on the control systems. On the third day, he came back with a list of modifications.

“The control rods are sluggish. The Soviet spec called for replacement every eighteen months of operation. Belov put in fresh ones, but I want spares on hand.”

“How many spares?” Li asked.

“Full set. Plus redundant coolant pumps.” He looked up from the notes. “You want this thing running for years, yes?”

“Yes.”

“Then we do it right.”

Pavel continued, checking the hot loop with a Geiger counter. “Darrin, where is your polonium trap?”

Darrin was confused. “There is no polonium, Pavel. This is just lead and bismuth. Lunar background is higher than on Earth.”

“It was lead and bismuth. The reactor makes polonium.” Pavel ran the counter slowly over the pipe, noting the spikes. “We need an extractor.”

Pavel walked Darrin through the sparger design. “We inject helium here, where the metal comes out of the reactor.”

“How will we force the helium in without hot metal pouring out?”

“Sparge on the lee side of a wedge and the metal won’t come back into it. We only need to overcome the Laplace pressure. A standard air pump will have adequate pressure. Not much volume.”

He drew a diagram on his slate. “Inject here, at the bottom of the hot pipe, right where it comes out of the reactor. Three feet later, we collect the bubbles. Run them through a Graham condenser.”

“Like in chemistry lab?”

“Same thing, but made of tungsten to block radiation and cooled with ammonia. We make two cartridges, so we can swap them out. We will need someplace to dump the polonium, though. Far away and easy to get to.”

“How about the ridge near the solar panels? The cableway goes by it, so we can get there easily, and it is miles from anyone.”

“*Da*. That will work well. I’ll bury a heavy steel pipe there. We bring the condenser up, heat it electrically, and the polonium will drip into the pipe. Then we cap it.”

“Won’t it fill up?”

Pavel shrugged. “When it does, we can make another. The half-life is short. In five years, it is quite safe.”

Li noticed that Pavel was asking the questions they had never considered. She gave Pavel the budget and let him shut down the reactor while he tinkered. He ordered the parts through Lunar Tug, spent a month installing them, and another week running the reactor through every failure mode he could simulate. When he was done, he pronounced it safe. 

“Safe enough for what?” Darrin had asked.

Pavel shrugged. “For us to sleep at night.”

After that, the reactor became his. He ran it steadily, kept the temperature curves smooth, and made sure it didn’t kill them.

Luke Miller was hanging around Tan’s Cantina until Lunar Tug had space to bring him back to Earth. He saw Darrin eating and sat beside him. “I see you are landing regolith faster than you are processing it. What’s your plan?”

“Eat fast, get back to processing.”

Luke chuckled. “That’s a good plan. I was noticing you winch the bags up before dumping them in the oven. I’m stuck here for a couple of days. Mind if I make a suggestion?”

“What do you mean?”

“I’ve been watching the cableway drop bags on the crater floor, and you climb down, attach them to a cable, and winch them up. I was an industrial process engineer. I’d set it up so the cableway drops the bags on a chute conveyor. If you have a wide impact bed, you can easily handle surges, and your operations will be smoother and faster.”

“I don’t have time to build that.”

“You don’t have time to do what you’re doing now, either. Can I ask how hot the waste dump is?”

Darrin’s chest puffed a little. “We’re running them at over a thousand degrees.”

“I thought so. You can alter the ovens for heat recuperation. Getting four times the bake with the same amount of thermal input would be easy.”

“You want to do all that?”

“I thought you’d never ask.”

With that crew in place, Li and Darrin had time again.

In the morning, the automatic lights in their hut hadn’t come up yet. Earthshine spilled pale blue across the bed. The Earth hung unmoving outside the viewport. She lay still, listening to the hum of the air circulator and the slow, even breathing of Darrin beside her.

“You awake?” she murmured.

“Mmhmm,” came the answer, muffled by the pillow.

She stretched beneath the blanket, her muscles sore in a different way now. “We need more huts.”

Darrin rolled onto his back. “For workers?”

“Sure,” she said. “But that’s not what I meant. Miners come in during lunar night. They have a meal, maybe a drink. But when they are tired, they have to go back to their own hut in the cold, in the dark, to sleep. They spend their batteries. Then they wake up with nothing to do, so they come here again.”

“So you aren’t talking about a few huts.”

“No. Dozens of rooms.”

“A hotel?”

She swung her legs over the edge of the bunk, the floor cold under her feet. From the drawer, she pulled her undersuit, sliding it on with practiced ease. “You can call it that. Air, heat, water, and they would be close to the cantina. If that existed, miners might even work a little into the dark, because they wouldn’t be afraid of darksiding. I’d keep rent low, like half a gram per night.”

Darrin laughed. “Low.” Putting on his own undersuit, he continued. “You’re not wrong. Just sounds like a lot of work.”

“They’re coming here anyway. We should give them a bed and a door that locks.”

“You’re turning this into a town.”

“Not yet. But we’re past the camp stage.”

“Alright. I’ll lay it out. Water, power, oxygen. Just don’t ask me to handle linens.”

“You’re the best city planner I’ve got.”

Two days later, Darrin had a plan: a five-by-ten grid of used huts, with utilities, thermally anchored under regolith. 

People started staying through the lunar night. Some stayed into the day. It wasn’t enough. By the next month, he was laying out fifty more.

The music system had burned out again, leaving only the low hum of the recycler and the occasional clink of a glass. Darrin sat in the corner, tracing a cableway route on his slate. A lot of miners had built their own lines from their claim to the main route, and he was trying to make sense of the tangle. The new links made people more social. The regolith still all went to Tan’s Refinery, but the miners also visited their neighbors. Without plan or permission, the cableways had become the Moon’s primary transit system.

Li sat with her boots off, legs stretched, staring through the viewport at the growing pile of processed regolith. Mounds of lifeless gray ash lay half-lit by Earthshine beyond the huts. 

Tseng had taken to spending evenings here, claiming the background noise helped him think. He was relaxing in the cantina at the far table when Li noticed him. 

“Remind me again,” Li said without turning, “what you said was in the tailings piles.”

“You have different minerals coming in,” he said. “Ilmenite is particularly valuable, and you can pull it out magnetically.”

“Why would we do that?”

He shrugged. “It’s an iron-titanium oxide. We could, in theory, extract the titanium from it. That ore is over thirty percent titanium by weight, and valuable by metal standards. *³He* is measured in parts per billion. The titanium is actually worth more than the *³He* per ton of regolith.”

Li took a sip of water and leaned back in her chair. “What would it take?”

Tseng set the slate down and leaned forward. “You serious?”

“Serious enough to listen. You said titanium. Helion Dynamics is paying very well for titanium right now.”

He rubbed his jaw. “Alright. The first problem is heat. You’d want the FFC Cambridge process. It strips oxygen from ilmenite and leaves you with titanium and iron without using a lot of other chemicals or air.”

“If you need heat, we’ve got a reactor.”

“Sure, but it’s already busy. You’d need to slow down other processing. Better yet, get a second core just for the furnace.”

Li gave him a look. “The reactor’s barely running.”

He frowned. “You’re pulling at least two megawatts.”

“Electric,” she said. “Yeah. The generator gives us ten if we want it, but we’re pulling around two. Add another megawatt thermal for the ovens. But the reactor’s rated for a hundred fifty megawatts thermal. We’re barely tickling it. With Pavel on board, we can pull the full one fifty.”

That made him sit up. “A hundred fifty megawatts?”

“If we feed it right. You give me a reason, and Pavel will give you the power.”

He leaned back, already calculating. “That changes things.”

“How do we separate the titanium from the iron?”

“That’s trickier. You’d need carbon monoxide. The Moon is carbon-poor, but the gas gets recycled, so it’s not impossible. You can buy some from Earth, and we will just keep reusing that.”

“And then we’re sitting on titanium bars?”

“More like sponges. You’d still have to melt them. That’s phase two. Clean it, melt it, cast it, ship it.”

Li let the silence stretch. Outside the viewport, the slag pile lay pale in the Earthlight.

“We can send bars back on Lunar Tug,” she said.

Tseng shook his head. “No. That will never work. Helium works that way because it’s light and pricey. Metals, even at Raines’ rates, don’t fly well.” He paused, eyes narrowing. “But if you sling them with a mass driver, that changes things. Aim for the Pacific, and let them drop.”

She laughed. “You already picked a splash zone?”

“Not yet,” he said, lifting the slate. “But if this project is a go, I will work it out.”

“How big?”

He tapped a few numbers. “Thousand-ton slabs. Big enough to recover, small enough not to vaporize the ocean. Slow them with surface area and an ablative heat shield, keep them under Mach two at impact. Most of the heat shield burns off, and the rest shatters on impact with the ocean. Make them bowl-shaped for stability and cast an eye into the back so a salvage crew can winch it out. It will land flat, and it won’t bury itself in the mud.”

“You’ve thought this through.”

“It works,” he said. “Raines will pay for titanium, even if it’s salty. He’ll fish it out without complaint.”

Li brought up her slate and began calculating. “Tell me more about the mass driver.”

“We’ll need a precision instrument from Earth,” Tseng said. “We can mount it on the ridge. Hitting Earth is easy. Getting it to slow down right, break into the atmosphere correctly, and land where it’s supposed to? That takes real calibration. No shortcuts.”

Li went to the window. The tailings piles caught the faint light, their long shadows stretching across the basin floor, marking her life, devoted to power. Tseng’s idea would erase that pile. 

“It’s not just about helium anymore,” she said.

Tseng looked up. 

When she saw her reflection in the window, she thought about her family photo. She realized her parents were smiling. This was what goodbye felt like. 

“Neither am I.”

She turned toward the kitchen. The stew would still be warm, and someone would be hungry soon.

## 

## **Chapter 7: Salty Metal**

| Price of *³He* | $7,800,000/kg |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** | **32 tons/month** |
| **Current Consumption** | **40 tons/year** |
| **Time Since Start** | **28 months** |

José Torres was glassing the sky at night when he saw the fireball. He cranked *Peces de la Luna* up to forty knots, watching it disappear beyond the horizon. Seven minutes later, a second fireball appeared.

He glanced at a picture of his kids above the wheel, riding their bicycles.

*Peces de la Luna* was designed for a specific task: ten rapid deep-sea recovery operations at a time. The hull design was borrowed from a high-speed Italian ferry, the MDV 3000 Jupiter, allowing it to reach forty knots when fully laden. The ship was painted bright yellow, with a dark blue racing stripe, the fastest ship in Lázaro Cárdenas. Tonight, it was prepped for eight recoveries.

A second fireball showed as the first one hit the water. The sound of the splashdown took eight minutes to arrive.

Lunar librations gave a one-hour launch window each month. Tseng had launched eight targets during the window, three days ago. As they arrived, the Earth’s rotation was stretching those impacts into a thousand-mile line.

When the target struck the seabed, the thud played on the ships’ loudspeakers. The acoustic analyst triangulated the signals, calculating precise coordinates. Precision didn’t always mean accuracy. Five miles out, José called for the crew to shut off the engines. The sea around *Peces de la Luna* was suddenly still. He released a UUV, which swam a search spiral around the calculated impact site, finding the target less than a thousand yards away. 

José ordered a recovery glider to be prepared. An air compressor kicked on, inflating six support pylons attached to the fantail. When they were filled, the stern crane lowered the glider down onto them. The ropes, hawsers, and the lift bag had all been packed in the tail with the recovery kit. 

Once the glider was secured, a chute laid three hundred tons of construction rubble evenly across the ballast box. Liquid oxygen and liquid hydrogen, each in corrugated thin-walled cans, insulated by syntactic foam, were strapped in place, completing the recovery kit. 

José held off on launch. It took ten minutes to get a clear image from the UUV over the acoustic modem. When he was satisfied that the target’s shackle was undamaged, he signalled for the drop. The pontoons deflated, and the glider slipped off sideways. In a few seconds, the glider disappeared from view. He dropped an acoustic transponder buoy at the site so he could continue to communicate with the UUV, and *Peces de la Luna* sprinted to the next destination, two and a half hours away, to do it all over again.

The glider dove fast. Every minute spent in the water meant more heat transfer into the cryoliquids and more boiling loss. As the pressure rose, the pressure relief bubbles became more of a shimmer than individual gas pockets. In thirty minutes, the glider flared and settled beside the target, seventeen thousand feet below the waves.

The hawser extending from the lift bag was fourteen inches in diameter. The UUV guided the latching end to the shackle and locked it in place.

Secured, the lifting bag was spread over the hydrogen tank, and the valve was cracked open, letting gas vent slowly into the bag. At this pressure, it was not so much a gas as a supercritical fluid, but it still rose in seawater. The seawater that came into contact with the hydrogen flash-froze and sank to the bottom of the bag. As the bag took shape, it centered itself over the bowl. The feed rate was increased, dumping tons of hydrogen into the bag. The UUV sprayed seawater into the bag at high pressure, warming the hydrogen. Snow fell softly through the supercritical hydrogen, collecting at the base of the bag. This continued until the hydrogen was warmed to ambient, around 3°C. The bag was forty percent full, pulling five hundred tons.

The UUV fed oxygen in through a catalytic combustor, heating the hydrogen from abyssal cold to 400°C. The heat expanded the hydrogen until the bag was filled, reaching eleven hundred tons of buoyancy. The thousand-ton bowl weighed less than eight hundred tons in seawater, but the lift wasn’t enough. Nothing moved.

José relayed another command to the UUV, ineffectually trying to rock the bowl. He then had it circle the bowl, using its thrusters to blow the silt away. 

As he sat watching the UUV work, he looked at the size of the bowl. “A billion pesos for just that one bowl, enough to keep the bank from seizing my company.” 

Forty minutes passed before the bowl shifted. The lift of the bag overcame the suction of the silt. A snap sounded over the ship’s loudspeakers as the suction let go and the bowl lifted free. The crew on *Peces de la Luna* cheered.

The bowl rose quickly, spilling gas from the base of the lift bag as pressure decreased. The bowl was fluttering, jerking the hawser violently. José had the UUV pull the vent line, reefing the base of the Kapton gas bag. At five hundred feet, the vent was reefed further, slowing ascent to six inches per second.

When the bag breached, the bowl’s momentum kept it rising, slackening the hawser. With no lifting force, gravity won. The bowl descended, snapping the hawser taut, pulling the bag back under. It continued bobbing dangerously for a minute before the bowl hung dead in the water. José made a note: “Next time, slow to four inches per second.”

In two hours, *Pleamar* arrived, well deck already awash. Divers pulled a rubber donut under the bowl and inflated it until the rim of the bowl rose above the waterline. The gear was hauled up onto the dry half-deck, where it was washed and fogged with deionized water. The ropes, hawsers, and lift bag were all transferred into a freshwater soak tank with Salt-Away.

*Pleamar’s* heavy three-hundred-ton crane could not lift the bowl, but it had enough authority to precisely position it over a pedestal. Once in place, the donut was vented until the bowl made solid contact with the wooden shoes on the pedestal. 

*Pleamar* purged its tanks. When the cargo deck was dry, they retrieved the donut, washed it, and stowed it for the next recovery. The bowl was rinsed with soap and deionized water. Recovery had taken four hours from arrival to having the bowl strapped in place. *Pleamar* was again underway, headed to the second target.

After the final drop, José called Felipe. “Every bowl was intact. A couple of shackles were bent, but no real problems. How is the mill?”

“*Mano*, good to hear from you. I have the crew scrubbing the walls and ceilings now. This will be a surgical cleanroom when you get back. How far out are you?”

“We don’t have enough fuel to sprint back. We dropped down to thirty knots. I should be back in two days.”

“Should I deploy the grillage?”

“We can do that after I get back. The cargo won’t arrive for another six days.”

“We will scrub the dock down, anyway. Now, *carnal*, I have someone here who wants to talk to you.”

Felipe handed the phone to Maria. “José, the bank called again. They are starting foreclosure proceedings.”

“It’s going to be OK*, vieja*. Once I sell this load, we’ll have enough. They won’t take our villa. Where are the kids?”

“I brought them here with me to help Felipe clean the factory. Mateo is scrubbing the lunchroom floor. He works very hard for a ten-year-old man.”

“And Sofia?”

“Sofia is why Mateo needs to scrub the lunchroom floor.”

*Pleamar* picked up the five nearest bowls over the next two days, and *Bajamar* got the three farthest ones. José stayed on the phone with the captains for every recovery. There was no time for more than short naps on the trip, and when he got back, there were more preparations.

*Bajamar* made it back to port first. José and Felipe had laid out the plywood and two-inch steel plate grillage before it arrived. When *Bajamar* backed into its berthing slot, they extended the grillage to cover the ramp and the ship’s deck.

Then SPMTs came like an army of ants, lifting the bowls from their pedestals, carrying them slowly off the ship to the fabrication room.

The alpha case was milled off under argon. The clean bowls were melted under vacuum and poured into ten-ton molds. The outer three millimeters of each ingot were milled away, remelted, and cast into industrial titanium. Each bowl took thirty hours. When they were done, the aerospace-grade titanium was loaded onto different boxcars than the industrial titanium. Felipe swept up the spatter and alpha case swarf and sold it separately to ArcelorMittal*.*

José signed the shipping documents, and it was the CPKC Railroad’s responsibility to deliver the titanium. One week until the next operation.

Maria put away the bills when she saw him coming in the door. “José, we haven’t seen you in so long.”

José opened his arms, hugging her and his kids all together. “Maria, I’ll be home more this week. What’s for dinner?”

He fell asleep during dinner. Maria and Mateo moved him to his bed, where he slept sixteen hours straight. When he woke, the kids were already at school, and his wife was getting groceries. He sat in his chair and drank a Coke, staring at the clouds. 

The next day, *Pleamar* and *Bajamar* were preparing to leave. He helped scrub the decks. They each took four hundred tons of MGO. When he paid the fourteen-million-peso bill, he wished they ran on LNG. Or at least were painted more brightly.

Over the next few days, the gear was all inspected and prepped for the next operation. One of the hawsers showed internal fraying under X-ray and was discarded. Two lifting bags needed patching, and one of the gas bags tore and needed to be replaced. All the eye thimbles were hammered and had to be replaced. The marine supply store behind his mill had woven all his bags and ropes. They were happy to do the repairs and replace the parts. 

The crew was loading up *Peces de la Luna* when José got a call from Elin. “You sent me seven thousand tons of aerospace-grade titanium and seven hundred tons of industrial-grade. I got a report back from metallurgy. The quality is outstanding. I can pay seventy dollars per kilogram for the aerospace-grade titanium, and thirty per kilogram for the rest.“

José calculated his cut. Twenty per cent would give him $1.8 billion pesos. Suddenly, his mill’s two hundred million peso debt didn’t seem so bad. “Yes, ma’am. Thank you, ma’am.”

“Do you plan on sending us any more?”

“I’d like to. I am about to pick up some more.”

“Look, I am behind my targets, and your titanium is really helping. You run a steel mill? Do you know how to fabricate high-purity titanium?”

“Sure,” he lied.

“If you could send me twelve-gauge titanium sheet metal, I’d pay double. I can send you the specs. You would really be helping me out.”

“Of course, *Senora*.”

“Thank you, José.”

“*De nada*.”

José called Felipe. “I just got off the phone with Dr. Elin Sarai.”

“From Biloxi? Did she buy it?”

“She loved it. The money is already in the company’s account. She wants more.”

“Well, she’ll get more. You are shipping out tonight, right?”

“Yes, but she wants us to make sheet metal. She will pay double for twelve gauge. Can we do that?”

“I’m on it, *carnal*. You go get the next delivery.”

José put to sea. He had twelve hours to the first deployment. He glassed the sky, but there was nothing yet. 

He was in the mess hall when Li called. “I saw the deposit in my account. Four hundred million dollars.”

“Yes. How is that?”

“That means my cut is fifty million per bowl. We just launched ten more.”

“Tan, she said if I can roll it into sheets, she will pay double.”

“Whatever you want to do is fine with me, but if you screw up the sheets, and Elin won’t buy them, I still expect my five hundred million dollars.”

“Yes, ma’am. I have been wondering, why me? I mean, there are much bigger names in metal and shipping and whatever you want to do. Acero del Pacífico is small fry.”

“Your location is convenient for me. I could have asked ArcelorMittal to help instead, and I am sure they would have agreed. This would be one of their most important product lines. I would sit in the front row. That’s nice. But with you, I don’t sit in the front row. I sit alone. I told you I wanted you to have ships, a new dock, and cogeneration ready in a year. ArcelorMittal would agree, but there would be analyses and meetings, and two years later, they still wouldn’t be done. I lent you the money; you did the impossible. I told you I wanted to get paid, regardless of any other problems. You said, ‘Yes, ma’am.’ I trust you. Not because I know you or you are trustworthy, but because you have no choice. That takes the stress out of it, for me.”

“I understand. But Tan, you have lent me ten billion pesos to buy the ships and build out our dock. It is so much.” 

“The loan is real, and I expect to get paid back, but don’t hurt your business to pay me back. When you are comfortable, let Elin give me part of your share. The first months are hard, but it will not be hard forever.”

The run had ten targets. A couple of the shackles were bent, as before, but this time, one shackle sheared off completely, making it unrecoverable. José called on the way back. “Felipe, I am coming in. The third bowl was unrecoverable. The shackle was shorn clean off.”

“Tan is going to take that out of our cut.”

“Not if I recover it. I am not sure how. I need you to set me up with two packs and a bunch of extra hawsers. I don’t have a real plan yet.”

“If you are shipping out as soon as you get back, does that mean I will be handling the offloading and processing myself this time?”

“Are you OK with that, Mano? You only need to process *Bajamar’s* load. *Pleamar is* keeping station now for the rescue.”

“I will take care of it. I am sending the truck to Liquide Aire to get the oxygen and will have two hydrogen containers and all the gear ready when you arrive.”

“Gracias, carnal. I am two days out.”

*Pleamar* had held station for three days when José got back to the site of the unsalvaged bowl. The captain had already sent down two UUVs to search for the shackle and found nothing. He dropped a fresh UUV and the first glider.

When they got down, he had them wrap a long hawser around the bowl, then used a mechanical cinch to tighten it. When the lift bag started pulling, he saw the problem. The bowl had been settling since it hit the bottom. If he had taken another week, it would probably be covered in silt. The sticktion force was beyond anything he had anticipated. He kept the lift bag hot at eleven hundred tons of buoyancy while he sent the second glider. 

This time, he used a net made of one-inch rope. Two UUVs pulled it under the bowl, moving through the silt. Progress was imperceptible. José had them saw it back and forth under the bowl. Progress was still slow, but he could see it, every stroke a few inches. Four hours later, the net was completely secured. José connected it to the second lift bag and put in a little hydrogen to give it tension. The net could only hold about two hundred tons safely.

The problem now was the first bag. It was losing lift fast. The first oxygen tank was empty, the hydrogen was cooling off, and slowly permeating the Kapton inner bag. The second glider still had cryogens, but the syntactic insulation was meant for a fifteen-minute dive, not four hours of sitting. 

José put another five tons of hydrogen in the second bag, but didn’t heat it. He moved what was left of the second hydrogen tank to the first bag and used the oxygen to heat that. 

While the first bag warmed and expanded, the UUVs roped the two gliders and the cryotanks to the net so they wouldn’t get lost. When the temperature rose to three hundred degrees, less than a thousand tons of lift, José thought he saw the bowl lifting. There was no pop. He asked the crew if they were seeing what he was seeing. As they tried to decide if it was moving at all, it sped up. There was no pop, but the bowl was rising.

The net had broken the sticktion. Pulling that through had given water a hundred channels to flood in behind the bowl as it was lifted from the mud. Everyone cheered. The Pleamar captain blew the foghorn. José loved that and blew *Peces de la Luna’s* dual-tone foghorn.

Because of the noise, José didn’t notice it at first. There was a metallic crashing sound as the bowl rose. The UUV showed the culprit; two gliders were smashing into each other and the cryotanks, now filled only with water, were beating each other like drums.

He reefed the hem of the first bag, but by now, the hydrogen in the second bag had warmed to ambient, and as the pressure dropped, it expanded. He heard a loud pop. A UUV flew under, and he could see that a link in the net had broken. He was past the safety margins. He gave the command for the UUV to reef the second bag, but as it started going up, a couple more segments popped, then it was like tearing fabric. The second bag was now lifting a torn and empty net and the gear. It shot up like a rocket, hitting the first lifting bag, forcing it to burp its lifting gas. One of the gliders hooked on the first bag, dragging it up a ways before letting go. The equipment on the second net was going far too fast to be stable. The tanks and gliders were kiting back and forth, smashing into each other. 

It hurt to watch his equipment smash itself to pieces, but it could all be replaced for a million pesos. The bowl was worth nearly a billion pesos. He tried to keep it in perspective.

The screen had the rate of rise. The bowl had good momentum, and the lift bag was still pulling, but the numbers were slowing down. Twenty meters per second dropped to fifteen, dropped to ten. If it stopped rising, there was nothing he could do for it. As long as it continued to rise, the gas was expanding, increasing the lift. He was helpless and didn’t know what speed would show failure. 

The second bag breached. His crew pulled it and the smashed gear up on *Peces de la Luna’s* deck. José kept watching the rise rate, which had dropped to less than a meter per second. He remembered he would slow them down more than that when they breached. If the issue was buoyancy, he was fine. If it was momentum…

At seven thousand feet, the number went back up to a meter per second. José let his breath out. He realized he hadn’t breathed for over a minute. 

The rise continued and accelerated, but José kept it reefed tight. No extra strain on the gear. *Peces de la Luna* and *Pleamar* both waited, giving a half-mile clearance for the assembly to surface. This time, *Peces de la Luna* took the lift bag and hawsers. When the bowl was secured on Pleamar, the teams got together for a moment of celebration before going home.

José called Felipe on the way back. “We got the bowl.”

“Fantastic. How did it go?”

“Smooth and easy. Sort of.”

“José, you need to talk to Tseng. He can fix this problem.”

“It wasn’t a problem. I just had to go out again.”

“Carnal, this was a problem. Talk to them.”

José called Li, Darrin, and Tseng. “On the first drop, there were bent shackles, but I didn’t worry.”

“How badly?” Tseng asked.

“I didn’t see it as a problem. We were able to recover them. But on this run, one of the shackles was shorn off completely, probably on a sideways splashdown. We never found the shackle.”

“You lost one of my bowls?” Li asked.

“No, ma’am. We recovered it. It took an extra week, but it is on a pedestal now, heading back to port.”

“Then what’s the problem?”

“Well, Felipe has suggested it before, and I agree, we need something sturdier.”

Darrin was drawing on his slate. “I’ve seen similar problems in the North Sea. A shackle works well in port, but at sea, a crossed shackle is much stronger.”

Tseng agreed. “Of course. Next shipment, cross shackles, and heavier bars.”

“*Gracias*. Tseng, if you can send Felipe the geometry, he is going to make specialized castings for the connector hardware for the crossed shackle. My current hardware is too weak, anyway.”

When José returned, Felipe had already processed the five bowls from Bajamar. They had a moment to talk.

“José, the sheet metal is not so simple. I have ordered the equipment we will need, but it isn’t only the equipment. We need to hire more staff.”

“Of course.”

“Yes, well, the men we need are specialists. Harder to place. And it is not just the staff, either. This has to be done in a separate building. That is the only way to deliver high-purity titanium sheets. I made an offer on the marine supply shop behind us. I think they will take it. Then I will need to build a new building and extend a connection to it.”

“I like the maritime supply shop. They make our gear.”

“They still will, but from a new storefront.”

“I wanted to make sheet metal this month, or worst case, next month. Now it sounds like it may take six months.”

“No, carnal. I will not leave you waiting for six months. But you need to know the constraints.”

“*Gracias*.”

“How is Maria?”

“She has bills to pay, and nothing to pay them with. And I am gone more than ever.”

“It’s not easy.”

“How is Lupita?”

“She is doing well. Speaking of Lupita, I need you to come with me for dinner at her father’s house.”

“Of course. Should I bring Maria?”

“Yes, and your children.”

“And my children? Is there a special occasion?”

“*La Petición de Mano.*”

“*¡Felicidades, carnal\! Ya era hora, ¿no?*”

“*Si*, I should have done this a year ago. But, *mano*, I need to show you something.”

They walked back to the main office.

“When we processed the titanium last month, we got a pile of alpha case millings and copper-contaminated swarf.”

“We were pretty efficient. How much swarf was there?” 

“Less than half a percent, but that was still thirty tons.”

“What did you do?”

“I talked to a buyer at ArcelorMittal. They wanted titanium since Elin has been buying it all up. They gave me a finder’s fee off the books.”

“How much are we talking about?”

“See those two duffel bags?”

José opened one, finding mostly five-hundred and two-hundred peso notes. “Well done, Carnal.”

“Bring one to Maria and see if it brightens her day. For me, I think mine is enough to start a family.”

José ate dinner at home that night. “Maria, work is not going to get easier for me. But last month’s load sold, and Acero del Pacífico is now solvent.”

Maria looked up at him in tears. “José, they set a date. We have to pay in a week, or they will force us out.”

José dragged out the duffel bag and set it beside his wife. “This is for you. It is enough to keep the villa.”

Maria opened the bag of cash. She cried and hugged him. “We still miss you.”

José fell asleep watching TV. 

When he came into the office the next day, his secretary updated him. “José, you have a visitor.”

“It seems I always do. Can I meet him after lunch?”

“No, not this visitor. I sent him into your office. He has been there half an hour already. You’ll want to see him.”

“Marcia, we’ve discussed this. Do not let visitors into my office.”

“I think this was the right time to make an exception.”

José walked into his office. A tall, blond man was staring out the window overlooking the Acero del Pacífico docks. José cleared his throat. “*Buenos días, Señor.*”

The man turned to face him. Tobias Raines. José dropped his coffee.

“I was just looking at your dock here. You import steel from Asia?”

“Yes, that is my business. And some small fabrication jobs.”

“I see. I talked to Dr. Sarai last night. She mentioned you had just sent her over fifteen thousand tons of titanium. Nice job for a steel importer.”

“Yes. Thank you, sir. Elin has been very generous. I hope to do more for her.”

“I hope you do, but I am curious. Where is the titanium coming from? I am looking at your fleet. You are doing marine salvage?”

“Yes. Salvage. I find the titanium on the ocean floor.”

Raines looked away. “I notice most of the money goes to an offshore account, and the rest goes to Acero del Pacífico’s Banorte account.” 

“Yes, I only get a portion.”

“That offshore account, I know it. I buy *³He* from that account. The most prolific miner on the Moon. But I don’t know his name.”

“It’s not a man, it’s a woman. Tan. She said she would drop high-purity titanium on the ocean floor. I pick it up. She gets eighty percent of the ingot fair value, I cover all expenses and losses, and keep the rest.”

José went over the details, Tseng’s bowl designs, and the salvage process. He took Raines on a tour of *Peces de la Luna.*

“Why did you use LNG?”

“It is six hundred tons lighter than MGO, which helps. It cuts maintenance costs on my engines. But, mostly, the fuel costs half as much. This is a steel mill. I already have gas mains. That is also why I have a cogen plant and a hydrogen reformer.”

“Rubble ballast?”

“I need three hundred tons of ballast per recovery. I wanted something inexpensive. I have a barge collecting construction rubble. People pay me to take it.”

“Doesn’t it wreck your hull?”

“I deadbed with silt from channel dredges. It’s worked out well. I rake it when I get back to port.”

“You’ve built a very nice system. I have a question for you. What would you think of expanding your recovery operation?”

“Well, sir, *Peces de la Luna* is built for ten recoveries. I could go out more often, but Tseng only launches once per month.”

“What if it were someone other than Tseng launching, and the metals were something other than titanium?”

“I am completely ready for any metal, if they follow Tseng’s design.” 

Raines knew he wasn’t.

“I’m not ready to ship now. I won’t be for a couple of years, at least. But when I am, we can do good business, if you don’t mind expanding.”

“Yes, sir. If I need to expand my fleet, can you give me notice with a contract I can show a bank?”

“We can do that. We can do much better than that.” 

They shook hands.

## 

## **Chapter 8: Ceres**

| Price of *³He* | $6,000,000/kg |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** | **95 tons/month** |
| **Current Consumption** | **91 ton/year** |
| **Time Since Start** | **37 months to 60 months** |

Raines sat alone in the narrow conference room, eyes fixed on the empty chair across from him. The air was too cold. His tea cooled, untouched, beside a decision he did not want to make. He disliked what he was about to do, but the list of palatable options was gone.

He faced a wall of demand that was both exponential and geopolitical. Every nation wanted a terawatt-class tokaplex, and they wanted it now. A terawatt meant energy sovereignty, freedom from oil and coal, and independence in every negotiation.

Between that ambition and reality stood refractory metals. Ten-gigawatt plants were feasible, but costly; a terawatt-class core would consume more strategic metals than Earth could produce. The fusion age demanded mass. 

Doubling the spot price had increased mining. Production had risen an incredible twenty percent. But a twenty percent increase was never going to build terawatt tokaplexes. Raines had not taken the meeting with Chen Zhengrong lightly. He needed a tenfold increase, and he was done asking.

The Asteroid Belt held the minerals, but it was untested, hazardous, and expensive. Raines saw no alternative. Helion Dynamics would build its own supply chain of ore pulled from the asteroids, refined, and sent Earthward. A heavy-metal pipeline from the Asteroid Belt to the Pacific.

Hundreds might die before the first shipment reached Earth. That did not change his decision. Success would depend on the director.

Catherine Dahl had run hazardous mining projects: Antarctic mining under miles of ice, deep-sea mining at enormous pressures, and magmatic brine extraction from the roots of active volcanoes. She had always delivered the ore, even when the loss of life was catastrophic.

Ceres would be worse. The odds of the first crew returning whole were slim to none. They might all die. He needed someone who would do it again.

There were many people whom he might ask to run this project. Many would refuse, squeamish at the thought of blood on their hands. Others would paper over the problems with endless procedures. Then, when something inevitably did go wrong, there would be investigations, recriminations, and resignations. A stalled project. 

Dahl was different; she understood projects where miners die. She acknowledged the risk to investors and to the miners upfront, then delivered anyway. Not from indifference, but from clarity.

Raines didn’t need sentimentality; he needed metal. Dahl could deliver.

The door opened without a knock.

Catherine Dahl stepped in, unhurried. No slate, no assistant. Just a canvas jacket worn smooth at the elbows and a look that said this was already taking too long. She scanned the room, gray eyes fixed on Raines.

“Catherine Dahl\! It’s a pleasure to meet you. I trust your flight was pleasant.”

“What do you want?”

“Helion Dynamics is in a bind. We need refractory metals in enormous quantities. The Earth can’t supply us. We are going to the Asteroid Belt.”

Dahl pulled out a chair and sat.

“Ceres,” Raines continued. “Not a pilot project. Our supply chain would depend on steady, massive output from this operation. Mine the Asteroid Belt, smelt it on Ceres, ship it to Earth.”

She studied him, measuring the gap between words and meaning, then leaned back. “I’ve never mined in space,” she said dismissively.

“No one has,” Raines responded. “Who do you think would do a better job running this operation?”

“It’s not just technical,” she said. “Ceres will put crews under pressure, long cycles in low light, and almost no gravity. The kind of work where people make mistakes. Keeping them focused and productive will be the real job.”

“I have to have this metal.”

She held his gaze, then gave a single nod. Not acceptance, but willing to talk.

“Finding people for technical jobs on Earth will be straightforward. Hiring people to go willingly to Ceres is different. Were I to be involved, I would be honest about the risk upfront. The hires would need to be men who won’t fold when things go sideways. You cannot send men who can’t do the job. The training must be as brutal as the work, or they will break when they get on site. I’ve seen it happen.”

“I am not looking for a butcher. I expect the miners to be treated with respect and integrity. While that won’t make this operation safe, we can work out hazard pay and insurance policies honestly.”

Dahl watched the Jet d’Eau through his window. “I can get you your metal,” she said. “I expect to be fairly compensated.”

“Of course. Helion Dynamics will compensate you more than fairly. Bonuses for meeting early delivery goals will ensure our motivations align.”

“Yes, but there will come a time when I will take the fall.”

“Helion Dynamics would never do that, Catherine.”

“I appreciate what you are saying, Raines, but this is not about you.”

“What is it, then?”

“You are willing to accept harsh conditions and a high death toll. As we become successful, society will accept neither, and you will no longer be in control of the story. This is not about you, it is about Ceres.”

“What do you need?”

“A substantial stake in Helion Dynamics. Five percent.”

“Elin’s share is five percent.”

“Elin won’t be scapegoated.”

“You are asking too much. So am I. I will bring it to the board, but they will not approve this lightly.”

“Then make them understand the importance of this project. At that point, they will have no choice. When do we get started?”

“Here, now. This is not some far-off goal. I need metal yesterday. I have already lined up two General Atomics ships for this project. You can do whatever you want, but cargo launches in two months. Crew follows fifteen months later on a faster ship.”

Dahl did the math. “Power, life support, base construction, full mining and refining. All in orbit in two months?”

“You figure out what you need. But I expect metal flowing six months after the crew lands.”

She gave a low whistle. “That’s aggressive, Tobias. It will take everything lining up.”

“That is the requirement.”

Dahl looked in his eyes to see if this was theatrics. “OK, we will do it. The first interplanetary operation.” She was in.

“Who’s on the ground on Ceres?”

“No one yet.”

“You’re not sending me?”

“No. You stay Earthside. But you choose who goes. Your team, your rules.”

She closed her eyes for a moment, letting the scope of the job take shape in her mind. She pictured the crews, the equipment, the operation in motion. Then she said, “Dr. Silas Knox.”

Raines leaned back, skeptical. “Knox? He’s radioactive. Half the ports on Earth have his face on their banned miners list.”

“I know. That’s why he’s still sharp. No safety net. He can’t afford to be mediocre. And his brilliance makes him worth the risk.”

She slid a slate across the desk. Knox’s record was there in black and white. Brilliant work, often illegal.

“Knox doesn’t get second chances. He can’t waste a shot, can’t afford to screw up and wait for someone to catch him.” She leaned forward slightly. “He’s exactly as good as he has to be, which is better than anyone else.”

Raines studied her, then the file. “You sure he won’t bolt when things turn bad?”

Dahl’s mouth tilted toward a smile. “Out there, there’s nowhere to run.”

“It’s your team, Catherine. Your call.”

Knox had been Scripps Mining’s golden boy, a rising star in the cutthroat world of deep-sea mining. He didn’t just survive it; he thrived. He pulled off impossible recoveries, turned marginal claims into record-breaking yields, and built fortunes for his employers and himself.

For a time, he lived like a king: a mansion in San Diego, cars bought on impulse, parties where champagne flowed from taps. But it was never about the money. It was the sense that he could beat the ocean itself.

The ocean doesn’t play fair.

One bad payout, a mine collapse, furious investors, and panicked insurers left him over-leveraged. The mansion and the lifestyle became a noose.

He panicked. Took a quiet side job. Helped a shady outfit steal ore from a competitor’s reserve, replacing it with sea rock convincing enough to pass. No one saw it happen. It was perfect.

Until it wasn’t. A man he’d worked with was arrested on an unrelated drug charge and, in a plea deal, he named Knox. 

Knox did time. Lost his license. Lost everything except his ability.

After that, he worked where no one asked for papers, the gray zones where contracts were enforced with guns, and betrayal was just another line item. The jobs were brilliant. They were also illegal. Eventually, he stopped caring.

Two days later, Dahl found him in a half-lit cargo bay, elbow-deep in a crate of stolen spectrometry modules. The ship belonged to black market ocean miners, rough, half-legal, trawling the Atlantic floor for minerals no one could prove they had stolen.

“Dr. Silas Knox?”

He didn’t look up. “Depends who’s asking.”

“Catherine Dahl. I’m building a mining operation. Legitimate work. You would run site ops.”

That earned a flicker of interest. His eyes flicked to the shadows, still wary. “What kind of mining?”

She stepped closer, boots ringing on steel. “Ceres.”

Knox snorted, dry as cracked concrete. He straightened, wiped his hands on a rag black with grease.

“You serious?”

“Dead.”
