He studied her, eyes sharp and cold, like a man measuring exits with nowhere left to go.

“You think I’ll live long enough to deliver metal?”

“You might.” She didn’t flinch when she said it.

The silence stretched. Dahl didn’t press.

Knox sealed the last crate, slow and methodical. When he straightened, his eyes were on the floor, as if the decision had been made before she even arrived.

“I got into ocean mining for the freedom,” he said. “You dropped off the map, ran your own operation, answered to no one but the weather.”

He glanced at the rust-streaked walls, the flickering lights, the smell of salt, oil, and rot.

“That’s gone. Now it’s just jurisdictions and contracts and lawyers with maps. You try to cut a trench, and five governments file injunctions before your rig hits mud.”

He met her gaze, and for a moment, the cynicism cracked.

“I used to build things that weren’t supposed to work. Projects that should’ve failed in weeks, yet I made them hold.” He swallowed. “I don’t do that anymore.”

Dahl said nothing.

“I thought maybe I’d crawl my way back. Do enough gray work, stay off the grid, wait for someone to forget.” He shook his head. “Earth doesn’t forget. Every mistake’s on record, every port scans your name, and the only thing they want is you gone.”

He looked toward the open hatch. Outside, the hull creaked with the tide. A cold wind cut across the dock.

“Ceres is a rock. No borders, no laws, no one telling you what can’t be done. It’s not clean or safe, but it’s empty. And empty’s the best deal I’ve had in a long time.”

He turned back to her. “I’m not doing this to run. I’m doing it because I remember who I was before I had to run.”

He stepped closer, boots ringing on the deck. “You’re offering me a hostile place with no rules and no margin for error.” His voice was steady. “That’s where I started. Maybe it’s where I belong.”

Dahl held his gaze. “When can you be ready?”

He stood up and walked out of the cargo bay without looking back. “I already am.”

Dahl built the home team. None of them would ever set foot on Ceres. Their job was to make sure everything that did go worked. She brought in Dr. Feld, a geologist with a long history in remote analysis. He would be searching the Asteroid Belt with a space telescope, cross-referencing spectral returns with mineral data. When samples came back from Ceres, he would read their structure, grade their potential, and flag new targets. He would be in charge of choosing which asteroids were mined.

Dahl’s system engineers went over the equipment lists. Make sure the team survives. Her chemical engineers focused on extraction. What to refine, how to do it, and what form the metal should take to survive reentry and recovery. If the chemistry didn’t work on Ceres, there would be no way to fix it, no way to refine that metal. 

The General Atomics spine ship: a long, skeletal truss with a nuclear-electric drive at one end, was already parked in high Earth orbit, its frame slowly filling with extractor ships, tenders, folding habitats, smelter kits, environmental modules, anything bulky or heavy they would need. It looked less like a spacecraft than a junk cart piled high. Once the ship left, there was no means of correction. That was final.

Food was the last thing anyone wanted to talk about. It came from algae and bacteria, grown in sewage and nutrient broth. The tanks would convert it into a slurry of macros, micros, and amino profiles. Balanced. Efficient. Knox sampled a swig during prep. It hit the back of his throat like rot and copper. He gagged. Swallowed. Didn’t complain. Told Dahl it was an acquired taste. Then checked the manifest for a redundant water processor.

Knox didn’t involve himself in the logistics. He skimmed manifests, asked a few questions, and turned back to what mattered, the crew.

While Dahl engineered the infrastructure from Earth, Knox focused on the people who would inhabit Ceres. His job was to hire the people who would live and die on Ceres. Miners, riggers, smelter operators. The crews who would drill into rock, refine metal, cast it into reentry shapes, and fire it toward Earth. They answered to Knox and Knox alone. 

Dahl wanted to start with fifty men on Ceres. Men who would not flinch. Skill was optional. Desperation was not. She told him to recruit two hundred.

“Do you want two hundred or fifty?”

“Fifty, plus a few that may go later. But I expect most candidates to wash out. If they are going to be a liability, I don’t want you coddling them. I want them out. If they get to Ceres and can’t handle it, everyone’s lives will be at risk.”

“What happens to the washouts?”

“That’s not our concern.”

“It is mine. I will know these people.”

“Be careful, Silas. Don’t get too close.”

He didn’t like it, but couldn’t fault her logic. He began recruiting. First, he talked to miners who had started as children and were now trapped in the most hazardous corners of the trade. They had lived with danger for so long that they treated it as routine. They had watched friends die and developed a practical instinct for when a job was drifting off script. They were quick on their feet. Ceres would be harsher than anything they had faced, but the pay exceeded what they were used to.

He next recruited from deep-sea mining crews. They worked in conditions that punished small mistakes. They understood pressure, machinery, and the routines that kept both from turning lethal. Knox had worked with some of these men, and they respected him enough to consider the offer seriously. Only a few were willing to sign on. They were not as desperate as the others and understood the risk. Knox was satisfied. The few he got would be enough.

He couldn’t promise them safety. Only work, and a paycheck if they lasted long enough to collect it. Their estates would be paid if they didn’t.

Dr. Knox finished selecting two hundred candidates three months after the cargo ship departed. In San Diego, they boarded two C-130J-30s. One held all their equipment, gear, luggage, and Catherine Dahl with a dozen of her technical staff. The other plane, the sardine can, was packed with two hundred candidates, facing the rear cargo ramp. Dr. Knox acted as their flight attendant. 

“Buckle up, this is going to be a long flight,” Knox said over the rising whine of the four turbines. “If anyone needs to use the bathroom or get nauseous, raise your hand and a plastic cup with a lid will be passed to you. When you are done, pass that back. I will empty it and rinse it in the washroom. There will be no food or beverages on this flight. You’ll thank me for that.”

They stopped at Dutch Harbor to refuel. As they descended, Knox noticed one of the fishing warehouses seemed to have a Helion Dynamics logo. He could only wonder why.

The stop was worse than the flight. The pilot parked on an incline, with the front of the plane being two feet higher than the back. A firehose was brought to the front door, and the rear cargo bay was opened. The cold air hit like a blizzard, turning the interior to fog.

“Everyone, feet up\!” Knox barked.

A few seconds later, seawater came out of the hose, washing the floor and pouring out the cargo ramp. When Knox was satisfied that the water was running clean, the hose was pulled, and the hold hissed shut. The temperature started to rise, as did the humidity and the marine stench.

Knox reappeared with a long pizza peel. “One cup per person. Drink it down, throw the cups forward.” He set the water on the pizza peel, eight-ounce cups, twenty at a time, reaching them out to the candidates. 

When everyone was done, they took off for Dahl’s training camp in the Gobi Desert. The plane only had enough fuel to get there. An hour past the Kuril Islands, Knox spoke up. “Brace yourselves. We are going into a toboggan maneuver.”

Their stomachs felt it as the plane pitched down, losing five hundred feet per minute. The plane sped up as it descended, pacing the KC-130 tanker. Sixty thousand pounds of fuel were transferred in about twenty minutes. They disengaged, and the pilot struggled to level the flight, then began a slow ascent. 

Six hours later, they saw the airport, a hardened, dirt track two miles long. They arrived on April first, during the Karaburan. The winds were over forty miles per hour, and the air was dark with dust. The sun was a pale disk. To the candidates, it looked like heaven. 

The other plane was already in a hangar and had been unloaded. They towed the sardine can into the hangar beside that, and everyone offboarded and scrambled to get their gear. They picked up their duffels, put on their coats, and headed to the barracks, a low, circular building made of rammed earth. 

The training hall had once been a warehouse. Fluorescent tubes buzzed overhead. Rows of folding chairs faced a cargo net.

Day one, Knox called them in for orientation. “I am looking for fifty people to go to Ceres, with another twenty in reserve. A couple of you may become trainers. For the rest of you, thank you for coming out. While you are here, you will become an expert with every piece of gear we will use on Ceres. If you cannot master it, a truck will take you back to town, and Helion Dynamics will fly you back to San Diego. Four of your classmates did not enjoy the flight out here and have already departed.”

He made a slow circuit while the trainees waited. Each had a survey laser in front of them, a foot long and an inch and a half in diameter, oversized for gloved use. Beside it sat a rock, a dummy mass spectrometer, and stainless steel cams they’d use to stay anchored.

When he had finished the circuit, Knox stood on a half-buried 55-gallon drum. He spoke calmly. “The rocks in front of you are hematite iron ore,” he said. “Dense, but the rocks on the Asteroid Belt will be even denser.”

“The extractor ship lands first on three legs. It drills in slowly. Anchors screw in, locking it to the asteroid. That is your home. You are always connected to the extractor.”

He gestured to the gear on the tables.

“Once the extractor’s anchored and your tethers are clipped, the hatch opens. Gravity’s point-zero-two. You’re not walking; you’re crawling. Every motion’s a maneuver. Every ten meters, you hit the rock with the survey laser, open a crack, and jam in a cam. Ours are stainless steel for durability. The extra weight won’t matter at the G forces you’ll face.”

He stepped down from the drum and moved between the rows.

You don’t trust your boots. You don’t trust handholds. You trust the line. If it’s not clipped, you don’t move.”

He paused.

“You fall off, gravity won’t bring you back. You’ll reel yourself in slowly. Stay calm. Pull gently. If you come off the tether, the extractor’s bolted in. It’s not coming to get you. Maybe another miner can, but don’t bet on it. Their hold is as tenuous as yours.”

A few chuckles. Knox didn’t smile.

Off to the side, Dr. Feld watched the projection. Then he looked down at the rocks.

“Go ahead,” Knox said, waving to the trainees. “Use the survey laser on your rock. Break off a chip.” He demonstrated, steady and simple.

The class leaned in. Gloves on. Fingers careful. A soft snap-crack echoed through the hall as laser pulses sheared off thumb-sized fragments from the training rocks.

“Drop the chip in the spectrometer,” Knox continued. “Lid closed. Ready.”

There were a few fumbles. One trainee dropped his sample. Another held the laser backward. Knox ignored it.

Feld stepped up. “A mass spectroscope requires hard vacuum. Out there, you will have it. That’s why these little bottle-sized devices work. Simple time-of-flight measurements. When you put your sample on the sample plate, hit it with the laser. Full strength, but millisecond duration. You will vaporize a point. The spectrometer reads the cloud and tells us what minerals it sees. The mass specs you have here are dummies, since we have no vacuum.”

Knox was walking between rows, observing.

Feld continued. “You’ll get a green or red ping. Green means it read. The ship relays that data to Ceres. Someone there will make the call or send the data to Earth. “Sometimes it’ll take longer. Especially early on. Engineering will model the sample against the recovery cost. That’s when we decide whether to move the rock. As we gain familiarity, both on Ceres and on Earth, the response times will improve.”

Silence. The class looked down at their equipment. The task was simple.

Feld continued. “Once the ore gets back to Ceres, the samples go through another round of tests. The planetside mass spectrometer is much more informative. We also run other assays, chemical, density, hardness, magnetic, the works.”

Feld folded his arms.

Knox let them fumble for the first ten minutes, answering questions, demonstrating technique. But then he started tapping them on the shoulder, directing them to go talk to Catherine. At the end of the exercise, there were eight fewer candidates.

Feld had worked out the doctrine of asteroid mining. Lunar mining taught them how to deal with low gravity and fine regolith, but asteroids were different beasts, tumbling, porous, occasionally hollow. Tools had to change. Cutters needed adaptive bracing systems. Drills required multi-point stabilization. Every rig needed to lock itself to rock or drift uselessly into space. Explosives were out. Precision pulsed cutters and hydraulic crackers were in. The crew practiced with each machine. “99% of this is simple, routine operations,” he stressed. “But if you can’t do the other 1%, you won’t last.”

“When you’re working the dig, you’ll load ore into these,” he pointed at a ten-ton polymer bag. “Cinch it closed. Haul it back to the extractor ship. On Earth, it’s too heavy to move, but on an asteroid, it will be light. At the ship, start loading up one of the tumblers,” he gestured to truncated steel cones that looked like paper cups scaled up to handle hundreds of tons. “Two tumblers screw together at the wide end, containing the load. Then the extractor’s mass driver launches the pair back to Ceres. You’re not firing it at Ceres, you’re giving it a shove. From there it coasts, maybe for weeks.” The projector behind him showed a simulation of the tumbler pair arcing away from an extractor in slow motion, curling toward a pale gray speck labeled CERES. 

A hand went up. Young guy. Tattoo on his neck. “What if it misses?”

Knox didn’t blink. “They will always miss, but not by much. Ceres will send up tenders to make the catch. But if you are mining, don’t worry. The extractor will do all the calculations and launch the load itself. You just fill bags and pack them in the tumblers.”

Dahl watched from the side, clipboard in hand. She watched how Knox was doing the presentation. He had a way of making bad news sound like instructions. Every night, she got the staff together to go over the day, making sure that the next time, when Knox was gone, they would be able to train new recruits.

The candidates drilled hard nonstop. They got in and out of mock-up extractor ships in suits. They trained with survey lasers, drills, cutters, loaders, and everything they’d use in the Belt. Every move was deliberate. Just like it would be in space.

Two months in, the program switched up. Knox brought them to a mockup of the Ceres receiving station. There, they found tumbler pairs filled with ore in a designated impact zone, transport sleds, and ore processing, smelting, and casting equipment.

“Ceres has low gravity. That helps. Tumblers land softly. We haul them to the plant for comminution. First pass is a jaw crusher, then a burr grinder. It turns rock into coarse sand.”

Someone in the back asked, “Why not just skip that and smelt it whole?”

“You burn energy doing that,” Knox said. “Smelt garbage, waste heat. That reduces throughput. Beneficiation saves time and energy. Basic gravity separation with vibration tables. Light stuff floats up: Those are the tails. We keep the heads, the dense fraction. That’s the value.”

“What happens to the light stuff?”

“We use some of it to make the ablative shields for the metal we are sending to Earth. Most of it will go to tailings piles.”

He turned back to the group.

“Today’s smelting practice isn’t a game,” Knox said. “You’ll haul tumblers on sleds, crush ore, sort by density, then smelt. Each step can kill you if you’re careless.”

His tone shifted.

“Molten metal runs at thousands of degrees. One slip, and you’re cooked. Respect the process. No shortcuts.”

The crusher was loud. The team was focused. Two hours in, someone screamed.

A trainee had leaned into the burr grinder to clear a jam. Just a bit of rock caught on the lip. He reached under the guard plate. The machine caught the fabric of his shirt, then the arm, then everything else. It was fast, just seconds. No one could have stopped it.

Knox stood beside the blood-slicked housing. He didn’t look away.

“It’s not fair. It’s not just. Any one of us could have made that mistake. He should’ve gotten a second chance. None of you will,” he said. “Every machine out there is stronger than you, and it doesn’t care. Respect it, or it kills you.”

Feld threw up. He stayed in his room the rest of the day.

Catherine got a few people on her crew to clean up the grinder. The drills resumed an hour later in silence. Seven more men were heading to town.

At the end of the day, Knox lifted a 100-pound casting from the mold.

“Small batch here,” he said. “On Ceres, it’ll be thousand-ton ingots. Mistakes don’t just kill people. They stop production. Remember, production is why we are there. This isn’t safe work. It’s not meant to be. But done right, it’s productive. And that’s the difference between a graveyard and an operation.”

That night, Knox found Feld in the cafeteria. The fish stew was tasteless, which was a blessing. Knox sat across from him. “Bloody day.”

“Yes.”

“Mining is like that. It will be worse on Ceres. What I need to know is if you can train the next crew.”

”Surely you would train them.”

“There is a good chance, according to Catherine, that none of us will survive. But you will survive, and you understand the operation. Can you do the job?”

“I will make sure they are safe.”

“Then they will surely die on Ceres. Make sure they are alert.”

“Of course I will do both.”

“That death today probably saved ten lives on Ceres.”

“He didn’t do anything wrong.”

“No one does. There is a bus leaving tonight. Are you going to be on it?”

Training continued. They were copying Tseng’s system for delivering titanium from the Moon: thousand-ton, bowl-shaped ingots, a retrieval eye on the back. wrapped in an ablative glass mesh. 

Feld brought up a video and explained the last step. 

“Crew uses the crane to load the ingot on the launcher. Aiming, timing, and firing are automated. A small, solar-powered, ionic-thrust rocket will make fine corrections until the package is about half an astronomical unit from Earth. At that point, the guidance package separates, goes around the sun, and heads back to Ceres. The ingot continues to Earth. The flight is about eight months.”

They trained hard with Knox beside them the whole way, demonstrating, guiding, and helping them become a skilled team. After six months in Dahl’s desert camp, the crew was down to a hundred-twenty.

Catherine and Knox talked. “Fantastic job, Silas. Training went really well.”

“Thanks. Are we ready to send these guys to Tycho Crater?”

“I am ready to send the hundred you select.”

Knox didn’t like it. In the morning, a bus brought twenty men back to town, while the hundred loaded back on the sardine can. It was a lot easier with half as many people.

Their trip went from the Gobi to Florida, where they were lifted to low Earth orbit and then ferried to Tycho. 

Darrin met the incoming crew as they disembarked. “Welcome to metallurgy in a vacuum.” They all moved to their huts. Four to a hut, narrow racks against the walls. 

Darrin gave them an overview. “Earth sees this as a titanium operation. That is what we sell them. But we also make iron for Lunar infrastructure, and our most valuable product, oxygen,” he said. “We make enough for breathing, and tons more to power the Lunar Tug ships. They load up with Hydrogen in LEO. Hydrogen is light and relatively cheap to launch from Earth, but oxygen weighs much more. We dramatically reduced the cost of deliveries.”

“On Ceres. You’ll get enough oxygen to survive. Recyclers will split the carbon out of *CO₂*, so you can keep on breathing, but every time an airlock opens, you lose a little. Mistakes will happen. You’ll need to replenish. Ore buys you backup oxygen.”

The crew performed well. Drilling, transport, sorting, smelting, they followed protocol, worked efficiently, and made no excuses. Darrin and Knox were both impressed.

Darrin’s mass driver differed from the Ceres design, but the operation was the same. The crew loaded the mass drivers and waited to watch as the thousand-ton payloads were launched towards Earth, delivering metal on target in the central Pacific.

They trained on the crater walls too, rock climbing in pressure suits, cutting ore from vertical faces. The suits were uncomfortable, but they adapted.

Early in the third session, a trainee cut his leg on a fractured edge just below the knee. The calf depressurized, but no one panicked. He felt the pressure drop instantly, sealed the tear with a wrap of suit tape, and made his way back to operations for repairs. His fast action had minimized the trauma to his calf.

Darrin was watching. “These pressure suits look new. Different seals?”

Knox beamed. “Multiple seals at every joint. A cut glove doesn’t vent the suit. Just the hand. Same for knees, elbows, and shoulders. You lose the segment, not your life. Built for field failure.”

He brushed a hand over the reinforced joint. “Dahl had them designed after talking with lunar crews. Ripped suits have killed a lot of people up here.”

Darrin gave a low whistle. “I want some for my team.”

Two weeks later, they were working a steep section of crater wall. The man ahead of Lennox reached for a handhold. The rock crumbled. His cam popped free.

He dropped. Lunar gravity was weak, so it looked like slow motion, but the fall was real and could be deadly. Lennox moved without thinking. He lunged, hooking the other man with a carabiner to his own suit. They both fell off the face, but Lennox’s cam was secure. Both men slammed against the crater face, but held.

The man gasped into the comm. “Jesus. Thanks.”

Lennox didn’t answer immediately. He was breathing hard, checking the anchor points, making sure everything was secure. Finally: “Check your clips before you weight them.”

That night in Tan’s Cantina, they bought Lennox a drink. Knox watched from across the room and made a note on his slate. Lennox was solid. Quick thinking. Good instincts. He’ll make a good team leader.

After five months, Dahl called from Earth. It was time. Lunar Tug took fifty, including Knox, down to high Earth orbit, where their transport ship was waiting to take them to Ceres. 

Their ride was a General Atomics nuclear thermal rocket, stripped down with no space and no concessions to comfort. Fifty seats packed into a hull that looked more like a shipping container than a spacecraft. The mass budget was tight. 

“You guys remember the sardine can?” Knox asked. “This will be tighter and will last a full year. But no toboggan maneuvers, so relatively pleasant.”

It was still six months before the cargo would land when they started. The nuclear thermal rocket was much faster, so the trip that took the cargo twenty-one months would only take them twelve. They passed the time quietly, reading and playing cards. 

Six months in, the monitors came up. The cargo ship reached orbit around Ceres. With rapt attention, the crew watched as the cargo ship began deploying descent pods down to the rocky south pole.

From the cargo ship, cameras showed the first wave of machines being activated. Their programming unfolded without voice or oversight, executing commands written years ago.

First, the surface was insulated. Self-expanding foam burst from canisters, boiling outward in slow waves. It spread over the South Pole, curing in vacuum to a fragile, velvet skin.

“That’s too fragile. When we step on it, won’t it be crushed?” Lennox asked.

Knox smirked. “Keep watching.”

Next came the hard foam. It was black, dense, and load-bearing. It formed the structural surface: walkable, buildable, and thermally resistant. Low albedo, high emissivity. Ceres had to be shielded from heat. The south pole was rocky, but most of the dwarf planet was not. Too much warmth, and it would erupt with steam, destabilizing everything.

Smelting and forging would bring immense heat. Every watt from the reactors, every ton of molten metal poured in the foundries, would soak downward. The foam had to stop that.

Only once the insulating layers had cured to spec did the rest of the deployment begin. 

The hauler finally landed, its massive spike pointed skywards. It was built for only that one trip. Its nuclear reactor, still humming from the long burn out of Earth, was now repurposed to power the camp. It would power the foundries, the workshops, and life support. 

The barracks modules unspooled themselves, delicate and empty as abandoned eggshells. A thin layer of black foam spread over the building. The crew watched, imagining life in their new home.

“Those look too weak to live in,” Jameson said.

Massive, blocky foundries followed. They landed hard and locked themselves onto the cooling slab. These were set on an extra meter of black surface foam, with coils of ammonia for active heat removal.

Storage barns. Fuel depots. There were five extractor ships, all parked in a row. Water processors, food supplies.

They still had six months before they arrived. That was time enough to get the systems running and confirm stability. The future was coming. It would not pause for tungsten, or for tantalum, or for anyone.

## 

## **Chapter 9: Ceres Operations**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

The flight had been long, crowded, and without reprieve. No one slept well. The air thinned over time in moisture, warmth, and patience. They expected space to be an empty void, but the cramped ship, poor food, and lack of diversion became its own form of pressure. For nearly a year, they floated in the slow, shared erosion of appetite and privacy, caught in a loop of chemical meals and scrubbed breath.

When Ceres finally came into the viewport, no one spoke.

It expanded from a dot to a looming face of dust and fracture. Gray ridges and black cavities etched a surface older than Earth’s oceans. There were no soft curves, just a slow-turning mass, pocked and grooved, where sunlight caught on crater ridges.

The ship descended into low orbit. The station appeared on schedule, a square kilometer of hard foam, angles, and scaffolds. It looked temporary and skeletal, a foothold blown into place by machines.

Most eyes went to the barracks, a dull rectangle lit in amber.

Knox stood at the forward viewport, arms crossed, gaze fixed. The station looked like a thin exoskeleton clinging to rock. But he could already see its future; the rhythms were already in his head: power draw, tailings piles, feedstock flow. He had revised the protocols, rewritten the drills, and practiced until decision became reflex.

“We go in thirty,” he said.

They suited up. Pressure gear sealed. Packs checked. When the hatch opened, ten of them spilled out, crowding onto a rover built for two. They jostled for footing, half-laughing, half-shouting in Local Band. The wheels groaned under the load as they lurched toward the barracks.

From the outside, the module looked intact. The lights were on. The air lock didn’t pressurize and open, though. They cycled through by hand and stepped into cold metal. Emergency lights pulsed, casting the room in a slow, amber rhythm. There was no air. No warmth. No weight.

For a few seconds, no one moved. The silence wasn’t just in the room. It was on the channel too.

Knox scanned the room, jaw tight. He had run this scenario in his head a dozen times. And here it was. Real.

“Start tracing,” he said. The voice was flat, and his order steadied them.

They moved fast. The leak was easy to find. Frost marked a seam on the outer paneling where a misaligned duct junction was improperly fused. They clamped it, sealed the breach with epoxy, and reinforced the joint with a crossbar from the maintenance rack.

Once the repair cured, they restarted the air feed, slowly. Full pressure would take hours. They watched the numbers rise as the module stabilized.

The gauge inched past the safe threshold. The station was breathing again. The crew cheered and started moving into their rooms.

Knox made his rounds through the other modules. Life support: green. Power: steady. Food systems: operational. Water reserves: full.

He checked the water processor status panels.

Three showed green. The fourth had a warning indicator.

He pulled up the diagnostic log. The warning had been active for five months. Processor four: catastrophic failure.

He suited back up and went to inspect it physically.

The housing was intact from the outside, but when he opened the maintenance panel, he saw it immediately. A puncture the width of a pencil was punched clean through the primary heat exchanger. The metal around it was deformed, petaled outward. Micrometeoroid strike. The damage to the tubing and electronics was beyond their ability to fix. The processor was destroyed. Completely unsalvageable on Ceres.

He stood there for a moment, staring at the hole. One piece of rock, smaller than his fingertip, traveling faster than a bullet. It had hit during transit or early setup, and they’d been running on three processors ever since.

He checked the production logs. Three processors had kept the reserves full during automated operations. Three was enough.

He made his way to his office and stopped in the doorway. The L desk. The storage locker. The soft chair. He’d walked this room a thousand times in his head, checking sight lines, testing layouts, and memorizing every detail. Now it was real. The door had his name stenciled on it. Dr. Silas Knox, Captain.

He sat down, pulled off his helmet, and queued a voice packet to Earth. “Hi, Catherine. This is Silas. We arrived. Air leak in habitat unit fixed, habitat pressurized. One water processor is shot. Water reserves are full.”

She wouldn’t receive it for half an hour. He sat for a few moments before going to dinner. He could smell lithium grease in the air. 

The people pulling kitchen duty had found unexpected items in the freezer: cake and ice cream. The cake said, “Welcome to Ceres.” Knox had sent that with the cargo ship years ago. It had come with the supplies and sat frozen for six months in the mess hall freezer. The men cheered when they brought it out. There was plenty for seconds. 

After dinner, Knox stood on a table. “We are here to get metal. The first people to set foot on an asteroid.” He pumped his fist in the air. 

His voice shifted register. “Don’t get carried away with the excitement of it all. Asteroids are more unpredictable than any place you have mined before. Keep your heart rate low.”

Knox paused in silence. “I designed our system. I will lead the first crew. I need three volunteers.”

Three others slowly stepped forward.

Knox logged the names and sent the list to Dahl, his own name at the top.

They prepped in silence. Gear was checked, packs filled, timelines drawn. As the crew suited up, his terminal blinked. A private message had come in from Paris.

Knox read it alone.

*Silas, you’re going to have to be the adult now. That whole first crew may be killed. If they die, your job is to make sure a second crew goes out and does it better. If you go and you’re lost, then everyone on Ceres dies. That’s the math. Stay put.*

He stared at the screen, jaw locked. Verified the timestamp.

Radio delay meant he didn’t need to respond right away. He could think first, or pretend he didn’t get the message until after he left.

Knox put on his suit and the leader armband. He read the nametag. Dr. Silas Knox, Captain. He strode into the mess hall and watched the three volunteers. They were calmly waiting for his direction.

Then he looked at the rest of the crew. Forty-six other men. The cleanup crew was hauling the plates back after dinner. Their faces looked the same. 

If he died, what would happen to them? He looked around the room.

He removed the leader armband and returned his attention to the crew. “I need a fourth,” he said.

Lennox stepped forward. Knox gave him the armband. 

Knox pulled up the regional map. A target was marked in red on the display. 

Knox explained the plan. “Feld believes this rock has tungsten ore. We are going to find out. You will be on that rock for one month. You will be bringing a thousand bags and a hundred cups, stacked just like a pack of Dixie cups on top of the extractor. Fill the bags, tie them off, connect them to the tether, and let the extractor pull them back to itself. The bags hold ten tons. Gravity is weak, so they feel light. You can lift them. Don’t be fooled. They retain their momentum, so the key is to let it move slowly. The cups hold ten bags each, twenty when you screw them together. You load that tumbler pack onto the extractor’s mass driver.” 

“How are we going to lift two hundred tons onto the top of the extractor?”

“Gravity is weak. The tumbler will weigh about fifteen pounds. Get Jameson to help if it is too much.” Joe looked at Jameson.

A soft chuckle went through the room.

Knox continued. “Right now, our target is approaching. It will reach periapsis in a month. At that time, you come home. Get the job done. That asteroid will drift farther and farther away, so we won’t be able to access it again for decades.’

The first crew boarded. The target was loaded into the extractor’s autopilot, and the ship calculated its own approach. They came in slowly and smoothly. No burns inside two kilometers. Hall effect thrusters hissed in short bursts, guiding them toward the sunlit ridge. The surface emerged slowly, revealing a rugged texture of silicate rock interspersed with metallic veins. The asteroid bore the scars of countless impacts. Dusty hues of gray and brown mingled, highlighting the mineral richness in its ancient crust.

They coasted forward until a flat segment emerged, broad enough for gear deployment. Landing gear touched with a whisper. They were on the asteroid.

The crew stayed strapped in, eyes on their readouts. With sub-milligee gravity, even a shift in posture could break contact. The extractor balanced on three legs, each tipped with a fast-spinning, low-bite drill. The bits skated, then caught. The frame hummed as the drills bored downward, slow and steady. No weight to help them, only friction and time. Once the holes were cut, the anchors extended and began threading into place. One turn at a time, the ship moored itself. When the procedure was complete, a green light came on. They were fixed to the rock.

The sigh of relief was audible as they unstrapped. They checked their maps again, running overlays, marking terrain features, and blind zones. Then they clipped in, one by one, and, hearts racing, fanned out over the surface.

Moving slowly enough was difficult. A few bounced off the surface, rising into long, slow arcs. Tethers snapped taut. They reeled themselves back slowly and tried again.

The footing was unreliable, with loose regolith in some places, hard plate in others. They moved carefully, staying low, using gloved hands for balance and traction.

At a ridge near his waypoint, Lennox steadied himself and unholstered his survey laser. A short pulse traced the edge of a visible seam, heating the surface until a fracture opened along an old fault. He waited, tapped it with a glove. The split held.

He reached back, retrieved a cam, and worked it into place. Locked. He tested it, then moved on, slow and low, anchored with each step.

At the next site, he used the laser again to chip a sample, dropping it into the mass spectrometer. Seconds later, data reached the extractor and was relayed to Ceres.

Knox read it: scheelite.

He forwarded it to Earth but didn’t wait for confirmation. He keyed back: “Tungsten ore. Begin harvest.”

Lennox returned to the extractor and retrieved a ten-ton ore bag, drill, expanders, and saw. Moving one handhold at a time, he made his way back, anchored the saw, verified the clamps, and powered it up.

The blade bit deep. He followed, inserting hydraulic expanders, breaking rock, and packing it into the bag. At the end of the first cut, he tied the first bag shut and sent a signal to the extractor to pull it in. Slowly, the bag inched away from him.

He shifted the saw a meter, and started it again. He went back to cutting and inserting expanders. From the corner of his eye, he noticed something drifting. The saw had cut through his tether smoothly. He stood reflexively to grab it, but he stood too fast. He continued up, his boots losing contact with the asteroid.

“I’m drifting,” he said over the radio.

That was when the others looked up.

One miner froze. Another dropped to all fours and began crawling toward the ridge, pulling hand over hand. A third unhooked a tether line and threw it. The shot was wide, but, under tension, the working end moved towards Lennox.

“I’m close,” Lennox said, reaching for the line. It spun inches out of reach.

A second tether fell short.

“Still got eight hours of air,” he said. “Plenty of time to think.”

Silence followed.

The miner who’d been crawling reached Lennox’s cut site. The saw was set firmly, as solid as the extractor. He clipped himself to the saw housing and pulled a tether from his pack. He jumped toward Lennox, paying out line as he went.

As he got close to the end of his own tether, he threw another line to Lennox. The throw was accurate. The working end was going straight towards Lennox. The reaction from the throw sent the rescuer tumbling backward. Suddenly, the rescue line snapped taut. The working end of the tether pulled back when it was about ten feet from Lennox. Close enough to see the clip spinning.

The rescuer reeled himself back in, repositioned, and tried again. This time, the line was thirty feet short. Lennox continued his slow drift, now over a hundred meters out.

Eventually, the crew turned back.

Bags were filled, sealed, and labeled. One by one, they were brought to the extractor’s rail. The extractor calculated trajectories and launched each in a low delta-v path toward Ceres.

Four hours later, drifting cold and clear, Lennox understood the arc wouldn’t return him.

He unlatched the survey laser from his belt.

“The site’s solid,” he said. “Keep harvesting.”

Then he angled the beam and fired once through his own faceplate.

The transmission stopped.

The miner anchored to Lennox’s saw watched the tether drift. “Alright. Back to work.”

Back on Ceres, the crew had begun preheating one of the smelters. They were expecting the first tumbler to arrive the next day. The crew moved slowly in thermal suits, checking and rechecking seals and insulation. Every motion was deliberate. Hot mirrors redirected much of the radiated energy back into the furnace, keeping the crew just outside the danger line.

Water use was constant. Cooling circuits, steam traps, and intake purges. Knox tracked it all by hand, logging fluctuations. The system was closed-loop, but not perfect. Recovery was never one hundred percent.

By mid-shift, the reserves weren’t rebounding, despite continuous cycling.

He ran a full diagnostic. Another water processor was nonfunctional. Corrosion had eaten through a weld where the titanium plating had flaked away. The remaining processors would also have a limited lifetime before the corrosive water ate through their welds, too.

He stared at the readouts, checked them again. The station could function with three processors. Two would force rationing and a slower mining cadence. Hydrolox production, drinking water, food systems, and, as they saw now, thermal regulation for smelter operations were all affected.

He drafted a notice to Dahl.

*Second water processor dead. Two functional but strained. Corrosion failure \- titanium plating compromised. Remaining units likely on same timeline. Water more corrosive than anticipated. We’re in trouble. Have not shared this with the team.*

The response came three hours later.

*I saw the telemetry. Keep mining.*

Dahl’s message confirmed Knox’s decision. The station existed to extract metal; nothing else was guaranteed. 

A second crew was sent while the first crew was still working. Another target, another haul. Tantalum this time, mixed with niobium on a stony asteroid.

In Paris, Dahl stared at the water system logs for hours. The text blurred. Her window overlooked the Seine, but sound, color, and distance all felt muted. She thought about Lennox. She put away her slate and left her apartment.

She walked down the street, turning where it was unfamiliar. A few blocks later, after a couple more turns, she found a bar she didn’t recognize. She walked in, looking to see if she recognized anyone there.

She ordered a carafe of the house red, downing her first glass in a single gulp. The second went down more slowly. Her shoulders loosened. She drank again.

Her eyes fixed on an old poster on the wall, with a redheaded woman wearing a white hat, then returned to her wine glass.

“Of course, Ceres is dangerous. They knew. It’s not my fault.” She sipped her fourth.

“Why Lennox?”

The couple next to her moved to a farther table.

“The reserve tank is full. I hate corrosion. The manufacturer promised me it wouldn’t corrode.”

“I can’t send anything. I don’t have a rocket, and the transfer window won’t open for seventeen months. You think I wouldn’t? Why is it corroding?”

The bartender asked her to keep it down. She ordered another carafe.

“They can’t just stop. Do nothing, wait to die? They can keep pulling metal. I’m not a monster. I am giving them purpose. I’d send them the Seine.”

“They’re all going to die up there. I just don’t know how fast.”

Another patron moved closer to her. “Is it water?” he asked.

She turned. He was sitting at the bar, finishing a beer. She hadn’t noticed him before.

“That’s part of it,” she said. “It’s all of it.”

“What would it be worth, if I could get you water?”

She blinked. Laughed, briefly. “What, like magic?”

“Let’s just imagine it’s possible,” he said. “What would the number be?”

She studied his face. Calm, curious. She pulled a napkin from the bar and wrote on it. 

*One hundred tons. Pure. FOB destination: Ceres. Weekly.*

She paused, then added the numbers and signed it.

*Two hundred dollars per kilogram. Paid on arrival.*  
*Catherine Dahl, Helion Dynamics.*

He put the napkin in his pocket, and the two of them drank and talked through the night.

## 

## 

## **Chapter 10: Magazine Beach**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

Catherine woke to the ringing of her phone and a splitting headache.

“Hello?”

“Hello. That contract you signed last night, do you still mean it?”

She blinked at the ceiling, trying to place the voice. “What? Who is this?”

“My name is Jakob Raskin. We met last night. You said you would pay two hundred dollars per kilogram for water delivered to Ceres. You asked for one hundred tons weekly.”

She remembered the stranger she met last night. She even remembered the contract. “Sure,” she replied. “There’s no way to do it, but if you could do it, I’d be more than willing to pay that much.”

“You may be right, Catherine, maybe there is no way. But I can tell you this: I will do everything I can to get you that water.”

“If you make any progress on that, let me know, and I will get an official contract from Helion Dynamics, signed by Tobias Raines.”

After the call, Jakob opened his contacts. He didn’t need many. Just the right ones.

That evening, he boarded a flight to Boston. The next afternoon, they all met in Magazine Beach Park in Cambridge. They sat in the shade at a picnic table, the Charles River flowing slowly by.

Mira Davis was the youngest, eighteen, still in her freshman year at MIT. She’d shown up in work boots and a tank top with grease under her fingernails.

Tom Wilson had come straight from his shift at Eversource, still wearing the utility vest with the company logo. He had his slate out, already working on something.

Ellie Thompson sat with her arms crossed, radiating impatience. Her degree was in Ocean Engineering. She had worked as an undersea medic and moved into habitat design. Sharp edges, the kind of person who’d seen too much to be impressed by mystery.

Pachakuti Quispe was the only one who looked relaxed, leaning back against the table, watching the river.

“Thanks for coming,” Jakob said.

“You didn’t say what this was about,” Ellie said. “Just that it was a huge opportunity.”

Jakob pulled a napkin from his jacket and spread it on the table. Wine-stained, wrinkled, covered in hasty handwriting.

*One hundred tons. Pure. FOB destination: Ceres. Weekly.*  
*Two hundred dollars per kilogram. Paid on arrival.*  
*Catherine Dahl, Helion Dynamics.*

No one spoke for a moment.

“That’s…” Mira leaned forward, squinting. “That’s twenty million dollars. Per week.”

“It’s real,” Jakob said. “I met her in Paris two nights ago. She’s desperate. Ceres is dying. Two of their water processors failed. They need water, or everyone up there dies.”

Tom looked up from his slate. “Where do you get water?”

“Mars,” Jakob said.

Ellie laughed. Short, sharp. “You’re joking.”

“Mars has ice,” Jakob said. “Polar caps, subsurface deposits. We mine it, purify it, and launch it to Ceres. They pay on delivery.”

“We,” Pacha said quietly. “Meaning us.”

“Meaning the five of us here at Magazine Beach. Whoever’s willing to go, anyway.”

Silence. The Charles River flowed past, indifferent.

“No one’s on Mars,” Ellie injected.

“Not yet.”

“There’s no infrastructure. No base. No support.”

“Can we build it?” Jakob asked.

Ellie burst out, “This is insane\! There is no way we can do this.”

“It’s twenty million dollars a week,” Mira said softly.

Tom was staring at his slate, fingers moving across it. He’d gone quiet.

“Even if we could survive,” Pacha said, “how do we get the water there? Mars to Ceres isn’t simple.”

“No,” Jakob conceded. “It’s anything but simple. If it were simple, they wouldn’t be offering eighty million dollars a month. I don’t have answers. I think, though, that you four might. Not right away, but if you think about it, discuss it, I think you can work something out.”

“We’d need some equipment,” Tom stated. “We would need a mass driver to launch the ice blocks to Ceres. Similar to the system the Moon is using to deliver titanium to Earth, but much stronger. It will take time to spec that out. Getting out of the Martian gravity well all the way up to Ceres won’t be easy.”

“What will we eat?” Pacha asked. 

“I was hoping you would tell me. Do you think we will starve?” Jakob asked.

Pacha hesitated. “Amaranth, oca, cavies. High altitude crops, low pressure tolerance. It’s possible. But I’d need a closed system. Water recovery, waste recycling, full nutrient loop.”

“We’d have sewage,” Mira said.

“That’s the point,” Pacha said. “Everything recycles. No waste. Nothing is replaceable. Everything has to be reused indefinitely.”

Ellie’s mind was reeling with the dangers. “If the habitat breaches, we die; if the power fails, we die; if the crops fail, we die. This is a long list of easy ways to die.”

“What would you need?” Jakob asked. “For the habitat.”

Ellie exhaled slowly. “3D printer. Regolith processor. Plenty of polymer. Get life support components from lunar suppliers. Those are proven. Air recyclers, heaters, air locks. It’s doable. I’ve done pieces of it before.”

“Power?” Jakob asked.

Tom was quiet, still looking at his slate.

“Tom?” Jakob said.

“Mars gets about forty percent of Earth’s sunlight,” Tom said distantly. “We’d need a lot of area. And dust is a major problem. Wiring, batteries, redundancy.” He paused, looking at his slate. “Kicker is, launch window opens in six weeks.”

Everyone turned to look at him.

“What?” Mira said.

“Earth to Mars transfer window,” Tom said. He looked up, and his expression was strange. Not excited. Not afraid. Just… certain. He turned his slate to show them the chart. “Favorable alignment. If we don’t launch in six weeks, the next good window isn’t for two years.”

The weight of that settled over the table.

“Six weeks,” Ellie said. “To build a Mars base.”

Pacha was watching the Charles. “If we go, we’re not coming back. Not for years. Maybe never.”

“I know,” Jakob said.

Mira leaned forward. “How much do we get? If we do this.”

Jakob smiled slightly. “That depends on how long we survive. But if we run for six months, that is twenty-six deliveries.” He let them do the math.

“A hundred million dollars,” Mira said quietly. “Each.”

No one spoke.

Tom was still staring at his slate, but his hands had stopped moving.

Pacha broke the silence. “What do you need from us?”

“A plan,” Jakob said. “Enough that I can pitch an investor. Can we actually do this? Can we survive? Can we eat? Can we save Ceres? I need a loose plan by tomorrow morning. “

Ellie was skeptical. “We’d need to test everything,” she said. “Habitat, life support, food production. We can’t just guess.”

“We have six weeks,” Jakob said.

“That’s not enough time.”

“No, but it’s the time we have.”

Ellie looked at the others. Mira was leaning forward, eyes bright. Tom was calculating something, lost in numbers. Pacha was unreadable.

“I need to know you’re serious,” Jakob said. “If I get the money, if I get the launch, are you in?”

Ellie pressed Jakob. “How much money are you asking for?”

“Money for equipment. I am hoping you guys can figure that part out. Plus the launch.”

“How much is the launch?”

“Half a billion. Maybe more.”

Ellie whistled. “Who’s paying that?”

“I’m working on it. I have a lead.”

“You don’t have the money yet.”

“Not yet.”

She stared at him. Then at the napkin. Then back at him.

“If you get it,” she said slowly, “I’ll go.”

Mira nodded immediately. “I’m in.”

Pacha studied Jakob for a long moment. “You’ll need a better plan than this.”

“I know.”

“But yes. If you can make it real, I’ll go.”

They all looked at Tom. He was still staring at his slate, silent.

“Tom?” Jakob said.

Tom looked up. His face was calm, but his hands were shaking slightly.

“Six weeks,” he said. “We have six weeks.”

“Is that a yes?”

Tom closed his slate. “Get the money. I’ll make the plan work.”

Jakob looked around the table at this group. Four people who barely knew each other. Four people who were about to save Ceres.

“Alright,” he said. “Let’s figure out what we’re actually building. I need to leave this in your hands. I have a flight to catch.”

The group continued their discussions without Jakob. He headed back to Logan International to catch a flight to Lagos, Nigeria. He had an appointment with a man he had only ever heard of, never met. A man who might listen.

Max Juno woke with gravity in his bones and smog in his lungs. It was his seventh month back on Earth. It still felt wrong.

The Moon had been sterile, hostile, and dangerous. He missed it every day.

He’d been a *³He* miner who got a polar claim in perpetual darkness. He had set up his solar panels on a ridge, giving him nearly perpetual light. It was incredibly productive. After two years of mining, he leased out the claim, returned to Earth, and continued to collect royalties. Now he paced through his penthouse suite in Lagos, irritated by the weight of the air.

The meeting was at a half-finished skyscraper downtown. He could smell wet concrete when he got out of the cab. He didn’t need a palace. Just a clean room and a screen. This room would be adequate, and the carpet was plush. 

Jakob was already waiting when Max entered.

“You’re the lunar exit, right?” Jakob asked.

“And you’re the Mars guy,” Max said. “Which is funny, because there’s no one on Mars.”

“Yet.” Jakob gestured, and a screen flickered on. No graphs, no pitch deck. Just a satellite photo: a pale streak of ice, buried in red Martian dust.

“Ice?” Max asked.

“Mars has plenty. Poles, craters, and pockets under the regolith. The volatiles are clean. All I need is a shovel and a mass driver.”

Max narrowed his eyes. “You plan to sell it?”

“Two of the Ceres water processors failed. They’re begging Earth to fill the gap, but Earth can’t help. Launch window’s years out, and even then, it’s a long ride. They’ll be dry before anything gets close. I can send water for a fraction of the cost. And Mars has a launch window early next month.” Jakob pulled a folded napkin from his jacket. “This is the offer.”

Max skimmed it. The money was incredible, but so are the expenses in space. He looked at the signature, scrawled crudely at the bottom.

“Is that the Catherine Dahl from Helion Dynamics?”

“The same. I met her in a bar in Paris.”

Max rocked back. He started to believe the opportunity was real. He also doubted this scrawl would count as a legitimate contract when it came time to collect. 

“Obviously, I will need to talk to her about this.”

“Of course. Of course you will. I have her number; you can call her right now.”

“For now, I will listen. If you have something to say, maybe I will call her.”

Jakob was nervous. “I have a crew from MIT. I met them all at school and alumni events. Our plan is to go to Mars, set up a stable living environment, and send large blocks of ice by mass driver to Ceres.”

“How are you going to hit Ceres at that distance?”

“No way. That isn’t our goal. Ceres has a small gravity well, and there are lots of other asteroids to mess with it. But they are already collecting ore. They have tenders, small ships that work in and around Ceres, catching payloads and guiding them in. We just have to get within tender range.”

Max had lived on the Moon. He knew how hostile Mars would be without Lunar Tug to bring them supplies. ”What about food?”

“We’ll grow it. Algae’s simple. But one member of our team is planning to grow high-elevation crops, since they can grow at lower pressures. He has a plan to make regolith into dirt.”

“You’re serious.”

“I just have to feed five people. Nothing fancy.”

Max studied him. Gaunt. Sharp. A little unhinged. Definitely broke.

“What’s your name?”

“Jakob Raskin.”

“What do you want?”

“Maybe thirty million for equipment. Plus a ride to Mars. With that, I pay the team and build what we need. You arrange the ride. In return, you get forty percent of the company. First Martian exporter. First to deliver offworld water.”

Max looked at the photo again. Ice, red dust.

“And if it doesn’t work?”

Jakob shrugged. “Then we die on Mars.”

Max frowned. “You say that like it’s obvious.”

“It is,” Jakob said. “This isn’t the Moon. Resupply is nearly impossible, and there’s no lift capacity for a return stage. No launch window for two years, even if you could afford to send a rescue ship. We go, we live off what we brought and what we make. If that fails, we die. The crew knows that, and they are working right now to ensure we don’t die.”

Max sat back. He knew the math. The Moon had always offered at least the illusion of rescue. Mars didn’t. If they failed, they wouldn’t be coming back. 

Jakob watched him. “We have to make it work.”

Silence. The kind of silence you only get when someone actually means it.

Max thought, remembering his days on the Moon, knowing even if he did everything right, a stray micrometeoroid could end him at any time. Risky, but exhilarating. This project filled him with the same exhilaration. So many ways to fail, but there was a way to succeed, too.

“Jakob, let’s see what we can do. I’ll fund your shopping spree. You guys get started now. I have some other phone calls to make, starting with Catherine Dahl. We will see about getting you your ride. Maybe Lunar Tug has something. In the meantime, what can I call your company?”

Jakob looked at Max, caught completely off guard. He had no answer. Then it came to him. “Magazine Beach.”

## 

## **Chapter 11: Martian Ice**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

Five weeks after his meeting with Max Juno, Jakob stood alone in the cargo bay as the last of his team trickled in.

The ship was built for function, not comfort. The five of them were packed in with their equipment and a few cavies in cages that reeked of hay and ammonia. The seed stock and tubers were sealed in insulated crates. Three air recyclers stood lined up against the wall. One was all they needed, but if anything went wrong, help was years away.

They gathered in the main bay, where Jakob had been staging equipment for a week. The warehouse still felt temporary. Crates lined the walls. Cables coiled like snakes. The air smelled of steel, plastic wrap, and lubricant.

Jakob let them settle. No introductions. No speeches. Just the team and the tools.

“We have one shot at this. Don’t screw it up.”

His voice was neutral, tired, stripped of anything unnecessary.

“Max has paid for this cargo hull and all our equipment. He bought a rocket from Lunar Tug that they were not using. Helion Dynamics graciously fronted us the money to get this all into LEO, where we will connect to that rocket and head to Mars.”

“This isn’t a dry run. There’s no backup. Mars doesn’t care if we make it. If something breaks, Max might be able to send a replacement, but launch windows only open every twenty-six months.”

He gave them time, then continued. “If you don’t think we can do this, you shouldn’t be here. If you do, then you already know what’s required: working without a net. Each of us is doing more than we were trained for. Living with whatever comes. But if we pull it off, if we send clean blocks of ice, hit delivery windows, and hold the contract, then we become the first Martian exporters. More than that, we have a chance to build something real up there. And maybe, down the line, find a way back. On our terms.”

He looked at each of them in turn. “Got it?”

They nodded, one by one.

Mira cracked a grin. “You won’t have to worry about me.”

It broke the tension just enough. Jakob didn’t return the smile, but he moved forward.

There was no time to waste. Each person checked their own manifest, then verified someone else’s. No second chances.

They rode a heavy-lift to low Earth orbit in their cargo hull. Mira loved the heavy acceleration, then weightlessness. Tom worked through the transfer sequence, moving them in their cargo hull from the heavy lift rocket to a rocket Lunar Tug no longer wanted. This one would take them to Mars. 

Mira was excited. “I love the feeling of acceleration. I can’t wait for our burn to Mars. It just totally crushed me down into the gravity couch last time\!”

Jakob humored her, counting down to launch. “Five, four, three, two, one, go.”

Tom threw the switch, and the hydrolox rockets lit with a roar. The gentle push barely registered. The look of disappointment on Mira’s face brought laughter from the rest.

“Not what you expected, Mira?” Jakob asked. “This is a shuttle rocket, not heavy lift. It will burn for an hour, but at 1/10th G, enough to insert us into a Hohmann transfer orbit to Mars. After this, it will be six months of true weightlessness.”

Mira pouted.

The time was spent in tight, unchanging air. The ship was the size of a bus, but none of the space belonged to them. Every surface was spoken for: cable runs, storage racks, sealed containers, and equipment. Every tool, part, and system they would need was packed inside with them. There was no separate cargo, no second flight, no margin for error. The entire mission was compressed into this one hull.

They slept belted into stacked bunks and moved through narrow corridors. It was warm and humid, so they dressed in the bare minimum. Most days, just underwear.

There were no showers. Just sealed hand wipes.

The first time Ellie had to clean herself, she wedged into the corner by the water recycler, back to everyone, and worked fast. Her shoulders were tight. No one said anything, but everyone looked away. Studied their screens. Checked equipment they’d already checked.

She pulled her shirt back on immediately after, still damp.

“It’s fine,” she muttered to no one. “It’s fine.”

By week three, she stopped retreating to the corner. Just wiped down at her bunk, quick and mechanical. Still wore the underwear. Still kept her back turned.

By month two, the underwear started to smell. Everyone’s did. The fabric held moisture and body oil with nowhere for it to go. The water purifier was not built for laundry. Pacha stopped wearing his first. He seemed completely natural that way. Mira followed a week later.

Ellie held out longest.

Month three, Jakob woke to find her sitting on the edge of her bunk, underwear balled in her fist, staring at nothing.

“There’s no point,” she said quietly.

She tossed it into the waste bag and didn’t put another pair on.

After that, no one wore anything unless they were cold. Hair matted. Skin dried and cracked. The air smelled like polymer, machine grease, and breath. No one mentioned it, but everyone noticed.

Ellie stopped turning away when she cleaned herself. Just did it, same as eating, same as checking the boards.

One morning, Jakob caught her looking out the tiny porthole at Mars, growing slowly brighter in the distance.

“Can’t wait to have a door again,” she said.

Jakob nodded. “Yeah.”

“Dinner time\!” Pacha called out cheerfully.

No one was happy. Food came in pouches, nutrient-balanced and soft. No flavor, no texture. No one wanted to eat. You ate because the others did. They were all losing weight. 

Tom didn’t like that. He and Mira set up a stand to hold a cargo bicycle in place and connected it to some paddles, like an assault bike. Everyone got thirty minutes a day. Jakob hated it, but he knew Tom was right. As their leader, he showed enthusiasm he didn’t feel.

They also used resistance bands. Not the same as real weights, but it slowed how fast they were wasting. Ellie kept their schedules for resistance bands. Push, pull, legs, rest. 

Time passed in repetition with seemingly no progress. Same lights. Same air. Same bodies. Nothing changed. No distance closed. They slept and woke in the same space. Thought the same thoughts. Waited for the gravity to shift.

The only way to be alone was to close your eyes and stop reacting. Then Jakob made an announcement. “We are about to do our slowdown. Watch out the window.”

Mars was big. No longer a red dot, the landscape was clearly visible. And, as they watched, the craft tumbled. A controlled and slow reversal, the craft now pointed away from Mars, and the engines came on. They ramped up slowly, giving the crew the feeling of gravity. Softly, for about ten minutes at a tenth of a G as they performed a Mars orbital insertion. Tom put them in a highly eccentric orbit. They stayed in that orbit, heating at periapsis as they entered the atmosphere, cooling during apoapsis. The heating cycles grew longer each time, and the cooling cycles shorter. 

Then the real entry. The heat shield was ablating as they came in. The whole frame began rumbling. Jakob kept his hand on the parachute deployment lever, while Tom watched the gauges. At Mach 6.0, Tom called out, and Jakob pulled the lever to deploy the high-speed parachute. When it unfurled, it felt like an impact as the craft was suddenly decelerating at two G. Mira didn’t like it this time. The G force decreased as the square of the speed. Tom kept a steady eye on the gauges. At Mach 2.0, the force had decreased to about Martian gravity. Tom called out, and Jakob pulled the second lever. The high-speed chute detached and drifted away, and the much larger medium chute deployed, putting them back to two G. Pacha threw up, but no one could clean anything. When they got to 1G, Tom called out for the final chute. It was enormous, but they had already slowed significantly. They only reached 3G. As they neared the landing site, Tom started the engines. This burn was harder than they had used for the Hohmann transfer orbit burns. The soft jolt of touchdown came like an afterthought, as if the ship had changed its mind at the last second and landed anyway. They sat at an angle, but the legs slowly adjusted until the deck was level. Everyone cheered when it stopped. Through the portholes, they saw a new world. Red, with a rosy sunrise. 75°S, 30°W. Smooth, near the edge of the ice cap.

Pacha began cleaning himself up. Mira came over and lent a hand.

Jakob felt the impact in his ribs before he felt it in his boots. The noise was low and wide as the ship settled. He stood too fast, while the legs were still leveling the ship. He cut his head on the cabin bulkhead and swore. 

“First blood on Mars,” Ellie muttered as she cleaned him up and added a bandage. “Good omen.”

Jakob didn’t smile. He wasn’t here for moments. He was here to keep the mission from falling apart one piece at a time. He called them together. “OK, we have eight months of summer remaining. The ice cap is retreating, so each day we will go a little farther to collect. Be greedy. Pull as much as you can as fast as you can, because it is all going away anyway. We have thirteen days before we launch on the shortest transfer orbit to Ceres, and we have a lot to get done. The clock has already started.”

He posted the basic tasks, taping the list above the airlock.

Solar Panel Deployment  
Dig foundation for Agriculture dome  
Collect Ice in ten-ton blocks.  
Deploy mass driver  
Start growing crops  
Build ag dome  
Make fiberglass ablative boxes to carry the ice to Ceres

Sol 13: launch package 1, one hundred tons of pure ice, shortest transfer time to Ceres.

The solar arrays went up first. Nothing moved without power.

Tom and Pacha were out of the lock within minutes, boots crunching through red dust. The panels were flat and wide, packed awkwardly for transit. One caught halfway through its extension. Tom swore, dropped to one knee, and cleared the rail with a multitool.

“Dude. Dust already?” Pacha laughed as he drove a stake into the regolith a few meters away.

“Fine as talc,” Tom muttered. “Static pulls it into everything.”

“I bet the first storm is going to knock this all down,” Pacha smirked, watching a distant dust devil.

“Low atmospheric pressure means low force from wind,” Tom replied. “But when the first storm hits, I’ll be watching.”

He finished the bolts and moved to the field generator: carbon frame, three-phase, clipped into the central rack. When he flipped the switch, the panels hummed faintly, and a shimmer passed across the glass.

“The alternating electric field pushes dust off,” Tom said. “Indium tin oxide electrodes on the surface. A slow three-phase charge walks the grit downhill.”

Pacha watched the effect, the dust slipping downward in slow motion, as if peeled by invisible hands.

“Reduces the buildup,” Tom said. “We will still need to rinse the panels weekly to remove what this doesn’t.”

Pacha nodded. Then turned back to his line of panels and kept working.

Mira passed them without slowing. She’d pulled out the saw sled and had assembled the disk. Six pie-shaped wedges were screwed together to create an enormous wheel fifteen feet in diameter. She got it all assembled and went straight to the edge of the shelf.

“This ice better be worth it,” she said over the radio.

Jakob followed. The regolith cracked faintly under his boots. It didn’t feel like dirt. More like baked ash. He stopped before joining Mira. He found a shovel and went about a hundred yards off to dig. After ten minutes, he stepped back and took a picture for Max. The words “Magazine Beach” were dug into the regolith.

The shelf wasn’t flat. That was the first problem. The models had shown clean, horizontal strata. What they found was a tilted slab with wind-carved seams and dust-packed hollows.

Mira stood at the edge, eyeing the slope. “The grade is too steep,” she said. I can’t cut straight on that.”

Jakob reached for one of the adzes. “I’ll fix that.”

The adzes were carbon steel heads on collapsible shafts. Mira joined Jakob, knocking down the surface in ragged swings. Tom joined in. The crust came off unevenly in slabs, shards, and soft sections that crumbled like stale bread. Red dust had seeped into everything. Some layers were dark, almost black from meteorite soot. It wasn’t easy work or fast, but the surface had to be flat before the saw could track true. 

Inside, Ellie was running diagnostics. The water recycler was green. Air mix: oxygen levels were good. *CO₂* was low enough, hovering near 1600 ppm. The organics were too high, though. She tapped at the console like it had insulted her, lips tight, not speaking. Numbers came in slowly, sometimes delayed by dust interference. She looked at the catalytic cleaner, a U-shaped pipe packed with nickel dust. Countercurrent flow minimized heat loss. Normally run at 300°C, she set it at 500°C and turned up the fan. “This whole vessel needs to be scrubbed with bleach,” she thought.

Meanwhile, Pacha moved gear off the ship. Cargo that could be outside was stacked and covered with tarps staked against the wind. With space cleared in the aft bay, he began assembling the grow tent. Ellie watched as he snapped the framework together, section by section. She said nothing, but her expression softened.

Jakob keyed the team channel. “Tom, Pacha, thank you for getting the arrays live. This is summer here, so the sun won’t set for long, and twilight will persist much longer than we are used to. Too easy to work past the edge of exhaustion. From here on, we follow a schedule. Dinner, chat, then sleep. Your body won’t tell you when it’s tired, so we’ll use the clock instead. He let that settle, then continued. “Two weeks. We cut, refreeze, package, and launch ice for Ceres. If we miss it, we don’t get paid. And Ceres runs dry. This isn’t theoretical, it’s existential.”

The team line stayed quiet. They already knew the plan, but now in the light, they understood it.

Pacha called them to dinner. “Hey, guys, are you getting hungry? We are working now, so we need more calories. Eat up\!” 

There was some grumbling about the continued lack of meat. After a few minutes, Pacha spoke up, “We have limited feed pellets, so we won’t breed the cavies until their feed is growing. And not just algae.” He gestured toward the tent in the bay. “It is germinating now. Once we have an agriculture dome, I will plant. Give me twenty-five sols, and I’ll get us roots and leaves. Then we breed one sow. No more. Crops come in, we expand. Not before.”

Tom grumbled, but everyone understood. Rations would hold. 

The sled creaked its way toward base, trailing a fine mist of powdered frost in its wake. Mira stood behind it, boots planted wide for stability, pushing hard. The traction assist wheels pulled, sometimes slipping. On top, the sled held a triangular prism of ice, seven feet high, twelve feet long, a mass of ten tons, faceted and glinting faintly in the sunlight. It looked like an enormous gemstone from a fairy tale.

She parked the sled with practiced slowness, locking the wheels into place beside the melt chamber, which Pacha had already hauled out. It was a pressure-sealed tank with water filters. Jakob stepped out to meet her, brushing ice dust off his gloves.

“Not bad for a first haul,” he said, voice crackling over the suit comms. “Smooth cuts.”

Mira smiled. “There was a seam full of rust-dust. I had to trim it on the fly with the adze.”

They both stared at the block for a moment. Jakob keyed the panel, and the chamber hissed open. Using the sled’s crane, they lifted the block up and into the chamber.

Inside, air pumps brought the pressure up to 1 bar, coils of heating wire glowed faintly red. The block started warming up from about a hundred degrees below zero up to where it could start melting. That operation was slow. As the water came off, it was filtered and poured into the massive freeze chamber, making an enormous lemon shape that would weigh a hundred tons when complete.

At night, their beds were uncomfortable. Gravity felt weird now. The ship creaked in ways no one liked, and the silence outside pressed in like a weight. With the equipment unloaded, they had a lot more space, though, which helped. 

Jakob stayed up late. Max had pinged him from Lagos just before comms cutoff:

“Nice picture. I put it on my wall. Ice ships in twelve sols or you lose the money.”

No pressure. Outside, the ice shelf waited. Tomorrow, they would cut nine more blocks. 

The second sol on Mars started with shovels. First, the foundation needed to be dug. They moved the top meter of regolith without encountering any large rocks. Next, they sorted out the finest dust. They filled the hopper of the 3D printer with that powder and set it to make foam. They laid out six inches of foam, about half fine powder and half polymer. The reddish-orange color looked beautiful to Ellie. 

They let it cool for about an hour, then changed the settings. Now they were making the solid walls. Ellie jammed her shovel blade into the regolith, scooping up load after load, and dumped it into the intake hopper of their 3D printer. The dust didn’t puff like it would on Earth. It fell sluggishly, clinging to itself in dry clumps, static-charged and oddly heavy for something so fine. The printer’s auger rumbled beneath the housing, pulling the dirt toward the heating chamber.

Pacha stood at the controls, eyes on the temperature readouts. The polymer reservoir stayed just above the melt point, viscous and glassy, ready to bond with the crushed stone and dust. It made the regolith printable. Stronger than concrete once it cured, or so the specs claimed.

No dramatic hum of motors. No hiss of steam or whine of servos. Just the low mechanical growl of the extruder and the broad scrape of oversized shovels against broken stone.

The print pattern was simple: a low hemisphere, six meters wide, cut cleanly on one side for an airlock. Shelter from radiation and micrometeoroids. No windows. A transfer box with a pipe for air, water, and sewage. This was the agriculture dome, but they would be living in it for a while. After this came the equipment storage barn. Eventually, the habitat.

Ellie paused, leaning on her shovel with its wide blade waist-high, more like a snow scoop than a spade. In this gravity, it worked. The regolith was loose, fractured, and dry. She watched the curve rise slowly with each pass of the nozzle. It looked layered, almost clumsy, but she could already picture it finished. Not a home, exactly. But the first place on this planet built by hand and meant for life.

“Load’s holding,” Pacha said in his headset, voice clipped and dry. “Poly’s stable. We keep pace, we finish the shell by bedtime.”

Ellie nodded, though Pacha couldn’t see it. She just kept shoveling.

It was hard work. No gravity to help the shovel bite. Just pressure, leverage, and grit. But neither of them stopped. There was nowhere else to be, and nothing more important to do.

The sun hung high. No shadows moved. No breeze stirred the dust. Just the dome, growing layer by layer, like a promise they meant to keep.

By midmorning, they’d settled into a rhythm, trading roles when the one on the shovel was too tired to continue.

Pacha took the shovel, breathing slow and steady inside his suit as he fed the hopper with one scoop after another. Ellie stood at the printer’s interface, fingers flicking through the displays, keeping the temperature in the right band, watching the extrusion for skips or warps. The dome was waist-high now, a rough curve taking shape against the orange dirt.

From the east, a figure approached, slow and broad-shouldered in his suit. Tom. He dragged a sled behind him with a rectangular shape strapped tight beneath a dust blanket.

“Hey,” his voice came through their headsets, distorted a little by the local mesh relay. “Mass driver’s online. She hums like a dream. Got time for a door?”

Ellie glanced over and nodded. “We’re at the cutout. You’re just in time.”

Pacha stabbed his shovel into the ground and leaned on it, panting. “How many tons to Ceres?”

“None yet. But she’s aligned, powered, and ready. I’m gonna do a test launch tomorrow. Could hit a boulder on Deimos from here.”

Ellie raised an eyebrow. “Please don’t.”

Tom just chuckled and stepped around the printer. He pulled back the blanket and revealed the airlock, composite, pre-fabbed, pressure-ready. Not big. Just wide enough to crawl through in a suit. Airtight seals, no windows. Practical. Ugly but adequate.

He propped it next to the rising wall. “You want me to anchor it now, or wait?”

Ellie waved him over. “Do it before we print too high. We’ll match the layers around it.”

Tom got to work without another word, pulling out anchor rods and the little bolt gun they’d brought for this kind of thing. Ellie watched for a minute, then turned back to the readouts.

Polymer holding at 145°C. Flow rate steady. No signs of clog. She gave Pacha a nod. “Back at it.”

He groaned, picked up the shovel again, and dug.

“Your hole,” Tom said to Ellie, with a flourish after he finished bolting it in place. “I’m going to grab lunch and yell at Jakob about calibrating the mass driver scope. You two shovel heroes can finish the shell.”

Ellie checked the interface again. Still green. “We’ve got three more layers to seal the door. Then we start capping.”

Pacha kept shoveling. “Better get moving, then. Before Tom eats all the rations.”

“I didn’t think about that,” Ellie exclaimed and turned the machine to standby. They finished filling the hopper together, then started walking back to the ship.

Five more blocks had been cut by lunch. They were lined up by the processor, going in one at a time. Everyone was in high spirits as they ate. Mira talked about the ice. “That saw is a monster. Huge, and when it cuts, it is like a blizzard, but it cuts smoothly and fast. When we get the hang of it, I bet we can cut two hundred tons a day. I cut fifty so far.”

Tom jumped in. “The mass driver is ready to go. I tossed a rock with it slowly. It flew about ten miles. I will calibrate it tomorrow with a bigger push, but it is at least functional.” 

After lunch, Pacha and Ellie returned to the printer, and Tom joined Jakob and Mira on the ice field. Jakob was starting on block seven. The sled saw ran smoothly. His movements were measured, calm, and smooth. Eyes on the cut line. Depth consistent. No rush. The shelf was cooperating. 

Then the whine started. Jakob hit the kill switch immediately. Too late. The saw shuddered once, then died. A coil of white smoke unspooled from the motor housing.

“Mira,” he said into the comm. “We’ve got a problem.”

She was there fast. No wasted movement. She didn’t ask what happened. Just knelt, glove hovering above the housing. She could feel the heat radiating from it.

“Something’s cooked,” she said.

Jakob bent down. Even through his gloves, the heat was obvious.

“Motor’s burned,” Mira added, flat.
