Jakob didn’t answer. He stood, slowly, swearing low and hard. He yanked the saw blade off and put the saw and cutting sled on a cargo sled. He and Tom started pushing it toward the ship, boots grinding over the ice shelf. Mira got up with the saw and began opening up the motor.

“All hands,” Jakob said over the team channel. His voice cracked. “Emergency. Sled saw motor’s blown. Meet at the ship.”

## 

## **Chapter 12: Ice Saw**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

The line went silent.

Pacha dropped his shovel and ran. The printer was still running when Ellie flipped the switch to standby and sprinted. Dust kicked up behind her boots.

When they arrived, Mira had the casing split, revealing the motor’s internals. The coils were scorched black. Red dust clung to the interior walls, packed into seams and corners where it should never have reached. The insulation had frayed and burned, peeled back like scorched fabric. Mira stood beside the motor, arms crossed, eyes locked on the damage.

Jakob didn’t look up. He pointed at the motor guts. “No backups,” he said. “I brought blades, belts, bearings. Not the one thing that matters.”

Tom stared at the open casing. “You didn’t pack a spare motor?”

Jakob didn’t answer.

“I should have,” Jakob said. He didn’t look up. “I knew better. Two is one, one is none. I say it. I believe it. I missed this one.”

Ellie leaned in, frowning. “We can maybe rewind the motor. Depends on how deep the damage goes.”

Mira stayed quiet. Her jaw was tight.

Pacha finally said what everyone else had been circling. “Can we repair it?”

Jakob didn’t answer right away. He exhaled, long and steady. “Not here. Not with what we’ve got. Not in time. We have eight days to cut and process three more blocks. Then the launch window opens. That’s the contract. That’s the water Ceres needs. If we miss it…” He stopped, not finishing the sentence.

Ellie knelt beside the housing, touched it with one gloved hand. “We don’t have another way to cut blocks?”

“Not the way we need to,” Jakob replied. “The sled gave us depth, speed, and control.”

He looked at each of them. He had no answers.

Mira finally spoke. “So what do we do?”

Jakob looked down at the wrecked saw. Then out the airlock window, where the sky bled into dust.

“We figure it out,” he said. “There is no backup here. We figure it out.”

No one spoke. 

Dinner was hot stew, thick with lentils and dried vegetables. No one said much. Forks scraped quietly. 

Mira sat stiff, back straight, eyes forward. She hadn’t touched her food. Tom pushed things around on his plate with slow, absent motions. Ellie sat, not talking, not eating much either.

Jakob didn’t join them. He sat at the end of the main corridor, near the airlock. Helmet off, still in his dusty suit, elbows on knees, staring at the saw motor on the floor in front of him. The casing was cracked open, the blackened copper glinting under the overhead lights. He hadn’t spoken since dinner started.

They finished processing the last two ice blocks quietly. The filtration system hummed in the background, water melting in the chamber, trickling through screens and mesh filters into storage. Steam rose in thin threads, caught the lights.

When they finished, Ellie stood up. “I’m going to take care of the printer.” She then wordlessly put on her gear. Her boots thudded in the corridor. The printer had to be cleaned before they went to bed, before the polymer inside started to break down. If it did, it was a loss they couldn’t afford.

Back in the corridor, Jakob hadn’t moved.

He sat alone with the motor at his feet, the weight of the entire mission heavy on his shoulders. They had planned for everything. Redundancies, backups, contingencies. Two is one, one is none. That had been the rule. And now here they were. One saw. None left. No launch. No contract. No way forward. Jakob didn’t say it out loud.

The hum of the water processors. The clink of utensils left in bowls. Jakob composed the message with mechanical fingers. No grand speech, no dramatics. Just the facts, numbers, part codes. Three sled saws. Ten motors. Redundant systems for a world that didn’t forgive mistakes.

*To. Max Juno*  
*Subject. Equipment requisition – Priority*

*Three sled saws. Ten compatible drive motors. Full specs attached.*  
*Ship ASAP. No substitutions.*

*\-Jakob*

He stared at the screen for a while after it sent. He didn’t expect a reply. He had no idea what time it was in Lagos. Max would get the message when he got it. But no matter what Max did, no help would come soon enough. Years, if everything aligned perfectly. He needed it in days.

He closed the message. Then stood. There was still a job to do.

Back in Nigeria, Max Juno was already halfway through his second cup of coffee when the message arrived. Max sat for a long moment, staring at his own reflection in the glass. He opened the procurement system, out of habit more than hope. Three sled saws. Ten motors. Full specs. He filled in the form and stepped through the menus.

Estimated delivery: thirty-four months. He sat back. By then, it wouldn’t matter. The cost was hundreds of millions. 

Max stared at the screen, then reached for the cancel key. He didn’t place the order.

There was no point pretending he could help. 

Back on Mars, Ellie dragged a power conduit across the red dirt. It snaked from the ship’s auxiliary port to the half-finished dome. The doorway stood open, rough-edged, unsealed.

She moved deliberately. Not quickly, but without allowing hesitation where despair might settle. 

Pacha joined her at the printer a few minutes later, ready to help with the shutdown. She looked at him oddly.

Then she spoke. “Pacha, I can’t do anything for that motor. I wish I could. But there is something I can do, if you’ll help me. Let’s finish this dome.”

Pacha nodded. They had worked hard all day and deserved rest, but the whole team needed someplace better to live than the ship. 

Ellie watched the controls while Pacha shoveled regolith into the hopper. Then she joined him. Shovel after shovel. The printer extruded each layer in a slow, molten spiral. The curve of the dome was closing now. More overhang. They had to print slower, wait between passes for the material to cool and hold shape. Ellie adjusted the print speed manually.

Four hours in, they had said nothing, but looked at each other, complicit in their transgression.

Pacha pumped his fist when the printer stopped. Ellie didn’t smile, but she looked up at the finished dome and exhaled. Dust already clung to the surface. The polymer looked dulled, aged, like it had weathered years instead of a sol.

She ran the electrical hookup and initiated the systems check. Ellie watched the green light blink on the control panel, steady and soft. The pressure test held. Airflow was stable. The dome was sealed. It would hold. The compressor started pumping in the Martian atmosphere, and the recycler struggled to keep up, converting the *CO₂* into oxygen and soot.

She stood for a moment, one gloved hand resting on the junction box. Then she turned and walked back to the ship.

Inside, she passed Tom and Jakob without a word, ducked into storage, grabbed her sleeping roll, and cycled through the airlock again.

She didn’t say anything. She didn’t need to.

Mira guessed what was going on. She came next, pack slung over one shoulder. Then Pacha, carrying the cavies in a pressure box. “The floor will be warmer than my bunk,” he said.

That was as close to an announcement as there was.

They were going to sleep in the ag dome tonight. It wasn’t fully ready. It had air and heat, though, and space. And they built it. It was theirs.

Tom and Jakob brought their gear last.

Even if it all failed tomorrow, even if they missed the launch window, they would spend this one night in the world they were trying to build. A night in a home they built. A claim on a distant planet staked in sleep.

They put their cots near each other, on the side opposite the cavy pen. As they all prepared to sleep, Jakob took a moment with Ellie. 

“The setting here is much calmer. I still don’t know what we will do tomorrow, but we will sleep better tonight. Thank you.” 

In the morning, the dome was dimly lit. Pacha programmed the lights to start low, build toward midday, then fade again at the end of the sol. The air inside still held a hush, like a cathedral at dawn, the natural reddish hues of the regolith lending a regal feel.

They stirred from their sleep slowly. It had been the best night since launch. The sleeping pads were soft, the heat even, the air dry but warm. There was space to stretch out. No clanging bulkheads, no humming walls a meter from their heads. Just quiet. 

When Mira sat up and rubbed her eyes, she didn’t bother to put a shirt on. She looked around the peaceful dome. She could smell the polymer faintly, but mostly she could smell the soil and the cavies and the people. It was calming.

Pacha was the first one moving with purpose. He checked the cavy pen. They all looked warm, nestled together like furry little coals. He topped up their water and scattered feed, murmuring to them in Spanish like they were his children. If the mission had any hope of surviving long-term, it ran on cavies and calories.

After the animals, he turned to the stove. It was just a hotplate and a pot, but it was enough. Oatcakes, protein mash, dried fruit. He called them to the table, and they all gathered. Some of them had put on light cotton thermals as they got out of bed. No one wore shoes. This was home.

They ate together, quietly at first. A few soft laughs. It felt like something new had started. The motor was still broken, but the world hadn’t ended last night.

When Jakob came by the table, he had shaved. Just a rough pass with the clippers, but it was the first time since Earth. It made him look less ragged. He watched the others as they ate, not with concern, but with clarity.

When the dishes were cleared, he stood. 

“Six blocks,” he said. “Mira?”

She nodded. “Done. All filtered. All refrozen. Sixty tons are ready.”

He looked around the dome. “We lost the saw. We lost time. We can’t afford either. But we’re not finished. Ceres still needs water. That’s why we’re here.”

He met their eyes, one by one. “Here’s the plan.”

He stepped forward into the light. “We are not in danger of dying today. Let’s be clear. We have power. We have heat. We have clean water. The dome holds pressure. The cavies are fed. Pacha’s crops are germinating.”

A pause. “But no one’s coming to get us. Not because they don’t care, but because they can’t. Max said he can get us the parts in three years. The only ship that brings us back to Earth is the one we pay for. We pay with water.”

He gestured to the ceiling above them.

“This dome, it’s better than the ship. It’s warm. It smells like soil. We made it. We can live here.”

Ellie, quietly, pulled on a thin undershirt. Tom put on boxers.

“We’ve got sixty tons of clean water ready to go. If we ship that now, Ceres gets a week’s supply. Longer, if they ration, and they will.”

He exhaled once, steady.

“Last night I thought we were done. But now I’ve got a plan. It’s brutal, maybe stupid. But it will work.”

He let the silence stretch. 

“We brought three adzes. They’re meant for smoothing the surface, not cutting. We’re going to hand-cut the next forty tons. Muscle, breath, repetition. I don’t know if we can cut even a ton at a time, but whatever size we can cut, we stack it up on the sled.”

He looked to Mira. Then Tom. Then Pacha. Then Ellie.

“You swing until your grip gives out. Then you rotate. Fix a filter. Feed the cavies. Make a shipping container for the ice. Then back to the adze. We keep going.”

No one moved. 

“We’ll burn a lot of calories. So we eat more. I’m not rationing. If we run short, we wait on Pacha’s harvest. That’s the gamble. We move forward. If we succeed, we launch on time. If we fail, we lose the window. We launch a partial load, or we launch late. But we don’t stop.”

He met their eyes again.

“I’m not asking for volunteers. There’s no committee. This is what we will do.”

Silence. He looked around at a team, people who knew what they were doing.

When Jakob got to the ice field, he looked around.

Pacha had used a pole to chip out the final cut on the seventh block. His arms hung heavy, shoulders low, but the job was done. Tom and Mira were guiding the ice onto the sled with ropes and winches, careful, methodical. Jakob watched them work.

Ellie was starting an adze cut. Lots of chips, but chips are also ice. She was cutting a long line. They would break off one-ton chunks later. 

They weren’t moving fast. But they were moving. And that was enough for now.

Tom’s voice came over Jakob’s radio, a direct call. “Jakob, we still need to calibrate the launcher. I need your help.”

Jakob started walking out to the Mass driver. He caught up with Tom, who was carrying a few boulders on a sled.

Tom began speaking. “I need to walk you through something.” He pulled up a slate layered with elliptical arcs, transfer windows, and annotated times. “How do you see this project right now?”

Jakob frowned. “Twelve sols until Day Zero. First launch. Then one shipment per week for ten weeks. Then we wait for the next window.”

Tom shook his head. “That’s the wrong perspective.” He turned the slate. “Ceres doesn’t care about launch dates. They care about delivery dates. If we want a shipment to land there every week, we have to hit a moving target.”

Lines flickered across the screen: curved orbits from Mars to Ceres. Tom tapped the first. “Day Zero is the fastest transfer. But to get them a shipment one week later, we can’t launch one week later. Ceres has moved. The second shipment takes two days longer to arrive, so it launches five days after the first. Then three days later. The curve compresses.”

Jakob leaned closer. “We’re overlapping launches?”

“It inverts,” Tom said. “Week eight ships before week seven. Week nine before week six. The curve bends backwards.” He changed windows. Four early orbits glowed green. “These launches happen before Day Zero but arrive at Ceres in weeks eleven through fourteen. Four extra shipments. Eighty million we didn’t count on.”

Jakob stared. “Send ice early; it gets there late.”

“Exactly.” Tom swiped again. Longer curves swept the far side of the solar system. “And here \- if we launch the other direction before blackout, we hit weeks sixty-three and sixty-four. Nine extra deliveries. That cuts the blackout from sixty-one weeks to forty-eight.”

They got to the mass driver, and Tom loaded up a boulder. He programmed the machine and asked Jakob to reload every time there was a launch. Tom hiked down range a couple of miles and set up a monitor. The first boulder flew, landing ten miles downrange. 

“Hook, two minutes off,” Tom called out, adjusting the parameters. 

Jakob loaded another. He reflected on Tom’s plan. “Some of these blocks will go halfway around the solar system.”

“That’s how you hit a moving target.”

The second boulder launched fast. It also landed about ten miles downrange. “Perfect. Dialed in.

Jakob loaded the third boulder. He looked down at the slate again. This was an opportunity. A narrow one. Exciting, if they had a saw. But now it meant nothing. They would be lucky to make their first shipment. Hitting extra shipments was not possible. 

Tom spoke, “Look, Jakob, I know we have a lot of stress right now, and I am not helping with this. I wanted to explain this to you before, back when we were making ice. Now it seems pointless. But I think you should know. It changes all our scheduling.”

The third boulder launched faster. It flew over a hundred miles down range. “Still perfect. We are dialed in,” Tom shouted. He walked back to Jakob, and together they returned to the dome.

“We’re burning out,” Jakob said. “Everyone’s trying, but the adzes are killing us. Shoulders, wrists, lower backs. Another week like this and someone’s going to tear something.”

Tom didn’t argue. He’d seen Mira wrap her wrist at lunch. Ellie’s gait had shifted. Everyone was compensating.

Jakob kicked at a rock. “What if we reforge the adze heads? Hammer them out into axe blades. Something that swings straighter.”

Tom gave it a second. “I can do the work. Might help with bite depth. But it won’t solve the strain. Still heavy tools, still tough ice.”

Jakob nodded. “I know. I just don’t see a good option.”

Tom didn’t answer right away. He kept hauling the empty sled back over uneven rock. Then he said, “We’ve still got the cargo bikes. Both of them. The ones we used for cardio.”

Jakob frowned. “You want us biking across the ice?”

“No. Mount a bike frame on a rig, use the drive wheel to turn a belt. I’d make a smaller saw blade. No tungsten carbide. Not eighteen feet in diameter. Maybe six feet in diameter. The bike spins that. It won’t be fast, but faster than hand-chipping. And we’d shift the strain to legs instead of arms.”

Jakob turned that over. “You think the bikes can handle that kind of load?”

“They’re built for cargo. I’ll weld a stand to a sled to hold the frame steady, and fix a belt to a pulley. Add a guide system for the blade. Maybe a screw mechanism to control cut depth.”

“We’d still wear ourselves out.”

Tom shrugged. “We’d be cutting ice, not cartilage. It’s an upgrade. Overall, it is much less effort, smoother, and uses stronger muscles than cutting ice with the adzes.”

Jakob exhaled through his nose. “Make two. We’ll rotate crews through them.”

“I’ll start tonight.”

Jakob looked at Tom seriously. “Let’s do it.”

Tom looked quizzically at Jakob. “Yeah, I just said I would.”

Jakob shook his head. “No, the other thing. Let’s launch tomorrow. Let’s hit every extra launch date you suggested. We are depending on your bike saws, you realize, but let’s do it.”

Tom’s pace picked up. “Yes. I mean, uh. Yes, I’ll get on it. Mira is good at this stuff. She and I will deliver two bikes by tomorrow.”

Jakob didn’t know if he was a fool or a genius. But right now he felt excited.

When they got to camp, Jakob went in the ship. He pulled on a headset and took a seat at the comms board. The air felt thinner here, despite the pressure seals. He stared through the window, scanning the empty horizon like Knox might somehow materialize from it.

Calling Ceres was always strange, like shouting into a canyon and waiting half an hour for the echo.

“Dr. Knox,” he began, voice steady, measured. “I’ll be direct. We lost our saw. We’re cutting with adzes now. I don’t know how much ice we’ll be able to harvest.”

He paused, then continued.

“But here’s the thing. We’re prepping the shipment for week 14, launching tomorrow. We believe we can still reach weeks 11 through 13 if we push. I’ve attached the full launch schedule and estimated arrivals. It’s aggressive. Maybe too aggressive. But we’re going to try.”

He ended the message and queued the transmission.

The red sky hadn’t changed. The sun burned low on the horizon, indifferent. Dr. Knox didn’t yet know the message had been sent.

When Jakob got down to the icefield, Tom was hard at work. Jakob took Pacha’s adze and started cutting. Pacha exhaled, exhausted. He gave Jakob a status update. “Jakob, I gotta tell you, the ag dome’s working. I’m washing and breaking down regolith, building soil. I added some carbon powder from the air recycler and some liquid fertilizer I brought up. So sweet. Sprouts are growing. It’s like a dream. The cavies aren’t cramped anymore, and they’re actually happy.”

Then he paused.

Jakob heard it before it came. “But?”

“I need a greenhouse,” Pacha said. “Not just grow lights. Real surface exposure. I’m doing what I can under LEDs, but if you want real food, we need sun-grown plants.”

Jakob closed his eyes. “You need space. Light. Water. Air.”

“Water’s not the hold-up right now,” Pacha said, gently. “It’s space.”

Jakob nodded, but didn’t answer. The truth was starting to tighten around him. Water for Ceres. Food for Mars. There was no room for both to be first.

Then his radio crackled. A long delay, followed by the calm, even voice of Dr. Knox, the kind of tone you develop when every decision has consequences.

“Jakob, good to hear from you. I want you to know we see what you’re doing. It’s dangerous work, and we’re grateful. What you’re giving us, we can’t get any other way.”

There was a pause. Knox continued.

“We’d been planning around ten shipments. That’s what the window looked like. But if you can really hit fourteen, we’ll make it worth your while. I’m not offering charity. We’re rationing already. Your ice lets us keep the mines open longer, keep the factories warm, keep people working. It buys us time.”

Jakob stood for a moment longer, processing.

Then he started swinging his adze again.

In a few minutes, the comm pinged again.

“Jakob, one more thing,” Knox said, almost sheepish. “We’ve got iron. Thousands of tons of it. Comes out with the molybdenum. But we’ve got no carbon. We can’t make steel. If you ever stumble across graphite out there, we’d be happy to pay for that, too.”

Jakob let out a short laugh, low and sharp.

Iron with no carbon. Ice saws with no motors. Plants with no space.

Everywhere, a piece missing.

Jakob looked over the ice. The others were already swinging. Mira was steady and rhythmic, while Ellie leaned into each strike with the quiet focus of someone burning calories just to stay warm and functional. The blocks glinted under the low sun, their edges still sharp from the adze work. They would pile up about five tons on the sled, now, a mixture of smaller blocks and chips from the adzes. They had processed eighty tons, and the next load was almost ready. 

Tom spelled Ellie, and she came over to talk to Jakob.

Jakob was direct. “Pacha needs a big greenhouse.”

That stopped her. She exhaled sharply, not quite a laugh. She tilted her head. “You want that printed now?”

Jakob shook his head. “I want it, yeah. But not yet. We can’t live on cavies and algae powder. Pacha’s right. We need real space for things with roots. He says five acres. That was always the plan. But that plan went out with the motor. For the next ten weeks, we are all ice cutters first. Everything else waits.”

Ellie understood. There was nothing to debate. She stepped back, caught her breath, and watched him swing. “I’ll start planning it, though. We get through these ten weeks, I will build it fast.”

They hauled another sled up to the processor. Only five tons at a time, to keep it from falling off. Three more loads to go. Jakob hoped to get another one loaded before dinner time, but he was going to be strict about no cutting after dinner. They could get two loads tomorrow. 

Pacha came back from processing the ice. He called them all to dinner. “It’s seven O’Clock according to the ship. Time to eat.”

Jakob looked at the load, only half full. Whatever they loaded would be enough. 

After dinner, Tom caught Mira. The two of them suited up and went to the ship. Jakob knew why. No one else even thought about it.

Morning came quietly. The warmth inside was a minor miracle, and no one wanted to leave it. Everyone ached from the work the day before. 

Pacha got up first. He moved silently past the others, pulling on a light shirt as he made his way to the cavies. They chattered softly at him from their enclosure, little noses twitching. He fed them from a bin of pellets, checked their water, then turned to set the table. No one had said it was his job, but it had become one of those things that simply happened because he did it.

Ellie woke slowly, stretching in the warmth of the dome. After six months of cramped ship quarters, the cot and gravity felt luxurious. She stood, rolled her shoulders, felt her body move properly, then went to find her base layer, letting it slide on slowly. Mars was supposed to be harsh, but right now? Right now, it felt like freedom.

Pacha glanced over, then back to his work.

“Morning,” she said, grinning.

“Morning,” he replied, not looking up. “There’s hot water if you want it.”

She sat at the table and sipped. As the others woke, 

The others drifted in, rubbing at sore shoulders, flexing their hands. The work left its mark, even if no one complained. Tom came in from outside. The first rig was standing, a cargo bike locked into a welded frame, a belt looped down from the rim to a saw spindle, rough depth screw bolted in. It wasn’t elegant, but it spun.

At breakfast, Mira couldn’t stop. “Guys, we built a pedal-powered saw,” she announced, arms wide. “Not a toy, either. I mean, it’s smaller than the old one. We had to make blades for it. Six foot, not eighteen foot. But that’s still real. You can feel the bite. Smooth cut, no chatter. Way faster than the adze.”

Ellie looked up. “Seriously?”

“Seriously, seriously. No shoulder pain, either. You use your legs. I could ride that thing all day.”

Pacha raised an eyebrow. “That’s a promise?”

Mira grinned. “You want a turn? We’re making a second one today.”

Jakob leaned in. “Is it stable? Will it hold alignment?”

Tom nodded. “Good enough. And if it isn’t, we will fix it.”

The mood shifted. Not giddy, but lighter. Bodies still ached, the math hadn’t changed, but the path forward suddenly seemed a lot better.

Jakob looked around the room at tired faces, calloused hands, too-thin bodies made lean by too much work and not enough food. No one had given up, but this couldn’t last. 

They all left together to try it out. Tom popped it up on a sled to get it down there. The sled didn’t roll. Tom just put it on another sled, and they all went down except Mira and Jakob. Mira crouched beside the sled that didn’t roll, her gloved hands steady. Jakob brought her a tarp. Soon, it was dotted with tools. mallet, wrenches, a grease gun, and a box of spare parts. She didn’t look up. Her focus was on the left front wheel housing, already disassembled and resting on its side like a tired limb.

The sleds were simple machines, flat aluminum frames mounted on twelve aluminum wheels, six to a side, with minimal suspension and no steering. They were built low to the ground, squat and stable, each one rated to carry a hundred tons. Their only concession to technology was the electric assist built into the hubs, small motors connected to an onboard battery, just enough to take the edge off pushing.

“This wheel’s been dragging,” Mira explained to Jakob. “Bearings are shot.”

She tapped the old bearing out with a soft mallet. It dropped onto the tarp with a dry metallic click. The outer ring was scored, and the ball cage looked warped, with too much grit in the housing, too much wear.

“Dust gets in,” she said, mostly to herself. “Regolith fines. Starts grinding everything down. Same as the motor.”

From a foil-lined pouch, she took a fresh bearing. She expected this before they left. She had maybe a hundred in the supply locker. She pressed it into place with slow care, aligning the motor housing before bolting it shut. The work was quiet and methodical, practiced. When she was done, she spun the wheel, then cleaned up. 

“These motors aren’t for pulling,” she added, almost offhand. “They’re too weak. They are just there to keep you from wrecking your spine. Light motors mean I could pack more replacements. Looks like we are going to need them.”

By lunch, both rigs were running. The bikes weren’t comfortable, and no one rode them for fun, but the saws cut faster. They were cutting one-ton triangular blocks, but these stacked up well. They would load up eight tons at a time. Soon they had finished a hundred tons, and Tom and Jakob loaded it into a fiberglass carrier. They loaded more ice into the processor. 

Tom grabbed some food at lunch, then He and Jakob left to load up the first hundred-ton block of ice in the mass driver. Together, they slid the block into place. The box it rode in was shaped like a giant lemon, made out of glass fiber spun from molten regolith. Ellie had made four before the saw disaster. Tom pulled up his slate, checked the programming, and triggered the sequence. The launcher responded with a hum, its alignment shifting minutely. Jakob watched the arc plot stabilize. It didn’t launch. The driver would wait for the right microsecond and launch then.

Without another word, they turned back toward camp. Inside the dome, the others were wrapping up lunch. Mira looked up as they stepped through the airlock.

“Launch is set?”

Tom nodded. “Automated. The clock handles the microsecond window. We’ll know in an hour if the launch worked.”

“Like hell,” Ellie grinned, putting on her gear. “I’m going to watch the launch.” 

Pacha started putting on his gear. “Dudes, no way I am missing this.”

The rest followed. Tom and Jakob looked at each other. Tom spoke up. “Jakob, there is no reason to watch. If it doesn’t launch correctly, there is nothing we can do. I will program in a new launch tomorrow.”

“No, Tom,” Jakob answered. “We are not going to fix it if it doesn’t work. We are going because we want to watch the inaugural flight of our business. Everything may look kind of hard right now, but we are still launching a hundred tons of ice to Ceres on a launch window we didn’t know existed. Let’s go.”

The team got to the mass driver about ten minutes before the launch. Tom propped his slate up with a rock, showing a countdown timer. They chattered excitedly, the pains from the day’s work receding, replaced with excitement and a sense of accomplishment. 

They all counted the last ten seconds together. “Ten. Nine. Eight. Seven. Six. Five. Four. Three. Two. O…”

The railgun clicked, and the package started moving before they finished “one.” It was faster than they could believe. Even Tom, who designed it and knew exactly how it worked, was amazed by the launch. They could see a tail of red dust coming up behind where the ice had already passed. The Martian horizon is only two miles away, so they couldn’t watch it go down the entire path, but they did see it when it started across the sky. A glowing red ball, faster than they could believe. Everyone cheered. 

The team was excited on the way home. Mira spoke up. “We did it. We actually did it. And the ice saws Tom and I built. Are they great or what?”

Pacha laughed. “They are great, Mira. I still finish exhausted, but not all beaten up as the adzes left me. And they are so much faster. So, does that mean Ellie might have some free time to build for me?”

Ellie thought about it. “Well, we only have two saws. If we are keeping up with the schedule, I will start your greenhouse. If we fall behind, I stop. Deal?”

“¡Trato\!” Pacha shouted. “Let’s do it.”

The math had changed. They could load multiple shipments a day. They returned to the ice fields. No time to celebrate. They kept cutting. But if they kept working, they would be able to keep Ceres well stocked.

Their third morning in the dome was quiet but not slow. People moved with purpose. The sleds were loaded. The ice was pure. The mass driver hummed and launched again, without an audience this time. They were actually cutting faster than they needed to. Tom had found a way to send more water than they had ever planned.

For now, it was working.

## 

## **Chapter 13: Success Breeds Hard Work**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

The shutters of *Le Puits Perdu* were drawn against the light, though the Parisian street outside was already beginning to hum with delivery bikes, mopeds, and the low murmur of pedestrians threading through narrow corridors of the sixth arrondissement. Inside, the café was quiet. The tables were unset except one, where two white plates rested on clean linen, and the smell of baking butter and yeast softened the cold stone walls.

Emmanuel, the owner, was somewhere in the back. He had opened early for Catherine Dahl and no one else.

Catherine sat alone, her coat folded neatly beside her. The waiter brought a basket of pain d’épi, still warm, its golden crust flaking under the cloth, and a terrine of whipped salted butter. Beside it, a small plate of Saint-Marcellin, a wedge of Ossau-Iraty, and two slices of a dense Alpine tomme. The second plate held a small galette filled with caramelized onion and mushroom, topped with soft goat cheese and a single runny egg. Her espresso was untouched.

When Max Juno arrived, the door opened without ceremony. He stepped inside, blinking once at the dim light, then smiled as he spotted her.

“You look rested,” he said.

“I fake it well.” She gestured to the empty seat. “Come in. It’s good to finally meet you.”

Max slid into the chair opposite her. The waiter appeared without prompting, placing a fresh plate for him, along with his own galette, tomato, melted comté, and thin slices of dried lamb shoulder.

“I told Emmanuel you eat what’s in season. If you’d like something else, tell them.”

The waiter returned again, placing a small dish of fig preserves, a bowl of clementine segments in thyme syrup, and a second espresso.

Catherine reached into her folio and placed an envelope on the table. She slid it forward without commentary.

Max glanced at it but didn’t pick it up. “That for me?”

“Four hundred twenty million,” she said. “That’s more than the agreed price. Payment in full for the first fourteen shipments, with a fifty percent bonus for delivering full loads all fourteen weeks.”

He reached for the envelope now, weighed it briefly, and tucked it inside his coat. He never dreamed of this much money, even when Jakob had explained it.

“You didn’t think we’d pull it off.”

“I didn’t think you’d go to Mars,” she said. “When I made the offer, I thought Jakob would try to source from Earth. I knew there was no way he could move that much water from here. He never mentioned Mars. He’s full of surprises.”

Max smiled faintly. “He is.”

“Still,” she said, slicing a piece of bread, “he did it. That water’s not just for drinking. It’s in our smelters, our reactors, our cooling cycles. It’s Hydrolox for our ships. The algae tanks are running full tilt, and the team is taking showers. What you delivered is the reason my project made it through its first phase.”

Max spread a bit of fig preserve on a corner of bread, took a bite, and nodded. “Then we’re in business.”

Catherine wiped her fingers on a napkin and pulled out a second envelope. Thinner than the first. She placed it beside her plate.

“This one isn’t a check. It’s a proposal. New contract. Triple the water. Same quality, same schedule. I want to add one hundred tons of refined carbon per week. And food.”

Max raised an eyebrow.

“Meals,” she clarified. “For my crew. Prepared. They are to include meat, vegetables, and bread. What they are eating is horrid. I tried it. I want the miners to remember what a meal feels like.”

He looked at the envelope but didn’t open it.

“You’ve got production running. Why upscale so fast?”

“Because Raines wants more. Because demand from Earth isn’t slowing. I am sending another sixty miners, ten replacements, and fifty new headcount, bringing the total up to a hundred. What we’ve built on Ceres works, but only just. We sent people up there to dig. Jakob’s team is living. Mine is surviving.”

Max leaned back slightly. “You know what this would cost you from Earth.”

“Which is why I’m not sourcing from Earth. You have a working supply chain. The margin is yours.”

He took a long sip of coffee, savoring it. “I’ll talk to Jakob. No guarantees.”

“I wouldn’t trust you if you offered one. See what Jakob can do.”

They ate quietly for a few minutes, the soft scrape of cutlery and faint clatter from the kitchen the only sounds. The bread was still warm. The clementines were perfectly balanced, acidic, sweet, and herb-laced.

After a while, Catherine added, almost as an afterthought, “Tell him thank you.”

Max looked up.

“Not for the water,” she said. “For listening that night. For letting me feel like someone out there actually cares and wants to help.”

He smiled. “Jakob is remarkable. When he first presented his plan, I almost dismissed him, but I saw his determination and unique perspective. He is inventive. He never ceases to impress me.”

When Catherine was done, they said their goodbyes, and Max stayed. Not a lot of people came down to Lagos, and Max liked the idea of investing in other ventures. He had made some calls and set up a few meetings while he was up in Paris. 

Some of the people he met, he didn’t trust. Some other ideas were good, but didn’t speak to him, per se. Max was focused on space. His time on the Moon had left that hunger in him.

By midmorning, the café had opened to the public. Max lingered at a side table near the rear window. Emmanuel, out of sight but never far, kept the space open for him between bookings.

One fellow impressed him. An Australian arrived without fanfare. Broad across the shoulders, wind-chapped, and moving with the careful assurance of someone accustomed to uncertain footing, he stepped inside and paused just long enough to scan the room. He spotted Max, gave a brief nod, and crossed over.

“Callum Haldane,” he said. His voice carried a dry, gravelly cadence, shaped more by red dust and engine noise than formal instruction. He set down a narrow duffel and offered a calloused hand.

Max shook it. “Glad to meet you.”

The waiter arrived without being summoned and poured coffee for them both. Callum watched in silence, waited until the cups were settled and the waiter had retreated, then leaned back slightly and took stock of the room.

“I’ve been watching what Raines is buying,” he said. “He’s driving up the price of beryllium, trying to stockpile for those tokaplex builds. Ceres gives him tungsten, tantalum, molybdenum, vanadium, all the big refractory metals. But beryllium’s not there in ore form. Too diffuse. No concentration.”

He paused for a sip of coffee. No reaction to the taste.

“That’s what got me thinking about Mars. Volcanic history, past water flow. You get both, you might get proper ore bodies. So I pulled old Martian data. Started combing through rover sweeps. Found a crater about sixty klicks east of Olympus Mons. One of the polar satellites passed over on a low-angle sweep. I found a density anomaly. Off-pattern. I ran the spectrum against Earth-side samples. Closest match was beryllium.”

Max said nothing. He took a slow sip, watching Callum over the rim of his cup.

Callum continued. “I’ve got three others ready to sign on. Crew from Western Australia. We’ve worked lithium fields, deep copper. We know how to run a mining site. What we don’t have is infrastructure.”

Max nodded slightly. “What are you missing?”

“Transport. Shelter. Food, water, air, heat, sewage, the full stack. We’ve got rigs, partial automation, but we’re not building from bare regolith. You get us to the site and keep us alive long enough to dig, we’ll pull ore.”

He glanced at Max, then added, “And we both know it won’t sit on the shelf. Raines has a standing buy order for every ounce of beryllium he can find. Those tokaplexes aren’t made of dreams and kind words.”

Max rested a hand on the table. “I think we may be able to help you with that.”

Callum nodded once, almost to himself. “You drop Mars-pure beryllium in Raines’ lap, and he’ll crawl over his own funding board to get more.”

Max leaned back slightly. “I can fund that. I can put you where you need to be. I want forty percent. What do you think?”

Callum’s expression didn’t change, but he took a moment before answering.

“Forty’s heavy,” he said. “But you’re taking the risk up front. If you’re covering transport, infrastructure, and coordination, I can live with that. But once we’re operational, the terms stay fixed. You don’t touch the technical, and we don’t chase investors.”

Max gave a slow nod. “Not exactly. I’ll buy equipment and pay your early expenses, but you’ll pay those expenses as soon as you start making money.”

They sat for a moment, the soft sounds of the café rising around them. Max stirred his coffee once, then looked out toward the street.

Eventually, Max nodded. “There is a team on Mars. Magazine Beach. They can get you housing. They are on the South Pole now, but they need to move to a temperate location. They might be happy to move near Olympus Mons. They’ll want to be paid. They’ll also need mineral mines for fertilizer. Could your people help locate that?”

“If we get dug in, yes,” Callum said. “We’re not agronomists, but we know how to find what’s buried.”

“You’ll need to handle smelting and casting yourselves.”

Callum gave a small nod. “We’ve got someone lined up. Not fully industrial, but enough to get clean metal. We’re not planning to ship raw ore.”

“The South Pole team can take care of getting it to Earth.”

Callum rose, adjusted the strap, and met Max’s eye.

“If you’re in,” he said, “we’ll be ready.”

Then he turned and left, without looking back.

Max stayed seated a few minutes longer after Callum left. The maps were still in the duffel. Nothing on the table but a cooling cup of coffee and a few grains of sugar near the saucer’s edge.

Forty percent. Housing, logistics, launch. If the site held, it would be worth ten times that. If it didn’t, well, they’d burned fuel on worse guesses. And they could help Magazine Beach move.

Outside, the sky was quiet. Dust curled just above the regolith, lifted, then dropped by cold crosswinds.

Max sat with a new cup of coffee and opened the second envelope Catherine had left that morning.

It was a full draft contract. The figures were typed cleanly in the margin.

Max set the paper down, took a slow breath, and looked out the window. The city moved with its usual rhythms. Nothing was scarce here.

But out there, between three other worlds, scarcity defined everything. And now, just barely, a few people had begun to price survival like a market.

It was time to speak with Jakob. He keyed in the transmission and let the recorder settle. The slate light blinked once. Then he spoke.

“Jakob. Pacha. I’m sending this from Paris. Just a short update to begin with…”

On Mars, the dome lighting was fixed at midday levels, but Jakob hadn’t adjusted. He and Pacha sat at the main table in the fabrication wing, both with slates open, half-finished reports abandoned in the corner of the screen. A tone sounded, low and deliberate. Message in queue.

Jakob pulled it up and scanned the header.

“It’s Max. He’s in Paris today,” he said.

Pacha pushed his tablet aside. Jakob tapped play.

Max’s image appeared, seated alone at a narrow café table. He looked rested, measured, maybe even a little too calm. The message played.

“Jakob. Pacha. I’m sending this from Paris. Just a short update to begin with. Dahl was pleased. Ceres got more than they expected, and the ice was cleaner than they hoped. She gave a fifty percent bonus. She wants more. Three times more ice, but also carbon and food this time too. Processed, sealed, frozen. She is impressed with your agriculture and wants to feed her team better. I attached the contract.”

Another pause.

“I also talked to Tan. She wants ice and food as well. She is willing to pay what Dahl is paying. She has less need for water, but more need for food. She runs a cantina there and is feeding a lot of Lunans. Right now, she is feeding them Earth food, but believes Martian food would be a real hit.”

Pacha sat forward when he mentioned food. Jakob said nothing, but his eyes narrowed slightly at Tan’s name.

Before it ended, Jakob was already flagging Tom, Ellie, and Mira. They needed to hear it. Jakob replayed the message when they were all there.

Max continued, his voice lower and more deliberate. “What I’m about to propose wasn’t on the table when we last spoke. But the opportunity came fast, and I think it’s real.”

He glanced down, as if reviewing something. Then back to the camera.

“A miner named Callum Haldane came to see me this morning. Australian. Solid. His team’s been analyzing Raines’ buy orders and looking for new sources of beryllium. According to some old rover data and old orbital telemetry, they found a crater about sixty kilometers east of Olympus Mons with a high-density anomaly. Spectrum scan lines up with beryllium signatures.”

Another pause.

“They’re miners. Not settlers. They’ve got the tools, partial automation, and one smelter tech. But they have no life support, no base. They need housing, power, food, and water. They need someone to keep them alive long enough to get the ore out.”

Max leaned slightly toward the camera.

“I told them I could fund it and coordinate logistics. But that only works if you’re willing to establish the site. You’d build it for them, not just for you. If it goes ahead, they’ll arrive with a nuclear thermal ship. Once they land, the engine becomes a reactor. You’d have more base power than you’ve had since the landing day. Enough to expand food, keep lights on, and maybe even grow.”

He let the weight of that settle.

“They’ll refine the metal on site. If you can get it to Earth, we can sell it. Raines needs it.”

He looked straight into the lens.

“I know this complicates your move. But Olympus might be the right place to build for real. They can help you move if you can take care of them. Think about what you would need.”

When the message ended, there was a brief silence. Then Tom gave a short, dry cheer. “Bonus\! No one said anything about a bonus. And now three times the water? Sweet\!”

Mira tore up some paper to make confetti and threw it in the air. “Four hundred twenty million. We’re all rich. And next year. Another three billion. Yes\!. She began doing cartwheels, alarming the cavies.

Ellie just fell on the floor, half crying. 

Tom and Pacha sat thinking. This was a lot of work for them. The money was on Earth, not here. They both started making lists of equipment they would need. They hoped the Aussies could bring it.

Jakob was all business, maintaining his calm composure. But his mouth didn’t get the message and was grinning of its own accord.

Jakob let them have their moment. Then, when Ellie sat up and wiped her eyes, when Mira stopped bouncing, and Pacha looked up from his notes, he leaned forward. “We need to talk about what this actually means. Four months more here, then we relocate north. If we don’t move above the Antarctic Circle before winter, we’ll die. No sunlight means no solar power means no oxygen.” 

The team sobered. Pacha nodded slowly. “And the food scaling. Feeding the four miners is just more of what we’re doing. But a hundred people on Ceres? That’s industrial agriculture. Different greenhouse designs, different water chemistry, and completely different waste management. We’d need to figure out how to even ship it without it spoiling.” 

“One step at a time,” Jakob said. “First, we tell Max we’re in. We can handle the water and carbon. The miners bring the reactor, and we build them a base at Olympus Mons. Food production, we’ll scale up as we figure it out.” 

Tom was already sketching on his tablet. “If the miners arrive first, that actually helps us. We can use their ship’s reactor to power the move, build the new base with real power instead of scraping by on solar.” 

“Exactly.” Jakob pulled up the recording interface. “Let’s tell Max what equipment we will need. By the time the Aussies arrive, we’ll be ready.”

Back in Paris, Max was packing up. He thought about the other groups he had talked to that day: a chemical engineer ready to build a chemical industry on Mars, a hotelier ready to provide hospitality for tourists and Cereans on vacation, and a lunar helium miner who thought Mars would offer easier living than the Moon and still be profitable. All good pitches, but not tonight. 

He thought about what made Mars special.

Earth had power. The Moon had money. Ceres had metals.  
Mars had a business plan.

## **Chapter 14: Moving Day**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

Mira and Pacha had been out cutting ice all morning.

Jakob had pulled up insolation charts. “Tom, looking at our current solar power output, do you believe we can still make the trek north on our own?”

“It’s a race against decreasing sunlit hours and how far we can travel in a day. By delaying until now, the decision to wait has been made.”

Jakob knew that when he had not pushed back during the call from Paris, there was no other possible outcome.

Ellie sighed. “Yes, we need to go north, but not to Olympus Mons. The ice is at Planum Boreum. We need the water, and, to be frank, so does Callum. He just doesn’t know it.”

“When we first arrived, we already knew we needed a way to get to Planum Boreum for this season,” Jakob reminded them. “Callum is that way. We stop a month or so where he is, get his team operational, then he will take us north to Planum Boreum, and we will be back in business sending ice to Ceres. It’s a shorter flight for Callum, too, so he can supply his own camp.”

“As long as no one gets attached to the miner’s site,” Ellie cautioned. “We have to have water. We have to move on.”

Jakob looked back at Ellie. “OK. Now, what I need you to do is get ready for the move. And be excited about it. Agreed?”

As Ellie nodded, the airlock began cycling, and Mira and Pacha came in. 

Mira was excited. “Tom, we dropped ten blocks in the cycler. Another launch is ready.”

“Good. I’ll slow launch that load tomorrow. That will give us one more week before we need to resume shipments.” 

Callum hadn’t arrived in ten hours or twenty. The hope of a daylight landing was gone. When it got dark, Jakob climbed atop the ag dome and began slowly waving a beacon light northward, scanning the horizon. After a few hours, he saw a new light near the horizon, too bright to be a star. Jakob froze, afraid to move in case the light was some trick of dust or fatigue.

The radio crackled a moment later. “G’day, mates. Callum’s my name.” 

Jakob’s shoulders relaxed. The calm message erased a mountain of worries. As the gyroplane aligned with the makeshift runway, its huge coaxial rotors set it down softly. Callum unlatched his suit from the ship’s oxygen and electrical feeds and stepped out. Tom stepped forward, wordlessly hooking up the hydrolox lines to refuel.

“Welcome to Magazine Beach\!” Mira called out, her voice warm and inviting. “Come on in.”

They all went through the airlock into the ag dome. When Callum removed his helmet, he was hit by the sudden, thick scent of damp soil, growing things, and the musk of the cavies. It felt earthy and alive. 

“My crew’s livin’ in our ship for the last four months. Rank doesn’t even cover it. Your place smells sweet and full of life. Is this what you’re gonna put together for us?” Callum asked.

Ellie explained her priorities. “More or less. When I get up there, building an ag dome is my first priority. That lets us raise livestock and gives Pacha a place to start crops and mess with the dirt. We will all sleep there until food production is stable.”

Pacha gave him a tour, showing him the plant beds and trays under light. Callum leaned closer, smiling when he saw the cavies in their pen. After months of algae and supplements, the sight of anything fresh made his mouth water.

“So you’re Pacha,” Callum whispered. “Max sent me these, said to hand ’em over quietly.” Callum pulled out two baggies of soil, one dark, one more multicolored. “One is Andean, one is Himalayan, but they both just look like dirt to me. What’s the go with ’em?”

Pacha moved between Callum and the rest of the crew. “Thanks, man. Keep it down. The rest of the team doesn’t need to know. My legal mycorrhiza cultures are failing, hurting crop yields. I need a healthy soil biome. The robust, wild fungi and bacteria in the dark Andean soil and the more colorful Tibetan soil are promising for our low-pressure environment. But, you know, we aren’t supposed to do it this way. I’ve just run out of options.”

Callum gave him a wink, and no more was said. Pacha put the soil away in a drawer, and they rejoined the rest. 

Jakob was sitting at the table. “Hey Callum, you must be famished after that flight. Pacha is a first-rate cook. Let’s have some dinner.”

Callum took a seat. “Nothing could sound better. I’m starved.”

Mira set the plates and cups of water. Pacha first served up a simple salad of microgreens with freshly baked dinner rolls. 

Callum devoured it. “It’s been too long since I ate real food. These rolls are amazing. They have an earthy flavor I love, and they are actually chewy. After months of paste, it is great to have something to sink my teeth into. What are they?”

“Amaranth is our staple here. It grows well at low pressure, even without mycorrhiza, and is more nutritious than other grains. I’m glad you like it, because you’ll be eating a lot of it.”

Pacha then served the main course, roast cavy with sliced oca. The aroma of roast meat enveloped Callum. It was mouthwatering. He picked it up and began eating, letting the fats roll down his chin. Then he looked around at the rest of them, using knives and forks. “Sorry, mates. Reckon I got a bit carried away. That meat is delicious.”

Pacha was amused and threw Callum a napkin. “I’m glad you like it. It’s the only sort of meat we have here, but we have it with every meal. How’s the oca?”

“Is that what these little potato things are? Yeah, like lemon zest. Their crunch goes great with the meat. My boys are going to love this food.”

After dinner, Callum excused himself to get cleaned up. Mira showed him the restroom.

Pacha smiled. The Magazine Beach crew had always appreciated the meals, but never so viscerally as Callum. 

When Callum returned, Jakob brought up the plan. “We’ll send Tom and Ellie with you first to get started on a new ag dome.”

“That’ll work,” Callum said. “We’d love a dome like this.”

Jakob nodded. “It’s Pacha’s dome, really. Agriculture trumps everything else. We can stay as long as we are not in his way. We need to get everyone under cover before the move from here is final.”

Tom and Ellie began loading the sleds. Ellie also needed a glassmaker for plumbing. Most people looked at a dome and thought the walls were what mattered. Ellie knew the real work was waste management, lighting, power, air, laundry, and running water. Even in the ag dome, that was what counted.

Callum checked the straps himself. “That’s all you need to get started?”

Ellie pulled her visor down. “It’ll do.”

The gyroplane had been designed for this trip. It needed a full tank to make it back. Tom checked the fuel level before disconnecting the hoses. “All filled. Let’s go.”

Ellie stopped to hug the people staying behind before leaving. “Mira, you remember how to clear it if the sewer backs up, right?”

Mira’s eyes were wet with tears. She didn’t answer. 

Jakob cleared his throat. “We will keep things running here.”

With that, the three of them got in the gyroplane. They plugged their pressure suits into the gyroplane’s oxygen and electricity. Callum checked everyone’s connections, then started off. 

Jakob was fascinated watching the gyroplane ascend. The six-bladed tractor fan was large by Earth standards, twelve feet in diameter, but in Mars’ thin air, it didn’t have enough bite to get the machine rolling. The electric landing gear provided most of the acceleration, with the tractor fan maintaining speed as it lifted off. Then, at fifty feet, the enormous pusher prop extended and unfolded, its thirty-foot radius delivering efficient thrust that carried them steadily north. Jakob watched the bird slip past the horizon, then turned back to the dome. 

The gyroplane kept a steady pace. Callum had set the autopilot almost as soon as they leveled out. “We should arrive around three. I need some shuteye.”

After Callum nodded off, Tom followed suit. Ellie stayed up. The surface of Mars didn’t even look black; it just looked like she was travelling in space, and there were no stars below. The stars above were spectacular. Earth was visible, and Jupiter. Ellie felt at peace among the wandering planets. The gyroplane hummed evenly, a mix of the electric drive whine of the tractor motor and the deeper pulse of the huge pusher prop.

It was past 3 a.m. when Olympus Mons came up on the horizon. She couldn’t see it, but it began blotting out the stars. The gyroplane descended in a long glide. Callum woke and retracted the pusher prop. The basal scarp soon blotted out everything forward except a small light on the landing strip. They gently autorotated down with the tractor fan still pulling, landing on the pediment, a mile south of the scarp. Ellie imagined how this would look when it was built. Larger, stronger, better domes. Enormous greenhouses. Mines. It needed a name that fit both the miners and the Magazine Beach crew. 

When they got out, she grabbed a shovel and started digging. Twenty minutes later, she stood back to admire the word carved deep into the red soil. “Bounty.”

Callum’s transit ship stood a hundred yards away, legs planted deep into the regolith. The hull showed streaks of re‑entry heating and red dust accumulation. Around the base, the three solid rocket boosters looked like battered iron pillars. Their bottom ten feet were crushed and splayed outward where they had absorbed the final impact.

“That ship is enormous,” Tom commented. “It looks beat up.”

“Yeah, nah. You blokes ordered some pretty massive gear, and my crew’s got extensive mining kit. They assembled her in high Earth orbit. She never felt a lick of Earth’s pull, so size wasn’t as much of a problem. Landing her was dicey. None of us thought we would live. It was all autopilot. The mass injection rate increased first. We could really feel the thrust increase after months of barely any gravity. Then we started the skip, bringing us down to subsonic. The parachutes came out, getting our speed low. We dropped just below the shield that way, then the candles lit. Those solid rockets have the raw thrust we needed to avoid making a crater. They stuck out, so when they hit, they crumpled, giving us a soft stop.”

Callum, Tom, and Ellie were all in the airlock. As soon as the pressure normalized, Callum made a sweeping gesture. “Welcome aboard.”

The smell hit them as soon as the inner door opened: lithium grease, warm metal, and the stale tang of long‑cycled air. The interior, for its size, was still close‑packed, crates strapped into the passageways, personal kits lashed to bulkheads. Tom and Ellie remembered their own trip. The Mars transit is rough. They took off their pressure suits and slept on the floor until morning.

In the morning, Ellie got up first. She nudged Tom, and they began cooking pancakes with some amaranth flour from Magazine Beach. The miners appreciated the break from standard rations. As they finished eating, Ellie spoke. “First task is to build the ag dome. Can you guys dig the foundation for me?”

“We’ll get that dug out in no time,” Callum replied. “One thing us boys can do is dig.”

They suited up after breakfast and began unloading. Tom was already at the hatch, helping unstrap the sleds. The miners hauled down the excavator, a tracked backhoe with a frontloader. They had a slew of other equipment for mining, from jackhammers to sizing screens.

Tom and Ellie hauled out the Sabatier unit for polyethylene production. It was small, capable of a ton an hour, matched neatly to the output of the Bosch carbon powder line. The water inlet was underneath, and had an *H₂* pipe coming up from it. Ellie studied it to find the hydrogen bypass. She found the water intake and looked to where the electrolysis section broke off into hydrogen and oxygen. There was the inlet where she could inject hydrogen directly, as long as she kept a couple of gallons of water in the cracker to keep the gas contained.

Ellie started talking to Callum. “This ship was a nuclear thermal rocket,” she said. “Hydrogen propellant?”

“That’s right. Fastest way out here.”

“Making polymer can eat water,” Ellie said. “Back at Magazine Beach, we didn’t care, because we had plenty of water. Here you are parched. The plan is that I am going to use your hydrogen. Is that OK?”

Callum thought for a moment. “We used most of it. Maybe thirty tons are still in the tanks. Take all you want. It will boil off soon, anyway.”

Ellie did the math, then nodded. “That’s two hundred tons of polymer. We can make a larger dome and a few greenhouses.”

Callum smiled. “That sounds great. There is a hydrogen takeoff in the cabin and another one near the door. Now, see that place over there where Petey is standing? Is that where you want the dome?”

Ellie nodded. “Roughly, yeah. Let me mark it out. I am going to need graded regolith, so please move it over by the sizing screens.”

Ellie drew a circle on the ground showing where she wanted the dome, and the excavator came alive, putting a low, electric vibration in the ground. Ellie watched the first cut into the regolith, the loader bucket peeling away the surface in broad, light scoops. Dust slipped through the teeth like dry powder, curling into the thin air before falling back. They fed the first load through the shaker box, separating the dense minerals that block radiation from the light silicates used for glassmaking. 

Callum gave a rowdy wave as he headed back to the gyroplane. “Righto, listen up, you lot\! Be good to Ellie and don’t go actin’ like a bunch of drongos. We’re in dire need of a decent home. I’m flyin’ south now to grab Pacha and bring him up.”

The miners waved as Callum left, then got to work helping Ellie. They understood processing dirt. Sorting and grading went fast. On the pediment, the regolith was shallow. They pulled up five feet of regolith, then started on the bedrock with drills and jackhammers, digging three more feet to finish the basement. 

While they chipped away, Ellie started making polymer with the hydrogen takeoff near the door. That ran by itself while she took the silicate tailings and started up the glassworks. By the time excavation was complete, she was ready to line the basement with glass wool and set up rebar to support the domes.

The miners were fascinated when she pulled out one of the 3D printers and began laying in polymer/dust foam. The foam insulated the basement floor and walls, locking the rebar in place. They were running out of daylight when Ellie switched over to using ninety percent regolith to make the stronger surface walls. She barely got the double wall structure started, sealing in the airlock and the utilities block before dinner time. The rest of the dome would have to wait.

While Ellie was working on the ag dome, Tom was working on getting power. The ship had come in on a small nuclear thermal rocket. The reactor made two hundred megawatts of thermal energy. But for electricity, they used a thermocouple that only managed two hundred kilowatts. That was plenty for running the ship in transit, but not enough for operations on Mars. Tom and Petey had stayed in the ship, rooting through the gear to find the generator.

“Have a squiz at this, mate,” Petey said, grinning as he hauled out a large crate. “They reckon this little beauty can pump out a hundred megawatts of spark if you give it some welly. Got a fair few pipes and bits and bobs with it. Reckon we can piece it together, or are we dreamin’?”

Tom’s eyes widened. He opened the crate and started inspecting it. Simple design, built to interface with the General Atomics reactor. He just needed to send supercritical *CO₂* through the pipes instead of hydrogen.

The system needed forty tons of liquid *CO₂* for the working fluid. Tom pulled out his slate and did the math. Callum’s big twin screw compressor could make the *CO₂* in two days. He set the pressure relief valve to eighty bar to drain off the nitrogen and other unwanted gases. Tom started the motor, then checked with Petey. “You think we can get this together in two days?”

“Rockets aren’t exactly built to be pulled apart, are they? But once we’ve yanked that nozzle out, I reckon we’ll be right as rain. I’ll get the jacks and blocks sorted and get stuck in straight away.”

Tom helped him set up the blocks and jacks on a couple of sleds. As soon as the nozzle was supported, Petey worked his way up into the nozzle connection and rows of nuts. His pneumatic wrench strained with each one. 

Tom was impressed with how quickly he got to work. “Need a hand?”

“Bit of a squeeze in here for the both of us, mate, but if you can worm your way over to the hydrogen tank connection, you can turn the thrust shutoff valve and disconnect the reactor intake pipe. Once that’s done, we can get this show on the road.”

Tom looked at where he needed to get to and thought about how much better Mira would be at getting there. He squared his shoulders, grabbed a nut driver, and started winding his way in.

By evening, Tom had the tank shut off, and the reactor intake pipe was connected to the turbine return pipe. Petey called out as he got the last bolt off the nozzle. Together, they lowered the bell until it cleared the hot pipe and trucked it out from under the ship. They slid the turbine into the space. With no nozzle, this was no longer a rocket; it was a power plant.

Tom and Ellie ate with the miners on the ship that night. 

At dinner, Ellie began updating people on construction. “We have finished the subgrade work. Tomorrow, the dome should go together pretty easily. We have one more night with the smell of lithium grease and recycled air. Tomorrow we’ll move into the dome.”

Tom’s fork stopped mid-motion. “You’ll finish tomorrow?”

“Just the structure, and air. Pipes, wires, and furnishing have not begun.”

“Well done. Me, I don’t have good news. Just work. I need a one-acre section of bedrock scraped clean. All the regolith piled up near the ship. And I need a dozen six-inch holes about four feet deep. Does anyone here know how to do that?”

Luca gave a bit of a sheepish grin. “I’m your man for that, then. The regolith’s only about five feet deep in these parts. No worries, mate. I’ll have it knocked over in a couple of hours.”

“Hours? Perfect. That space will be our primary heat sink. The reactor makes the *CO₂* hot, but I need something to make it cold.”

The next day went very smoothly. Ellie and Angus worked on the inner dome first, while Luca cleared away regolith and Petey drilled the holes. Tom followed Petey, using a three-foot wrench to twist each heat sink, expanding the diameter and locking them into the bedrock. He set up the spider connections to and from the heat sinks, and piped the return back to the impeller. Tom and Petey worked together to attach the intake of the turbine where the nozzle had been. When everything was in place, Petey slowly opened the valve to feed *CO₂* into the turbine until the liquid reached the fill line on the sight glass, about halfway up. Tom opened the intake valve coming from the reactor and hit the starter. The system bogged, trying to move the liquid up into the reactor, but after a minute, there was no liquid, just a hot, supercritical fluid, putting out real power for Bounty.

When Luca came back with the excavator, dome construction sped up. He could keep the hoppers of both printers full as well as dump regolith in the space between the walls. By lunch, the dome was sealed. Ellie started compressing the air inside it and set the recycler to keep the *CO₂* under a thousand ppm.

Callum and Pacha arrived just as they were all sitting down to lunch. Pacha brought all the cavies into the ship and opened the air valves on their flats. 

“Those guys hate the flats. I’ll let them breathe regular air again.”

Ellie looked around the ship. “I am not sure this is any improvement, Pacha, but you do you.”

Angus looked at Callum. “Hey, we finished the dome. Want to check it out?”

Callum looked back at Ellie. “Is that true, lass?”

“We have to wait for air. The recycler is running hard, but the *CO₂* level is still too high. When we have enough oxygen in there, a green light will come up on the utilities block. Give it another hour. Just so you understand, the dome walls are done, but it is not yet plumbed or fully wired.”

Callum smiled. “When it’s ready, let’s unload all Pacha’s stuff to the dome, and you can give me a quick tour before I go.” 

After lunch, they brought a couple of sleds over to the gyroplane and started unloading his terra pretta, plants, seeds, and two baggies of dirt. When Callum saw those, he looked over at Pacha, but Pacha said nothing.

They brought it all back to the dome, but the light wasn’t green yet.

Ellie took the opportunity to remind them of the rules. “Now, listen. Since it is not plumbed yet, for the next few days, if you need to go to the bathroom, you will need to use the head on the ship. I’ll get the plumbing sorted soon, and then we can use the restrooms in the dome.”

They nodded. Petey looked baffled. “So, if I need to hit the loo in the night, you want me to chuck on my pressure suit, head through the airlock, stroll over to the ship, go through its airlock, pull off my pressure suit, hit the loo, then put the pressure suit back on, head through the ship’s airlock again, come back to the dome, go through the dome’s airlock, take off my pressure suit again, and then finally head to bed? I’d totally miss brekkie doing all that\!”

“Yeah, mates, no worries. I’ve got a better idea,” Angus whispered.

When the green light finally came on, they all helped move Pacha’s gear inside. Pacha set his cavies loose in the dome and began setting up soil regions for the plants. Everyone moved their own gear, too. They brought cots, blankets, and a few buckets with clothes and personal items.

This time, he was planting a sunflower relative called goldeneye. “It’s good for the soil biome,” he said, looking meaningfully at Callum. 

Callum couldn’t stay. After a brief look at the dome, he was back in the air, heading down to pick up Jakob and Mira.

Ellie continued, bringing Pacha downstairs. He was glad to have someone to talk to. “Ellie, I missed you guys. Magazine Beach feels empty, now. Callum landed while I was asleep. I hope I got everything. It was so dark the whole ride up here, so I couldn’t see anything. It was like our transit to Mars. Just stars and blackness. Callum just got in the air and went straight to sleep. I didn’t even have anyone to talk to. But it was dawn before we got close to Olympus Mons. It is so huge. The scarp is just everything when you come in. Sorry if I am talking too much.”

Ellie laughed. “Well, I’m glad you’re here. What do you think of the vertical garden space? The basement gives us more flexibility.”

“Hey, Ellie, this dome is totally beautiful. Exactly what I need. Thanks,” he gushed. “This wall will be so much easier on my back. Love it.” 

Ellie showed him his quarters. “I also set up a private bedroom down here for you, when the upstairs is overrun with plants and livestock.”

“Perf, Ellie. You always were looking out for me. But I gotta know, what about the greenhouses? For five of us, we just needed a few acres, but if I am feeding hundreds, which sounds like the plan, I am going to need a lot more space.”

“Yes. I am aware. I’ve laid out plans for a greenhouse district east of here, a ten-by-ten grid of ten-acre structures. We aren’t planning to build them all right away. One at a time. But we’ll get you all the space you need.”

Pacha looked at the plans on Ellie’s slate. “That’s cool. That is space for crops, pastures for cavies, and land where I can grow moss and lichen just to build soil. Fantastic.”

That night, dinner included cavy meat. This was the first meat the miners had had since they left Earth. “Cheers, Magazine Beach\! Meat, vegetables, and bread. I haven’t eaten this well since we left Earth\!” Petey called out, to a round of cheers from the other miners. 

Pacha did a fake bow. The teams were all exhausted. After dinner, they talked a little before turning in. 

In the middle of the night, Ellie woke to the sound of water spraying onto metal. It made no sense. She hadn’t attached a water supply to the dome yet; it was completely dry. She sat up, alarmed. Then the pungent odor hit her. “Miners are going to be interesting to live with,” she thought, and went back to sleep.

A few hours later, Ellie heard the sound again. “Miners,” Ellie thought and rolled her eyes. As she listened, the footsteps came back over by where she was sleeping. “Pacha. What are you doing?” she whispered. “I expect that from the miners, but not you\!” 

“Ellie, it’s so convenient,” Pacha replied, sheepishly. “I was about to suit up when I realized they’re right.”

Ellie didn’t fight it, but when she woke the next morning, she pulled him aside. “I am not sleeping in a room that smells like an outhouse. You and I are completing the sewage system now.”

Pacha nodded without argument. They gathered their tools and began the work. Ellie showed him how to heat the glass stock and draw it over the mandrels to make the tubes they needed. Once he understood the technique, she let him handle most of the forming while she assembled the anaerobic digester, the photobioreactor where they grew algae, and the distiller that gave them back pure water. Pacha used the dry minerals left by distillation as soil amendments. The system they built was four times larger than what Ellie had built at Magazine Beach, with capacity for twenty people. With Pacha’s help, the ag dome bathroom was operational by noon.

That same morning, Angus got on their cargo trike and rode out to the southwest slope and then up to the shield. He set an anchor ten yards back from the edge and tied himself off, then set another one a yard away and laid a polymer trough over the edge. Angus looked out, almost three miles above Bounty.

Petey was watching him through binoculars. Local Band couldn’t reach that far, so they were on FM. “Angus, Petey here. We see you on the edge. Are you tied off? Over.”

Angus pulled the jacketed spectra line from the cargo bike. “Tied off and solid, Petey. The view’s a ripper. Volcanic chaos to flat sand. I’m ready to send down the cable. You lot ready? Over.”

Angus started feeding the cable down slowly, then realized he could never hold the weight. After a couple of hundred yards, it pulled out of his hands. He called out, “Cable released. It’s running\! Take cover\! Over.”

The last two hundred yards hit the talus apron hard. Petey and Luca anchored the base. Angus attached the electric seat and took the first ride down the cable. After that, they could move easily up and down the scarp. They spent most of the day at the mine site, coming back down before dinner with rock chips and core samples from the mining site. The miners were talking to each other.

“Looks like that vent shrivelled while it was still pulling out beryllium. We have both Bertrandite and Phenakite.”

“Two minerals? That’ll complicate the processing. Did ya give the gangue an assay, then?”

“Bang-on, mate. I checked that first thing. A few iron-rich bits and some feldspar. The feldspar will need to be knocked out chemically.”

“No wazza. Callum was dead set on that during the trip, wasn’t he? Reckon he’ll be wrapped.”

During dinner, Petey was talking to Tom. “Look, mate, you’ve got all this power now. I need to start making steel bits. You mind if I wind up the power? I have an FFC Cambridge refiner that can drink the whole hundred megawatts if I let her, and we need the steel.”

“Sure, knock yourself out. We aren’t doing that much right now. Just the glassworks and the Sabatier machine.”

To make a cableway, they needed metal, both for the towers and the cable. They had planned for that and set up their FFC Cambridge refiner, using a magnet to pull iron ore from the regolith. After an hour, they had a large pot of sponge iron. They alloyed in carbon from the Bosch recyclers and made simple steel. 

Ellie’s next concern was equipment maintenance. Cleaning was impossible in the wind, on the regolith that was causing trouble in the first place. Electrostatic attraction meant there was always more dust coming in. She knew they needed a spacious building to use as a garage. She planned to add more later, but the first one was built toward the cableway. No airlocks, just oversized doors in and out. It had thin walls supported by glass rebar. The garage gave them a place to work, sheltered from the sun and dust, well lit, with a clean floor and tools handy. The digger and rovers could be kept there when not in use, out of the dust and sun. She moved three sleds in, too.

With the garage in place, Ellie started defining Bounty. All traffic would go in and out through the garage. She began marking the borders of the settlement. She used a pole to draw a line around five acres. The ag dome was the center. There was also space for apartment domes, the sewer system, a metal shop, and glassworks. The ship and generator were a few hundred yards outside the border, and the Sabatier machine was kept still farther away because Ellie deemed it too dangerous. She then built a small wall along the boundary, a foot high and a couple of inches wide. Not straight, it looked like corrugated tin on edge. This made it stiffer and allowed her to save material. When the entire wall had been built, she got Tom and Pacha to help her pull out rolls of *LDPE* sheeting. They used tape from the ship to quickly assemble a bubble cover while she anchored it on the wall using fitted caps. 

When it was ready, she called everyone over to watch. “Are you guys ready to see Bounty become your home?” she asked, as she hit the air pump switch.

Slowly, the bubble inflated. No real pressure, less than a millibar, but enough to give it shape. They would still wear pressure suits inside, but there was now a space with no dust. Pacha cheered, and Tom gave a thumbs up. The miners loved their home.

Polymer accumulated slowly. Pacha understood that, but he didn’t like it. When they had enough for the base, he and Ellie went down to where the greenhouses would go. They were not as fast moving dirt as the miners, but they excavated the first greenhouse site down to bedrock. Ellie laid down an inch of foam and set the glass rebar. Pacha started the hard work of converting regolith into soil. Using the miners’ ten-ton ball mill, he added five tons of regolith and one ton of precious ice. He pumped up the pressure to three psi and began tumbling. As the ice tumbled, it melted and washed out the perchlorates and rounded the grains of regolith into something more like sand and clay. As it processed, he would regularly draw out half the water and vacuum distill it to leave the perchlorate dust behind, recycling the clean water back into the mill. When the water ran clean, he pulled out the sand and laid it in the middle of the base. The Herculean task was going to take several days, but he didn’t stop until Ellie came by and told him it was dinner time.

He and Ellie set to work preparing dinner. Jakob and Mira should have arrived by now. Radio would only give them a five or ten-minute notice of their arrival. Outside, the temperature in the covered yard was dropping to the night’s cold, but inside, the smells of the night’s meal were intoxicating. The miners had never eaten oca. Pacha was roasting it with herbs and algal oil, and it smelled intoxicating. Just before dinner began, Callum arrived with Jakob and Mira, bringing one ton of frozen food, another ton of ice, and a small pressure box. Before anyone even said hi, Mira blurted out in a singsong, mocking tone, “Hey, Pacha, you forgot one of your cavies.”
