“You know, Mateo, wth Felipe gone, I will need a partner. Since you were a boy, I always dreamed of this becoming ‘José and Son’s Acero del Pacífico.’” 

“I’ve been watching you, though, this last year, and I don’t think that really makes sense.”

“What do you mean, Papá? I’ve worked very hard. The beryllium project is going well.”

José held up his hand. “Don’t try to change my mind. I discussed it with Felipe while we were doing the pickup, and more recently, when I was buying him out. We had to refile the paperwork, anyway. Felipe recommended one man he has worked with, who has been in metals all his life and knows the deep-sea recovery business. I have talked to that man, and agree with Felipe.”

“*Si*, Papá. I trust you and Felipe.”

They had been walking as they talked and were now standing in front of the sign. The workers were bolting it in place. When José looked at the workman, he pulled the paper wrap off the sign. It read “Mateo’s Acero del Pacífico.’” 

“Happy Quinceañero, son.”

## 

## **Chapter 17: Lunar Control**

| Price of *³He* | $8,000,000/ton |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** | **2 tons per month** |
| **Current Consumption** | **8 tons/year** |
| **Time Since Start** | **4 years** |

Abundant water from Mars changed operations at Tan’s Cantina. No more gritty, chemically clingy frost scraped out of lunar shadow pockets. Mars had overcapacity, and the Moon had thirst. The pipeline was open.

Steel production, using Lunar iron and Martian carbon, expanded the settlement. The cableway grew, and new habitat shells could be assembled on site. Miners arriving from Earth brought just the core with them, and bought the steel structure in Tycho. 

Li and Darrin kept their original hut.

Martian food was the staple now. The cantina smelled like roasted garlic, with cavy and amaranth chicha the most popular menu items. Earth food seemed foreign.

People could come in at any time to get a hot meal, but they did more; people lingered. They talked and laughed. 

And Li made money. From the cantina, of course, but also because taking care of miners increased *³He* production, and she got her twenty percent.

Helion Dynamics was now serious about building the terawatt tokaplexes. Biloxi had built a large, crescent-shaped artificial island to support their construction. Raines was stockpiling every gram of *³He* he could get. When the terawatt tokaplexes came online, each one would consume five tons of helium per month. The Moon was producing two. 

Li was in the cantina when a Lunar Tug pilot radioed in. “Tan, we’ve got four cheechakos. Certified claim about a hundred miles from Shackleton. They wanted a direct drop, but I told them the nearest Odd Point Rendezvous near there is in two weeks. They don’t want to wait, and I’ve got nowhere to park them. I need to refuel at Tan’s Depot to make the hop, and that’s going to cost them. Can you work some of your magic on them?”

Li smiled. “Tell them that while you are refueling, they can come over to Tan’s Cantina, meet some fellow miners, and get a free chicha.” 

Six hours later, the cheechakos arrived. Li didn’t say anything, but the bartender slid them some chichas, and they began talking. One of the miners took them to see the big map on the back wall of the cantina showing active sites, mined sites, and cableways. Li was strategically seated nearby. They were shocked to see their site had already been mined. Li stepped up. One of the miners introduced the cheechakos to her. “Tan, these guys just came up. Still drinking their free chicha. Looks like their claim is down south, and has already been mined.”

Li shook their hands. “You know, Darrin just put in a spur on the southern line, if you like that latitude. Makes dropoff easier, if you want us to process your regolith.”

“What do you mean? We have an RF extractor. We can process it ourselves.”

“You can. That’s up to you. Do you want to show me your gear?”

They all put their pressure suits back on and left the cantina. Li showed them how to clip their suits onto the cableway, and they rode back to the depot. 

She made comments as she looked over their gear. “That RF extractor is pretty small. I have some larger units for sale. Of course, you’d want more solar power, too. How are you fixed for extra pressure suits? Do you all plan to live in that one hut? I have larger ones, if you’d like.”

Li was thorough, and they appreciated her attention to detail. They replaced some of their equipment and left some with Li. After a tour of the *³He* extraction facilities, they agreed to let her process their regolith. Her twenty percent cut seemed cheap compared to the cost of learning how to operate an RF extractor efficiently. Finally, they agreed to work the location she asked them to mine, as long as Darrin would show them where it was.

They called back to the pilot. “You’ll never believe what happened while you were getting ready for the hop.”

The pilot suppressed a laugh. “What happened?”

“We were in the bar, and we met a character. She helped us out and set us up with a better location. So I guess we won’t be needing the hop.”

“Was her name Tan, by any chance?”

“It was. Have you heard of her?” 

“She does alright by the people nearby. Well, since you don’t need the hop, I am going to get some *³He*. Good mining.”

Li was using a hundred-kilogram cryo-cylinders filled with 99.9% pure *³He*. Helion Dynamics paid a premium for those shipments. They were reliably pure, and the large containers made for easier logistics. The pilot loaded three and headed back to LEO.

Tan was back in the cantina, adding the cheechako’s new claim to the map when Andrei called. “Tan, I just got a request. A man is looking to go to visit the Moon. He needs a hotel. I told him I would ask around. Do you have space?”

“Sure, we have space, if the price is right. Did you get a sense of how much I can charge this guy?”

“I don’t know. He sounds like a mid-level bureaucrat, but he works for the UN. Name is Tadeusz Vassiliev. Didn’t say why he was going, but if it is official, you can probably charge more. I should have thought of that. I just blurted out my rate.”

“You charge dollars. I charge the miners a gram per night, but this guy doesn’t have *³He*. Float $20,000 per night and see what he says. Mention it comes with free oxygen and water.”

“What will he eat?”

Tan had to think. She was charging so much for meals here, no Earther could afford it, but if she cut him a deal, others would expect it. “Suggest he bring his own meals.”

That evening, Li talked it over with Darrin. He was curious. “Why would the UN be sending someone up here?”

Li looked around. The big map. The Martian food. Employees. None of this was actually authorized. It just all kind of came about after they got the reactor. Li froze. “Darrin, he’s an inspector. That is the only reason the UN would send someone to the Moon.”

“What would he inspect up here?”

“The UN still thinks they have jurisdiction up here. And they damn sure won’t like the reactor. That reactor is why we have success here.”

“What’s the play, Li?”

“We have a week before he arrives. When he gets here, Tycho will be a ghost town. Nothing interesting to report.”

Darrin looked at the cableway. “I don’t know how we can hide that.” 

Li called a meeting in the cantina. She and Darrin explained the problem to Pavel, Luke, and Tseng.

Pavel spoke first. “Do we know that he is coming to look for a nuclear reactor?”

“No. We know a man is coming to the Moon and works for the UN. But what else would he be doing here?”

Pavel nodded. Li was right. “OK, if he is looking for nuclear power, he is going to bring radiation monitors: scintillators, neutron detectors, Geiger counters. I will shut down the reactor, but that won’t be enough.”

Luke jumped in. “Tan, when you shipped the reactor, you logged it as a double RF, right?”

“Yeah. That was Belov’s idea.”

“Well, if they are looking for a double RF, we give them one. A large sheetmetal structure with two doors and two compartments. I’ll bury a bottle of *⁴He* under the device. Put in tailings, send a puff of helium out into a collector.”

Darrin was looking at the equipment connected to the buried hot loop. “He will see everything else. I don’t see how this can help.”

Tseng nodded. “He won’t see anything. We will bury it all, set up Luke’s show closer to Tan’s Cantina. I will smooth it so it looks like none of us have ever been near the north wall. The tailing pile has more than enough regolith to do that.

Pavel liked the plan. “I am going to need to scrub for polonium. Increase the bubble rate of helium and slow the reaction. When I shut it down, we will switch out the cold trap. It will still be detectable if he knows where it is, but at a distance, it will be invisible.”

“I like the plan. If there is anything you want from Earth, you need to order it in the next two days, or it won’t get here before our visitor,” Li said. “Darrin has the accounts, so let him make the actual orders.” 

With that, the meeting ended.

Tseng started bagging up tailings and moving them to a minor, secondary crater twenty miles east. Darrin reprogrammed the cableway to drop real bags in a crater just west of Tycho. 

Luke set up the fake RF near a cableway pylon north of Li and Darrin’s hut. It measured two meters by two meters by five meters, built from heavy eighth-inch steel. Hidden in the frame was a small tube, connecting a buried bottle of *⁴He* to an outlet nozzle. A tiny battery powered a few status lights. It looked convincing to anyone completely unfamiliar with gas extraction.

Tseng inspected the door. “Luke, this doesn’t close properly.”

“It latches. What’s the problem?”

“It doesn’t seal.”

“It’s a prop. No need to seal. Just look good.”

“Doesn’t look good to me.”

“Let’s hope Vassiliev is less discerning than you.”

Luke practiced loading, collecting, and unloading until it looked natural. He kept it in for a minute, then hit the helium button. 

Li watched. “Ten seconds is enough. If it’s longer, he’ll start thinking. We don’t want that. Unload before the tension is drained. This is theater, not extraction.”

Luke adjusted his performance.

Two days before Vassiliev arrived, Li sent out the news that Tan’s Cantina would hold a one-day-only sale: half-priced meals and free chicha. Every miner dropped whatever he was doing to attend the blowout.

While they ate, Li briefed the miners. “If you see the inspector, talk slowly and move slowly. Stop by the cantina, but bring your own food. I will dispense water and oxygen. While he’s here, nothing else will be served.”

When everyone had gone home, Li vented the Cantina and removed the airlock. Looking around, she noticed the map. She pulled it off the wall and rolled it tightly, bringing it back to her hut. 

Pavel scrubbed and shut down the reactor. Darrin and Tseng disconnected the ovens and the turbine and moved them out of the crater. They then buried the entire site in a meter of regolith. As a last measure, Darrin turned down the speed on the cableway to about a mile per hour.

Li greeted Envoy Tadeusz Vassiliev in Tan’s Depot while the pilot was unloading a pallet of meal kits. 

“You’re Tan?” he asked.

“Yes,” she said. That was her first lie. “I can show you to your room. I will have your luggage brought over. Do you need a guide?”

“No, thank you. I’d just like to look around, get a feel for the place.”

Vassiliev opened a case. Inside, Li noticed a Geiger counter, a scintillator, and a thermal scanner. She didn’t let on that the last one surprised her. “Very well. If you’d like to meet up later, your room has a phone.”

They put on their suits, and Li brought him to the cableway. “You can clip in and go wherever you want. Don’t go too far or you can run out of oxygen.” Not exactly a lie. She just hadn’t told him other places he could get oxygen, or how long his suit would last.

As soon as Vassiliev was out of sight, Li called up Pavel. “Pavel, good and bad. No neutron detector, but he has a thermal scanner. Are we in trouble?”

“A thermal scanner? Yeah, those pipes were still hot when we buried them. He won’t see anything under the sunlight, but he might during lunar night. Do you know how long he is staying?”

“He didn’t say. I asked for a week’s pay upfront, and he didn’t complain.”

That evening, Li returned to the depressurized cantina. Vassiliev came by while she was there.

“I saw your large RF extractor,” he said. “It’s just a double?”

“Oversized double core. For regolith processing. Much faster than processing out in the fields. Miners send their stuff here to save electricity.” That last part was totally true.

“I noticed some regolith falling out while Luke was running it. I thought it was leaking. But, in the end, Luke collected two grams of helium. It must have been cruft from loading. Where does the power come from?”

She looked up on the North rim of the crater. “Have you seen the solar panels on the rim? They get light even when the crater floor is dark.” True, she mused, for about ten hours a month.

“I’d like to see outside the crater.”

“Of course. Would you like someone to guide you?”

“I’d like to move around on my own.”

“The cableway is designed for just that. How much oxygen will you be bringing?”

“I’m not sure.” He turned without a word and left.

In the morning, she saw him with a K-bottle of oxygen, heading north to the rim. She watched him get off the cableway and check the solar panels. She was glad they never disconnected them. 

Li nudged Darrin. They watched him with binoculars. 

“Li, what is he doing up there?”

“He wanted to see where our power came from. I pointed out the panels.”

Darrin called Pavel, who was in his hut. “How far is the polonium waste tube from the panels?”

“About ten yards. Why?”

“Look at the rim.”

Pavel suited up and came out on the crater floor, watching with binoculars. Vassiliev wasn’t just checking the panels. He had his Geiger counter out. Then he stopped and knelt down. 

“Oh, fuck\! He found something\!” Pavel shouted into the team channel.

“That’s in the middle of the panels. Nothing there, right?” Li asked.

“That’s not where the waste pipe is, but that isn’t how a man acts when he finds nothing. Maybe a drop of polonium fell out there?”

Vassiliev pulled out a trowel and began digging around. They watched him for twenty minutes. Finally, he put a sample in a bag and got back on the cable to continue north. 

Darrin spoke first. “He doesn’t know what it is. There are other sources of radioactivity on the Moon.”

Li shook her head. “He doesn’t know. As far as he is concerned, you’re right. But he bagged it. It is going back to Earth, and they will find out.”

Pavel closed his eyes.

Li came back on the team channel. “Forget it. It is out of our hands now. Let’s make sure nothing else happens.”

She thought of other ways this could go wrong. A gabby miner might say too much, but anything contradicting Vassiliev’s own observations would sound like the ravings of a madman. Whatever was said, she might be able to talk her way out of. 

If, however, he died out there, that would bring enormous attention. Not another inspector, but a battalion with the authority to dig, seize, and incarcerate. That could not happen. She had to keep him safe.

It was time for Rook. Li sent Darrin over the east rim to find him. Rook was on his claim, bagging regolith, when Darrin arrived. For a hundred grams, he dropped what he was doing and followed Darrin straight back. They met Li in the cantina.

Li was pacing. “Sam, I know you don’t like to talk. That’s fine. Lean into that. But I need you to meet this guy. He is carrying around a K-bottle.”

“A K-bottle? Why?”

“I might have suggested it would be safer to have oxygen on hand.”

Sam slapped his thigh. “Cheechakos are the best. No extra battery, but a bottle of oxygen.”

“Yes, well, I need you to accidentally bump into him. Ask if he wants help carrying the bottle. Never act like it is weird. Maybe even make a show of refilling it.”

“Sure. What should I say to him?”

“As little as possible. Move slow, talk slow, don’t know.”

Sam grinned. “That’s me.”

“One more thing. Can you bring this oxygen K-bottle with you? Make it look normal to carry a bottle around.”

Sam laughed. “Anything special I should do?”

“Just keep him alive.”

Sam agreed. Outfitted with the K-bottle, he headed north.

After an hour, Rook was ready to backtrack. He started mapping out different routes Vassiliev could have taken. Before he got off, he saw an odd sight: a miner was sitting on a rover, chatting with a man with a K-bottle. 

“This is the talky part,” Rook thought to himself with a grimace. He got off the cable and started walking over. When he got in Local Band range, he stopped and listened.

Vassiliev was trying to figure out who was in charge. “How much regolith do you process in a day?”

The man blinked slowly, checked his bag weight. “Whatever we collect.”

“And your quota?”

“We don’t run quotas. I process my claim at my own rate. I take my own time to get my ton.”

“Your ton?”

“Whatever the claim has. Usually around a ton.”

Vassiliev had seen others, moving slowly, like they had all the time in the universe. 

Sam decided to come over. “Hi, guys. I think I went the wrong way on the cableway. Can you help me out? I need to refill my K-bottle.”

The miner switched to FM, leaving Vassiliev out of the conversation. “What is with these K-bottles?”

“Makes our guy easy to spot.”

“Dumbest thing I ever saw.”

They switched back to Local Band. “Two strangers in one day. I can refill both your bottles.”

Vassiliev’s eyes perked. “Thank you. I don’t know how much I have left.”

When the miner took their bottles inside, Rook plugged into the rover and signalled Vassiliev. “Hey, buddy, recharge. You don’t want to freeze out here.” He took a cable from the rover and plugged it into Vassiliev’s hip pack. “Your charge is low. How long have you lived up here?”

Vassiliev wasn’t sure what to say. “I am on vacation from Earth, and was curious to see the Moon.”

Sam shut off his mike while he laughed. Vassiliev continued, unaware. “Look, if you have some time, what would you say to helping me? The Moon has dangers I am not prepared for. Tan offered a guide, but I don’t trust her. I want someone she doesn’t know about. I can pay.”

“What’s it worth?” Rook asked, barely looking up.

“How does five thousand dollars sound?”

Sam smirked. It sounds like a quarter of a chicha. “That would be fine. My name is Rook. Where we going?”

The miner brought back the full K-bottles. 

Vassiliev grabbed his like it was a life preserver. “Thank you for the conversation and for the oxygen. I’ve taken enough of your time; we’ll be on our way.”

The miner looked surprised. “You two together? I didn’t realize that.”

“I just hired him,” Vassiliev explained.

Sam kept track of where they were and made sure Vassiliev kept his battery charged. They moved around under the cableway and even past it.

Vassiliev watched miners from behind his visor. Watched a man bag maybe ten kilos in half an hour. No music. No jokes. No camaraderie.

They looked like ghosts.

“I expected… more life,” Vassiliev said.

Sam looked at him. “They’re mining.”

Sam pointed at the sun. “If you don’t want to get caught out here in the cold, I suggest we head back to Tycho Crater by sunset.”

“Sure. Nothing to see out here, anyway.” 

The ride back to Tycho Crater was long and quiet. No conversation, no confrontation, no evidence. When night fell, Vassiliev took out his thermal imager and started looking around. 

When they came over the ridge, he imaged the entire crater. Li’s hut door and the tube for her Earth window stood out, and the area near the RF extractor. Tan’s Cantina glowed faintly. Some of the regolith in the waste pile was hot.

He dropped by the Cantina to talk to Li. “I took a thermal image of this crater as we came back. The only signature I saw was the things you showed me and some of the regolith on the tailings pile.”

“Probably something fresh out of the RF extractor.”

“Probably. Well, I’m checking out. Lunar Tug will be picking me up here tomorrow.”

“Luke and Darrin are in Luke’s hut, if you want to talk. I heard Luke has some beer, if you’re interested.”

“You know, if you can keep it quiet, a beer might be very nice right now.”

Li escorted him down to Luke’s hut. They came through the airlock and hung up their pressure suits on the wall. As promised, Luke had a six-pack of Earth beer. Pavel was there, too. They chatted for about half an hour. Pavel remembered he had some frozen Earth bread and excused himself to go to his hut. He was back in about ten minutes, and they microwaved it and enjoyed the food. When Luke mentioned he was tired, they all suited back up and left.

Li and Darrin helped Vassiliev get his gear back to the depot. While on Lunar Tug, he called his supervisor. “Tim, this place is like a graveyard. They use solar power, so they just sleep for two weeks at a time, hoping their batteries don’t die and they don’t run out of oxygen. We were looking for black markets? The people are all loners, barely able to talk to others. They couldn’t begin to start a black market. Honestly, it would be better if they did.”

“You didn’t find a reactor?”

“No, they are using solar panels. I verified the connections. Luke is very fast and efficient with the RF extractor. I came over the rim during lunar night and ran a thermal scan on the entire crater. If there was a reactor, even a buried one, it would have shown. There was nothing.”

“No radiation?”

“Yeah, well, yes, I did find some radiation. I searched the area and didn’t find any more, but I bagged what I found. I am curious to see what it is.”

“Get that to the lab. They’ll figure it out. Who is running the show up there?”

“Darrin. He lets Tan do all the legwork, but it is clear that Darrin is the one who is running things. Tan just sits in the cantina all day. She offered me algae soup in a squeeze bag so I could drink it suited. It was disgusting, but she loved it.”

He watched through the porthole as Tan’s Depot shrank in the distance.

“Look, no reactor. No illegal food service. No commerce that I can see. Honestly, Tim, I think we’re worried about nothing. These people are too dead inside to cause trouble. And I mean that literally. This place creeps me the hell out. Do not send me back here.”

Li watched the Lunar Tug shrink to a glint and vanish in the black sky. 

Darrin came in and found her. “Li, the Lunar Tug pilot just sent me a recording of a call the inspector placed. He thinks I’m in charge.” 

“Good. If they do come back, they won’t arrest me. Now, let’s get back to business. Turn it back on. All of it.”

Darrin didn’t hesitate. “With pleasure.”

Tseng and Luke dug out the reactor, and Pavel began the slow process of bringing it back online. Once there was power, the cableways sped back up to normal speeds, pulling in bags of regolith. Miners could see the motion from their huts and clipped themselves to the lines, gliding in.

Li reinstalled the airlock on the cantina and started repressurizing. 

In about ten minutes, the pressure was enough that they could start the music. She played it loud and pumping. The staff flipped the tables upright and set up stools and chairs.

Li grinned. “All drinks are half price. Anyone who can’t get a room is welcome to sleep in the cantina tonight.”

Miners were coming in fast, leaving their pressure suits hanging on the wall. The bartender cracked open a jug of chicha. The earthy smell filled the cantina as he started pouring mug after mug of the cloudy amber. 

Li and Darrin stood by the wall, watching the festivities. Luke, Tseng, and Rook showed up. The five of them pushed two tables together and started talking. About fifteen minutes later, they were joined by Pavel.

Sam was talking about the ride back. “It was night, and Vassiliev pulled out a thermal imager. Fine, nothing to see out there, but when we came over the rim, I totally knew the reactor was going to glow like midday. But nothing. Were the pipes really cold?”

Pavel laughed. “When Li told me he had a thermal imager, I knew exactly what our problem would be. I can’t let the pipes freeze. Electrical power was keeping the metal coolant molten. So, while he was gone, we scraped six inches of regolith and replaced it. We dumped all that hot regolith on the tailings pile.”

Sam slammed his chicha down. “Hah\! That’s why the pile was hot. I wondered how a fake RF heated the regolith.”

Luke was next. “How was the Earth beer? I had to go with that, you know. Vassiliev isn’t ready for chicha. Pavel, you were smooth.”

“Who? Me? All I did was share some bread. And accidentally slipped a sample bag out of Vassiliev’s suit when I was putting mine on.”

“What did you do with it?” Darrin asked.

“I dumped it near the wall and refilled it with some normal regolith. Looks the same.”

The party continued for another six hours. Finally, Li and Darrin went to bed, leaving the bartender in charge.

Li looked up through the Earth window. “Productivity has made us friends and earned us enemies. Our allies will not always protect us.”

# Part II  Jovian Resource Corporation

## 

## **Chapter 18: The Charter**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |

| **Time Since Start** |  |

Restrepo arrived early. The Kuwaiti delegation had called the meeting, but arriving first meant watching the others enter, reading their faces before the diplomacy locked into place. He chose a seat near the window where he could see both the room and the city beyond.

New York stretched outward in its familiar grid, financial towers pressed against older districts, everything held in place by quiet authority. For a century, oil had been so organized: predictable, distributed, controlled. Now *³He* threatened to redraw the entire map. And Venezuela, already bleeding, would be the first to die.

The others arrived in silence. Al-Rashid from Kuwait took the head of the table with the easy confidence of someone who’d orchestrated a hundred meetings like this. Morrison, the Canadian delegate, looked tired, his jaw set. Okonkwo from Nigeria carried himself with the alertness of someone determined not to be sidelined. Dr. Lais, representing Indonesia, moved with careful precision, her expression giving nothing away.

Al-Rashid opened his slate. “Thank you for coming. The numbers have worsened. Fusion uptake is accelerating faster than our models predicted.”

Restrepo didn’t wait for the polite circling. “We are running out of time. Venezuela cannot survive another decade of this. Oil revenue is the only thing between us and complete collapse.” His accent felt thicker when emotion crept in. He forced himself to slow down. “Fusion is extinction for my country.”

“Nigeria faces the same reality,” Okonkwo said, leaning forward. “We are funding infrastructure, education, healthcare. All of it depends on petroleum revenue. We will not be pushed aside while others decide our future.”

Morrison’s voice carried a bitterness Restrepo recognized. “Alberta’s been keeping Canada solvent for years. Now Ottawa vilifies us for it while spending every dollar we send. Fusion gives them an excuse to turn off the taps completely.”

At least Morrison’s resentment came from being unappreciated. Restrepo’s came from watching his country starve.

Restrepo pulled up his own slate. “I’ve been looking at the output numbers from the Moon. They don’t make sense. I know they’re using unlicensed nuclear power up there. What did the inspector find?”

Al-Rashid winced. “Vassiliev found a radioactive sample, but nothing else. I’ve talked to him extensively. When we gave the sample to the labs, they found ordinary ilmenite. The lab says it must have been cosmic radiation. Based on what he found, I’m surprised the Moon is delivering as much *³He* as it is. We thought Tan had a nuclear plant, but he checked it personally. Solar arrays on the ridge of her crater and a very large dual RF processor and absolutely no heat signature anywhere else.”

Morrison shook his head. “No way. They can’t deliver those numbers without nuclear power. He didn’t look hard enough.”

“I share your frustration.” Al-Rashid’s tone carried the weight of someone who’d already fought this battle. “But Vassiliev is a good inspector. There may be a nuclear plant up there somewhere, but we need to accept we cannot find it. And even if we did, our options are limited. Besides, since we authorized General Atomics to build nuclear ships, Mars and Ceres just park the ships and use the reactors for power. All legal.”

Morrison looked up. “Then we hit the supply chains. Can’t touch the reactors themselves, but we can restrict what feeds them. Lunar operations depend on regular fissile shipments for auxiliary power. Mars and Ceres too. Slow those shipments, production tapers.”

Okonkwo’s arms crossed. “How?”

“Environmental safeguards,” Morrison said. “Several delegations have already raised concerns about moving enriched materials through Earth’s atmosphere. The language is vague enough to expand.”

Restrepo saw it immediately. The logic was elegant, almost beautiful. They couldn’t stop fusion, but they could starve the mechanism feeding it. “Frame it as international responsibility. A phased restriction tied to environmental review. No one will understand the impact. It’ll pass overwhelmingly.”

“Perfect,” Al-Rashid said. “Most delegates won’t consider the implications for lunar production. It looks prudent. Responsible.”

Al-Rashid’s expression darkened. “Next topic. There’s an urgent development. Raines is pushing a Jovian Resource Corporation. He claims the Moon cannot supply enough *³He* to meet global demand. He wants the UN to charter a corporation to unlock unlimited fusion fuel from Jupiter.”

Restrepo felt his stomach drop. “The vote?”

“Will pass easily,” Al-Rashid said flatly. “Raines has built a coalition we cannot break. Too many nations see it as their path to cheap, unlimited energy. We cannot stop the charter.”

Morrison shook his head slowly. “If Jupiter opens up, lunar production becomes irrelevant. Everything we’re doing won’t matter. We’ll be finished.”

Restrepo looked at the city beyond the glass and felt the old helplessness rising. Venezuela had been outmaneuvered for decades. OPEC had done it. The Americans had done it. Their own incompetence had done it. Now fusion was finishing what sanctions never quite managed. And Jupiter would be the final nail.

Lais looked up from her slate. “I think Jupiter may be an impossible problem. This corporation won’t matter at all. Let the baby have his bottle.”

Okonkwo’s face darkened. “I was sold that promise before Ilha Fusão. Then they produced steady fusion. I was told they would never fuel it, but now they’re mining the Moon. These people will find a way. They are very inventive, and we cannot tolerate that risk.”

“Influence the board,” Lais said, her earlier skepticism replaced by calculation. “Fill it with the right kind of people. Treaty specialists. Governance experts.”

“People who move slowly,” Morrison added.

Restrepo nodded. “People who look ideal for a new space institution. Impartial. Scrupulous. They conduct studies, hold consultations, write reports. Progress looks like it’s happening, but the corporation never advances beyond planning.”

Okonkwo uncrossed his arms. “And if someone notices?”

“They won’t,” Al-Rashid said. “It will be interpreted as best practice. No one objects to caution in space.”

The room settled into understanding. Restrepo felt the weight of what they were doing. They were buying time for their economies. They were also sabotaging humanity’s expansion into space. He pushed the thought away. Venezuela was dying *now*. Whatever Jupiter might offer humanity could wait.

“We coordinate quietly this week,” Al-Rashid said. “The fissile resolution needs the right sponsors. The board nominations need discreet support. Nothing overt.”

They agreed without ceremony.

Restrepo stood, closing his slate. “My country is dying, and we are trying to buy enough time to find another way to survive. I hope you all understand what is at stake.”

“We do,” Al-Rashid said quietly.

A week later, the General Assembly chamber hummed with anticipation. Restrepo took his seat in the Venezuelan section, several rows behind the Helion delegation where Raines sat with his aides. The American looked confident, relaxed. He had no idea.

The fissile ban resolution appeared on the docket. Restrepo kept his expression neutral as the reading began, though his heart rate picked up. Everything depended on the sponsors holding, on the environmental language resonating, on dozens of delegations voting without thinking through the implications.

The electronic board flickered green and red.

The resolution passed. Overwhelmingly.

*Coño.* Relief and guilt hit simultaneously. The ban was binding. Lunar reactors, Martian grids, and Cerean foundries would all feel it within years. A decade at most. The installations would wither slowly, starved of fuel they couldn’t acknowledge needing.

And hunger for energy would not go away. Venezuela would sell oil again. 

Across the chamber, Raines rose to speak. Restrepo watched the man’s confident cadence, the rehearsed gestures, every appeal to global prosperity. The Jovian Resource Corporation speech was polished, persuasive. Raines believed every word.

The vote was called. Overwhelming approval.

Raines sat to polite applause, relief visible in his shoulders. The man thought he’d won.

Then the board appointments were read aloud.

Dr. Emile Carson, the author of the seven-volume treatise on the Outer Space Treaty.

Ambassador Chidinma Okafor, the steward of treaty review committees.

Professor Liu Wei, a sustainable development theorist, cited in every UN resolution, but never put into practice.

Restrepo watched Raines’ face. The smallest flicker of confusion. A tightening around the eyes. The man was beginning to understand that something had gone wrong.

Delegates swarmed Raines afterward, offering congratulations. Restrepo remained in his seat, gathering his materials slowly. He felt no triumph, only a hollow kind of satisfaction. 

He stood and made his way toward the exit. As he passed the Helion section, he caught a glimpse of Raines accepting praise with a strained smile. The man understood now. The fissile ban would strangle the outposts. The Jovian Resource Corporation, his great victory, had been hollowed out before it began.

Outside, the New York evening pressed against the glass facade. Restrepo walked toward his hotel, phone already buzzing with messages from Caracas.

He had done what he could. The fissile materials ban would slowly strangle the *³He* supply, and Jupiter was decades away, if it ever happened. Venezuela was back. Maybe they would even find another path forward, diversify, prepare for a future that no longer needed its oil.

He told himself it was enough. This time, he believed it.

## **Chapter 19: Twinkle, Twinkle, Little Star**

| Price of *³He* |  |

Max Juno was the first to hear about it. Sitting in his Lagos office in the newly completed tower, he was on a routine call with a contact at Shibata Capital in Tokyo. They drifted through the usual topics: metals contracts, new alloys, logistics, until a small aside broke the rhythm.

“There’s a reactor program. Kagutsuchi Vortex. Early runs show stability at two, maybe three gigawatts. Machine about the size of a city bus. They claim it is very stable.”

Max noted the words, asked no follow-up, and let the talk slide back into routine. Only after the call ended did he allow the weight of it to settle. Small fusion, if real, could be very disruptive of the fusion industry Helion Dynamics was building.

He composed a message to Tobias Raines:

 “From a source at Shibata Capital. New reactor: Kagutsuchi Vortex. Two to three gigawatts. 20m footprint. Early data promising. Sharing because you should know.”

He hesitated over the last sentence, aware it revealed more than he liked. After all these years he still did not know whether Raines’ driving aim was to remain the richest man alive or to see fusion carried into every corner of civilization. 

In Geneva, Raines read the note at his desk overlooking Lake Geneva. He pressed a button and gave three instructions without pause.

 “Get Max Juno on the line. Clear my schedule. Ready the jet for Tokyo.”

Max Juno was still in his office when Raines’ call came through. He excused himself smoothly and answered the call.

“Hello, this is Max speaking.”

“Max, how soon can you get to the airport? A jet is waiting for you there in Lagos. We’re meeting them in person.”

Max hesitated, just for a moment. “You’re sure?”

“I’m sure,” Raines said, and in that certainty there was something larger than business. something closer to faith. “You found this. You belong in the room.”

The line went dead, leaving Max staring at the polished surface of the conference table, his own reflection looking back at him, eyes sharpened by the weight of what was coming.

Max was already in a limo when he called his Shibata Capital liaison. “Secure a slot at your headquarters. Tonight. Raines is coming personally. He wants Kagutsuchi Vortex present; CEO and chief engineer, no substitutions.”

The liaison swallowed hard, bowed reflexively though it was over the phone, and set to work.

Raines next called Dr. Elin Sarai. “Elin, listen, I am going to need your technical expertise. Have you heard of Max Juno?”

“Max Juno. Maybe. Is he the guy who financed the Mars operation?”

“The same. Smart man. You’ll like him. He and I will meet you in Biloxi. We’re going to Japan.”

They converged in Biloxi, where Sarai’s manufacturing plant dominated the Back Bay shoreline. She was already waiting on the tarmac, helmet under her arm, coveralls marked with graphite dust from the shop floor.

Raines stepped down first. She studied his expression before speaking.

 “Tobias, what is it?”

“Another fusion plant,” he said simply. “Smaller than ours. If the reports are true, it’s quite amazing. We are going to visit the company that made it.”

She had brought her crash bag, the things she needed if she was suddenly called away on a business trip. Together they boarded the jet bound for Tokyo. Max filled in the details he had. 

In her excitement, Elin’s words spilled out fast. “They have a self sustaining stellarator? That’s amazing. We must see it.” As the flight continued, Elin gave an impromptu lecture on the technology, bringing Max and Raines up to speed on what to look for when they arrived. “Stellarators are generally more stable than a tokamak, but none have been able to generate the confinement needed to sustain fusion.”

“We sell tokaplexes, not tokamaks. Why did you say ‘tokamak’?” Raines asked.

“The fusion core itself is a tokamak. It is also fairly small, though larger than the Kagutsuchi Vortex reactor. The process of using that and keeping it fed and stable and extracting electricity from it requires many more buildings and structures. That complex of buildings is our tokaplex. It is the turbines, cooling towers, emergency systems, and controls that take up so many square miles, not the reactor. 

Max sat back, unsettled, staring at the darkening horizon. He had delivered the message because Raines deserved to know. Max had made his first fortune selling lunar helium to Helion Dynamics. Raines had backed Magazine Beach when no one else would. The loyalty ran deep. But even now, uncertainty gnawed at Max. Would Raines see the Kagutsuchi Vortex as . threat or an opportunity? Would he crush them, absorb them, or enable them?

At 35,000 feet, Max spoke briefly with Shibata Capital’s liaison, confirming the attendance of Kagutsuchi Vortex’s two senior figures: Kaito Ishikawa, the reserved CEO, and Dr. Naomi Fujimoto, whose early work on vortex confinement had become a foundation for the field.

“They are honored to meet with you,” the liaison said with practiced formality. “And eager.”

“Good,” Max replied, ending the call without ceremony.

The Gulfstream touched down at Haneda under a velvet blue sky. A black sedan waited, its driver and attendant already in position. Elin stepped out after Raines, a small carrycase in her hand, posture sharp but restrained. She glanced at Max, as though reading uncertain data, withholding judgment.

Raines turned to Max, understanding his uncertainty. “Max, you did the right thing. If this is real, it changes everything. If it isn’t, we lose a day in Tokyo.”

The car carried them through Tokyo’s arteries of neon and glass before arriving at Kagutsuchi Vortex headquarters, a low, modernist complex on the edge of the bay. Security was discreet but thorough. Waiting at the entrance was Nakamura, the director from Shibata Capital. His bow was precise, his English polished.

“Welcome. Ishikawa-san and Dr. Fujimoto are inside.”

Nakamura led them into a large hall where the reactor dominated the space, coils gleaming under hard white light. Around it, consoles and scaffolding suggested both research and ongoing construction. By the machine stood two figures waiting for them.

Kaito Ishikawa, tall and angular, carried a stillness that suggested calculation as much as patience. His suit was flawless, his hands folded behind him.

Beside Ishikawa stood Dr. Naomi Fujimoto, slight and sharp-eyed, a slate in her hand. Her posture was alert rather than ceremonial, the stance of someone who lived between theory and machine. Her eyes were already on Elin, measuring.

As Raines, Elin, and Max entered, Ishikawa bowed lightly.

 “Welcome to Tokyo,” he said, voice calm and deliberate. “We have much to show you.”

Raines returned the bow, not as deeply, then gestured toward the machine dominating the hall.

 “Let’s see what you have built.”

They moved closer. The stellarator loomed, coils curving in elaborate arcs, a twisted cage of superconducting fields surrounding the core chamber. Technicians stood back as Fujimoto touched a console.

“The central challenge, as you know, is maintaining density and temperature while keeping the plasma stable. We have achieved this with a field geometry of our own design.”

Lights across the console shifted. Nothing changed in the room. No noise, no light, no vibration. Inside the stellarator, the plasma kindled, a deep blue glow held in its magnetic cage. The confinement lines shimmered faintly against the chamber walls. Elin watched with fascination as the needles on the reaction gauges moved up and remained steady.

“It is stable,” Fujimoto said. “We have sustained uninterrupted operation for seventy hours.”

Max raised an eyebrow at the figure. Raines showed no outward change, but his attention fixed.

“There is some waste,” Fujimoto continued. “Deuterium and *³He* near the boundary must be vented to maintain stability. We replenish continuously. The venting also removes *⁴He* ash.”

Elin leaned in slightly, studying the graphs that appeared on the console. As it continued, the outer shell of the stellarator began to glow dull red from residual heat.

“Our present limitation is not fusion,” Fujimoto said, her tone matter-of-fact. “It is recovery. Our steam cycle cannot absorb the generated energy. Turbines saturate, and over ninety percent of output is discarded.”

Elin spoke without hesitation.

 “Mechanical bottleneck. Not thermonuclear.”

“Correct,” Fujimoto answered, her eyes sharpening in acknowledgment.

While Ishikawa and Raines spoke quietly at the main table, Elin and Dr. Naomi Fujimoto stepped aside. The hum of the reactor hall softened behind them. A small cocktail table stood between them, forgotten by the others.

“Your machine is beautiful,” Elin said, her tone precise but sincere. “Can you make it larger?”

“There is a limitation in size,” Fujimoto explained. “At larger sizes, the field cannot contain the plasma once fusion begins. At smaller sizes, edge effects dominate, and ash sweeping removes too much reactant. We found this sweet spot at two gigawatts thermal.” 

“How do you keep heat from destroying your magnets?” Elin asked.

Fujimoto gave a small smile. “This project is less about fusion and more about materials and heat exchange. Ishikawa sees the small footprint and calls it revolutionary. He compares it to your facilities without realizing most of your acreage is turbines, cooling, and power distribution.” She paused, then continued in a quieter voice. “The real advances are in the walls. We use silicon carbide for both the first wall and the containment shell. Then a spiral of magnesium oxide, so helium can enter cold, absorb heat as it winds through, and exit hot enough for generation. We’ve reached outlet temperatures over twenty-five hundred degrees Celsius. Different ceramics might allow us to boost that temperature, if needed.”

Elin’s eyes sharpened at the detail. “That’s impressive. You know, electricity isn’t always the primary use-case. Offworld, they need steady process heat for smelting. Something like this could serve directly, without turbines at all.”

For a moment both women stood silent, each considering how the other’s work reframed her own. When they looked up again, it was with the recognition that they had learned something neither had walked in knowing.

Elin nodded. “What you have built here is phenomenal.”

Elin talked to Raines briefly afterwards, explaining her thoughts. There was a pause, a silence where fortunes are decided. Raines nodded once to Elin, then broke the silence, his voice deliberate, cutting cleanly across the room.

 “How scalable is production?”

Ishikawa lifted a hand. “I regret to say that development has exhausted our capital. We have no manufacturing facility. Even if we did, each reactor requires months of precision assembly. Hundreds would be impossible. We could perhaps produce a handful, each essentially handmade.”

Raines did not look away from Ishikawa.

 “You’re telling me you can’t produce a hundred. Fine. Can you produce ten, and can you commit to pushing output as far as you can take it?”

Ishikawa’s reply came without hesitation. “Yes. We would love to. As much as we can, as fast as we can, but we have no manufacturing facilities.”

“Then I have a proposal,” Raines said. “I’ll build you a facility in Gulfport, not far from Elin’s base in Biloxi. Helion Dynamics has infrastructure there already. And when you start producing, I will buy everything you can make.”

The room absorbed his words. For a moment, no one spoke. Then Nakamura exhaled slowly, the relief unmistakable. “He means to build with us,” he murmured to himself. “Not against us.”

Raines continued, “The Kagutsuchi Vortex will have many uses on Earth, particularly in remote areas, and we will help you find those markets. But right now the UN is blocking the export of fissile materials to space. The claim is safety, but the real goal is to stop fusion power. The reactors currently powering operations in space won’t need refueling for years, maybe a decade if they’re careful.”

Ishikawa’s eyes narrowed slightly, understanding.

“But your Kagutsuchi Vortex can replace those fission reactors and provide more power. Much more. That will expand lunar production, increase metal flow from Ceres. We need this technology, and we need it fast. These reactors aren’t banned, yet. If they’re in orbit before anyone moves to regulate them, we control space power. But I’d like this kept quiet until that happens. Agreed?”

Ishikawa’s composure cracked slightly, relief visible in his expression. “Of course. We would be honored to help, and we will keep this to ourselves.” He bowed, deeper than before. “We are very grateful for your faith in us.”

Raines then stood, ending the meeting with the same clarity with which he had begun it. “Good. Then we are aligned. Build. Scale. Deliver.”

The group rose. There were bows, brief handshakes, but no ceremony. The air carried the quiet charge of a compact sealed.

On the jet that night, Tokyo receded into a scatter of lights. Inside the cabin, the atmosphere had shifted. Raines sat with a drink untouched in his hand, silent. After a long pause, he turned to Max.

“This will affect you too. I want you scouting Martian ventures. Risk more. Even ventures that fail may help get the right expertise to Mars, and an entirely new venture may work. Mars is the natural core of operations off Earth.”

Max nodded once, already thinking.

The silence returned. Max glanced at Elin. “He just guaranteed the market before the machines even exist.” He paused, still processing. “I wasn’t sure what he’d do. He could have bought them out, buried the technology. Instead he’s building them a factory.”

“You thought he might crush them?” Elin asked.

“The thought crossed my mind,” Max admitted.

Elin’s tone was more contemplative than surprised. “He doesn’t see fusion as his. He sees it as humanity’s. He doesn’t want to own the light, just see it shine. The rest of us have to keep up.”

The engines hummed, steady and low, as the three of them sat with the weight of what had just begun.

## **Chapter 20: Baracoa**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

The province of Guantánamo no longer belonged to Cuba. By vote of the United Nations, its territory had been transferred to the jurisdiction of the Jupiter Resource Corporation, an arrangement argued in back rooms then framed as visionary. Washington objected to surrendering its naval base, Havana to losing land for symbolism’s sake. Amira Bastos, CEO, and Dr. Emile Carson, Chairman of the Board, were charged with turning this provincial corner into JRC’s visible capital. Neither relished the assignment, but both understood its necessity. They were stewards of a prestigious enterprise, and appearances required that they be seen arriving here.

The seaplane banked wide, engines straining against the humid air. A Grumman Albatross, old and noisy, vibrated through the floorboards, its interior faintly scented of oil and damp canvas. Bastos and Carson exchanged glances. Neither liked it, though it was the only practical way here.

This was not the way they were accustomed to travel. Amira Bastos and Emile Carson had flown between New York, Geneva, and Vienna in wide seats with polished attendants. Neither spoke of it, but both regarded the flight as an indignity, the kind of compromise expected of lesser officials.

Through the windows, the coastline broke into ridges and narrow valleys. Rivers cut through the forest and spilled brown into the sea. Ahead lay Baracoa, pressed between the hills and the surf, its colored houses fading under salt and heat. To Bastos and Carson, it appeared not as a town but as a stage. What mattered was how JRC would be seen arriving here, what image they could project against the landscape.

The Albatross came down hard on the bay, its hull shuddering as spray burst across the windows. The fuselage groaned while the engines throttled back, leaving only the uneven slap of water against metal. Bastos tightened her grip on the armrest. Carson muttered something under his breath, then fell silent. For both of them, landing this way felt provincial, as though international executives had been reduced to tourists arriving at the edge of nowhere.

The seaplane drifted toward the spider dock, a wooden structure that swayed with the tide. Planks shifted underfoot when they stepped out, knocking against each other with every wave. Salt rose in the air, mixed with the odor of fuel. The dock was too narrow for ceremony, but that did not stop Bastos from imagining how it would look when foreign visitors first arrived. The sightlines mattered. The dock should lead straight toward JRC’s offices, visible immediately, not tucked behind warehouses or houses.

Baracoa opened before them in narrow streets and painted walls, uneven rows pressed between forested slopes and the sea. Pastel facades were faded where the sun struck them and brighter in the shadows. Murals chipped but recognizable still carried scenes of boats and storms. Children ran through alleys, their voices carrying above the sound of waves. For Bastos, these details suggested opportunity: texture that could be framed as authenticity. Carson, watching in silence, saw only how small the town was.

They left the dock for the narrow street beyond, their shoes striking uneven stones polished by salt and years of rain. The air was heavy, almost sweet, carrying the scent of fruit and wood smoke. Vendors had laid out baskets on low tables: guava, plantain, mango. Children darted past without slowing, their games ignoring the two officials in pressed linen. Bastos slowed only long enough to place a hand against a sun-bleached wall, testing its rough plaster.

“This is where it should be,” she said, her voice measured. “Headquarters at the water’s edge. Not behind warehouses or on some hill, but here, facing the dock. A visitor should step off the plane and see us immediately. The message is that we belong.”

Carson kept his gaze on the slope above the bay, terraced with houses and patches of green. “Belong,” he repeated, though his tone was unreadable. He tapped the stone with his heel, frowning at the uneven surface. “And where do we live in this arrangement?”

Bastos smiled faintly, as though she had anticipated the question. “The hillside. Villas already under construction. Terraces cut for privacy, each room with a view of the water. Gardens where you can walk after meetings. Not offices dressed up as houses, but actual homes. Mine near the beach, yours higher up where it is quieter.”

Carson’s expression softened, though his voice remained flat. “So, a retreat. That I can accept.”

Bastos nodded. “A retreat, but one that shows we came not to dominate, but to fit. Walls in color, courtyards open to the light, walkways shaded with vines. We will make the right impression. That is what matters.”

They walked a little farther, past a courtyard where laundry hung between two balconies and a mural of a fishing boat peeled against the plaster. The streets bent inland, opening onto a view of the fields before the ridges swallowed them. Bastos slowed, her hand resting on the edge of a gate.

“Headquarters, villas, docks, I can picture those clearly,” she said. “But what comes after, I do not know. We are chartered for Jupiter, yet Jupiter is distant. Something here should show we are moving forward, but what should it be?”

Carson studied the open land beyond the last houses. The fields were dotted with palms and cut by a narrow road, then rose into slopes streaked with forest. “An institute,” he said finally. “Not a factory, not a launch site. A place for research, debate, engineering trials. Just out of town, within reach, but separate.”

Bastos tilted her head, as though weighing the word. “An institute. Yes, that feels right. Not too large. A few hundred people. Laboratories, classrooms, housing along the cliffs. Visible enough to suggest progress, but not so much that we overextend.”

“It would buy time,” Carson added. “A structure people can point to. Something to fill the gap while we prepare for what comes later.”

She nodded slowly. “Yes. A language for Jupiter, without the risk of failure.”

They stood for a moment, both looking across the bay. The town continued around them; children were still shouting in the alleys, shutters half-open against the sun, a vendor calling out the price of oranges. To Bastos and Carson, the sounds were no more than background. What mattered was the picture that could be drawn from this place, and how well it would play elsewhere.

They turned back toward the dock as the afternoon light stretched across the bay. The ridges glowed green in the distance, and the sea flattened into silver. Bastos walked a step ahead, already outlining in her mind how the headquarters would look from the water, how the institute would rise against the cliffs. Carson followed, content with the thought of terraces and gardens above the town.

Neither spoke again until the dock came into sight. The Albatross waited there, rocking gently in the tide, its engines quiet for the moment. Bastos paused, watching the sun drop lower behind the ridge.

“It will work,” she said with certainty. “Headquarters, villas, institute. Everything in place.”

Carson gave a single nod, as though no more needed to be said. They had seen what they needed to see. The rest would follow.

## **Chapter 21: Lunar Power Up**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

Darrin got off the call with Felipe. Planetary Metals had agreed to send him the tungsten pipe he needed, but they didn’t have the tin. Catherine Dahl was the only person who could help him out. He had never called her before, but Li had given him her number. He went over what he was going to say in his mind. Tin was of no value on Ceres. Raines sent him the reactor. If she has any tin that she could send to him, he would appreciate it. However many times he went over it, he felt like he was grovelling. All he wanted was to ask. He waited until noon Paris time, put in the number, and closed his eyes, finally pressing the dial button. 

Catherine came on. “Tan, what a pleasant surprise. I haven’t heard from you in months.”

The time delay meant she would wait two and a half seconds of silence before she heard his response. Not long, but awkward, and every moment he didn’t speak only made it worse. “No, sorry, this is Darrin. She gave me your number. I am looking for Catherine Tin. Catherine Dahl. Tin. I need tin. Can you sell us tin from Ceres? Planetary Metals doesn’t have much stock, and it is already committed.”

The delay meant people spoke in longer segments. On Earth, the call would have gone back and forth several times with hellos and softer greetings. That didn’t work with the delay.

Catherine was laughing too hard to speak, so she waited until she was composed. “Darrin. I’ve heard so much about you, but I don’t think we’ve met before. Dr. Knox told me he often has leftover tin when he purifies nickel. He and Mateo have been talking about an iron-cased bowl, but so far, it has never been shipped. Do you want it processed on Earth or shipped directly to the Moon?”

“No, Ma’am. We don’t want that at all. I mean, we want it shipped directly to the Moon. We will melt it here, so we don’t need any fabrication. I can send Knox the location of the regolith-filled landing pit. He has sent us metal balls before. We need twelve thousand tons, total.”

“I am talking like an idiot,” he thought during the delay. 

Catherine sounded calm as ever. “I am sure that there is no problem. We can swap straight across for titanium, if that works.”

“Thank you so much. And a pleasure to meet you.”

The call ended. “Did I really just end with a greeting? Either way, the tin is coming.”

Planetary Metals delivered the pipe, and Darrin, Luke, and Pavel assembled it one mile west of the OK-550. Four loops like a cloverleaf, each two miles long. There was a takeout every five hundred feet, and the lines were insulated with a foot of aluminum oxide aerogel, fragile but able to withstand the heat. 

Darrin and Luke set up the expanded cold ammonia radiators on the inside face of the north wall. Pavel was afraid of heights, so he acted as ground crew, keeping them supplied with pipes and reflectors, and using his survey laser to show them where to set each bolt. 

Six months later, the tin started falling, in thirty-ton balls inside copper spheres. They weren’t round after impact, but most of them stayed together. Darrin fished them out of the regolith landing pit and hooked them up to the cableway.

They pulled out the pure tin and ground it down to pebbles, which they poured into the pipes. There was a standpipe for adding more pebbles to keep it full when it all melted. 

Finally, Lunar Tug brought the Kagutsuchi Vortexes. 

The Kagutsuchi Vortex came in twelve crates, each containing an intricate structure of magnets and stainless steel. The enormous and complex magnetic fields made assembling the sections a difficult project. 

It was lunar night, as it all came together. They were illuminated by Earthshine and worklamps. 

“We are short one trim magnet, bottom-right quadrant,” Pavel Kerensky announced from the reactor floor. “And the manifest says ‘Molecular Turbine Vacuum Pump.’ There’s no pump. Just the large hole where it is supposed to be mounted.”

Darrin glanced at the hole, then at the gauge on his pressure suit showing the impossible vacuum of space. “We’ll be fine, Pavel. What pressure was it supposed to reach?”

“The manual says it needs to be pumped down to 10−10 torr, and I have no pump.”

“Check your gauge. My suit is showing 10−12 torr. The instruction manual is written for Earth engineers.”

“It is not about the vacuum,” Pavel muttered. “It is about the principle. The package is incomplete.”

Li Yun Tan descended from the mezzanine, a slate in her hand casting a pale glow on her face. “I’ll order another magnet, but that will take weeks. Let’s check again.” 

The three of them spent the next two hours looking through every container and bit of packing material that had come with it. 

Li found it. “The magnet was in the ‘Oven Mitts’ box. It’s huge. I thought a trim magnet would be a tiny thing. This is the size of a coffee mug.”

“It’s strong, too,” Pavel warned. “Keep it in the holder until we line it up.”

Pavel and Li worked together with the magnet, but the field strength made it impossible to line up well. Pavel set up the press, and Li began winding it slowly into place. 

When the magnet was fully installed, he called out, “It’s ready. Let’s start melting tin.”

Darrin had been running a megawatt across the first loop for more than an hour already, but it wasn’t yet hot enough to melt the tin. He moved to the control panel on the OK-550 and increased the current until it was running two megawatts. “Pavel, you tell me when it reaches temperature.”

Pavel was measuring the temperature on takeout number eight. He called out the numbers for fifteen minutes, then, “Temperature’s not rising steadily. It must be melting.”

He moved around to the control panel and switched on the pump. It bogged, and he hit the off switch immediately. “Nyet. We wait thirty minutes.”

Luke got them to help install the new Ipsen tumbler ovens on takeouts three through nine. They went on easily enough, but it still took two hours. When they were ready, Pavel went back to the panel and tried again, and this time, a trickle of metal moved. With something moving over the walls, Pavel was ready to start.

“Say good-bye to this world,” he said as he started the Kagatsuchi Vortex. He kept the deuterium density low as he brought the temperature up. The walls were heating. Pavel increased the density of deuterium until he could see radiation from the D-D side reaction. Then he let in a puff of *³He*. There was a brief thermal spike, as expected, then all the gauges held steady. 

“It’s working. I am feeding it again,” he called out, and started a longer, slow stream of *³He*. Gauges showed fusion increasing steadily. The tin hadn’t all melted yet, but the reactor wall was over five hundred degrees. As the hot tin mixed with the still solid beads of tin in the pipe, it melted grain by grain. Pavel increased the pump flow rate a little, but it quickly ran dry, and he pulled it back to the slow trickle. 

While the electric current had been heating the entire loop evenly, the reactor was heating the beginning of the loop the most. That meant even though all the tin up to takeout three was liquid, there was only a little liquid on the bottom of the pipe by takeout twenty, where the generator was installed, and less by the pump inlet.

“No need to hurry it. The wall still needs to heat up.” Pavel increased the *³He* stream, letting the wall rise to a thousand degrees. 

Luke was measuring the temperature on takeout nineteen. “Gauge is moving. Try ramping the pump again.”

Pavel didn’t increase the flow rate much, but it held. The tin had melted. It was under 300°C, but the pipe was flowing. Pavel brought the flow rate all the way up before increasing the density in the stellarator. 

Once the tin was flowing, he increased the *³He* feed rate until it reached the Lawson criterion. All the electricity was still coming from the OK-550’s generator, but now that was just pumps and gauges. Pavel stayed calm. “We hit self-sufficiency. I am no longer heating the plasma.”

The temperature of the tin kept rising, and the pipe started glowing. Luke was watching the Pyrometer on the takeout when it glowed cherry red. He called out the temperature every hundred degrees until they hit white heat at 1300°C. 

At that point, the takeouts were glowing too brightly, so Darrin draped them all with woven alumina blankets. “I didn’t know this came with free lighting.”

Pavel kept increasing the *³He* flow, driving the temperature to the theoretical maximum. “Look at that loop glow. We are going to have to cover it with regolith to save our eyes.”

Darrin throttled open the s*CO₂* turbine. They held their breath as they watched electric generation move up to 200 MW. The temperature on the radiators began rising. “Running at forty percent capacity, output is steady, and the radiator bank is stable at 38°C. Pavel, you can shut down the fission plant’s electric generator. This one has us more than covered.”

While Pavel went over to the OK-550 to start the shutdown sequence, Luke started a tumbler oven with a hundred tons of regolith. 

“Oh, this is smooth. With the bags automatically dumping on the conveyor, and a cableway to carry away the tailings, these will go a long way to helping me catch up with the increased demand,” Luke said.

“While I am shutting down the generator, can I shut down the entire OK-550 reactor?” Pavel asked.

“Not yet. I still need the old ovens running to help with the backlog. I’m going to be running both systems for a while. Darrin, I am going to need another twenty Ipsens, especially if operations continue to expand.”

When they were all satisfied, everyone but Luke headed back to the cantina. The blankets were glowing bright cherry red.

Pavel went to the bar and lined up four chichas.

Li and Darrin took Li’s favorite table, next to the claim map. “Darrin, we have the power, now. Can we distribute that power by the cableway? There was another darkside death last cycle. A solo miner. He didn’t push things, but his battery had been slowly degrading, and he didn’t notice. We can do something about this.”

“Tseng is making aluminum, and I use that for conductors. I can put HVDC cables on the cableway pylons. We can provide emergency power. What we can’t do is power everyone’s camps.”

“If we could power sweeping during the night, we would double our production.”

“The sweepers take too much energy. We can power lights and huts. We can provide emergency power. But we can’t power thousands of miners running heavy equipment. We can do is provide oxygen at the towers, though. Tseng is producing more than Lunar Tug needs. If anyone is in danger, they get in a crawler and drive to a tower, hook up, breathe easy and stay warm.”

“That’s what I want. If chechakos know this area is safer, then all the sites under the cableway will be filled fast,” Li said.

“I’ve been thinking about Tan’s Depot. Right now, we can refuel Lunar Tug ships, they have a pressurised warehouse for shirtsleeve loading and unloading, and the soft massdriver saves them fuel both ways. But what would you think if we were to install a new tower, coming over the rim to the northeast. It could be five miles long, and really cut Lunar Tugs expenses.”

“What would it do for us?”

“Tramp ferries will start charging less, and I am sure Lunar Tug would follow suit. And they could move more cargo. We have the power, now. I’ll get the materials from Tseng.”

“The depot has been very helpful. If you think it will be good for business, go ahead. I don’t mind.”

An hour later, Luke came in to the cantina, and Li and Darrin joined him and Pavel at the bar.

One of the miners, Rook, had sent a notification that he was running low on bags. Easy enough to send him more bags, but Darrin liked visiting the miners. He liked to see their camps and operations. The next morning, Darrin set up his pressure suit with extra batteries and set out.

The new cableway ran a circle inside the crater with a single north and south line connecting that ring to another ring outside the crater. The steel towers were fifty meters high, five times taller than the old system, fabricated from Tseng’s steel made from lunar iron and Earth carbon. In lunar gravity, their height allowed two-mile spans.

Six primary cable lines radiated outward from the external ring. The titanium 5 cables had a very low thermal expansion coefficient and were completely impervious to UV which had been a problem for the old dyneema lines.

Darrin watched the loaded bags coming in on the line across from him. The large bags held thirty tons of regolith each. Some miners were still using the old ten ton bags, but the thirty ton bags meant less handling and simpler operations. That drove most larger operations to thirty ton bags, and there were requests for larger sizes. 

As he rode along, he watched a miner drop ten bags into the transfer station. The station would automatically load them on the line to Tycho Crater. The miner was still using the ten ton bags.

The visits helped Darrin understand the miner’s needs and it let him connect to them. 

Rook was the first miner Darrin met. He wasn’t on the new line, a little south of the northeast line. Darrin dropped off and followed hauler tracks to Rook’s camp. Just a squat hut burried in a pile of regolith and the long, smooth trails of his sweepers combing dust into low mounds. Darrin waited for Rook to finish what he was doing. 

“You’re early,” Rook said. He wore an old pressure suit, logo faded, patched with silver foil. His voice was rough, like a man out of practice with conversation.

“You’re on the schedule,” Darrin replied. “You flagged the system. Said you were exceeding bag capacity.”

Rook nodded and walked toward the docking rig where four bags hung from a low truss, each already sealed and tagged.

“New sweepers,” he said. “Bought ‘em from vendors at Tycho Crater. Tripled my coverage. Got more dust than bags now.”

“You know we have new bags now, thirty tons each.”

“My hopper is set to fill ten-ton bags. I just need more of them. I would like a cableway out here, though.”

“If you can do the work, I can bring you some old towers and Dyneema cable. It only works for ten-ton bags, but that will be enough for you,” Darrin said. 

Rook thought about it. “That would be fantastic. I could stop wasting my time driving and focus on mining. Are they complicated to set up?”

“Mostly easy. Let us know when you are ready to go operational, and a technician will come make any needed adjustments and tension the lines for you.”

Rook looked Darrin up and down. “Tan’s already pulling twenty percent of my income. How much will that cost?”

“Right now, she would just be happy if you could send more regolith. No charge,” Darrin explained. “The one thing is we expect that if you add a spur, you will extend it out to other miners beyond you. Does that work for you?”

Rook thought about it for a long moment, tapping his gloved fingers on the docking truss. When he spoke, the rough buzz of his voice was calculating.

“You’re offering me a faster turnaround on my dust, and all I have to do is run the line past my hut,” Rook summarized. He nodded slowly. “I’m good with that. But if I’m going to be the gateway for the whole sector out here, that’s real estate that costs me something, even if it’s just the hassle of dealing with technicians.”

Rook walked closer, his shadowed visor reflecting Darrin’s suit. “Here’s my offer. I allow the expansion, I place the towers where they don’t foul my sweepers. And I get a cut, maybe 10%, of the haul from any miner who hooks into the grid past my claim. That’s my premium for being the local node.”

Darrin shook his head before Rook had finished. His voice, clear and steady, cut through the silence.

“I appreciate the ambition, Rook, but that’s not how Tycho’s infrastructure works,” Darrin said. “The cableway is free to everyone. No fees, no cuts, no exclusivity. You profit from it. We pull out more helium and pay you faster. But you don’t get to charge the next guy your own cut. Darrin paused, letting Rook think about it.

“What I can offer you is a guarantee that we’ll maintain everything we set up here. We give you the tools for free, Rook, because we want you to succeed, but we can’t let you charge a tax on the rest of the network.”

Rook hesitated. “Appreciate that. With the system you built, I don’t need a crew. I drop the bag, and you guys do all the rest. I really like that. It lets me focus. OK, we have a deal.”

“Perfect,” Darrin said, retrieving his hook from the truss. “I’ll have the tower team prep the ground posts tomorrow. Expect a tech out here to set the anchors in two days. You just focus on filling more bags.”

Darrin and Rook spent a while walking the perimeter of Rook’s claim. Darrin loved seeing how each miner ran their site a little differently.

Finished, Darrin started his long walk back to the cableway. He gave Rook a final wave. He was pleased with the infill strategy, repurposing old equipment and extending the reach of the cableway. Darrin returned to the line and clipped onto the inbound cable, headed back home. He remembered when Li had flashed her laser across the crater.

## **Chapter 22: Sidestep**
