| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** | **One year after the start of JRC** |

“I need to reserve the Oaks Boardroom again this evening. I will be having a guest from out of town. And he will need a room.”

“Wonderful, Dr. Sarai. We are happy to set that up. We trust your Cyprus Suite is still meeting your needs. I will set up his room next to yours.”

“Thank you.”

Raines arrived late. Elin met him at the airport. “Tobias, always a pleasure. I have your room key.”

“If I go to my room, I will sleep for a week. Show me the boardroom.” 

After the taxi ride, she led him straight there. Raines keyed up a slide on his slate, projecting it on the wall. “Have you seen this\!” Raines exclaimed, pounding his fist against the wall. “JRC is building palaces, not launch facilities. I read their entire quarterly report. Lovely pictures of their headquarters building and the streets of Baracoa. Shots of the JRI campus. Jovian Research Institute. A college? Who even authorized that morass? No mention of Punta de Maisi or launch facilities. The entire report reads like a travelogue. They have had a year, and they’ve done nothing.”

“They are diplomats. This is what they do.”

Raines didn’t stop. “We need action. Villas don’t power fusion. Right now, the one thing that is keeping us operational is rapid growth on the Moon, but it is not making enough to feed even one terawatt tokaplex. I have prepaid orders for ten.”

“The Moon is working hard. Tan called me recently with new designs for lunar sweepers. She thinks some sites can be twice as productive with them. I introduced her to Case.”

“Well done, Elin. Tan is an absolute engine. But even a factor of two won’t get us where we need to be. Do you know if Tan has a Kagutsuchi Vortex yet?” 

“Yes, two were delivered to the Moon. Derrin powered one up. Mateo mentioned he is commissioning another fast boat to keep up with the increased titanium production. When I told him Mars and Ceres would be getting more power, too, he said he was going to order three. Manufacturing may not be the real constraint with them, though. With the fissile material ban, General Atomics told me they are retiring their one remaining fission-powered freighter after this next run.”

“So we are back to individually commissioned hydrolox ferries? Those are so slow.”

“It’s worse than that. Tan’s titanium production is tied to her *³He* production. Andrei will have a much harder time scaling than Mateo. His current fleet is built on repurposed used ships. I don’t know if he can handle the increased volume.” 

Raines looked back at the projection on the wall of the JRC quarterly report, his eyes flashing in anger. “This cannot work. We need interplanetary transit. JRC is not going to step up. What are we going to do?”

“We are likely going to deliver another Kagutsuchi Vortex every two months. You might talk to Andrei. If he can afford to commission new fusion-powered ships, he could improve lunar service, and maybe open up mid-system service.”

“I don’t know what the lead time will be, but that gives me hope.”

Raines brought up a new chart. “Elin, I don’t know where you are with the terawatt Tokaplexes, but I have a lynch mob of impatient customers who want that power now. I have some *³He* in storage. Are you ready to start on China?”

“I only have one construction area for the terawatt tokaplexes, but I keep it busy. You want me to ship it now?”

Raines stepped away from the projection and looked out over the bay. He could see Elin’s artificial atoll in the distance, where the terawatt tokaplex was being built. “I believe Tan will deliver five tons per month soon. When she does, I want you to be ready to ship. And when lunar production hits ten tons per month, ship another.”

“If you are willing to dip into your reserves, I can deliver that, but these will drain your reserves fast.”

Still facing out the window, he began talking softly. “Elin, let me be frank. This business, with machines you built, is immensely profitable. Even so, we cannot afford to go to Jupiter. JRC has the money and the charter to do it, but it doesn’t seem primed to act. They have five projects in their charter. They need to make heavy lift cheaper, so they can get their equipment into space. They need to work out interplanetary transport so they can get their equipment to Jupiter. They need to work out *³He* collection, and they need to figure out how to get that *³He* out of the Jovian gravity well. And they need to paint murals on their buildings. They are making excellent progress on that last project.”

He turned back to face her. “What I want us to do is to help them. If they have an idea, I want you to look it over and offer some suggestions. If they want to build something, we find a company to build it and, behind the scenes, cover part of the cost to make it cheaper for JRC. I want them to look like the luckiest team alive, because we will be backing them. We won’t get any credit. All our help will be disguised as third-party stuff. But we can sidestep their bureaucratic limitations, and, maybe, actually get somewhere.”

Elin was quiet for a moment. “Tobias, you know I will do whatever I can. But, irrespective of what I do, they may not succeed for decades.”

“I know, but I refuse to let that thought limit me. We need to make this work.”

“Right now, I am getting the tokaplexes built. The demand for the ten gigawatt units remains very high, and the terawatt units still require refinement. I am also helping Kagutsuchi build their vortexes. And, most importantly, I help Gonçalo Silva keep the operational plants working. Time spent on JRC projects will necessarily slow other work.”

“I know, but I also know none of these systems will work without Jovian helium.”

Room service delivered some Gulf Shrimp Cocktails. They watched the ships move across the bay as they ate.

## **Chapter 23: Space Freighters**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

Andrei Volkov and Max Juno climbed the narrow stairs into the jet. Once inside, the cabin closed around them, quiet except for the low vibration as the engines rose. 

Volkov settled into his seat, eyes half-closed, thinking of the work ahead. “Max, what should I know about Nakamura? How should I speak to him?”

“Nakamura is a friend and the lead investor in Kagutsuchi Vortex,” Max said, seizing the point. “He is grateful for the way Raines made that investment so valuable. He will take care of you. He is well-connected with the Japanese industrial technology sector. He will be able to help us find the right companies to make the equipment you need. You just need to be open and honest. He won’t play any games.”

Hours passed in silence. Volkov’s hands were steady, but his pulse wasn’t. He was about to bet everything on ships that didn’t exist for markets that might never form. Just like the lunar helium rush. Buy rockets, hire crews, and pray the business materializes. It had almost bankrupted him then. Now the stakes were higher, and he was no longer young.

Mercifully, fatigue overtook him on the long flight. When Tokyo’s haze finally appeared on the horizon, Max leaned across the aisle. “We are here, Andrei.”

Volkov nodded as the jet sank toward Haneda’s runways, his mind spinning. He’d come to build his company from cis-lunar courier to the mid solar system’s transport operator. If Nakamura could help, fine. If not, he would find another way. He would not stop until Lunar Tug reached its next horizon.

They arrived at the Shibata Capital Tower in the early morning. Not quite empty, but very quiet. A receptionist was waiting for them. She led them through a wood-and-glass corridor to a small conference room with a low table, no screens, and insulated walls. Nakamura was seated inside. 

Max gestured toward their host. “Nakamura, this is Andrei Volkov. He is the CEO of Lunar Tug. Raines has asked me to help him.”

Nakamura inclined his head, his tone measured but curious. “I know of Lunar Tug, though we haven’t crossed paths before. A friend of Max is a friend of mine.”

Volkov extended his hand, his expression direct. “A pleasure. Lunar Tug is at the point where expansion is critical. I need new infrastructure. I must have orbital hubs to reduce the number of intercepts. But that is not all. I wish to extend my range to Mars and Ceres. I will need a new class of interplanetary ship.”

Nakamura studied him for a moment, weighing the words. “Ambitious. Tell me more.”

Volkov projected an image from his slate. “The hub will be a large warehouse and an even larger pole warehouse. Refueling stations. Living quarters for up to a hundred people with light rotational gravity. Importantly, it will need a soft mass driver for both braking and launch.”

“How do you intend to power this, Andrei-san?”

“Solar panels and batteries will be adequate. The mass driver will only operate at one G, but it will be a ten-mile-long cable.”

Volkov switched to another image. “A major headache has been descent to Earth. I will need the hub to be able to handle descender craft so no one needs to send a rocket up just to bring cargo down. I think Mitsubishi Heavy Industries would be perfect. They have made modules for space stations before and I am sure they are capable of delivering. I won’t need many. One for Earth. Maybe two, eventually. Another for the Moon, Another for Mars. It’s a short run.”

Nakamura was silent for a moment. “One design, three orbits. That’s efficient. Once proven in LEO, replication becomes straightforward. Mitsubishi Heavy Industries works on space modules. They are exceptionally capable and would be an excellent fit for your hubs. However, I know of a smaller company in Oita, Kagayaku. They do exceptional work, and are hungry for a project like yours. As one of their primary investors, I can tell you they always deliver above expectations.”

Volkov leaned forward, skeptical. “An unknown sounds like a risk. Are you sure they can deliver?”

“They can,” Nakamura replied evenly. “The modules they build now are already designed for orbital assembly. That foundation is solid. While it is a small company, they will prove to be exceptionally adaptable. Their success will be as important to them as it is to you. Mitsubishi can build a hub for you, but it will be their hub, their specs. I believe your requirements are quite specialized. Kagayaku will deliver precisely what you require.”

Volkov leaned back slightly, then straightened. “That sounds promising. I will speak to them. Now, let’s talk about ships. My fleet today is all short-hop craft, lunar surface to LEO and back. What I need are ships that can push farther, to Mars, Ceres, even Jupiter, and return safely. Can Kagayaku build them?”

Nakamura folded his hands, considering. “That scale of ship requires a larger yard. Ishikawajima-Harima Heavy Industries comes to mind. IHI can build hulls of that size and integrate advanced systems. The challenge, of course, is power. You know there is a ban on fissile material in space.”

Volkov gave a short nod. “I am not planning to rely on fission. I want the Kagutsuchi Vortex. It has the capability I need.”

Nakamura’s expression flickered with interest. “Fusion for transport. An excellent proposition. IHI could integrate the reactor. With that kind of drive, what exactly do you have in mind?”

Volkov sent the list of specifications for docking, fueling, and mass driver interface to Nakamura’s slate. “I need four categories. First, passengers. A fast bus, sixty seats, designed to minimize transit time. People must spend days, not months, between worlds. Second, a pole ship for oversized cargo: hubs, other ships, equipment to build a city, items that no other freighter can handle, but which can be safely attached to a pole. And third, general cargo. Enclosed, versatile, but not as heavy. The workhorse. It can carry passengers or cargo or both.”

Nakamura scanned the specs line by line. “And what is the fourth type?”

“A very fast ship. Multiple engines, less room onboard. Something that could reach the outer solar system, if we go there. We don’t need it yet, but I’d like them to work on it.”

Nakamura set the paper down, his expression unreadable, then allowed himself a slight smile. “It is a bold beginning. And with Kagutsuchi Vortex powering the fleet, IHI will see the prestige. But to produce so few is difficult for any firm. Designing a ship costs far more than building one. Perhaps we can consolidate the designs of the main three, so the differences are only superficial.”

Volkov thought a moment. With used equipment, development costs were someone else’s problem. But this project would be engineering from scratch, and suddenly those costs were his. He wouldn’t just be taking out a loan on his ships. His entire business would be in hock, and it still wouldn’t be enough. He met Nakamura’s eyes. The man wasn’t selling; he was guiding. That guidance was what Volkov needed. “Yes. Seven total ships, based on a common frame. Three freighters, two passenger buses, and two pole ships.”

Nakamura bowed slightly. “I will bring your request to IHI. Even with your detailed specs, they will not simply build this. You will need to be involved throughout the process. You understand that, right?”

Volkov smiled. “That sounds great. I look forward to hearing from them.”

Nakamura grimaced slightly. “Much of what they will be talking about will be delays and cost overruns. We all hate that, but it is a part of the business.”

Volkov cringed, but appreciated the honesty.

The three men rose together. They shook hands, and the room emptied as quietly as it had filled.

On the flight home, Andrei talked to Max. “Max. I specced what I needed, but the price I set is all I have and more. I have no way to deal with cost overruns. What have I signed myself up for?”

Max smiled warmly. “It’s a massive bet. But you’re building the infrastructure everyone will need. Raines knows that. So do I.”

Andrei nodded but said nothing. Max poured two vodkas.

Andrei drained his. “Na zdorovye.”

## **Chapter 24: The Seine**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

Max Juno stepped into Catherine Dahl’s apartment and immediately moved to the tall windows overlooking the Seine. “God, Catherine, this view. How do you get any work done?”

“You get used to it,” Catherine said, as she set down two cups of coffee on the small kitchen table. “Though I admit, some mornings I just stand there watching the boats.”

The apartment had the comfortable, lived-in feel of someone who’d actually made Paris home rather than just renting space. Books in French and English lined the shelves. Her dishes appeared to be handcrafted. Max wondered where in her travels she had found them. The pictures on her wall were mostly of friends in amazing places. Max thought about all really incredible projects she had been a part of. One photo was of Dr. Knox on Ceres with a smelter in the background. 

Max settled into the chair across from her, cradling his coffee. “So how are things on Ceres? Your last reports sounded optimistic.”

Catherine’s expression grew animated as she pulled her slate toward them. “More than optimistic. I want to show you what we’ve accomplished.” She turned the screen so Max could see an aerial view of the ice mining operation. “Four massive steel piers screwed deep into the asteroid’s surface, forming a square a mile across.”

Max leaned forward, studying the image. He could see tiny figures of workers near the base of one pier for reference.

“We’ve got cables running between the piers,” Catherine continued, swiping to show the system in operation. “We use them to pull scrapers across the area. Regular scrapers wouldn’t have enough traction in the low gravity, but this cable system lets us load up enormous amounts of ice with each pass.”

“That’s ingenious. Whose idea was that?”

“Knox’s team worked it out after we lost too many conventional vehicles to poor traction. Sometimes the worst constraints force the best solutions.”

Catherine swiped again, showing processing facilities that looked like a cross between a chemical plant and a foundry. “Here’s where it gets interesting. We take that ice and separate everything. carbonates, phosphates, ammonia, other salts and minerals. But Max, the volume of pure water we’re extracting is enormous. Forty tons per day of double distilled water.”

Max felt something cold settle in his stomach. “Forty tons a day?”

“Plus about six tons of organic compounds as byproducts. The process is so efficient we’re running out of storage space.” Catherine’s enthusiasm was evident, but Max was struggling to focus on her technical explanations. Forty tons of water per day meant…

“I was thinking,” Catherine continued, “we could send the phosphates and ammonia to Jakob. Those could boost the agricultural yields.”

Max managed a nod, though his throat felt tight. “I imagine so. Jakob’s team makes ammonia now, but that might let them expand.” But she was talking oddly. If their water processor was working so well, what was Mars doing for Ceres?

“That’s great. I’ll let Dr. Knox know\!” she enthused. “Since we started this water mining operation, Ceres has become much more viable. I am planning to expand operations. We can support two hundred more crew.”

Catherine set down the slate and her expression grew more serious. “Max, I need to be direct with you about something. We’re going to end the water contracts. Also, the block carbon contracts.”

The words hit him like a physical blow. Max stared at her, his mind reeling. Those contracts were the foundation of everything they’d built on Mars. Without them…

“Catherine, those contracts are our entire business.”

“I know.” Catherine’s voice was gentle but firm. “And I can’t tell you how grateful we are for everything Jakob accomplished. If it weren’t for his team, the Ceres project would have failed. My crew would have died on Ceres and never delivered metal.”

Max felt like the room was tilting. He could already picture Jakob’s face when he heard. This would cut him deep, yet Catherine was leaving it to Max to deliver the news.

“We’ll work out fair compensation for the contract cancellations. We will pay for everything in transit and the value of one additional year to sever the contract,” Catherine continued. “We simply don’t need any more water or carbon shipments. Stop today. Ceres is completely self-sufficient now, as we had always planned to be.”

Max stared down at his coffee, watching the steam rise. His mind was racing through implications. Martian operations had been built around those enormous, steady, growing orders from Ceres. 

“I’ll let Jakob know,” he said quietly.

Catherine nodded. “I know this is difficult. I can’t thank you enough for what you have done. We will continue to support you. Ceres can help Mars. We make plenty of steel now, but it is not worth shipping to Earth. And the ammonia and phosphates I mentioned. We can ship those to you free of charge, for old times sake.” 

Max didn’t have anything to say. He was still thinking about how this would affect Jakob and his team.

Catherine paused, then slid a different document across the table. “There is something else I need to discuss.”

Max looked down at the contract. Food procurement. The numbers made him blink and look again.

“You want food for two hundred people? Three meals a day?”

“Two hundred and growing. Max, you can’t imagine what your food shipments have meant to Dr. Knox’s team. The bacteria and yeast slurry is horrible. I tried drinking that stuff once and gagged. That’s what they are eating three meals a day except when Jakob’s meals arrive. What Jakob’s been sending from Mars, real vegetables, actual meat, and that wonderful amaranth bread is not just nutrition. It’s sanity. It’s hope. It’s the difference between existing and living. That one meal per week is the world to them.”

Max studied the payment terms, his shock slowly giving way to amazement. The figures were extraordinary, more than he’d ever imagined food could command.

“The crew fights over portions,” Catherine continued. “They plan their days around meals. What you’ve built on Mars isn’t just farming, you’ve created something that makes life in deep space bearable.”

Max looked up from the contract, his mind spinning. “This is… Catherine, this is worth more than the water and carbon contracts combined.”

She smiled. “Good food is priceless when you’re stuck in the asteroid belt.”

Max sat at the table a moment, studying the numbers. He stood and he and Catherine shook hands. “Catherine, we are all excited by what you have been able to accomplish in the Asteroid Belt. And we appreciate your generous terms for closing the contract. I will discuss the food contracts with Jakob. I expect he will be as excited as I am.” 

They stepped together for a brief *bise*. “Good-bye, Catherine. And thank you.” He then turned to walk down the stairs. 

He crossed the street and stepped down onto the quai where he turned west. He replayed the conversation in his head as he walked. The original Mars business was dead but other businesses were growing. Pacha and his demands for greenhouses and livestock had always been a distraction. On the Moon and Ceres, people ate algae. That Martians had complex agriculture had always seemed like a distraction, a small tax he had to pay to keep them happy. That distraction was now becoming the main business. Mars was becoming the mid solar system’s grocery store, selling to the Moon and Ceres. 

Callum’s mine had played out. He made billions from it, but it was done. Instead of coming home, Callum found a second beryllium vein. And copper. Copper was not rare enough to justify shipping to Earth, but, as CuBe, it worked. And Callum also found minerals that Pacha wanted to add to the soil in the greenhouses, improving the yield.

As Max walked along the Seine, he watched the water flow beside him. Upwellings would spread out and move downstream. He couldn’t find a pattern to it, but could see the dynamic.

Max was still mulling the implications when he stepped up off the quai and crossed the street to the *Le Puits Perdu*. He noticed they now featured “Astran Cuisine,” made with Martian ingredients. That food came from his team. How did they get it?

Max had never tried to be controlling, but he didn’t realize this was going on. Jakob never said anything. Jakob wasn’t there to ship ice. Jakob was a person.

Jakob had listened to Catherine in a bar. He would listen to others. They aren’t pieces in his puzzle. They are actors. 

Emmanuel greeted Max when he stepped inside and showed him to the back where Tobias Raines was waiting. 

Max took him aside for a moment before meeting Raines. “Emmanuel, I notice the Astran cuisine. Has Raines ordered yet?”

“No, he has not.”

“Then I want two Astran plates. What would you pair that with?”

“With amaranth chicha brewed on the Moon.”

“Perfect. Let me just pay now.”

“Of course, sir. That will be forty two thousand Euros.”

Max drew in his breath. 

Then paid the bill.

As he made his way to the back room, Raines stood to greet him. “Max, good to see you. How was your meeting with Catherine?” Raines asked, sitting back into his seat. “How’s the Mars business?”

“A disaster,” Max explained. “The Cerean water business is the entire reason we went to Mars. That business is completely dead. So is the Cerean carbon business. But other businesses are growing, especially food. Financially, it’s the best day we have had so far.”

Raines studied the numbers. “The only reason your team ever grew food on Mars was for survival rations. When you came to me, I was impressed with how thoughtful your team was about food and shelter. You have fantastic housing compared to anyone else in space.”

“Yeah, I am constantly impressed by this team. They are making improvements in everything they do. Their new housing is much safer, agriculture is more automated, water collection is easier.” 

“Why are you having them spend so much time on that instead of their business.”

“I’ve been asking myself that same thing. But I was looking at it all wrong.”

“You’ve been pretty successful for someone looking at it all wrong.”

“I thought Jakob had found a good opportunity. That’s not what he did. Jakob put a farmer next to a builder next to a mechanic next to an electrician, and set them loose on civilization. When we added a miner, it didn’t add a revenue stream. The entire team became more powerful.” Max leaned in. “Mars can do more. I am going to add two new groups.”

“What are you thinking of?”

“I’ve been talking to a chemical engineer, Dr. Marc Lafevre, an assistant professor at the Sorbonne. I met with him last time I was in Paris. He wants to set up polyethylene production on Mars. Ellie is making some, as she needs it, but he wants to have actual chemical factories. We’ve got abundant carbon and oxygen from the atmospheric processors, some nitrogen in the air, and we are careful to recycle everything. With Callum’s mining operation pulling rare earths as byproducts, we could build a real chemical industry.”

Raines was quiet for a moment, processing. “That takes serious equipment.”

Emmanuel brought their meals.

Raines looked up.

“This is the Astran plate, sir. Roast cavy, baked oca, amaranth salad with lunar microgreens, amaranth bread rolls, and chicha made from amaranth grown on Mars, fermented on the Moon,” Emmanuel said. “Enjoy.”

“I saw that on the menu, food raised by my team, and I had to have some. We should know what they are eating,” Max said, picking up his cup. “Cheers.”

They both took a drink. “Creamy,” Raines said.

Max continued talking. “Yes, the equipment for the chemical industry will be exorbitant, and shipping will triple that, at least. I am planning on using Andrei’s new ships.” 

Raines paused his fork with cavy meat. “Are the ships ready yet?” 

“The first standard cargo ship will be rolling out in six months. I plan to hire it.”

Raines nodded and chewed.

Max continued. “When you are in Paris, where do you stay?”

“*Le Maurice*.”

“That is a very nice hotel. I was staying there recently, and met the director of operations, Bastien Laurent. He is fascinated by Mars, and wants to build a hotel there. Not rough crew barracks. He wanted to make a destination.“

Raines finished a bite of salad. “A resort on Mars?”

“The first interplanetary destination. Hotel, casino, spa, maybe some kind of theme park. Think about it. Where do crews from Ceres go for leave? Where do their families live while they’re working the asteroids? And when JRC finally tackles Jupiter…”

Raines was nodding slowly. “Mars becomes the staging ground.”

Max continued. “And the leisure hub. Mining Ceres is brutal. Working Jupiter will be worse. But if crews know they can take leave on Mars, hit a casino, catch a show, see their families…” Max spread his hands. “Mars becomes indispensable.”

“The JRC is moving slowly,” Raines said, half to himself. “But if they ever are ready for serious Jovian operations, they’ll need infrastructure. Mars can provide that.”

Raines smiled. “You’ve been thinking about this for a while.”

“It’s been on my mind since I signed Callum,” Max said leaning forward. “But I didn’t understand it until after I talked to Catherine.”

“This is going to stretch you, Max. Can you cover it?”

“It will be tight. Andrei is stretched too. But we’re both excited.”

Raines called out. “Emmanuel, another round of chichas\!”

After Emmanuel poured, Raines lifted his glass. “Vive Mars\!”

## **Chapter 25: Jovian Research Institute**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

The shuttle climbed inland from Baracoa, following the winding road past forest and coffee plantations until it reached the settlement of Jamal. Here, where the foothills of the Sierra Maestra began, the Jovian Resource Corporation had carved out something new from the Cuban countryside.

Amira Bastos stepped out into the humid morning air, squinting at the campus spread across the hillside ahead. It had been four months since JRC had established the Jovian Research Institute, the JRI, and this was her first visit to see what their money had bought. 

The campus spread across a natural amphitheater in the foothills, its buildings arranged in terraced rows. Low-slung concrete structures that opened onto sunlit courtyards were connected by terra cotta walkways. Abstract murals of planetary forms caught the morning light, and the pale plaster buildings glimmered against the green hillside. Small clusters of students and postdocs moved between laboratories, carrying slates, tools, and cups of coffee.

Looking northeast, Bastos could glimpse the curve of the beach half-hidden, glistening under the sun, only a couple miles away.

Dr. Ji-Wu Kim, the Jovian Research Institute’s director, appeared at her elbow with the practiced smile of someone accustomed to managing up. “Ms. Bastos, welcome to JRI. We’re honored to have you here for our first major symposium.”

“Beautiful layout,” Bastos said, noting how the buildings seemed to flow naturally into the landscape. “Good turnout?”

“Excellent. Sixty-two researchers from fifteen institutions. Graduate students, postdocs, senior faculty are all here to tackle the Jupiter problem.”

The Jupiter problem. Bastos liked how that sounded. Simple, focused, the kind of thing that looked good in board presentations. “And they’re making progress?”

“Significant progress. Why don’t I show you?”

Kim led her along a shaded walkway toward the main complex. The central building was larger than the others, its conference rooms opening onto a courtyard where a fountain trickled over carved stone. Through the glass doors, Bastos could see the main hall was already packed.

“That’s Rithika Patel,” Kim whispered as they took seats in the back. “One of our senior systems engineers. She’s presenting failure mode analysis for atmospheric collection platforms.”

At the front, a young woman was explaining something involving a whiteboard covered in equations that might as well have been hieroglyphics to Bastos. The presentation involved charts and technical jargon, but she caught the key phrases: “yield optimization,” “operational parameters,” “viable collection rates.”

She turned to Kim. “Kim, I am going to need Rithika to write an abstract for me. I want the board to hear about the kind of work we are doing here.”

When questions began, an older man with precise gestures raised his hand. “What about material degradation in the radiation environment?”

“That’s Dr. Leland,” Kim murmured. “Our materials specialist.”

“Excellent question,” Rithika replied. “We’re modeling accelerated wear patterns, particularly for membrane systems and structural components. Current projections suggest…”

Bastos tuned out the technical details and focused on the energy in the room. These people believed in what they were doing. The details mattered to them.

After the presentation, Kim guided her through the hallways of the main building. Along the way, there were signs pointing to various interest group meeting areas: Heavy Lift \- South Wing, Harvesting \- Central Hall, Jovian Ejection \- North Wing.

“The institute is broadly chartered with the impossible task of solving the ‘Jupiter problem.’ We have broken this problem into four subproblems.”

She ticked them off on her fingers. “First: heavy lift. Getting mass from Earth to orbit cheaply enough to build the enormous infrastructure needed for mining Jupiter. Second: interplanetary transit. That one’s handled. A private company, Lunar Tug, has announced plans to provide regular freight and passenger service throughout the mid solar system. Third: harvesting the *³He* from Jupiter’s atmosphere. That is where most of our people focus. Fourth: ejection, getting that helium back out of Jupiter’s gravity well economically.”

Bastos copied the list of talks. This was the kind of clear problem statement she could work with and share with the board.

“The harvesting problem has the best characterized solutions,” Kim continued, leading her to a different conference room in the central hall. “We’ve organized our harvesting research around three main paradigms, each with multiple variants.”

Bastos pulled out her phone and discretely started taking notes.

Kim introduced Bastos to a graying woman. “Amira, this is Jackie Morrison, formerly with NASA orbital mechanics. She is our department chairman for Helium extraction.”

Bastos smiled and shook her hand. “Pleased to meet you. What are you researching?”

Jackie launched into a catalog. “First approach: orbital intercept missions. The two main variants are elliptical orbits that dive into the atmosphere periodically, and low circular orbits that skim the exo atmosphere continuously. Both require significant propulsion to maintain their trajectories.”

“Second, atmospheric aircraft. Fixed-wing designs optimized for the density and pressure environment. Some maintain level flight while others dive repeatedly. These craft experience much higher pressures, so collection is faster and easier than with the exoatmospheric collectors, but powering them is a huge problem.”

“And third, buoyant platforms,. Various dirigible and balloon concepts for sustained atmospheric presence. The problem there is keeping them from being ripped apart.”

Bastos looked at her perplexed. “Which one’s most promising?”

“That’s… complicated. Each approach solves different parts of the problem. The dirigibles are probably most feasible for sustained collection, but they are the worst when it comes to ejecting that payload from the gravity well.”

By midday, the Caribbean sun had turned the courtyard into a furnace, and Kim suggested they take lunch in the cafeteria. The dining hall occupied the ground floor of the western building, with large windows overlooking a valley that stretched toward the distant sea.

Kim guided Bastos to a table with several of the researchers, picking at a plate of rice and beans while listening to animated discussions about membrane chemistry and orbital mechanics. At the next table, a group of graduate students were sketching diagrams on napkins, their conversation a mix of English, Spanish, and technical jargon.

“You know what strikes me?” Bastos said to Kim. “The energy here. These people aren’t just doing research; they’re on a mission.”

Kim took a sip of carbonated water. “That’s what happens when you give smart people an impossible problem and tell them to solve it.”

“Is it impossible?”

“Six months ago, I would have said yes. Now…” Kim gestured toward the windows, where they could see other buildings scattered across the hillside. “Maybe it’s not impossible. When you break the problem down, as they do, it is just many nearly impossible problems that need to be solved all at once.”

Through the windows, Bastos could see the landscape that had drawn JRC to this location. Jamal sat in a natural bowl surrounded by green hills, isolated enough for security but connected enough for logistics. To the north, the mountains rose toward the Sierra Maestra. To the south, the land dropped away toward the coast where Baracoa waited with its harbors and launch facilities.

After lunch, Kim walked her through the rest of the campus. The afternoon light slanted across the courtyards as students and researchers moved between buildings, animated conversations spilling out of doorways. 

As they walked through the main hall, filled with people working on their slates, Bastos asked Kim, “The talks were fascinating, but surely they exchange papers, so they already knew all that. Why do you have the symposium?”

Kim gestured to a young man sitting in a chair staring at the ceiling. “Several reasons. I may poach someone we need on our team. I get people talking to each other, to see if they can engineer something. But, mostly, I do it to feed thoughts to the creative thinkers. Like Nikola Savic over there. He’s exploring alternative approaches. He’s not ready to present them yet, and maybe he never will be, but independent thinkers like him are our real hope.”

This was something the board would not appreciate, but Kim’s plan made sense.

“How long?” Bastos asked as they began walking back toward the shuttle pickup point.

“For what?”

“For answers. Real ones. Not just more questions.”

Kim considered this as they descended the terraced walkways. “If we’re talking about proof of concept? Demonstrable technology? Maybe ten years for collection systems. Twenty for integrated operations. I don’t know.”

Bastos nodded. She didn’t have a deadline, she simply needed a number. Twenty years was long enough to sound realistic, short enough to maintain political support.

As the shuttle pulled away from the JRI campus, Bastos settled into her seat and watched the buildings recede into the afternoon haze. The vehicle wound back down through the hills, past small farms where cattle grazed in fields bordered by royal palms. The landscape here felt ancient and settled, a stark contrast to the cutting-edge research happening in the labs they’d just left.

The road curved through a valley where a river ran clear over limestone rocks. On the far bank, she could see the remains of an old sugar mill, its chimney rising like a monument to an earlier age of Cuban industry far removed from space travel and helium.

As they descended toward the coast, the view opened up. First the red tile roofs of Baracoa appeared through the trees, then the blue expanse of the Caribbean beyond. The JRC headquarters building sat prominently on its hillside overlooking the harbor, where even now she could see ships moving between the piers and work lights beginning to flicker on as daylight faded.

The shuttle approached the JRC complex, with its security gates and administrative buildings and the gleaming headquarters. Bastos didn’t understand it all, but the people working on it seemed to.

And that was enough.

## 

## **Chapter 26: L’Hôtel Bounty**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

Bastien Laurent never sketched on his slate. He had a B3 hardboard portfolio case filled with cream sketch paper. Marc Lafevre was watching him work under the sterile lighting of Lunar Tug’s Martian ferry. Bastien was drawing the interior of a dome. “Marc, look at Ellie’s pictures. Bounty is beautiful. I love the domes, how they blend in with the land, like they are supposed to be there. My hotel must be like that.”

“I’d have guessed those domes would be too plain for you, Bastien. Far from the classical French opulence of your *Le Maurice*.”

“Not at all. Ellie’s domes would look out of place in Paris, but Haussman architecture would look absurd in Bounty. I am working on a grand entrance to the Bounty Hotel. This would be travelers’ first contact with *L’Hôtel Bounty*. It is here that the patron must move from feeling comfortable to feeling well loved. A spacious dome. Inside, there is no counter, no separation. A hostess will greet them with a simple sparkling water, and check them in with their gear. We require a large seating area maybe with a fountain. Patrons may wish to get their bearings. Some may wash up in the pool.”

Marc looked at his drawing. It was a fifteen foot tall replica of Olympus Mons, with water running over the shield into a pool. Bastien had drawn two men in the pool *au naturel*. One was standing under a waterfall coming off the top of the shield. “That would be welcome. Using wipes to clean myself on this trip has been awful.”

Bastien winked. “Agreed. That is why I imagined the fountain. I am imagining what I want to be there when we arrive.”

“My goals when I arrive are much less beautiful. I need to increase ammonia production to support expanding agriculture. Did you try Astran Cuisine at *Le Puits Perdu*?”

“I just ate a roll. Earthy.” 

“I had the meal. Max paid for it. The absurdity of shipping food from Mars to Earth aside, the food is interesting and satisfying, and is in huge demand on the Moon and Ceres. They are expanding agriculture twenty fold. Max asked if I could expand *LDPE* production enough to meet that need. I can, but that is the wrong solution.”

“I thought *LDPE* was great. Admittedly, I heard that from Max, but what’s the problem?”

“*LDPE* is great. It is easy to manufacture, it mixes well with regolith, forming tough walls, and it is high in hydrogen, so it blocks radiation. I am planning to load it with boron for neutron shielding. It is easily foamed and it can be used to make sheeting, which is convenient. If you can only make one polymer, this is it.”

“You want to do more.”

“Right. Now they are buying ETFE sheeting from Earth for their greenhouse. We have some with us. It is stronger and clearer than *LDPE*, and is resistant to UV radiation. But shipping from Earth means they never have enough, and when it needs to be replaced, they have to sub in *LDPE* sheeting. I need to start making ETFE on Mars. I can reinforce it with aramid fibers and experiment with gauges until I find what we need. This means I need to find fluorine feedstock.”

“Where can you get that?”

“It’s not rare, but I need the right deposits. I need Callum to find them. Sulfur, too.”

“ETFE needs sulfur?”

“No, it’s a different issue. The primary use for *LDPE* is mixing with regolith to make buildings.”

“Yes, Ellie’s domes.”

“Exactly, but sulfur does that easier. *LDPE* is a radiation shield, but in places where radiation isn’t a concern, like foundations or greenhouse walls, sulfur-regolith concrete would be much faster, easier, and cheaper.”

“I am sure Max doesn’t mind the expense. Why are you worried about cheaper?”

“Cheaper means we can afford to build more, faster. Cutting costs means cutting total labor. If I can build the foundations for all those greenhouses with mined sulfur and loose regolith, I can put more effort into making ETFE sheeting.”

“Callum said he would point us to the right places, and I brought backhoes. Still, I don’t think Callum was completely happy with Max.”

“What do you mean?”

“Callum’s game is beryllium. Find a site, dig it up, make billions, find another vein, repeat. All these other minerals are a distraction.”

The ship fired its rockets to slow down and move into low Martian orbit. “Suit up,” the captain called over the loudspeaker. “We are about to depressurize.”

Bastien and Marc started getting their suits on, layer by layer. The captain had already begun moving the air into the long storage cylinders, bringing it from three psi travel pressure down to two psi minimum safe pressure. When everyone checked in that they were ready, the captain sped up the compressors, bringing the ship down to less than a tenth of a psi. Bastien watched a door close and heard nothing. 

They all assembled in the cargo bay. The captain’s voice now came over Local Band radio. “I need you guys to start loading up the cups. I will bring them in, you fill them with cargo. It is all color coded so you will know what goes with what. I hope you guys are good at tetris and Jenga.” The captain opened the bay door and there was a short puff as the remaining molecules of air escaped.

He tethered and scrambled out the door and on top of the ship. He came back down with a dixie cup the size of a semitrailer. “Red.” The team started loading up the red pallets. Weight was no problem. Arranging was. When everything was inside, they twist-locked the lid on. 

“What is this cup?” Bastien asked Marc.

“The moon is making more titanium than Earth, now. These are descenders used to drop from LEO to the surface. Lunar titanium cups and lunar heat shields, Earth made parachutes, electronics, and interiors. Did you think Lunar Tug was going to land?”

“Patrons would prefer that.” 

The captain verified the seal, and put it on the launcher. “We launch twenty degrees before we get to Bounty. Tell me when you see it.”

Bounty was far too small to see, but Olympus Mons was very clear. The ship lurched as the mass driver slowed the cup enough that it would intersect the Martian atmosphere. They watched as the lid started glowing white hot, then dulled, then a chute came out.

Green, Blue, Orange, and Yellow were all loaded and launched the same way. Then the captain came back with an unmarked cup with seats and life support gear. Everyone but the captain got in and buckled up. The captain moved them to the launcher. His voice came over the comm line. “Five Four Three Two One.” 

Instantly, Bastien couldn’t breathe. The launch was at eight g. But less than a second later, it was over. 

“Was that ‘comfortable’ or ‘well-loved’?” Marc asked when they could breathe again.

“We are going to have some unhappy patrons,” Bastien replied.

On impact with the ground, the cup flipped end over end twice, then rolled fifty feet before coming to rest against a boulder, leaving them upside down. No one said anything for a few seconds, until they were sure they stopped. One of Marc’s engineers was the first to release herself from the seat. She held onto the chair and landed on her feet. One by one they all came down and sat on what they thought was the ceiling.

Forty minutes later, there was a banging on the hull. Over the descenders FM radio, they heard Callum’s voice. “Goodday, mates. Anyone injured?”

Marc got on the radio. “Shaken, but not broken.”

“OK. I need you guys to release the lid. I can only take two of you at a time.”

Marc and Bastien watched the gyro take off with two of their staff. “Customer service may face some challenges,” Bastien confided. Marc laughed.

It was almost dark when Bastien and Marc finally arrived at Bounty. Pacha had prepared dinner for everyone. The ag dome was the only place large enough.

Jakob sat at the head table. He raised a cup of chicha. “Today we welcome two new teams to Mars. Sorry about your gear. It is scattered over forty miles. Tomorrow we will help you bring it in.” 

Marc didn’t say a thing. He just looked at Bastien, who rolled his eyes.

The food was good, better than what was served at *Le Puits Perdu.* They spent the night in the ag dome on cots. It was the best night they had in four months.

\*\*\*

After breakfast, Bastien insisted on a tour. Pacha and Mira took him to see the greenhouses.

Pacha wanted to start with greenhouse five. When they got through the airlock, Pacha and Mira removed their helmets. Bastien followed suit. 

“The air feels thin here. Are you sure it’s OK?”

“You see those?” Pacha asked, pointing to a cavy running under the leaves. “If they can run, we’ll be OK. Don’t exert yourself. We are at two PSI.”

“Why isn’t my blood boiling?”

“That’s not the real problem here. The real problem is the Armstrong limit. If the partial pressure of water vapor in your lungs exceeds the total pressure in the atmosphere, then you’re done. Here, the air is almost half water vapor, so you can breathe much easier. The other half is oxygen, with four percent *CO₂* and the balance sulfur hexafluoride. Your voice sounds normal, we are fairly safe from fire, and we don’t put too much pressure on the membrane.” Pacha pointed up at the clear sheet covering the greenhouse.

“So you can just walk around here? That’s amazing. This place smells wonderful. Why can’t you do this under Bounty’s dome?”

“It’s too big. The sheeting would never hold. Bounty has ten pascals overpressure. The strength of the sheets limits the width of the greenhouse.”

“What are you growing in here?”

“Pretty much everything. The purpose of this space is to raise cavies, let them graze, and feed the soil. Growing soil is the hardest part of Martian agriculture.”

“How about trees?”

“The sauco can grow forty feet tall. Here I keep it trimmed to six.”

“Can I keep them in pots?”

“They may be hard to keep watered, and they won’t grow as tall, but you can do it.”

“Why are you asking so much about the sauco tree?” Mira asked.

“Forgive me. I don’t mean to interrogate. But this space you have is beautiful. I must have such a space for *L’Hôtel Bounty.*”

Pacha looked at him funny. “A pasture in a hotel?”

“A park. People walking slowly, looking up at the night sky, sitting under a tree during the day. Flowers blooming, little beasts running around. And your pond\! I must have a pond. Do you have geese?”

Pacha laughed. “No way. Nothing bigger than a cavy. The only thing that flies are these butterflies,” he said, pointing to a few resting on the membrane.

“You have butterflies? You buried the lede, Pacha. Why do you have butterflies?”

“The air is too thin for bees. I have some beetles for pollinating, but butterflies do well for several plants. These are called Painted Ladies. No problem at this pressure. They seem to like the high humidity.”

“You are aware they are gorgeous, aren’t you? Are they poisonous or anything?”

“No, the cavies eat them sometimes with no problem. Not often, though. Cavies can’t fly.”

Pacha next took him to greenhouse twelve. When they came through the airlock, Bastien reached for the helmet. Mira noticed and jumped to slap his hand away. “No\! Stop\!”

She had knocked him over, so he stood back up. 

“In the pasture, you can take your helmet off. Here you can’t breathe. Running less oxygen helps the plants grow faster, and we don’t keep it as warm, so there is much less moisture in the air. More nitrogen and argon. This is for plants only,” Mira explained.

Pacha showed him around. “The amaranth grow well here. We share this with the cavies. They actually eat more than we do. The leaves make a good salad or cooked greens, and the seeds are grain. That’s where we get our bread and our chicha. We grow many types of plants, but this is our staple.” 

“These are all golden. In the pasture I saw some red ones, too.”

“I raise both, but more golden.” 

“Pacha, have you seen a field of wheat or corn? Soothing, but not beautiful. These are beautiful.”

After the greenhouse tour, they walked back to Bounty. Pacha elbowed Mira and switched to an FM band. “Mira, this guy gets it.”

“Gets what?”

“He loved the greenhouses. Jakob thinks they make food. Bastien sees they make life. He thinks its beautiful.”

“Jakob had to eat only algae biscuit soup for months until you got it growing. He has a different perspective. When Bastien eats at a restaurant with two michelin stars he thinks he is slumming.”

“Whatevs, Mira. He gets me.”

\*\*\*

That night, at dinner Marc and Bastien sat with their teams. One spoke up. “You know what they made us do today? They made us trek all over Mars and haul back our luggage. It’s not our fault the equipment was scattered all over.”

Bastien looked at him. “You are a great chef. We need you. But we need everyone else here, too. Whose fault was it? Jakob’s? The miners? Who should take time out of their day to take care of us?”

“The staff can get it.”

“That’s us. We are the staff. Tomorrow Marc and I will join you. How much is left?”

“We found all the containers, but only brought two back.”

“OK. Tomorrow we get the other three. And if we don’t finish tomorrow, you and I can finish the next day.”

“*Oui, Monsieur Laurent.*”

\*\*\*

Over the next week, Bastien went everywhere in and around Bounty with his portfolio case, making hundreds of sketches, ignoring Lafevre and the rest of the camp entirely. Jakob kept asking him how it was going, and all he said was he was thinking.

Finally, Bastien agreed to describe his plans. “Beauty comes from order and contrast. The opposite of beauty is not ugly, it is disorder and monotony. Here we have the mountain, the scarp, and the plain. Beautiful. This ag dome. The colors and texture are of Mars. The shape mirrors the craters. Beautiful.”

Ellie adjusted herself in her chair, sitting straighter.

“*L’Hôtel Bounty* must fit in and stand out.” He took a drawing from his portfolio, the one he had been working on in transit. “Here is the grand entrance. A cableway brings patrons on a gondola. They emerge relaxed and calm, their baggage with them. They are immediately welcomed and cared for.”

Callum looked quizzically. “So, I pick them up from a descender and put them on a gondola?”

“That would never work. Do you know how you land on the Moon? Tan has built a small mass driver that helps slow incoming ferries and gives them a boost on launch. When it lands, the ship goes through an airlock. When you step out, you are in shirtsleeves. When you are ready to return, you are again in shirtsleeves. It is calm and relaxing.”

Jakob raised his hand. “Sure, but the Moon has much less gravity. Lunar Tug could never get back into orbit. It is tight as it is.” 

“Tom, your mass driver is sixty miles long.”

“Sixty-three.”

“The Moon has a driver about a mile long. We can do this. The first stage of *L’Hôtel Bounty* cannot be a room. It is a depot. Lunar Tug comes in, refuels, and leaves. Before we disembarked from Lunar Tug, I looked at the captain’s face. I saw sorrow. Resignation. He was looking at six more months to get back, without even a break when he got here. Imagine instead, he came down, landed with us, ate with us, refueled, and went home much faster. That is the beginning of hospitality.”

“I’m pretty sure Max expects rooms and beds,” Jakob said.

“When you were telling me about Ellie and the domes, you said Ellie knows the difference between the structure and the building. Power. Air. Thermostatic control. Plumbing. Cooking spaces. Max expects patrons to enjoy their experience. We will get to structures, but that isn’t where we start. A depot, a gondola, and an entrance are more important than a thousand rooms and beds. If they sleep in the ag dome, packed like sardines, they will have been welcomed and treated with dignity.”

Tom raised his hand. “Your design doesn’t have an airlock. Are those revolving doors?”

“Yes. An airlock is designed for efficiency. A revolving door can do the same thing, but it is designed for the user. Foot traffic enters and exits through a series of three revolving doors. Here, above the doors, is the air pump, constantly, silently moving air from the entrance side to the exit side. Patrons would be unaware of this. They only feel welcomed.”

Bastien pulled out another sheet, and stuck it with pushpins on the wall above him. “*L’Hôtel Bounty* is part of Bounty. You can see in this drawing how he must be designed. The entrance connects by a sealed walkway to the central building. It looks like one of Ellie’s domes, but it is larger and steeper. The ground floor has twenty suites. Not rooms. Spaces where a family can unpack their luggage and themselves. Above that would be *la grande salle*. Meetings, events, dining, dancing. The beautiful curved ceiling.”

Ellie said, “We don’t dance on Mars.”

“Pity. But perhaps that will change.”

Ellie blushed.

“Around this, we put in smaller domes. Each will have a restaurant and rooms. Smaller spaces. We will add more as we gain a measure of demand. We must satisfy Jakob’s need for rooms and beds.”

Everyone laughed. It was Jakob’s turn to blush.

“But here,” he said, pointing to the space next to the walkway between the entrance and the central building, “Nothing must ever be built. Connected by a revolving door, the pressure is lower here. This space is a park. Flowers. Trees. A pond. Meandering walkways and benches. Those little beasts running everywhere.” He pointed to the cavy pen in the ag dome. “No pen. Unfettered, inviting the patrons to enjoy the space as they do. Marc tells me he will be making a strong, clear ETFE membrane. We use this, allowing an unobstructed view of the stars, sometimes even of Earth. And these.” In a smooth gesture, he released five butterflies from his pocket, letting them flutter around the ag dome. 

## **Chapter 27: Heavy Lift**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

Amira Bastos stood at the floor-to-ceiling windows of her corner office, watching the afternoon light play across Baracoa’s harbor. Behind her, Dr. Ji-Wu Kim settled into one of the leather chairs arranged around a small conference table. “Amira, you sounded upset when you called,” Kim said carefully.

Bastos turned from the window. “Did you see Raines’ interview in the Financial Times yesterday?”

Kim shook her head.

“I can tell you the board has. He was talking about the lunar *³He* project six years ago, how he solved it by offering loans and stable price guarantees, creating a rush of miners. Six months later, *³He* was flowing. Same with refractory metals. He funded asteroid mining operations, and within three years, asteroids were producing more tungsten than from all terrestrial mining combined.”

“Those were logistical problems,” Kim said quietly. “What we’re working on is fundamental.”

“Don’t tell me that, Ji-Wu. I have a board meeting in two weeks and I need to show them progress, not excuses. It’s no secret Raines wants new leadership at the JRC, and now he is making his case to the public.”

Kim was quiet for a moment, studying Bastos’s face. “Getting *³He* from Jupiter does not lend itself to quick solutions. I know just the group for you to talk to. The Heavy Lift group is tasked with improving the cost and cadence of launches from Earth to LEO. It is the most visible part of our charter.”

The next day, Kim brought Bastos to JRI. They went to a two-story structure labeled “Heavy Lift”. 

Kim brought her to visit one of the chief researchers. “Amira, may I introduce you to Dr. Marcus Webb, a propulsion specialist who joined us from SpaceX. He has been looking at a set of ideas that I think will suit your needs very well.”

Webb stood and shook Bastos’s hand. “A pleasure to meet you. Ji-Wu tells me you want to build a new launch facility.”

“The launch facility is Kim’s idea. I need something that makes a statement about the value of JRC,” said Bastos. “What is your plan?”

Webb turned to the whiteboard. “The fundamental problem with chemical rockets is that the fuel that accelerates the rocket also has to be accelerated. You need more fuel to accelerate that fuel, which adds to the weight, which, in turn, requires more fuel. This vicious cycle is called the rocket equation. Each increment in speed requires exponentially more fuel. While it looks grim, it has a bright side. Each decrement in speed requires exponentially less fuel. That is where we in the Heavy Lift group live.”

“What do you propose?” Bastos asked.

Webb began drawing towers, tubes, and dotted lines in sweeping arcs. “The system my team has been working on involves a six mile pneumatic tube supported by a titanium lattice. The tube accelerates the rocket to Mach 2.3 at the top, at which point a ramjet stage takes over, driving the rocket to Mach five at ninety thousand feet. The result is we move from three percent payload to twenty percent payload, eight times as much.”

Bastos looked blankly at Kim. 

“Marcus, that is a very nice system, but we are on a deadline. Tell us about needed research, construction time tables, and costs,” Kim said. “Your research is directed to finding the ideal system, but today what we need is expedience.”

“There is another system we can use, designed to minimize dynamic load. It still uses a pneumatic tube, but rises a half a mile. In six seconds, the rocket exits at Mach 0.75. It stays subsonic up to the stratosphere, allowing for a lighter rocket body. If we combine that with air augmentation, the system can launch twenty percent more payload from the same fuel.” 

“Twenty percent doesn’t sound so impressive.”

“This system allows a high cadence. The rocket goes into the launch tube, gets fueled, and launches. Six seconds later, you are ready to load another rocket. A typical launch pad requires weeks to months of inspection and reconditioning after every launch.”

Bastos made a note on her slate. Launch cadence had overwhelmed US and Chinese launch facilities. This could be sold to the board.

“Another advantage is reduced sonic loading. Takeoff typically has the engine noise reflecting off the ground into the payloads, which must be adapted to deal with that destructive force. With this, there is no launch noise. By the time the rockets are on, the ground is fading away fast. Tons of sound-absorbing blankets are no longer needed.”

“Coming back to your comments on cadence, did you say we could have a launch every six seconds?”

“No, that is just the launch itself. There are several other steps, but we could probably launch two or three times per hour.”

“What would it cost?” she asked.

“No matter what we build, it will cost around ten billion dollars. You always need a seaport, airport, and hangars.” Webb said. “The launch tower itself would cost ten billion beyond that.”

“Put together a full proposal,” she said finally. “Cost estimates, timeline, technical specifications. Everything. If I’m going to show this to the board, it needs to be bulletproof.”

“How long do we have?” Webb asked.

Bastos checked her watch. “The board meets in two weeks. You have three days to put it together, then I’ll practice my presentation with you until that meeting.”

“We’ll have something ready,” Kim promised.

Kim and Webb went to meet with other researchers. Bastos remained in Webb’s office, looking at the whiteboard covered in sketches of towers reaching toward space. She didn’t understand the thermodynamics or the structural engineering, but she understood opportunity.

The day before the meeting, Kim, Webb, and Bastos sat in her office. The images on the whiteboard had all been drawn by Bastos this time. “I feel I really got it this time.”

Webb walked up to the board and erased a four and wrote a three. “Try not to get too technical in the meeting. You got the key points.”

The board meeting started at eight A.M. Dr. Emile Carson spoke first. “Amira, JRC was chartered to do an enormous, maybe impossible job. That you have not accomplished your goal is no surprise. My concern, however, is that I don’t see any action.”

Bastos looked around the room. None of them would meet her gaze. Okafor had a large envelope in front of him, with her name on it. 

She went directly into her rehearsed talk. “I have the top scientists in the field working on extracting helium from Jupiter. In the meantime, there is one element of that problem that has global importance, and that is heavy lift. I have provided documents on your slates.”

Despite feeling weak in the knees, she stood and moved to a whiteboard, as rehearsed. “Our primary limitation right now is heavy lift, from the surface to LEO. Canaveral is backed up for three months. Boca Chica is backed up six. Wenchang only takes reservations three months in advance and is completely booked. The lunar population is capped not by demand or technology, but by heavy lift capacity. The world needs more.” She drew a diagram of the kilometer launch tower and the cavernous warehouse. She graphed the rocket equation beside that, drawing a dotted line from the top of the tower to the graph. “While this tower doesn’t boost height or speed much, as you can see, it has an enormous impact right where the most fuel is being used. It can reduce fuel costs by twenty percent.” 

Bastos stopped looking at the whiteboard and looked at the people. “Six months ago, I would have said fuel savings were paramount. But today, I see that as secondary. The goal is volume.” She continued drawing, showing a large arched roof connected to the tower. “The hangar has space to load payloads and stage several rockets at the same time. When ready, they are moved into a low-humidity pipeline.” She drew five rockets in a row. “The rocket spends twenty minutes in each station. First, we dehumidify the entire rocket body and all the internal bits to prevent frost formation. Bone-dry air is blown into every crevice of the rocket. After it is dry, we chill the entire rocket body to nearly freezing. In the third station, the body of the rocket stays close to freezing, the fuel tanks are cooled with refrigerated air to \-60°C, then, in the fourth station, with nitrogen vapor, leaving the tanks colder than they need to be to prevent boil-off when the rocket is fuelled. The fuelling takes place in the launch tube. When ready, the tube is sealed. The launch itself only takes six seconds.”

Carson was calculating on his slate. “Do I understand you can launch three rockets per hour with pipelining, which would be seventy-two per day?”

“Theoretically, yes. However, we don’t expect to reach that cadence. The research team expects to launch forty rockets per day.”

Professor Liu Wei, the sustainable development theorist on the board, was recognized. “Fuel expenditure remains my primary concern. I feel that a twenty percent improvement is too small.”

“Professor Wei, there are many at JRI who agree with your perspective. They have suggested an alternative project. With the board’s indulgence, I will describe that process.” Bastos moved to another whiteboard. “Mostly, the project is the same. We still need seaports, airports, barracks, and hangars. But they propose an enormous tower six miles high. This will take several years to build and will cost twenty times as much, but the rocket will exit near the stratosphere at Mach 2.3. This resolves several rocket problems very cleanly. The rocket would not need to handle the transsonic domain; it would be through seventy percent of the atmosphere, and it would have saved…” She drew another dotted line from the tower top to the graph of the rocket equation, showing a third of the fuel consumed at that point. Bastos noticed the directors were engaged. 

“At this altitude and velocity, JRI proposes a ramjet stage. While my first proposal uses a shroud for air augmentation, this ramjet is more complex and will require years of research before it can be viable. When it is viable, it will be able to accelerate the entire rocket to Mach 5.0 and release it at ninety thousand feet. At that point, the rocket lights. Much of the work has already been done. Even the nozzles that work at that altitude are ten percent more efficient. Net result, this system can launch around ten times as much payload from the same rocket. JRI exists to research academic ideas such as this. I don’t bring this to you at this time because I believe we need action now.”

The board dismissed her briefly and deliberated amongst themselves. When she was escorted back into the boardroom, she noticed the envelope in front of Okafor had been torn in half. 

Carson spoke. “Amira, we have reviewed your documents. Twenty billion is steep, but we all knew a spaceport would need to be built. We are authorizing that operation.”

Bastos was about to speak when Carson held up his hand. “Professor Wei and Ambassador Okafor are both insistent that progress continue on the larger project, as well. While it will take time and money, the board believes that it is consistent with the chartered purpose of JRC. We have allocated an additional fifty billion per year for that project. Will you be able to fit both projects in Punta de Maisi?”

Bastos looked at each of them. “Yes, they will fit together well. Following your plan, we will be operational sooner, and eventually, we will be efficient, too. The shorter tower will remain useful for non-standard launches. I will start immediately on the proposal I gave you, and get you a combined field proposal by next week.” 

That night, Bastos brought Kim and Webb to Restaurante La Colina. The waiter asked what they would be having. 

“Lobster in coconut milk and blue lagoons for everyone. And keep them coming.”

Kim asked first. “It looks like things went well. Spill.”

Bastos said nothing until she had taken a sip. “The board was prepared to let me go. It started off tense, but our prep worked. It didn’t even seem stressful. I just went through the motions we rehearsed. Carson understood cadence. But Wei hated it.”

“Of course she did. She hates combustion. And agriculture, too, if that came up,” Kim said.

“I felt that. But one thing she doesn’t hate is spending more money. The board approved the kilometer tower, but stipulated that we must also build the super tower.”

Webb dropped his drink. “Both\! You’re a genius, Amira.”

The busboy came over to clean up the spill while the waitress brought him another drink.

“Speaking of genius, Marcus, you are going to have to help me build a new proposal.”

The board approved the updated proposal online, and she sent out an RFP. She got one of the younger new managers to oversee the process, Terence Halden. Not many companies could operate at this level, and preparing a bid took time. A month later, Bastos checked in on him.

“Seventeen responses so far,” Halden reported, scrolling through his slate in Bastos’s office. “Most of them are consortium bids. This is too big for any single company to handle alone.”

“Any standouts in the bidding?” she asked.

“A few interesting approaches,” Halden said. “There’s a European consortium led by Bechtel that’s proposing a more conservative timeline but very robust engineering. A joint venture between Japanese and Korean firms that’s promising some innovative materials science.” He paused. “And there’s one Brazilian company, Construtora Atlântico, that submitted a bid about thirty percent lower than anyone else.”

Bastos looked up sharply. “Are they credible?”

“I’m having our procurement team investigate them now. Their proposal is surprisingly detailed.”

Three thousand miles away, Tobias Raines called Henrique Cavalcanti, the CEO of Construtora Atlântico. “Henrique, have you put in the bid?”

“I have been working on that for weeks with my team. The RFP was written by academics, but we worked it out. When I calculated the cost, I cut fifteen billion from that, as you asked. It is a sealed bid process, but I am pretty sure we will be the standout for low price.”

“That’s good, we need to get the bid. What I need to be sure of is you can get this built quickly. Every day it is not built is costing me money.”

Henrique looked up from the structural calculations. “Our current design is eighteen months. As you know, projects like this face more delays than advances. This may take two years or more.”

“That won’t do. Let’s target fourteen months. You let me know what we can do to speed this along.”

“Yes, sir. Can I ask a question? They say their plans are speculative. Are you concerned it might not work well?”

“We build infrastructure for them. That will definitely be useful, whether the tower works as advertised or not,” Raines said. “Even if the pneumatic launch system is a complete failure, we’ll have built the first step we will need to support Jupiter operations. And if it fails, I believe they will figure out how to fix it.”

“Understood. We will continue to press for ideas to get this done faster.”

“Fantastic, Henrique. Let me give you a number of a metal supplier. Felipe runs a large mill in Mobile, Alabama called Planetary Metals. He can get you what you need fast. Let him know I sent you.”

“Will do.”

The call came three days later. Bastos was reviewing quarterly reports when Halden entered, with rolls of blueprints under his arm.

“Construtora Atlântico is a legitimate contractor. They have done a lot of work for the Brazilian government.”

Bastos set down her reports. “You’ve finished the background check?”

“Yes. They have a long and successful history. And a fascinating proposal.”

Halden spread out the Brazilian proposal on Bastos’s desk. “They made several changes. Twinned towers don’t just double launch cadence. It creates fault tolerance. If something goes wrong with tube A, you can use tube B until it is fixed. The tubes are designed to be built by jacking the top and inserting a new segment underneath. That process can also run in reverse, bringing it down for needed repairs or when there is a hurricane warning.”

“They are using the local limestone to run their own cement plant on site, which I think is smart, but they have also requested mineral rights to sell off the limestone they excavate. I wrote a note rejecting this idea, but I haven’t sent it yet. I wanted to talk to you.”

“Don’t look a gift horse in the mouth. Selling limestone may be why they are cheaper. The worst thing for us would be a huge pile of broken limestone and no place to send it.”

“OK, I will approve it. Another variance is the eliminated rail.”

“No, we need that to connect Baracoa to Punta de Maisi.”

“Instead, they want to use Mexican Modutram. The top speed on the intercity route will be about forty-five miles per hour, which is slower than the spec you and Webb put together, but they do much more. Yours had three stops: Baracoa, the airport, and the hangar. Theirs has unlimited stops and routes. They let it run all over the construction site, depending on where the work is, and all they have to do is paint some lines. Here this week, two hundred yards away next week. And the cars are very flexible. They can carry passengers, freight, and food trucks that can roll right up to the work sites. They even have yellow steel modules for scrapers and graders.”

“That’s close enough to rail for my needs,” Bastos said, scanning the timeline. “They are going to finish the smaller project in eighteen months? That’s half what the European consortium was promising.”

“And they’re guaranteeing performance. If the system doesn’t achieve the specified cadence, we don’t pay the final thirty percent.”

Bastos studied the technical drawings, trying to process the scale of what she was looking at. “I want this started now.”

“Yes, ma’am,” Halden answered. “I will get them started today.”

Nine months later, Bastos stood on the observation platform that had been built specifically for this moment. The concrete pad stretched for nearly a hundred yards, positioned a mile from the construction site.

Around her, a small crowd had gathered. Kim and Webb from JRI, and Carson from the board of directors. The hangar was still just a skeleton, but the twin towers were complete and guyed in place. They watched as the first pneumatic tube was jacked into place, a hundred meters at a time. They started with the crown, which looked like a pepper pot muzzle brake. In less than three hours, the tube was ready. They didn’t yet have the entire pneumatic system ready, but they could run a low-pressure test. A stacked wooden slug, six meters across and ten meters high, stood where the rocket would go. Technicians began the countdown. It used a clock face with only a second hand. When it got close to the end, everyone on the platform was cheering, “Five, four, three, two, one.” Nothing. It had launched, but it was still travelling. Six seconds later, they saw the slug exit at about six hundred miles per hour straight up. Five seconds after that, the report struck, shaking them right through their bodies. The slug continued up for another twenty seconds, before beginning its descent. Vanes on board directed it to land safely at sea. 

“Linda,” Henrique murmured.

Bastos looked out at the impossibly tall structure, the first piece of humanity’s most ambitious attempt to reach space cheaply and routinely.

She thought about her desperate board meeting ten months ago, when she’d been scrambling for anything that might show progress. Now she had seen it launch a slug two and a half miles into the Cuban sky.

For the first time since coming to Baracoa, she felt like they were going to change the world.
