## **Chapter 28: Sinoplex**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

The tunnel stretched twenty kilometers beneath the East China Sea, its walls lined with gleaming superconducting cables thick as tree trunks. Dr. Elin Sarai walked beside Minister Chen Zhengrong and his technical team, their footsteps echoing in the cool air. Liquid nitrogen flowed through the outer jackets, keeping the cables cooled to 77 Kelvin. 

“Ten megavolts DC,” Chen said, pride evident in his voice. “No AC/DC conversion losses. No AC at all. Direct from the tokaplex to all of eastern China on a double backbone mesh.”

Elin ran her hand along the insulated housing. Of course he wasn’t mentioning the energy consumption of cryogenic cooling, or accounting for takeoffs. There would be losses, but this was still an amazing achievement. She’d reviewed the specifications, but standing here made it real. This was a powerline scaled for a civilization that had outgrown fossil fuels and was reaching for something bigger.

“The takeoffs?” she asked.

Chen gestured to a junction ahead where smaller conduits branched upward. “Aerial grid at one megavolt. We’ve phased out AC transmission entirely. Every substation, every industrial complex, uses DC. Anyplace with legacy AC equipment runs their own inverters, but everyone knows AC is the past. DC transmission uses the conductor more efficiently and has no radiative or inductive loss. Your tokaplex will feed this grid a full terawatt, continuous. That will allow us to proceed with hyperelectrification.”

“If we can keep it fueled,” Elin thought to herself, trying to keep her expression neutral.

They emerged from the tunnel onto the artificial atoll, a C-shaped ring of reclaimed land and reinforced concrete two kilometers across. The opening faced east, toward the open sea. Enormous voltage converters lined the inner shore, their cooling towers venting steam into the morning air. Connection points bristled with sensors and fail-safes. This was a fortress of infrastructure, designed to channel more energy than most countries could use.

“The platform arrives tomorrow,” Chen said. “Your team is ready?”

“We are,” Elin replied. “Gonçalo Silva will oversee certification. Your site general manager will have full operational control once we’re satisfied with the handoff.”

Chen nodded. “You will find General Manager Zhao to be quite capable. She managed the Tianwan Nuclear Power Plant for eight years and has just completed six months of training at the Monte Hermoso tokaplex. She is ready.”

Elin looked out at the empty water beyond the atoll’s mouth. Somewhere out there, the platform was crossing the Indian Ocean, pulled by a fleet of tugs. She had watched it leave Biloxi two months ago, a floating city of fusion technology, the magnetic containment vessel alone massing fifty thousand tons. It was far too big to cross any canal, so they had to route it around Africa.

“It will work,” she said, more to herself than to Chen.

The platform appeared on the horizon at dawn, a low dark shape flanked by eight ocean-going tugs. Elin stood on the atoll’s southern observation deck with Gonçalo and GM Zhao, watching through binoculars as the convoy approached.

“*Mãe de Deus*,” Gonçalo whispered. “I saw it in Biloxi. But out here…”

It was massive. The tokaplex core sat in the center, surrounded by heat exchangers, coolant systems, control buildings, and the power conversion equipment that would step the reactor’s output up to ten megavolts. The whole assembly rode on a stabilized floating platform designed to handle typhoon-force seas. Prefabricated in sections, welded together on artificial islands in Biloxi, the entire tokaplex was tested together for six months before the journey began.

The tugs positioned it carefully, fighting current and wind. Centimeter by centimeter, they nudged the platform toward the atoll’s opening. Harbor pilots called out adjustments over radio. Elin held her breath.

The platform’s bow crossed the threshold. She exhaled.

Three hours later, it was locked into place, connection booms extending to meet the platform’s docking hardpoints. The tugs released their lines and backed away, their job done. Now came Elin’s part.

“Zhao,” she said. “Let’s go see your reactor.”

Zhao walked them through the platform’s systems, describing the cooling loops, the magnetic coil assemblies, the plasma diagnostics without hesitation.

“I need to verify you know this unit’s startup sequence,” Gonçalo said, pulling up the checklist on his tablet. “Walk me through it.”

Zhao nodded. “Let’s begin.”

They spent three days on certification. Zhao ran through normal startups, emergency shutdowns, partial failures, grid synchronization issues while Gonçalo observed, checking her procedures against the design specs.

On the third evening, Gonçalo found Elin. “Zhao’s ready,” he said, exhaustion in his voice. “Better than ready. This is her reactor now.”

The test fuel arrived quietly. It was a small bottle of *³He*, barely fifty grams, enough for a thirty-second burn. They loaded it into the fuel system at midnight, ran the startup sequence, and watched the plasma come alive. The magnetic fields held. The fusion reaction stabilized. For half a minute, the tokaplex poured a terawatt into the Chinese grid.

Then Gonçalo shut it down, and they spent eight hours analyzing every data point.

“Perfect,” Zhao said, reviewing the thermal curves. “We’re ready for full operation.”

“Fantastic,” Elin said. “The production fuel load will arrive tomorrow.”

The delivery was scheduled for dawn, 6:02 AM, but Elin was on the atoll’s loading platform an hour early. A US Navy Destroyer had brought the ten-ton container from the Helion Dynamics warehouse in Dutch Harbor to a US Navy base in Okinawa. It was being delivered from there by tiltrotor to the terawatt tokaplex. 

Raines had arranged it personally. Ten tons of *³He,* worth eight billion dollars, representing two months of operation. That was the total lunar output. Helion Dynamics would be dipping into their reserves to keep all the other reactors running for a while. Raines was betting lunar production would increase fast enough to take care of that. The entire supply chain depended on this working.

Elin watched the eastern horizon. At 6:01, the sun breached the sea, and for less than a second the top edge flashed brilliant green, then it was replaced by ordinary gold. The tiltrotor appeared a minute later, on schedule, flanked by two escort craft. It descended slowly, rotors kicking up spray, and settled onto the platform. The rear ramp dropped, and a US Marine officer stepped out, followed by six armed Marines surrounding a cylindrical container the size of a small car.

Minister Chen was there, along with a PLA colonel and a squad of soldiers. The two groups eyed each other across the platform.

“Dr. Sarai?” the Marine lieutenant said.

“That’s me.”

“Captain Pak, US Marine Corps. I have a delivery for the Shanghai Terawatt Tokaplex Project. Transfer of custody requires your signature and authorization from the People’s Republic of China.”

The PLA colonel stepped forward. “Colonel Feng. I will be overseeing security of this facility and all future fuel deliveries.”

Pak handed Elin a slate. She scanned the manifest. 9.8 metric tons of *³He*, purity 99.97%, source Tycho Crater, shipped via Lunar Tug. She signed.

“Minister Chen?” she said.

Chen signed as well, transferring $7,840,000,000 to Helion Dynamics. The transfer done, Pak and Colonel Feng added their authorizations. The Marines rolled the container down the ramp. The PLA soldiers came up, face to face with the US Marines. Captain Pak crisply saluted Colonel Feng. The PLA took custody and, with Chen’s technical team, moved the container into the tokaplex’s *³He* injection site.

Pak and his detail were still standing near the tiltrotor. He turned and saluted. “Good luck, Dr. Sarai, Minister Chen. We’ll return in two months with the next load.”

The tiltrotor lifted off, and Elin watched it disappear toward the horizon. Colonel Feng was already setting up a security perimeter around the injection site. This was his domain now. Eight billion dollars’ worth of fuel, the lifeblood of China’s energy future, sitting in a tank beneath the East China Sea.

Raines would love the optics. The US delivering fuel to China while both governments postured about energy independence. But Elin knew the truth: Li Yun Tan’s operation on the Moon was the real bottleneck. Even with the Kagutsuchi Vortex online, even with production ramping up, the lunar supply couldn’t keep pace with demand. 

The Helion Dynamics reserves were currently over fifty tons, stockpiles Raines had been building since *³He* first flowed from the Moon. But reserves wouldn’t last forever. The Moon needed to produce more, or the entire terawatt program would stall.

She pushed the thought aside. Today wasn’t about supply chains. Today was about making history.

The activation ceremony was scheduled for noon. The observation deck was filled with ministers, corporate executives, and international press. Tobias Raines stood among them, shaking hands and making promises about second and third tokaplexes, about China leading the world into a fusion-powered future. Elin avoided the crowd, staying in the control room with Zhao and Gonçalo.

“Are we ready?” Zhao asked.

Elin checked the status board. Fuel loaded. Cooling systems nominal. Grid synchronization confirmed. Beijing, Shanghai, and Guangzhou were standing by for the surge.

“We’re ready.”

Zhao settled into the operator’s chair, her hands steady on the controls. Gonçalo stood beside her, watching. Elin took a position at the monitoring station, eyes on the plasma diagnostics.

“Beginning startup sequence,” Zhao said.

The magnetic coils energized, their hum vibrating through the platform. *³He* flowed into the reaction chamber. The plasma formed, a roiling ball of superheated gas trapped in invisible magnetic fields. Temperature climbed. Pressure stabilized. The fusion reaction ignited.

Power surged. One hundred gigawatts. Five hundred. Eight hundred. The numbers climbed smoothly, exactly as designed. At ninety-five percent, Zhao held for thirty seconds, confirming stability, then brought it to full output.

One terawatt. The grid absorbed it smoothly. Across eastern China, coal plants, no longer needed, began staged shutdowns. Gas generators went idle. The superconducting backbone carried the load that had been distributed across hundreds of smaller facilities.

On the grid display, Elin watched the power flow stabilize. This was just the beginning. The capacity was there now for the planned expansions in Shenzhen, for the western hydrogen pipeline, for the urban vertical agriculture complexes waiting for reliable power. For the hyperelectrification Chen kept talking about.

Elin allowed herself a small smile. They’d done it. The first terawatt tokaplex, operational and feeding the grid. Raines’ vision, her design, Zhao’s operation. It worked. But as she watched the *³He* injection metrics, she couldn’t ignore the math. This tokaplex was burning as much as the Moon could produce by itself, forcing the smaller tokaplexes onto reserve fuel. And Raines was already negotiating for five more this size.

Outside, the crowd erupted in applause. Minister Chen was giving a speech about China’s energy future, about leading the world, about the dawn of a new era. Raines was smiling, shaking hands, making deals.

Elin stayed in the control room, watching the numbers. Gonçalo stood beside her.

“It’s working, Elin,” he said quietly.

“For now.”

## **Chapter 29: Reach**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |

| **Time Since Start** |  |

Darrin loaded up a rover. Extra batteries, food, water, and a *CO₂* cylinder. Li noticed him packing. “You’re packing the high-wheel? Planning on driving somewhere?” she asked.

“Not sure yet, but I am ready for it. I am getting new QR codes. These claims aren’t on our map. I don’t know where they are coming from. What I do know is they are sending their bags to Faraday Crater, which is at the easternmost edge of the cableway. I’m going there to take a look, see what happens to the bags. I am bringing the high-wheel because the terrain might be tough.”

She looked at his survey laser. Having it didn’t mean anything. He always had it. But it brought a question to mind. “Are you planning on stopping them?”

“Doubt it, but maybe. I want to see what is happening.”

Darrin scanned the QR code on the high-wheel rover and punched in the destination: Faraday Crater. He hooked up and was off. The uptake was smooth. The crater floor dropped away as he climbed over the rim.

The lunar highlands unfolded beneath him punctuated by bright crater scars, illuminated only by blue earthlight. The cable carried him at fifty miles per hour. Below he could see the mining camps, sweeping, bagging, sending the regolith to Tycho. Even though the power was capped, they could still sweep slowly straight through the lunar night.

An hour and a half out he passed Henderson & Associates, eight domes clustered around a central processing hub. Power lines gleamed on ceramic insulators. Nearly a dozen bags queued at their transfer station. 

Thirty minutes from Faraday Crater he noticed a rover loaded with pressure bottles. A lone figure was at the tower, filling each bottle with oxygen. Darkside deaths had ended under the cableway.

After four hours, he came to the end of his cableway. There he found another cable, lighter, extending further east. Darrin had some of the new bags with him. He hooked them up to see where they would go. The platform switched them onto the minor line. Darrin hooked up and followed them out. 

This line couldn’t handle the thirty ton bags that had become the standard under the cableway. But three ten-ton bags held just as much. Darrin kept following the empties another fifty miles. Here, there were no more lines. The bags just sat on the platform, waiting to be claimed.

Darrin dropped down and parked his high-wheel a ways away where it wouldn’t be seen, then walked back to watch. He buried himself in the regolith, leaving a gap so he could see the platform. He waited.

While he watched, other bags were coming to the end and staying. A man came up, hauling ten bags. He hooked them all on. Darrin pulled out his slate and took pictures of them. The QR code checked out. He knew that man. He stayed partly buried, watching the bags. 

Another man drove up in a crawler, hauling a trailer with twenty bags. As he hung them on the cableway, Darrin noticed immediately that the bags had different codes, and none of them were people he knew. He watched the man take the empty bags and a few packages that had come in, toss them in the trailer, and leave the way he came. 

As soon as he was over the horizon, Darrin got up and ran back to his crawler. He followed the crawler’s tracks. They were easy to see. There were ruts from them being followed so many times. Darrin kept his distance. If the man was paranoid, he’d be seen.

The driver stopped. Darrin kept his high-wheel out of sight and crept up, staying low. As he watched, the man got off the crawler and put everything in a very large cup, then sealed that with another cup. The cup was huge compare to the load.

The man rolled the cup about ten feet. Then backed off. He walked to a table and tapped it a few times. Without warning, the cup started moving fast, then flew into the sky. Darrin recorded it on his slate. The slate estimated where it would land.

Far from the cableway, there was no way to get a message back to Li. Darrin got back to his high-wheel. He drove five miles north, staying out of sight, then let the slate take over driving. It was heading three hundred miles away. Darrin set a stop point fifty miles from the destination. He pressurized the interior and took his helmet off. While the slate drove he boiled water and dropped in an algae biscuit. He ate it with a daily multivitamin. 

He couldn’t send it, but he compiled a note, anyway. “Li, found where the bags are coming from. Going to investigate.” 

Darrin was watching as he drove through. Camps looked different out here. Light were off, since it was night. No one had power to burn. He saw K bottles stacked next to buried huts, nothing happening. He remembered living through lunar night, the biocore turned low, trying to rest and breathe less.

His lights were the only ones on, other than the Earthshine. Darrin started to doze when he saw a man running. He took control of the rover and turned towards the man. Looking at his right hand on the steering wheel, he was surprised to see the survey laser.

The man was waving his arms and running toward Darrin. There was a heterodyne squeal when the man got in range. “Hi, stranger\! Can you help? My wife and I are going to darkside\!”

Darrin sped up, driving straight toward the man. He got his helmet back on, and recompressed the air into storage. “On the way. Just two of you? Is she still alive?”

“She is in her suit in the hut. Regolith cut a seal on the airlock. We are leaking air. I saw your lights. I’m sorry, but we don’t have any other hope.”

“No worries.” Darrin pulled up beside the man. “Get in.”

The stranger got in. “My name’s Bill. My wife is Helen.” 

“Plug in to the rover, and call your wife.”

Bill plugged his suit into the electrical outlet. “Helen, get out here. Do you see the lights? Come this way.” 

Darrin kept driving to the pile of regolith the man had pointed out. Buried, it just looked like a hill. Then he saw a woman come around the corner walking slowly towards the rover. 

“My air’s thin. I don’t have enough battery to recycle it.”

Darrin pulled to a stop next to her and got her inside. Bill plugged her in, and Darrin gave her suit a charge of *CO₂*. The recycler started processing it, releasing pure oxygen into her helmet. He put a new destination on his slate, Faraday Crater. He overrode the speed controls. When Bill and Helen had both recharged and brought their oxygen levels up, Darrin remembered to repressurized the rover. 

Three minutes later, they reached four psi. He took off his own helmet. “You can take your helmets off if you want. We’re OK in here. Are you hungry?”

They nodded in unison as they worked their helmets off. “My cup is dirty, but the water is hot.” 

He dropped another algae biscuit in the cup and added water. They used his fork to eat. “Since we couldn’t take our suits off, all we could eat was tube paste. We ran out days ago.”

“Help yourself to another biscuit.” Darrin pointed to a cabinet with twenty biscuits, and some flavor packets.

The high-wheel handled terrain smoothly, even at speed. After a couple hours, the Faraday Crater tower showed. Darrin’s slate chirped, surprising him. It had just sent his message to Li.

They pulled up next to the crater. “We need to suit up again. I’m going to open the door soon.” He hit the compressor while they were getting their helmets on. Sound disappeared, except over Local Band. “I am going to recharge and refill my oxygen tanks. What are you two going to do?”

“We can’t go back. Our hut is unlivable. I guess we just stay here at the tower, taking oxygen and power until dawn.”

“Come dawn, what are you going to do?”

There was a long pause. Helen answered. “I have no idea.”

“Have you heard of Tan’s Cantina?”

They nodded.

“That’s where you are going. She will feed you, and there is a hotel.”

“We can pay. We have twenty grams back at our hut.”

“You’re not at your hut. You stay on this line for four hours, then you will see the cantina. Tan will take care of you. Can you wash dishes?”

Darrin waited until his rover was fully charged and their suits were charged. He programmed their destination and scanned their QR codes. He sent a note to Li to expect them.

They hooked on and waved as they left.

Darrin reset his destination to the package’s expected impact site. Three hundred fifty miles. Three hundred to his stopping point. The rover started driving. He fell asleep. 

\*\*\*

Eight hours later, an alarm woke him. He had reached the stopping point. Darrin started driving himself towards the calculated impact point. Another one flashed overhead, coming toward him. His slate calculated the launch and impact points. The new calculations were with a half mile of his original calculations. He continued along slowly. The high-wheel was good for driving in rough areas, but not so good for hiding. Ten miles from the calculated site, he got out and started walking.

He walked about two miles when multiple spotlights lit up all around him. An open-top rover came up to him. The driver turned toward him. “You must be Darrin Brooks. We’ve been waiting for you. John Fletcher. I appreciate you making the trip. Get in.”

Darrin got in the rover. John continued. “My guy watched you over in Faraday Crater, Watched you leave, watched you come back, watched you leave again. You aren’t Tan, so he figured You must be Darrin Brooks.”

“How did he tell you? Messages in the containers?”

“No, we’ve got a fiber optic cable under the regolith. It’s for timing the launches, but it keeps us in touch. Do you want to see our setup?” 

“Sure.”

Fletcher drove over to the launch site. Four steel cups were nestled together. Another pair was out, and each half was partially loaded. The mass driver was a mile long. “This is Riccius, half a mile from an Odd Point Rendezvous site. Three hundred miles from Faraday Crater. Everyone within twenty miles of here trucks in their regolith, and I launch it to Faraday. The mass driver on that end catches it.”

“How can you be so precise?”

“It’s the moon. No coriolis force, no wind. So we can get pretty close. And the vessels have some controls.”

“Where do you get the power?”

“We need less than you might think. Launches use a lot, but landings are regenerative. We have solar panels and batteries, and since we don’t sweep, it’s easier. Still, we send less during lunar night.”

He took out his slate and showed a diagram. “I’m thinking of building my own cableway. We would start with a fifty miles radius, move up as demand and income allows. I have not decided on a cross or an asterisk. Either way, it will make it easier for them to get to rendezvous, and, more importantly, it will serve as a collection system for their regolith, which we send you.”

Darrin brought out his own slate, showing their design. “I found that a loo out here with a single line coming in from the loop is efficient. It makes the cableway next to your operations less complex. Then an asterisk spreading out from that. Miners regularly add infill lines. It has worked pretty well for us.”

“That looks nice. I will tell the team on Earth. For us, though, it is a question of ROI. All those decisions are made downstairs,” Fletcher said, pointing at Earth.

Fletcher drove to where the container was being loaded. “Quarter inch steel cups. Cost a fortune to ship these up from Earth. They sent me six pairs. Tough. When we were starting, I put a few of them into the pebble berm. They came out slightly dented, but fine. We hammered them out with sledgehammers.”

Darrin ran his hand along the steel. 

Fletcher brought him to a cup that was on its side, being loaded. “At each end is a guidance module. This small, tubular mass driver ejects regolith pellets at a thousand meters per second. The pellets are just sintered regolith. I put a ton in the front and in the back. They typically fire off a couple hundred pounds. The launch crushes them, so it is dust flying out. No matter. Probably safer. The pellets just make loading easier.”

“You see them loading in the bags. When both sides are full, they will join the cups and roll the vessel over to the launcher. My man on this side opens the vessel, takes the bags out and hangs them on your cableway. He picks up anything headed this way, such as empty bags, food, air, tools, parts, anything that can take high G forces. He then reseals the vessel and launches it back. Two-way flow of material. Regolith going toward you, material and supplies coming back.”

Darrin stared at Fletcher for a long moment. The concept was audacious. Ballistic cargo transport across three hundred miles of lunar surface but here it was, working. “Your mass driver must be amazing.”

“It’s great. Lets go look at the muzzle.” They got back in the rover and drove up to the end, which looked like a huge mound of pebbles.

“You can see our tube poking out through the hill there. It launches at a forty-five degree angle. You see that set of rings? They are packed with electromagnets allowing fine tuning on launch and final corrections on catch. The hill itself is pebbles. If a vessel misses, it runs straight into that pile, absorbing the energy quickly. The mass driver is only a mile long, and only starts curving to forty five degrees in the last third of the launch. Same on the other end.”

“Only a mile? That would need over twenty G. You would crush your cargo.”

“Twenty-five. We don’t ship cargo that would be crushed. Once a month we make the drive back and forth, carrying people and delicates.”

As they drove around the site, Darrin saw a sign.

			 *Send your regolith to Tycho*  
				 *30%*

“What’s that?”

“We never did any marketing or advertising except for those signs. We have six of them around here.”

“How is that working out?”

“Very well. We are making money and people keep bringing us their regolith.”

Darrin pulled up a chart showing mining growth under the cableway. “You can see it is growing everywhere. The fastest growth is under the six main lines. But lets just look at Faraday Crater.” He shifted the chart to show growth at that tower. “This includes the miners near Faraday Crater, those a little further out for whom Faraday is the closest tower, and you.”

“I can see the bump when we started shipping.”

“Me, too, but look at the growth. People near the tower are growing fast and are the wealthiest. Fifty percent growth since the tower was installed. Next are the people east of the crater that need to drive a long ways to get to it. Solid growth. Thirty percent. But look at the unknowns. Those are your people. Essentially flat.”

“I should advertise more.”

“Your problem isn’t awareness. Your customers aren’t thriving.”

“They’re doing fine. Churn is under ten percent.”

“Do you know why the miners who are leaving are doing so?”

“We are not worried about those low numbers. All businesses have churn.”

“Not all. We don’t. Let’s look at miner growth.” Darrin brought up a chart showing the change over time of productivity by QR code.

“Miners under the cableway are making money fast. They’re buying better equipment, eating better, working safer. Every month they produce more. Your customers aren’t making enough to upgrade. The churn you see is miners darksiding or getting so scared of darksiding they can’t stay.” 

“No one asked your opinion. We are doing alright.”

“When you send the regolith to Tycho, you asked my opinion. If we agree to keep processing these bags, it will be because we believe you are helping these miners thrive.”

Fletcher took a moment to breathe. He got his pulse back down. “Darrin, it’s not just about them. I have to thrive, too. We plan to extend this model all around your cableway and, eventually, all over the Moon. The more I make, the faster I can grow.”

“Your model can’t grow right now. You are hauling steel vessels up from Earth. You are trying to live on solar. And miners aren’t excited to work with you. Instead, you work with me.”

“What do you mean?”

“You need to build a cableway. I sell you what you need for that. Drop it off at Faraday Crater. I send you light titanium cable. Now you can make a cableway that doesn’t flex in the sun. You need vessels? I can stamp them. I can even run electric lines out here. I’ll charge you for it, but it will be steady power, day and night. My one requirement is you cut the miner’s fees to ten percent. Let them thrive, too. That is what I will agree to.”

“I’ll have to talk to the owners downstairs.”

“OK, you do that. And, when they sign, we will start processing your bags again.”

Fletcher jumped out of the rover. “Wait right here.” 

He turned and ran into an office. When he took his helmet off, he didn’t turn off Local Band. Darrin couldn’t hear everything, just the shouted parts. “Then wake him up\!” “He cut us off\! Zero revenue\!” “Ten Percent\!” “No Choice\!” The mumbling got fainter. 

Darrin sat in the rover for hours. He wrote a note to Li, including all the details while they were fresh. He knew the message would not get sent until he was under the cableway.

Two hours later, Fletcher returned. “The board was delighted with your ideas. They are very excited about the possibilities of our cooperation. I am authorized to sign a contract with your terms.”

“I am less interested in you signing a contract than I am with you following the terms. There aren’t any lawyers up here, anyway. Start by changing your signs. Next, go talk to the miners. Talk to one every day, get to know them and their needs.”

Fletcher brought Darrin back to the high-wheeler. Darrin set the destination to Faraday Crater and went back to sleep. He slept through the entire trip until his slate chirped. His message to Li had been sent. He hooked his rover to the line and slept four more hours until he arrived in Tycho. 

The terminal platform rose to meet him, friction dampers engaging smoothly. He unclipped and dropped onto familiar ground, feeling the vibration of the cableway system through his boots.

He cycled through the airlock, stripped off his pressure suit, and found Li in the cantina. “Welcome home, Darrin. Do you remember these two?” She pointed to Bill and Helen. “They didn’t just have a bad seal. All their equipment was cheap. I threw away their suits and sold them new ones. They could afford them. They had probably ten grams of *³He*.”

“Li, are you nuts? Those suits cost…”

Li looked at him sharply.

“I forget how much they cost. How much did you charge them?”

“A gram.”

“They look nice.”

“While you were off playing outside the cableway, Bill and Luke went back and pulled everything they had: helium, sweepers, solar panels, batteries, their rover, even their hut. Helen was a CPA before she came up here. She agreed to stay on. You know I need an accountant. Luke has Bill working on the ovens, for now. Point is, they agreed to stay.”

“I got your note. Thirty percent was too much. How did it go?” she asked without looking up.

“I got him down to ten percent.” Darrin pulled up a chair. “But we need to talk about our extraction fees.”

She looked up sharply. “What about my fee?”

“I told Fletcher we’d consider dropping to fifteen percent for his incoming cargo. From twenty.”

Li’s expression went flat. “You told him what?”

“Hear me out.”

“Fifteen percent? Darrin, do you understand what we provide?” She gestured at the window overlooking the extraction facility. “We maintain all of that. We staff all that. And now you want me to cut our fee by a quarter?”

“I know what we’ve built. But I also know what we haven’t built. Ninety-eight percent of the Moon is waiting for you. Bill and Helen? Luck. Darksides happen daily.”

“If they had stopped here first, I’d have checked their gear.”

“You can’t check everyone’s gear. But you can expand. Fletcher thinks he is making money in transportation. I think he is extending Tycho Crater across the Moon. Help him build it, for us.”

Li was quiet for a moment, studying the schematic. “We still lose five percent on extraction fees.”

“Do we?” Darrin zoomed out on the map, showing the full lunar sphere. “Right now we cover two percent of the Moon’s surface and generates sixty percent of lunar *³He*. The other ninety eight percent of the Moon? It’s just sitting there. Billions of tons of regolith that’s barely being mined.”

Li studied the map in silence. She heard him. This was huge. Still, she felt he hadn’t heard her. “Darrin, the Moon is not London or DC. We don’t have lawyers. We need simple agreements. Twenty percent is simple. I change that, everyone will start looking for a deal. My fee stands.”

Darrin started to object, but she held up a hand.

“But,” she continued, “I’ll rebate five percent to Fletcher on all regolith that comes through his network during the growth phase. He charges the miners ten percent for transport, gets five percent rebate from us on their extraction fee, we charge twenty percent. Net cost to the miners is still thirty percent, Fletcher makes fifteen percent during construction, and we keep our nominal rate.”

“You sure?”

“I’m sure.” She turned back to her workstation. “Now go get some sleep. You look exhausted.”

He started to protest, but she was right. The adrenaline was wearing off, leaving him hollow and tired.

“Wake me if anything comes up,” he said.

“Nothing’s going to come up. Go.”

He made his way to the hut, stripped off his clothes, and collapsed into the bed. Sleep came immediately.

## 

## **Chapter 30: Batidas**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

The design came together quietly, over late evenings and sketches on slates passed around the table. There was no formal announcement, just the slow coalescence of agreement between engineers who trusted one another’s instincts. Daily there were seminars, small talk given by each researcher where they described their works in progress. 

Rithika Patel stood confidently at the podium, surveying the audience of her peers. She cleared her throat and began her talk.

“Good morning, everyone. Today, I’d like to present our design for a helium separation system.”

She clicked a button, and a diagram of the system appeared on the screen behind her.

“Our design uses a combination of compressors, membrane separators, and cryocoolers to achieve high-purity helium. The process starts with the ingestion of atmosphere at a steady rate, optimized for the density band around 2 atmospheres of pressure.”

Rithika gestured to the diagram. “The hydrogen-helium mix is ingested by a large fan which blows the air across a zeolite membrane shield. The membrane passes only the hydrogen and helium.”

A colleague in the audience raised his hand. “Rithika, can you explain the choice of membrane material for this shield?”

Rithika nodded. “The shield must block ammonia, sulfur compounds, and phosphines which would cause downstream problems. We are modeling this using a thin silicalite-1 zeolite with high porosity to minimize the pressure drop across the membrane. The relatively fragile zeolite requires strong backing to support it, which we provide with a glass honeycomb.”

She clicked the button again, and a graph appeared on the screen.

“Once ingested, the gas is compressed in an axial flow turbine. The ambient pressure is two bar. We compress it to nearly three bar. Compression also heats the gas. Here it passes through a cone twenty meters long, ten meters in diameter. The cone is lined with a five-micron foil of a palladium-silver alloy over a high-strength inconel mesh. Hydrogen passes through this foil readily. By the end of the cone, the gas mixture contains less than one percent hydrogen.”

“That is an astonishing level of extraction.”

“Yes, palladium grabs the hydrogen quickly. Silver helps the hydrogen pass through the foil quickly and, of course, the high temperature helps.”

Rithika continued. “The hot hydrogen is passed back through a countercurrent flow with the incoming gas, heating up that gas before compression. The retentate is still hot. An intercooler brings it down to ambient temperature, 165K, then it gets refrigerated to about six Kelvin in a cryogenic recuperator. At that point, the helium passes through a nozzle, where a Joule-Thomson expansion drops the pressure and rapidly cools the gas for the final isotopic separation.”

“How cold does it come out?”

She clicked the button again, and a diagram of the fractional distillation tower appeared.

“A little less than five Kelvin. Ready to enter the fractional distillation column. The column is packed with aerogel, and the top is kept at 3.7K. The *⁴He* rains out and goes back to the cryogenic recuperator. The final gas phase is high-purity *³He.*”

Rithika concluded her talk. “Our combined design isolates high-purity *³He* efficiently. We’ve run the numbers, and we believe we can achieve a theoretical yield of approximately ten tons per day per engine, using about a hundred gigajoules per kilogram.”

The audience applauded, and afterwards, several colleagues approached her to ask questions and offer feedback. 

Nikola Savic, a new grad student in orbital mechanics, was dazzled. He came up to talk to her. “Your system is the most efficient system I’ve seen. Off the record, do you have any idea how to get the *³He* off the dirigible?”

“As far as I know, that stage is theoretically impossible. Rockets can’t lift off from Jupiter’s atmosphere. I try not to think about it,” she said with a wink.

Savic’s mind was spinning on the walk home. Real extraction, as much as you please, as long as you want. There just had to be a way to get the *³He* off. When he burst through the door, his roommate, Marco, looked up from his tablet, eyebrows raised at Savic’s unusual energy.

“You should have been there,” Savic said, dropping his bag and heading straight for the kitchen. “Rithika just solved helium extraction. Ten tons per day from a single engine. It’s brilliant. They use membrane separation, fractional distillation, the whole thing integrated into a dirigible that can actually stay aloft in Jupiter’s atmosphere.”

Marco nodded politely. He was an atmospheric chemist, comfortable with theory but not particularly interested in engineering details.

“The problem,” Savic continued, grabbing a drink from the fridge, “is that it’s completely useless.”

“Useless?”

“All that beautiful engineering, and the helium just sits there in the dirigible. There’s no way to get it off Jupiter. The gravity well is too deep.”

Savic slumped into the chair across from Marco, the excitement draining from his face. “She solved the hard part. And it doesn’t matter at all because no one can solve the impossible part.”

Marco looked at him for a moment. “You’re an orbital mechanic, right? That’s what you do. You figure out how to move things through space.”

“This isn’t moving things through space. This is escaping from Jupiter. Do you know what the escape velocity is? Sixty kilometers per second. That’s not engineering, that’s physics saying no.”

“So figure it out,” Marco said, returning to his tablet. “That’s literally your job.”

The challenge hung in the air. Savic stared at the ceiling, then pulled out his slate and started sketching orbital diagrams.

For the next week, he attacked the problem methodically. The tyranny of the rocket equation made chemical rockets hopeless. Planes were better. Electric ramjets. They could lift the helium high in the atmosphere, but there was no way to get it from a plane out of the Jovian gravity well.

Mass drivers came next. Electromagnetic launchers were used on Ceres, the Moon, and Mars. They were very efficient, both in energy and mass. No propellant, just electrical energy converted to kinetic energy through magnetic fields. A theoretical best case.

Savic spent three days modeling a mass driver capable of launching a ten-ton bottle of *³He* from the Jovian atmosphere to escape velocity. The numbers were staggering. The launcher would need to be hundreds of miles long to achieve the necessary acceleration without crushing a steel bottle. The power requirements were even worse. To launch one kilogram of *³He* would require the entire output of a large fission reactor. 

He calculated the infrastructure required: massive superconducting coils, cryogenic cooling systems, power distribution networks that could handle gigawatt pulses. The platform would need to be the size of a small city just to house the equipment. That wouldn’t fit on Rithika’s elegant dirigible. 

Savic pushed back from his desk in frustration. The mass driver was clearly impractical, but maybe it would be theoretically possible if Rithika’s design didn’t cruise at two atmospheres. That left an impossible amount of atmosphere above the rig. The drag crushed all hope. It would vaporize the bottle.

He had another idea, imagining a very light tethered balloon rising high with just this one cargo, and launching from far above all the clouds. But it made too small of a change. 

Like everything else he’d tried, it ran up against the fundamental problem: Jupiter’s gravity well was simply too deep for current technology to climb out of economically.

He was an orbital mechanic, supposedly. That’s what his advisor said. But even orbital mechanics had limits.

At the end of the week, Marco found Savic hunched over calculations, barely moving. “Come with me, my man. You need food. And maybe some perspective. I found a Cuban place up in Baracoa, Café Havana. They have live music tonight. My treat.”

Savic reluctantly agreed. He wasn’t getting anywhere right now. “Alright. I can’t even find a fresh way for the physics to say no. Let’s go have fun. The problem will still be here when I get back.”

“I invited you, not your slate,” Marco said, pulling the slate from Savic’s pocket and putting it face down on the desk.

They took a JRC shuttle bus to Baracoa and walked down to the cafe. The evening was warm, and they grabbed a table on the patio where a guitarist was setting up in the corner. The familiar comfort of black beans and rice, the smell of garlic and cumin, the gentle pulse of música criolla were exactly what Savic needed.

“So,” Marco said, attacking his ropa vieja, “still trying to escape Jupiter?”

“Not tonight,” Savic said, raising his mojito. “Tonight I’m officially not thinking about orbital mechanics.”

The guitarist began playing a slow and melodic tune that mixed with the harbor breeze. Savic let himself sink into the moment and enjoy the food, the music, and the simple pleasure of not calculating mass ratios or power requirements.

Across the patio, two women were seated together, drinking batidas, a thick frozen drink. He could only see the face of one of them, but it was captivating. She was clearly enjoying the music. A young man asked her friend to dance, and the woman he was watching was alone. “I’m gonna enjoy myself tonight,” he told Marco.

Marco followed Savic’s gaze. “Out of your league, Nick. Just enjoy the music.” 

Savic stood and began walking toward her, marveling at the sparkle of her eyes, the bounce of her hair, and the way her cheeks looked when she drew in her batida through the straw. He watched absently as she worked the straw, pulling the drink from the bottom of the glass to her mouth. Such a simple mechanism. Direct connection. No intermediary steps, no complex machinery. Just suction and…

He stopped.

A straw.

He went straight back to the table and started talking to Marco. “You saw that woman drinking a batida through a straw? She isn’t really sucking it up, you know. She reduces the air pressure in her mouth, but it is the air pressure in the room that’s pushing it up through the straw. That is why her drink is flowing.”

Marco hardly knew what to do when Savic talked this way. Anything could suddenly turn into a lesson in physics, and Marco had no idea why it was so fascinating to Savic. 

“You’re thinking about work again,” Marco said.

Savic was lost in thought. He couldn’t even hear Marco. His head was filled with material profiles and energy diagrams.

When they finished eating, Marco paid the bill. On the shuttle back to Jamal, Savic’s mouth kept running. Marco was tired. He was happy to see his friend so animated, but he felt overwhelmed by the discussion. He rolled his eyes and let the words wash over him. When they got home, he went to bed. Savic opened his slate and began doing calculations.

The next morning Savic called together some other grad students who had been at Rithika’s talk and his own advisor, Dr. Miriam Vance, to talk about the idea. “I think we all know that Rithika’s dirigibles are the best way to collect *³He* from Jupiter, but getting the *³He* out of the atmosphere, much less the gravity well, is completely impossible.” Rithika nodded as his friends murmured in agreement. “No ship can fly in, pick up a bottle of *³He* and then leave. The delta V is just too much. So I spent days looking at mass drivers, hoping to send cryocontainers of *³He* out.”

“The gravity well is too deep.”

“Tell me about it. I looked at a five-hundred-mile-long mass driver to get the power down to three hundred megawatts per ton. And it still needs terajoules, even ignoring atmospheric drag. I hoped to use the magnetosphere to help, but I was really just grasping at straws. Fundamentally, it is impossible.” A few heads nodded. 

“Last night, I saw something new. A woman was drinking through a straw. And it struck me. The atmosphere already pushes itself up. With an engineered ‘straw’ the atmosphere would apply most of the force, and we would only need to apply a little extra to accelerate the *³He* and overcome friction, allowing it to rise up through the straw.”

“Do you want to make a *³He* fountain in the exoatmosphere?”

“At first I didn’t know what I wanted. An exoatmospheric fountain would have been a huge step forward. Then I remembered an old space travel concept, space elevators, which allowed you to launch heavy payloads into space without rockets.”

“Space elevators never worked.” 

“They never made practical sense because by the time we could build a cable strong enough, LEO had way too many satellites. For a time, they talked about anchoring it on a ship, and letting the ship move around to avoid satellites, but you could never dodge the satellites fast enough. It can’t work on Earth. But we are talking about Jupiter. No satellites inside the orbit of Callisto, and the planet spins really fast.”

“You realize, of course, that the gravity in the Jovian system is immense.”

“Yes, that’s true. Hence the original problem. So I did a calculation. If Rithika’s dirigible is in the equatorial stream, we can calculate based on equatorial, or System I, rotation. But there is a band above that in which the equatorial jet is five hundred kilometers per hour faster. Geostationary from there is 88,000 km. We will be well inside the Galilean moons, even with runout for a counterbalance. I am calling these split rigs, since there is a dirigible and a space station working together, separated by an 88,000 kilometer straw.”

“No material could possibly handle the stress.”

“I am not a materials guy, but it looks like carbon nanotubes might. The tube would need a strong taper, maybe twenty or thirty times as thick at Jovian stationary orbit as at the dirigible. Then it thins out again as the cable proceeds to the counterbalance.”

Hope seemed to glimmer. If they could pump the *³He* up, they could get around the impossible launch problem. The team grew cautiously excited. This was far from a solved problem, but some of the impossible parts had been converted to very difficult. 

Savic’s advisor, Dr. Vance, called a meeting with him a few days later. “Fascinating talk you gave, Nick. That sounded impromptu.”

“Yes, well, Rithika’s scheduled talk a week ago got me thinking, and last night I had a thought and wanted to run it by some people.”

“It is an intriguing idea. I expect it will not work, but even chasing down a dead end can be a fascinating adventure. Do you think this is a topic you can write your thesis on?”

“Are you kidding? I’d love to.”

“Here is what I will need you to do. I am going to get you a room for this. A small conference room with too many whiteboards. You and your friends are free to use that room whenever you want. What you need to do, though, is divide the problem into a set of smaller problems.”

“That sounds great.”

“Here is how you pay for it. Every week, someone from your group will give a talk on one aspect of this concept. I want clear titles, and deeply detailed talks.”

“Every week? What if I don’t have progress one week?”

“You can talk about any detail of the project that interests you. We learn as much from failure as success. More, actually, since it is so much more common and really makes us examine the research. On my part, I will see to it that there are always snacks at your talks.”

They began working on it, after hours, in locked rooms. The JRC didn’t pry as long as there were regular meetings with titles they could show the world to prove they were working on something. And Savic’s talks drew larger crowds each week. Savic let the others talk, too. Every week he would file the talk with a title, a transcript, the slides, and a count of the attendees. No one read it, but JRC tracked the attendee count.

There were numerous subproblems, and people picked them up as they could. Each problem got its own whiteboard, showing its current state.

The first was how to get the helium up the straw. Rithika herself came up with a plan for this. She proposed a series of simple booster pumps spaced five thousand kilometers apart, each adding about five psi. Not the only choice at all, and subject to optimization, but good enough. It was enough to keep the helium flowing. This was marked solved.

Jason, an electrical engineer from Michigan, attacked the energy problem. The total demand for extraction and pumping was around five hundred megawatts per engine. That would take eight thousand acres of solar panels near Jupiter. Not easy. Not possible. But Jason had an idea. Whisper-thin aluminized mylar formed to concentrate the light, bringing eight thousand acres down to eight hundred acres. Still enormous, and now there were new problems. Alignment had to be precise, and the mylar sheets were fragile. 

Distribution was another issue. They needed power on both ends of the split rig. The mass of an aluminum cable was enormous, and the resistance was too much. Jason split it up. He put a large solar collector on the rig, another large one five thousand kilometers up at the first booster pump, and a small one at each booster pump after that. His ideas drew interest, but were not a solution. No one had improvements. The whiteboard stayed static.

A related problem was maintaining the verticality of the tube. As gas would flow up, it would need to be accelerated laterally. With a little, that was fine, but for tons of gas, the tube would curve. Savic knew this was his problem. Tons of gas going up, all needing a gentle nudge to accelerate laterally. The force came from the sides of the straw in the cable. Savic added a second straw, but for this one he pumped in a propellant gas, and set up a series of ion emitters along the cable. Unprocessed atmosphere made sense and is very light, but it would also cause corrosion and hydrogen embrittlement. Then it struck him that Rithika’s design created enormous quantities of purified *⁴He*. Using helium as the propellant worked well. It did not erode the tube, pumps or ion emitters and had a little more mass reducing the energy requirements. Respecting Rithika’s booster pump plan, he put one ion emitter at each booster pump. Using this active system changed the energy consumption dramatically, requiring an extra four hundred megawatts just for the emitters, and requiring fifteen tons of *⁴He* to be pumped up per day to be used as a propellant, increasing the pumping expense another hundred megawatts. Savic finished his whiteboard and reluctantly restated the problem on Jason’s board, which still had no closure. 

Then there was material degradation. The tube would be bathed in Jupiter’s radiation belts, high-energy protons and electrons eroding the material on timescales measured in months. The atmospheric end would face ammonia crystals and hydrogen sulfide aerosols, each gust stripping away microns. The tether would fray unless wrapped in protective layers that added prohibitive weight and complexity. Savic prevailed on Marco to address this. The obvious solution was a nice, solid coating of gold. The mass was far too high. But Marco looked at only protecting the last thousand miles. This helped enormously. Rather than gold, he put on a coating of boron nitride nanotubes (BNNT) over this region. They were far more stable, but didn’t have quite the strength-to-weight ratio of CNTs. The induced weight penalty hurt everything else, and was made worse because it still eroded, so it had to be made overly thick. Marco outdid himself on that, though. He had crawlers moving up and down the tether inspecting it everywhere. When a defect was found, the crawler immediately did repairs using BNNTs. This allowed a thinner coating, because it would be repaired as it eroded. No one liked the weight penalty or the added complexity of this maintenance machine, but it seemed unavoidable. The team signed off on Marco’s board.

The dirigible skin faced similar problems. Hydrogen seeped through most membranes over time and it embrittled metals. Jupiter’s atmospheric chemistry was relentless. Materials that lasted years in Earth’s stratosphere broke down in weeks here. Each dirigible would be operational for months at best before needing a complete overhaul. Marco worked a separate whiteboard for the dirigible, using aluminized coatings for the ballonetes, BNNT coatings for the skin, and rigorous crew inspection and maintenance. These were not solutions, per se, but tolerable degradation rates. 

This was the furthest anyone had gotten. Better than anything else in the literature. The concept was sound; it was the execution that remained impossible. Materials never really got solved, and power was completely impossible. But every week, Savic or someone else in the group would give another talk, and Savic dutifully filed the reports. The split-rig stayed on whiteboards, a beautiful impossibility. At least it was an interesting topic.

## **Chapter 31: Tan’s Cantina**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** | **14 tons / month** |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

Li found Darrin working on the ovens. “Ready to go check it out?”

“Just a minute. I needed to replace the drive belt. I will be ready in five.”

“Don’t worry, Darrin, I’ve got it,” Bill said.

Darrin passed the wrench to Bill. “Thanks. I am being summoned.”

He then looked at Li. “Is it ready for a walk through?”

“It’s overdue.”

They hooked up to the cableway and glided across the crater floor, about twenty miles to the prominence. Darrin looked at the phase of the Earth. Ten days to dawn.

They got off the industrial cableway and hooked onto an ornate cable that went up a well-lit path. 

“I didn’t expect it to be so bright,” Li said.

Darrin looked at the enormous dome. “They added titanium dioxide to the mix. It looks like marble.”

The entrance was a large revolving door. There was a large emergency airlock beside it.

“These revolving doors are going to be a maintenance nightmare,” Darrin said.

“Ellie told me they did this at Bounty. It’s elegant.”

Near the entrance, a simple line drawing of her face hung over a sign: “Welcome to Tan’s Cantina.”

They walked past the empty bar. Li checked sight lines, noted the mirrored wall behind the counter, tested the Earth window placement from different angles. The window was set into the dome’s northern curve, two meters across. She looked at the full Earth, imagining the shaft of blue-white light it would cast across the floor when the lights were dimmed.

On the stage, a smooth jazz trio from Chicago was practicing.

“How long are they here?” Darrin asked.

“Three weeks here, then one week at Fletcher’s Antipodes before they return here for a closing show.”

“Fletcher built a club?” Darrin asked.

“I hear it’s nice. Much smaller, of course. He should build more. Miners need someplace to visit.”

“They need a haven.”

Helen came in. “Tan, Amazon sent another message. They want a million square feet.”

“I’ll site them a couple miles from the depot. It will be fine. But I want you to word the response like it is a huge problem,” Li said. “We are still negotiating the rent.”

“What’s up with Amazon?” Darrin asked.

“With Fletcher connecting the Moon, Amazon thinks they can get most deliveries to customers in a day. Helen, what would you have done if you and Bill could have had a delivery in a day?”

“We would have replaced the airlock and been fine. We would still be mining.”

“The whole Moon needs that, but we don’t need to tell them that, yet.”

Helen left, and Li and Darrin kept walking around the cantina. They stepped out the east balcony.

“Do you like having Case out there? Without them it is an unspoiled view.”

“I like having them. Easier to buy new equipment, and now there are mechanics.”

“Komatsu and Sany Group also want to open dealerships.”

“There’s plenty of space. We could handle ten manufacturers. My only concern is that we can kick them out if their equipment is no good.”

“It’s still my crater.”

They continued on the balcony until they were looking south. “The row houses look very comfortable. Helen loves hers. Things are growing so fast. Where are you hiring from?”

“I rarely get anyone under the cableway anymore. I have a deal with Andrei. Whenever someone is calling it quits on the Moon, he lets me know and I go talk to them. Some are happy to stay if they can be someplace like this: steady work, nice housing, power, hospitality, and community. For me, I love our hut. I think we chose well when we bought it, and it has grown on me since.”

Li glanced at him quickly. “I want to show you the hotel. Have you looked at it?”

“Only from the outside. Let’s see it.”

Li led him inside, and they walked through a few rooms. They had no windows, just small monitors in each room with a current view of Earth. She brought him to the top floor.

“Look at this room, Darrin. What do you think?”

Darrin went around the apartment. High ceilings. A real Earth window. The bedroom had a door and a full sized bed. It had a separate shower room with a bathtub as well. Kitchen, office, and a large room that was really just for nothing. You could sit there and talk.

“What is this room for? Visiting dignitaries? It is so extravagant. No one can afford this, Li.”

“We can. I built this for us. The area where our hut is interferes with the shopping mall I have planned. We could dig it out and move it, but I think this may be nicer.”

Darrin showed only his practical face. The tiny hut on the north edge of the prominence had been their home since they first arrived at Tycho crater. “If you like, Li.”

Li looked away. “Darrin, I need to stop by home before we go to the opening. I can’t be Tan tonight wearing a pressure suit.”

They took a rover to their home. Li asked Darrin to wait outside. Darrin felt alone when she entered the airlock. She was quick. Keeping her pressure suit and helmet on, she grabbed a large garment bag and came back out. 

“OK, I’m ready,” Li said, getting back on the rover. Darrin drove them to a cableway station, and they took the cableway up to the Cantina. 

Back in her office, Li opened the bag and took out a black silk qipao with red and gold embroidery and a simple suit. “Let’s get changed,” she said as she began undressing.

“Me, too?” Darrin asked, as he began changing. “I should comb my hair.” 

When they were ready, they stepped out into the cantina. Lights were low. The full Earth shone brightly through the Earth window, casting a dazzling beam of bluish light on the floor. There was a table stacked with champagne from France and another with bubbly amaranth chichas from Mars. Li picked up the oversized scissors from behind the bar and they went to the elevator landing where the miners were waiting behind the red ribbon. 

“Welcome to the all-new Tan’s Cantina\!” she said cutting the ribbon, letting the miners in. They flooded through, whooping and cheering as the band began playing “Moonland”, a reworked version of “Birdland.”

Darrin was immediately caught up by various friends he had met as he worked on the cableway. He sipped a chicha while talking to Rook. “Didn’t expect to see you here, Rook. I figured you’d avoid the crowds and drop by a week later,” Darrin said with a wink.

“What, and miss my one chance of seeing you all gussied up in a suit? Ha\!” Rook replied. “Nice tie.” Rook smirked and took two chichas and went to find some roast cavy. 

Darrin blushed a little, but it was a nice suit and the dark red tie looked great. He talked to some guys from Ocean State Resources. Most of the miners were in their singlets they wore under their pressure suits, but Ocean State miners put on jumpsuits over that. A little more formal. They had almost completed their second claim, and were talking about starting a new claim beyond the cableway.

Li was in a different world. Several corporate representatives had come up from Earth for this event. Amazon had sent a representative, Case had, but it was Mark Davis from David Clark Company that caught her attention first, eager to get up here before Oceaneering. “Tan, have you seen our new pressure suits? They are much more durable than what most people are using up here. I was thinking, what if we set up a storefront, nothing huge, out next to Case? Someplace miners could find replacements or upgrades.”

“How much space would you need?” she asked, playing a little more naive than she really was.

Mark pulled out his slate. “A showroom for a few different models and components, a couple dressing rooms, a workspace where we can refit and repair suits, and storage space. Total is about a thousand feet. If you had unpressurized warehouse space, we would probably like that much again.”

Li laughed when he mentioned dressing rooms. Earthers were so self conscious. “Yeah, I think we can accommodate that. Unpressurized space is easy. I will set that up in the north, near Amazon. Your retail space will be ready in two months, connected to the cantina. Two hundred dollars per foot, plus twenty percent of the gross. I will give you a premium spot near the entrance.”

Mark didn’t know what to expect on the space, but her taking a cut of the gross was not something his company would like. “How much would it be if we just paid upfront, no cut of the gross?”

“Oh, that would be fine. Tycho Crater is not for everyone. Do you have a claim?”

“No, I mean here. Here is where we want to be.”

“OK. Because here in my claim I can get you a thousand feet of premium mall space for two hundred dollars per foot plus twenty percent of the gross. Interested?”

“Say we were. Tell me about the space we would get.”

Tan led him to her office. She brought up the mall plans on a projector. “Here is the mall we are planning. Twenty thousand square feet on two floors. The main door will be facing north. First building from the depot and there is a cableway stop there. When they enter, you will be the first thing they see. I will let you stay there as long as you are the top performing business in the mall.”

“As long as we are the top performer?”

“Have you ever shopped in a grocery store? You notice that sometimes the canned food is on aisle one, but then you come back in a few months and it is on aisle two? I am not building a big mall here, and in that limited space I want companies that really make life better for miners. I will rearrange where the stores are to do that. Soon Oceaneering will be here, and Teijin Frontier will want a space. And customers will decide which of you is most important.”

“Customers? A minute ago you said sales.”

“Yes, well, sales are how customers tell me which company is most important.”

“OK, Tan, you win. We have to be here. Just give me the papers, and I will sign the agreement.”

“Papers?” Tan looked through her Earth window. “Earth is adorable. On Earth, papers would make a lease enforceable in court. But this is the Moon. No court. No papers. We agree, and you can move in as soon as the structure is built.”

Mark and Li returned to the cantina. Mark took a glass of champagne, thinking about how this would go over when he called the home office. They were not going to like the terms. They would blame him, as if he ever had a choice. He downed the first glass and picked up another.

Darrin caught her eye. He motioned to the floor. “May I have this dance?” 

Li blushed and demurred. “Let’s just stand here next to each other.” 

As she watched the other miners in the shaft of earthlight, it struck her. Tycho Crater had become a city. The only real city off Earth.

## **Chapter 32: Corporate Meddling**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

After the heavy lift project, Halden was promoted to Senior System Analyst. Every day Halden’s job devolved to making a report of daily launches, which he fully automated. He spent his days reading the titles of the scheduled talks to be given at the JRI. Everyone in JRC was allowed to attend any JRI seminar. None of his coworkers ever did. 

The talks were often exercises in academic diplomacy, full of progress reports on projects that were progressing nowhere in particular. Halden skipped past the safe research. Then, three pages in, a title caught his eye.

“Atmospheric Tether Systems for Jovian Gas Extraction”

Atmospheric tethers. He’d never seen those words combined before. The core concept was a physical connection between Jupiter’s atmosphere and orbital space. Direct resource extraction without the impossible energy costs of heavy lift vehicles.

Halden leaned back in his chair. This could be theoretical materials research dressed up with an exotic application. Or it could be the kind of systems-level thinking that actually solved problems instead of just studying them.

Either way, it deserved a closer look. He marked it in his calendar. 

The day of the talk he took the JRC/JRI shuttle to Jamal. Halden got a small plate with grapes and cheese and sat to the side. Savic was speaking about tapers and diameters of the tether straw.

Halden was intrigued by the talk, but he was fascinated by how involved the JRI people were. The talk drew a good crowd, including several professors. Halden put the series on his recurring calendar.

He sat in the meetings quietly, absorbing who the people were. He read abstracts of the earlier talks, and started attending other talks by the key players, learning who the people were as much as what the project was. He even began to chat with them after the talks.

During one such conversation, he followed them into the project room. He saw the whiteboards for the first time. The entire concept, broken into subproblems, each of which had been worked extensively. He read each one carefully, taking pictures. Back in his office, he recreated the whiteboards. Between the talks, he worked on them. Found inconsistencies. Wrote his own ideas.

About a month after he started attending, Rithika was giving a talk on *³He* purification. Halden raised a hand. “What is the pressure where your dirigible will be operating?”

She was caught off guard. “When I conceived of this, I was planning to operate at one to two Bar, but recently Savic changed the plan on orbital level. He noticed a high velocity equatorial band at fifty millibar. So I should adjust to use that pressure.” 

By the second month, Halden had reframed the problem. He caught Savic and asked him to bring just a few to the project room. Halden was standing in front of a new whiteboard. “What is your project here?”

“I noticed a way to use Rithika’s extraction system with a straw to get *³He* into orbit. We thought this might be the solution to Jovian extraction, but there are too many problems, especially energy and durability.”

“Two brothers back in North Carolina thought people could fly. They didn’t know anything about it. They just made bicycles. But they made the worlds worst glider with an engine and it hopped one hundred twenty feet. Their invention didn’t teach anyone how to fly. Planes never looked much like theirs. Why did it matter?”

“The Wright brothers solved the control problem,” Rithika answered.

“They did, but the reason it mattered was, until that one hundred twenty foot hop, flight was all talk. After that, powered flight was real. Everyone got involved. Within a decade, aviation was an industry. I don’t need you to build an airliner. I just need the Orville Flyer. I want a prototype that can endure Jupiter long enough to deliver a single kilogram of *³He* to orbit. We can get that sample to Earth and I will set up a press conference and burn that kilogram of Jovian *³He* in Ilha Fusão. If we can do that, the world changes overnight.”

“And if it fails?” Savic asked.

“It fails quietly. No one will know,” Halden said. 

Rithika was the first to catch on. “My design is for throughput. If you want just a sample, it shrinks down. A multi-stage centrifugal compressor, let the hydrogen bleed out. The dirigible was to support more equipment. We can just let the compressor hang from the tether. I need to simplify purification.” Halden handed her a marker and she put her new proposal on his board.

“Savic, is there something you can do to simplify this?” Halden asked.

Savic stood up and went to Halden’s board. “The work of a space elevator is not so much lifting the payload as it is accelerating the payload tangentially. Hence the string of emitters. But, for a short run, we dont need the complication. We start the process moving too fast, and let the tether straw’s momentum speed it up. After a month, it will be strongly arcing, and maybe break. By then, we will be done.”

Step by step, the goal simplified, until they came to energy. “Making it smaller helps, but it is still a hard problem. I have to power real pumps, and we do not have the power for it,” Jason said.

Halden took the marker. “JRC is not bound by the fissile material ban. What about a KRUSTY reactor? Those are the size of a roll of paper towels.”

Jason’s eyes widened. “If we can use nukes, we can do this.” He began looking up compact fission designs on his slate.

Marco raised his hand. “Excuse me, Halden, sir. I understand your objective, and all the great ideas my colleagues are sharing, but this project is not mostly about helium collection. Mostly it is that straw. My advisor and I have worked out the mass of the straw under current taper models. We believe a production system would weigh twenty million tons, maybe more. The prototype you are proposing will be lighter, and move less gas. We can make the straw a hundred times thinner. Even so, it is still going to be at least two hundred thousand tons of extremely high grade CNT reinforced polymer. That doesn’t look like a prototype.”

Savic was still at the board. “We are looking at ninety thousand kilometers from upper atmosphere to stationary orbit. We are looking at stationary with respect to the upper equitorial band, because that is moving faster. The faster we are synchronized with, the closer synchronous orbit is. I want to put this in a nuclear ramjet, moving at five thousand meters per second. We can do that on Jupiter. The advantage is, at that speed, we can move synchronous orbit down thirty thousand kilometers. Rithika, you started with a dirigible, right? Why was that?”

“The collection and purification steps require a lot of heavy equipment. A dirigible lifts all that without burning energy to fight gravity.”

“Now we don’t have all that heavy equipment, and I don’t mind burning energy for a short prooof of concept. The intake of your compressor could be bleed from the compressed intake.”

Marco stood up. “Your idea brings us a thousand new problems. We don’t know how to build the jet. But if we reduce the height to synchronous orbit like that, we can build this with less than five thousand tons of material. Exponentials are harsh when you ask more, but benevolent when you ask for less.”

The researchers were working together and didn’t notice when Halden left the meeting. On the shuttle, he reviewed the plan. The system would not last, but it would return material from Jupiter. JRC would celebrate the delivery. For a day. Then they would have nowhere left to hide.
