## **Chapter 33: Triangulation**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

Amira Bastos sat in her office looking out on Bahía de Miel, a perk of being the CEO. But right now she had a call to make. 

“Hello, this is Gary Thompson, General Atomics space division.”

“Hi, Gary, this is Amira Bastos. What is this request for authorization to refuel one of your reactors in space? You know there is a fissile launch ban.”

“Yes, ma’am. However, JRC is exempt from that ban. We have an order here from Terrence Halden requesting a refueled nuclear reactor for use in orbit around Jupiter.”

“Halden? Who is Halden?”

Gary brought up the requestor line on the order form. “Well, ma’am, it says here he is a senior systems analyst in applied research.”

“Hold that order. I need to look into this.”

“Very well, ma’am. I await your call.”

Bastos walked out to the receptionist and asked to see Halden immediately. The nature of a large organization like JRC is that something being done immediately may take a week or so. In this case, Halden was in her office in less than three minutes.

“What is this order from General Atomics?”

“Yes, the order for a refueled reactor? Well within my budget authority. We are planning a total PR coup for JRC.”

“You might have mentioned it to me first.”

Halden was amused at the absurdity of her comment. As if he could just drop by and chat. “Yes, ma’am. I should have let you know.”

“OK, what is this PR project about?”

“I had an idea about extracting *³He* from Jupiter. I got the lab guys at JRI to work out the details. It looks like we can pull out a kilogram of *³He* from Jupiter and bring it to Earth. We would burn that at Ilha Fusão, providing one month’s power with real Jovian fuel. I think that will get some pressure off your back.”

“Are you certain this will work?”

“I am not. My plan is no press until the helium is on its way here. If we fail up there, no one need know about it except us.”

Bastos looked out on the bay again. People did not understand that this was not as simple as just flipping a switch. Raines had been publicly complaining about the lack of progress repeatedly, and others were starting to join in. This might be just the project to buy some breathing room. But Halden’s leash was clearly too long.

“OK, here is what we will do. You proceed, but keep me informed. I want a status report every week. Two things: no nuclear power, and don’t let Raines or anyone from Helion Dynamics find out about it.”

“Yes, ma’am, but one thing.”

“What?”

“Nothing can get us to Jupiter and back except a General Atomics ship.”

“You can take a Lunar Tug ship.”

“They are promising to have ships that can do it soon, but right now they are limited to Mars, and they are very slow. With our payload, it would take several trips, probably decades.”

She stared at him for over a minute, searching for a weakness. “I hate orbital mechanics. Fine. Nukes are authorized. Keep it low key.”

“Of course, ma’am.”

At JRI, the prototype was coming apart under its own complexity. The light ramjet design was not coming together and there was no easy way to separate *³He* from *⁴He*. At the current weight, the odds of surviving a full harvesting cycle were marginal. Marco floated a solution in the project chat. “Top off the *³He* with *⁴He*. Symbolic payload either way.”

Symbolic. Halden read the message and understood. Halden knew he needed experienced engineers to get through this phase. Helion Dynamics had the engineering expertise and the drive to push this project forward, but Bastos had left him with strict orders not to involve them.

Halden took a walk down the street. He stopped at his favorite taqueria, *La Esquina*, painted yellow with blue trim. He didn’t order anything, just asked to use the phone. The manager let Halden use his office. Calling Raines in Geneva was too risky. He called the operator in Unalaska and got the phone number for the Helion Dynamics storage facility. He navigated the directory to Raines’ extension and left a voicemail. 

Halden gave the manager twenty bucks and his number, offering another twenty every time he relayed a message. The manager nodded. “Si, No problem.”

Raines got the voicemail. He called Elin. “Do you remember Terrence Halden, the heavy lift guy? I got a message from him. He’s taking a shot. The kids at JRI have worked out a plan, but they are going to need an adult to help.”

“You know Bastos won’t want me within a hundred miles of Baracoa. What can I do?”

“Can you talk to him? He left me a number.”

Halden just got back to the building when he received a message from the taqueria manager. “*Hola, Señor* Halden, *Doctora* Elin Sarai has called. She left a number.”

“I’m on my way.”

Halden didn’t even sit down. He sent her the designs as he walked back to *La Esquina*. At the restaurant, he called her back from their office. “Dr. Sarai?” he stumbled. He never thought he would talk to her. 

A moment of silence. Elin was scanning through the files. “Interesting concept. I may be able to help build that. I have a shop that specializes in limited-run, hard-to-manufacture prototypes. How quickly can you get me solid specifications?”

“They aren’t making solid specs; they are sharing ideas. Recently, it has started to overwhelm them. People were even talking about bringing back a bottle of helium without purifying the isotope.”

“Maybe I can help.”

And that was it. He looked at the waves on his walk back. The sea hadn’t changed. The clouds were still dragging themselves over the edge of the horizon. Inside, everything was shifting.

A week later, Halden waited by the whiteboard, hands behind his back. The room was packed already. Several postdocs, engineers from the cryogenics group, and a bunch of interns were eager for the next talk. The project was frustrating, but it also felt like the single most important problem on Earth. The morning sun over Baracoa flooded in through the wide louvered windows, pooling light across the tiled floor, throwing long shadows under the chalk trays. People were chatting, arguing over details, talking about designs.

Then Dr. Elin Sarai stepped through the door, and everything stopped.

She wasn’t tall, but she moved like someone who had never been second-guessed. No lab coat. No prepared slides. Just the calm certainty of someone who had already done the thing the rest of them were still designing. Her presence sent a shock through the room. Not noise, not energy. Silence. Reverent, involuntary. A couple of them actually stood up. One nodded stiffly and muttered something reverent. Another bowed. Halden didn’t smile. He waited, then spoke her name as though it needed no title.

“This is Elin.”

That was all. He stepped aside. She looked around, scanning their faces like she was reading field data.

“I’ve read your plans,” she said. “Conceptually, it’s good work. You’ve shown flexibility from core concept to prototype which senior engineers still struggle with.”

They sat straighter.

“The thing you must stay true to, in the midst of this flexibility, is your purpose. If this vehicle goes up and brings back a bottle of *⁴He* with trace *³He*, the world will see a meaningless gesture. If it brings back one kilogram of high-purity, Jovian *³He*, we wake up the next day in a different world.”

She paused.

“You’re building the first example of an extraction supply chain for fusion-grade material. Do not think of this as a mock-up. This is every bit as important as Ilha Fusão.”

The tension in the room shifted. Before, it had been the strain of trying to live up to a concept. Now it was the recognition of obligation. Their work had consequences.

“The demand for isotope feedstock is set to jump. China’s terawatt reactor burns four tons per month. We have twenty more on order. Lunar extraction is slow and inefficient. You get one kilogram back from Jupiter, one real kilogram, measured and sealed, and it will no longer be a dream, it will be demand.”

Halden finally spoke again. His voice was level.

“If we design a system that can deliver one kilogram, we will surely fail. Too many things can go wrong. The system has to be designed to survive long enough to deliver ten kilograms. Plenty can go wrong once it gets there. If you design for ten, we have a much better chance of getting at least one.”

The earlier proposal to top the tank off with *⁴He* to “simulate delivery” had evaporated. That kind of thinking wouldn’t survive in a room where Dr. Elin Sarai expected action. 

The room became a working room again. They weren’t guessing anymore. They were delivering specs to Dr. Sarai. In the quiet moments between tasks, a few still stole glances at her. That she had read their work and taken it seriously was an overwhelming validation.

Elin worked with them for an hour before leaving. Halden left a few moments later, and went to his office first. 

Elin arrived first. She looked like someone waiting for a ferry, not the engineer behind the largest change in electric power since Edison. 

The restaurant had once served construction crews during the early buildout of the JRC headquarters, and had never entirely closed. It served straightforward Cuban fare: rice, beans, fried plantains, slow-cooked pork. More importantly, the people who still ate there didn’t ask questions. It was a good place to be unseen.

Halden slipped in five minutes later. He ordered without looking at the menu and took the seat across from her.

Elin wasted no time. “This prototype is good,” she said. “But I don’t care about one kilogram or even a hundred. I want scale. I want to see if this pipe dream of yours can give me a hundred tons a month. I want production.”

Halden leaned back. “That’s not how the JRC management sees it. They need a headline. Something that photographs well and carries no risk.”

Elin didn’t bother to look surprised. “They don’t want helium,” she said. “They want relevance. I’ve met these people.”

“I report to them. My hands are tied.”

“So play along. Just don’t forget the real work. You’re not building a story. You’re building an economy.”

He watched her carefully. “I know the difference. But I can’t bring this to them. Not directly. Not yet.”

“That’s fine,” she said. “Bring it to me.”

They ate slowly. The pork was heavy, the plantains sweet. No one at the other tables seemed to notice them.

When the check came, Elin paid in cash.

She stood, adjusting her satchel, and glanced back at him one last time. “You’ll need allies, Halden. More than you’ve got now. When it’s time to move, don’t do it alone.”

Then she left, walking to the edge of the plaza and stepping onto the bus headed for Santiago de Cuba. From there, a hopper would take her to Biloxi, where the next terawatt tokaplex was already under construction.

Halden stayed behind, finishing his coffee. The restaurant was quiet again, the world unchanged. But the message was clear. The fiction had a deadline. After that, it had to be real.

## **Chapter 34: Proof**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |

JRI launched the prototype on a Wednesday without ceremony. The experiment itself had changed shape over the last six months. The dirigible had become a nuclear ramjet with a single centrifugal compressor and a palladium alloy membrane venting hydrogen. Isotopic separation was built into the first fifty miles of the straw. The Larmor resonance RF heater from a lunar helium extractor was used to keep the *³He* warm while the *⁴He* condensed on the sides of the straw. It would eventually plug up and need to be drained, but that was fine for a short term project.

Lunar Tug had their type I cargo ships ready by launch, and were happy to carry the gear to Jupiter. The new ships made the flight in six months.

Savic did not expect it to work. He had already explained that to his advisor, Dr. Miriam Vance. The atmosphere was hostile and the radiation could destroy it. The ramjet was operating too hot to survive. Dr. Vance scheduled his thesis defense, anyway. 

It did work. The tether held. The helium climbed up the tube. It was slow, less than fifty grams per hour, but it was pure. By the fourth day, they had bottled five kilograms of *³He* and the ramjet was still running.

A week later, the tip of the intake cone melted. The change in aerodynamics destroyed the ramjet within a minute. Without thrust, the ramjet tore free of the tether and descended into Jupiter’s depths. This was the expected end, the only possible end. 

The helium in the tether continued up past synchronous, to the platform. There it was pumped into a simple K-bottle. When the tether was empty, the platform released it. Without the tether holding it back, the platform went into an elliptical orbit that brought it past Callisto’s orbit. Lunar Tug had been waiting for it there, and moved to intercept. As they approached, the platform shot the K-bottle towards the Lunar Tug ship. It was caught in a net and brought into the cargo bay. The platform continued on its elliptical orbit, going a little higher, then descending on an arc that intersected the atmosphere. It was no longer necessary.

Savic went over the process and showed slides of the catch at the end of his defense.  Then his committee asked him to step out of the room. Behind closed doors, Dr. Vance asked a simple question. “Has any grad student at the JRI accomplished nearly as much?”

When Savic returned to the room, Dr. Vance popped the cork on a bottle of champagne. 

Meanwhile, the Lunar Tug ship burned hard to get out of the Jovian gravity well and brought the bottle back to LEO. There it was transferred to a descender and dropped into Earth’s atmosphere. It was recovered at Punta de Maisi. 

Twelve kilograms of high-purity *³He* from Jupiter was flown to Lisbon. A tiltrotor brought it to the Ilha Fusão tokaplex. Cameras swarmed. The Prime Minister of Portugal gave a speech that managed to include both Columbus and climate change in the same breath. Then came the moment, the ceremonial coupling of the feed line. The valve was turned, taking *³He* feed from Jovian helium.

Back in Jamal, JRI had a graduation ceremony.   Nikola Savic, Rithica Patel, Marco Bianchi, and Jason Cole were all awarded their Ph.D.s. On a large screen behind the stage, the events at Ilha Fusão were playing.

Power output from Ilha Fusão remained stable. Sensors lit green across the board. For the first time, a fusion reactor ran on Jovian *³He*. The press reported that it was stable, self-sustaining, quiet. Elin laughed to herself. Of course the reactor didn’t care where the *³He* came from, but news had to make it sound dramatic. 

It was exciting to her, too. This proved they could pull gas from Jupiter. No one had really believed that. Well, Raines had, but that was mostly because he needed it and didn’t know any better. He did not understand the gravity well problem, the radiation, the harsh atmosphere. Elin thought about the irony. The JRC, which seemed to Helion Dynamics fully intent on preventing mining on Jupiter, had actually found a solution that neither she nor anyone else at Helion Dynamics would ever have considered. It was because of JRC that this project would work.

Headlines followed in waves. *“Jupiter’s Fire.”* *“A New Helium Age.”* *“Lisbon Lights the Future.”* Financial analysts who had once called Jovian *³He* a vanity project now rebranded it as inevitable. Everyone wanted to know when the next bottle would arrive.

The world, it turned out, had been waiting for an excuse to believe.

The success hit JRC like a concussion. They’d managed the Jovian mining program as a political asset, expensive, photogenic, and safely in the distant future. The kind of initiative you could gesture toward in budget hearings without the burden of actual delivery. Now there was helium in a tank, power on a grid, and headlines naming them the architects of a new energy age.

Suddenly, they had to act like they meant it.

Internal memos pivoted overnight from risk language to rollout frameworks. Leadership scrambled for talking points. A new office was carved out of the executive floor, glass-walled and overlit, and handed to Halden with a congratulatory nod. The gesture was meant to look like reward, but it was containment, an attempt to box the fire they’d accidentally lit.

Halden knew what this meant. He took the office. He made no speeches. But the whiteboards at JRI started filling faster. 

Publicly, JRC embraced the future. They released videos touting their long-term vision. They featured archival footage of internal meetings that had never taken place. Executives who’d once argued *³He* was impossible now recast themselves as visionaries. The whole thing had always been part of the plan, they insisted. Jovian *³He* was well understood by JRC, and they were just waiting for the engineers at JRI to finally address the problem. 

But the pressure was enormous. With the headlines came demand. Production targets. Timelines. Questions from sovereign energy agencies. JRC, once the architects of risk assessment, now found themselves required to deliver. And they had no idea how to scale what had been, just months ago, a fringe experiment.

So they leaned on JRI. Hard. The message was clear. Commercialize this right now. Turn it into something that ships quarterly numbers. Halden, in his new office, nodded politely, and said he understood.

Halden sat alone in the new office, surrounded by silence and expensive materials. The glass was low-reflection and ballistic-rated. The walls were layered in real hardwood. A print of a tokamak torus hung on the wall. It was glossy, oversized, and meaningless. The desk was deep and shiny, the kind reserved for signatures and staged photos. 

The office was not about status. It was about visibility. The open walls, the proximity to the boardroom, the curious placement of the security sensors, he had stepped into a place where his privacy was no longer tolerated. They didn’t expect him to disappear again into the back rooms of JRI or meet Elin at the café where the rice and plantains were still served from tin pots. Those days were over.

He now had an assistant. Her name was Liora. Young, competent, unreadable. She had been hired directly by the board and reported to them in parallel. Her role was framed as support for scheduling, outreach, travel, whatever Halden needed to enable him to deliver. It was clear from her first day that she had another task, to ensure the board knew Halden’s decisions before Halden made them.

She asked questions lightly. She took careful notes. She had access to his calendar and kept it tightly managed. Her desk was directly outside his door. She offered coffee, then logged his answer.

Halden smiled through it all. He gave Liora every answer she asked for, then made sure to ask her questions back. Not to intimidate. He had no interest in a power game, and he knew he wouldn’t win that, anyway. He just wanted to see how closely she was watching, and how deeply she understood.

She was good. He appreciated that. It made things easier.

The board had reasserted control. They would not be left behind again. So they had elevated him, encased him in transparency, and attached a quiet observer.

Halden smiled again. Not performatively. Not defiantly. It was simply the smile of a man who recognized the shape of the world he now lived in.

And was already planning his next move.

Halden’s visit to JRI was announced a week in advance, scheduled down to the minute by Liora. She handled the logistics, vetted the agenda, and ensured every presentation aligned with the board’s expectations. She vetted every attendee. The mood at the institute was respectful, but clearly cautious. JRI had once existed outside the orbit of executive attention. That era was over.

The conference room was sunlit and scrubbed clean of clutter. A small cadre of engineers and researchers presented their ideas. They spoke in turns, showing modular designs, projected scaling curves, and flexible deployment models. The slides were crisp. The numbers were plausible. They spoke the language of commercialization now.

Halden nodded throughout. He asked a few pointed questions, mostly about throughput assumptions and power budgets. He thanked them when they finished, complimented their discipline. Then, turning to Liora, he said, “Let’s come again next week. Same team.”

She made a note.

As they left the room, Halden paused at the doorway, exchanged brief goodbyes, and stepped out into the corridor.

On the table behind him, unnoticed by his assistant, a single piece of paper remained.

It was not formatted. It bore no heading. Just a line of text in Halden’s careful handwriting:

Call Elin

Below that, a number.

Savic, still standing near the screen, picked it up and read it twice. No one else had noticed. The presentations had been pro forma, intended to look good to the board when they read Liora’s notes. But this note was for them. 

By the time he looked toward the hallway, Halden was already gone.

Within a week, the vacations began.

Officially, the rest periods were both earned and necessary time off after the long push for the first tethered collector. Unofficially, no one asked questions. One engineer scheduled a trip to Madurai. Another took time in Biloxi. A materials specialist found herself rerouted through a research exchange program in Malta. One by one, they slipped out of the official workflow for a couple weeks. No one raised alarms. Liora logged the absences, but saw nothing particularly noteworthy. Their replacements were already trained, briefed, and eager.

No one of them went to the same place twice. But each met someone. Dr. Elin Sarai had a long memory and a short tolerance for bureaucracy. She knew who could build, and who was just waiting for permission. Her introductions were quiet and deliberate.

In São Paulo, a propulsion tech found himself seated next to Catherine Dahl at a private lunch. She listened, asked two questions, and pulled out a slate. Her sketches were brutal, elegant, and actionable.

From the base on Ceres, Dr. Knox briefed another JRI fellow on radiation shielding and passive cooling systems. The meeting was ten minutes long. The packet he handed over was three hundred pages.

Across these meetings, the design began to crystalize. Not by committee. Not through official channels. But through the steady cross-weaving of minds too tired to wait for permission.

By the end of the month, JRI was a different organization. Officially, they were still a white-paper institute. But beneath the paper trail, they were building something real.

## 

## **Chapter 35: Split Rigs**

| Price of *³He* |  |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Current Production** |  |
| **Current Consumption** |  |
| **Time Since Start** |  |

Halden knew better than to try to run the project. He became a translator.  He put Dr. Savic in charge of design. After graduation, Savic took time off to see family. Only Halden knew he went to Biloxi. 

Elin brought him to her Back Bay facility. “Manufacturing is not just mostly logistics.  Logistics is at least a hundred twenty percent of the game. Fabrication is only a distraction.”

“JRC has no facilities.”

“So you find people who do.  And don’t expect just manufacturing. They will deliver expertise. Lean on them.”

“You didn’t do that. You built everything right here.”

“At first I did, but when I started moving operations off site, we became much more productive. Half the land here used to be metal warehousing.  Now I let Planetary Metals handle all that, and we are much more productive.  Don’t do what I did. Copy my successes.”

When Savic returned, he subcontracted the orbital platform component to Navantia, a Spanish company with a long record in pressurized structures and naval-grade hulls. Their space division handled segment fabrication.

Each segment was as large as current launch systems could manage, just under the payload mass and diameter limits for a heavy-lift orbital insertion. The segments went by boat to JRC’s Punta de Maisí launch facility, where they were lifted individually to the Lunar Tug LEO hub.

The Split Rig program had no major failures. Engineering reviews passed. Procurement delays were minimal. Logistics bottlenecks seemed to resolve themselves. This, more than anything, made JRC uneasy. Nothing could run this smoothly.

Halden knew what made him look effective. Someone kept intercepting weak points before they failed. Vendors were replaced before delays turned critical. Material tolerances were corrected before fabrication. Load structures were rebalanced just ahead of final simulation. No one said how it happened. But the pattern was visible in metadata. Subtle edit trails. Changes logged at the last minute.

That someone was Elin. She was never listed, never named, but always present. Her corrections ran ahead of the project like a shock front. Quiet, systematic, and plausibly deniable.

He was not allowed to contact her directly. Any trace of correspondence would end the relationship. JRC would read it as confirmation that Halden wasn’t in control, and control was his only real capital.

So he worked with it. If a vendor spec changed, he approved it without delay. If an engineer flagged a surprising correction, he noted it and moved on. He never asked where it came from. Never said thank you. He simply treated each intervention as luck. And his luck held.

By the time the platform neared completion, Halden’s internal evaluations had begun to shift. No longer just competent. Now strategic, resilient, effective under pressure. JRC flagged him as a potential executive.

He read the memo, deleted it, and gave no indication he’d seen it.

He knew the truth; it wasn’t his project. It was Elin’s. He just kept the chair warm.

The tether weaving system sat on the space platform itself, eight machines, mounted like outriggers, continuously extruding structural cable, four down into Jupiter’s upper atmosphere. Four up to the space platform. Three machines in each direction built solid braided tethers. The fourth was tubular, the straw that moved the helium.

The raw materials were shipped separately and sourced through different channels. The polymer came from Earth. It was a two-part epoxy, engineered for extreme tensile loading, rapid in-vacuum curing, and thermal resilience. JRC sourced it through duPont. Delivery was handled by Lunar Tug, which managed all outbound mass cargo from Earth to Callisto. The epoxy was packed into reinforced drums with RFID-linked seals. Punta de Maisí heavy lift moved them into the LEO hub, and Lunar Tug brought them to Callisto.

The yarn came from Mars. Lafevre had underbid Earth-based rivals for the carbon nanotube filament contract, partly on delivered price, mostly on quality. Their lines were cleaner, more uniform, and their facility had the vacuum extrusion capacity to scale without bottlenecks. The Martian yarn was packed into dense transport drums and launched from the Olympus Mons mass driver. The driver was extended and powered up to get it to the Jovian gravity well. From there, a navigating rocket took it on a transfer arc to Callisto, where tenders brought it in.

At Callisto, the drums were staged in orbital storage before being transferred to the tenders. Tenders could carry six drums at a time, one epoxy and five yarn. They brought the drums to the weaving platform. Automated mixers combined the epoxy on demand; filament feeders unspooled the yarn, feeding it into the braid channel with high-precision tension control. Each meter of tether contained Earth and Mars, resin and filament, locked in structural code, laid down strand by strand. 

The light in the polymer lab was flat and violet, filtered through silica cladding to blunt the Martian sun. Dr. Marc Lafevre was watching the production numbers. Operations were fourteen percent ahead of schedule. 

Across the room, one of his chemists, Inaya Cho, adjusted the alignment on a thin scaffold where a carbon nanotube thread had been stretched and exposed to cycles of heat, pressure, and trace reactive gases.

“Three thousand hours,” she said. “Then it starts to pit. At four thousand, you lose twelve percent of tensile strength. At six, the surface is flaking oxides.”

Lafevre didn’t answer immediately. The Jovian lower stratosphere, particularly near the water cloud band, was more chemically aggressive than JRC had modeled. Tethers made of carbon would not survive indefinite exposure.

He looked at the stress curve again, “What do you propose?”

Inaya stepped back from the rig. “Boron nitride nanotubes.”

He nodded. “BNNT has lower tensile strength, but with enough crosslinking, we can hit the load targets. It won’t oxidize the way carbon does in that soup.”

“Cost?”

“Maybe ten times higher. But not insane. And we only need it as a shell on the last thousand miles. I’ll send a note to Halden. He will understand.”

He drafted the memo himself. It was short, formal, with data appendices and a polite recommendation to coat or substitute the last thousand miles of the tether with boron filament. The overall budget impact was only about five percent.

No reply came that day. After a month, he began to wonder if it had ever been delivered. Then he got his answer.

**JRC Internal Correspondence**  
**Re. Proposal from Noctis Landing \- Dr. Marc Lafevre**  
**To. Halden, Split Rig Director**  
**From. Anya Choudhury, Budget Oversight**  
Per our earlier exchange, Dr. Marc Lafevre has submitted an unsolicited design deviation regarding the tether material. He proposes replacing the contracted carbon nanotube yarn with a new boron nitride nanotube variant synthesized under controlled vacuum on Mars.

While it may sound like a safer path, it threatens the kind of budget and schedule overruns this project cannot afford. Switching to BNNT, regardless of potential performance gains, would introduce a new qualification cycle, trigger fresh regulatory review, and potentially nullify two binding procurement agreements with Earth-side partners. More critically, the substitution would open the door to renewed political scrutiny from stakeholders who already view the Martian contract as overreach.

Rather than risk the budget and timeline of this project, we will follow the original spec provided by JRI. We will not rebalance material sourcing mid-cycle. Every change feeds the perception that JRC lacks process discipline. That cannot happen here.

Please encourage Lafevre to focus on quality control and filament consistency, which will surely resolve the issue.

A.

Lafevre read the memo.  He posted it on the wall and got back to work.    
When Cho saw the memo, she found him immediately. “They rejected the proposal.  So that’s it?”  
”This is a JRC project.  We are just here to make the yarn.”
